# Mystical Dimensions of Islam — takeaways

*Source: Annemarie Schimmel, *Mystical Dimensions of Islam* (The University of North Carolina Press,
Chapel Hill; copyright page prints `© i g75` [text-layer?: 1975]; this is the 35th Anniversary
Edition, with a new Foreword by Carl W. Ernst, `© 2 0 1 1` [text-layer?: 2011]). 543 pages,
transcribed `text_layer` on 2026-09-14; chunked into 29 chunks of ~8,000 words and noted
2026-09-24; consolidated 2026-09-26. Provenance in `out/ocr/schimmel_mystical_dimensions_of_islam/`.
Page references are PDF page indices; folio = printed page number where known, and on this book
**folio = PDF − 31** with zero violations measured across all 29 chunks, back matter included.
Reviewed: no.*

> **Three mapping questions this document does NOT answer, and must not be read as answering.**
> Imprint cities, the missing province keys (`sind`/`sindh`/`punjab`/`balochistan`/`kashmir`), and
> bare index entries were all declined by the Pass-1 workers, consistently and with the ids each
> reversal would take enumerated on one line. They are Rauf's to rule (CLAUDE.md RULE 5) and the
> cost of each decline is itemised under **Doubts and review points**. Nothing here was reversed on
> a consolidator's judgement.

## What this book is

Lecture notes turned standard handbook: Schimmel says the book grew out of "the notes that formed the basis of several courses on Sufism—notes that consist both of literary evidence and of personal experiences with numerous friends in the Islamic East, mainly in Turkey and in Pakistan" (p. 27, folio XXIV), and it sets out to "delineate some main features of Sufism, both historically and phenomenologically" while declaring at the outset that to write about Sufism "is an almost impossible task" that will "yield no result that satisfies everybody" (p. 26, folio xxiii). Its apparatus is deliberately thin — footnotes and bibliography were kept small "since this book is intended primarily for the general reader" (p. 22, folio xix) — and this edition's foreword concedes that Schimmel's work "avoided 'sociology,'" focused "on the poetic and the ideal rather than the realm of society and politics where Sufism has in fact been contested and transformed," and gave "little consideration to mysticism's social history" (p. 20, folio XVII); that is precisely the register a shrine archive needs, and where the book does supply it — chapter 6's "Community Life" (pp. 259–273) and the Index of Subjects' roughly 120 glossed practice terms (pp. 528–543) — it is unusually full. **Reliability:** 543 PDF pages, route `text_layer`, `folio = PDF − 31` throughout including the back matter; the one damage class a reader must know is the `fi`/`fl` ligature loss, which produces **real English words and so leaves nothing looking wrong** — `denned` = defined, `Suns` = Sufis, and `Sanaa's` = Sanāʾī's, which reads as the name of a city — and no siglum (`L 31`, `T 2:113`, `H 293`, `AD 100`, `AM no. N`) and no internal cross-reference is ever a page of this book.

## Shrines in the archive this book informs

*Twenty-eight archive rows carry site- or person-specific material and are given their own
subsection below, most material first. **Chapter 8, `SUFISM IN INDO-PAKISTAN` (PDF 375–433), feeds
twenty-four of them, and those stand first**; the four fed only from outside chapter 8 follow. A
further seventy-one rows are reached only at place level under ruling (a) or at person level under
the 22 September many-row sub-case, and are gathered in one run at the end of this section, each
bullet head carrying every one of its ids on a single physical line. **Ninety-nine distinct archive
ids appear in this section in all.** Read each subsection's naming line before using any bullet:
much of what follows is author-only or literary reference, which the 18 September ruling admits but
which must never be read as the book describing a shrine. Page references are PDF page indices with
the printed folio where the notes give it; `folio = PDF − 31` throughout this book. Every
`[text-layer?]` flag is the Pass-1 workers', carried onto the word it was attached to.*

**Roster of the twenty-eight rows with their own subsection, in the order they appear below** — chapter-8-fed first: **bhit-bhit-shah**, **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**, **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed**, **data-darbar**, **mazar-of-bulleh-shah**, **mausoleum-of-waris-shah**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine**, **garh-maharaja-shorkot**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala**, **shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori**, **shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht**, **shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan**, **shrine-of-akhund-darweza-baba**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir**, **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat**, **sial-sharif**; then, not fed by chapter 8: **mazar-e-iqbal**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**.

### bhit-bhit-shah — Bhit (Bhit Shah)

The most heavily cited Pakistani figure in the book, named in thirteen of the twenty-nine chunks.
His name is printed at least fourteen ways: `Shah cAbdul Latlt`, `Shah c Abdu D l-Latif`,
`Shah cAbduDl-Latif`, `Shah cAbduDl-LatIf`, `Shah cAbdu3l-Latif`, `Shah ^bdu^l-Latif`,
`Shah 'vAbdu^l-Latlf`, `Shah "vAbdu^l-Latif`, `Shah 'Abdu'l-Latif`, `Shah "Abdu'l-Latif`,
`Shah Abdu^l-Latif of Sind`, `Shah Latif`, `Schah Abdul Latif`, `Shah CAbdul Latif Bhitefi`, and —
inside Ernst Trumpp's title — `Sayyid c Abd-ul-Latif` [all text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf]; six of
those renderings fall within the five pages 420–424 alone (pp. 49, 144, 200, 216, 226, 244, 248,
265, 331, 358, 414, 418–424, 450, 482–496, 510, 525).

- **Saint**
  - Birth, with place and year: `Shah ^bdu^l-Latif was born into a family of mystics in 1689 in Hala,
    near present-day Hyderabad-Sind,` (p. 421, folio 390). `1689` prints cleanly in four digits.
  - **Two registers of his dates, both kept.** The running text gives `Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Bhit
    (1689-1752)` (p. 414, folio 383) and, separately, `His younger contemporary, Shah cAbduDl-Latif
    in Sind (d. 1752)` — `His` referring to `Sultan Bahu of the Punjab`, so the book places him as
    Sultan Bahu's junior contemporary (p. 200, folio 169). The `INDEX OF NAMES AND PLACES` prints the
    headword `Abdu3l-Lat!f Bhita3!, Shah (d. 1752),` [text-layer?: ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf Bhitāʾī, Shāh], with
    a detached ʿayn as a bare `c` on the line above (p. 510, folio 479). Elsewhere the book dates him
    only by century: `the eighteenth-century mystic` (p. 226, folio 195), `in the eighteenth century`
    (p. 265, folio 234), `the eighteenth-century poet` (p. 358, folio 327), `in Sind in the eighteenth
    century` (p. 171 material not in these notes — see p. 414, folio 383).
  - Ancestry: he is named as the descendant of `Shah cAbduDl-Karim`, whose poems are `closely related
    to Latif's poetry` (pp. 420–421, folios 389–390) — see **shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri**.
  - Wandering and the Hinglaj journey, with Schimmel's own hedge kept: `In his early years Latif
    wandered through the country in the company of a group of yogis and perhaps reached the sacred
    mountain Hinglaj in Balochistan, which is often mentioned as "the goal" in Sindhi mystical
    poetry.` (p. 421, folio 390).
  - Settlement: `He then settled down in Bhit, not far from his hometown, and gathered a number of
    disciples around him.` (p. 421, folio 390).
  - **No silsila is given anywhere in the book** — `(not stated)` at pp. 49, 144, 200, 226, 248, 265,
    331, 358, 414–424, 450, 494, 510.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - The **one** description of the tomb, and it is a craft attribution rather than a building
    account: the tomb is `covered with blue and white tiles (typical of Hala)` (p. 421, folio 390).
  - Beyond that sentence the book gives **no** ʿurs, **no** custodian, **no** endowment, **no**
    building date and **no** visitor number — explicitly `(not stated)` at pp. 144, 200, 216, 226,
    248, 265, 331, 358, 414–424, 450, 494, 510.
  - `Bhit` reaches most chunks only through apparatus, inside the title of a secondary study:
    `H. T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit: His Poetry, Life, and Times (1940; reprint ed., Oxford,
    1966).` (p. 216, folio 185, footnote 94; again p. 244, folio 213; and as the bibliography entry
    `Sorley, Herbert Tower. Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit: His Poetry, Life, and` / `Times. 1940. Reprint.
    Oxford, 1966.`, p. 495, folio 464). `1940` and `1966` print cleanly.
  - The index carries a **separate bare place entry** `Bhit Shah, 383, 390` (p. 513, folio 482). It
    is recorded and was **not** used to fire ruling (a) — the row is reached by the person rule
    instead. See `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - The epithet `Bhittai` is never used in running text; it appears only inside the footnoted title
    `Makhzan Shah ^Abdul Latlf Bhita?i` [text-layer?: Makhzan Shāh ʿAbdul Laṭīf Bhitāʾī] (p. 450,
    folio 419) and in the index headword (p. 510, folio 479).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **He recognises the boy Sachal**, and Schimmel undercuts the story in the same breath: `There is
    a story that once when a boy came to his convent, the mystic immediately recognized that he would
    one day "open the lid from those things" that he had kept secret.` — followed by `The anecdote
    may have been derived from the pen name Sachal used in his Persian poetry, namely, Ashikdr
    [text-layer?: Āshikār], "Open."` (p. 424, folio 393). The pairing is the material and should not
    be split.
  - **The Ḥallājian vision of the vegetable world**: `seeing in every tree and plant the vision of
    the suffering mystic, proclaiming anffl-haqq and therefore doomed to death` (p. 427, folio 306 as
    printed — the `folio = PDF − 31` rule gives 396).
- **Significance**
  - Schimmel's ranking: `The most outstanding master of popular Sufi poetry in Pakistan is, no doubt,
    Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Bhit. He is not the first in the long line of Sindhi mystical poets.`
    (p. 420, folio 389).
  - *Sufi / Kufi.* `still in eighteenth-century Sindhi mystical poetry the Sufi is called Id-kufl`
    [text-layer?: lā-kūfī]`, "non-Kufi," i.e., not bound to a particular religious rite.` (p. 49,
    folio 18), footnoted to Schimmel's own `"Shah cAbdul Latlt's Beschreibung des wahren Sufi," in
    Festschrift fur Fritz Meier, comp. Richard Gramlich (Wiesbaden, 1974` [text-layer?: undamaged]`).`
    (p. 49, folio 18).
  - *The camel and the nafs.* `it is only natural that poets all over the desert areas, like Shah c
    Abdu D l-Latif in Sind, are particularly fond of this comparison.` (p. 144, folio 113).
  - *The dry tree.* `devoted a whole chapter of his poetical work to the dry tree that needs the
    water of recollection to be revived.` (p. 200, folio 169). The chapter is not named —
    `(not stated)`.
  - *Iblīs as the one true lover.* `Even the poetry of the eighteenth-century mystic Shah
    cAbduDl-Latif in the remote province of Sind calls the reader to admire Iblis as the one true
    lover and to follow his example—a verse that has caused considerable puzzlement to the
    commentators.` (p. 226, folio 195), localised by footnote 6 to `Sur Yaman Kalyan` (p. 226,
    folio 195). The `°` of `Schah °Abdul Latif's` in that footnote is an **ʿayn**, not a digit.
  - *The Prophet as rain-cloud, twice.* `he may be compared, as Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Sind has done,
    to the cloud that sends down the life-giving rain to the thirsty fields, quickening what seemed
    to be dead.` (p. 248, folio 217); and `the poetry of Shah cAbdu3l-Latif contains a fine
    description of the Prophet through the image of the rain cloud, the symbol of divine mercy, whose
    help is implored for the Day of Judgment.` (p. 418, folio 387).
  - *Against the fetish of dervish dress.* `And in the eighteenth century Shah Abdu^l-Latif of Sind
    advised the true Sufi to throw his cap into the fire instead of boasting with it.` (p. 265,
    folio 234) — the sentence closes Schimmel's paragraph on the *tāj* and *khirqa*.
  - *The desert heroine's cry*, with the verse quoted in full: `O voice in the desert—as if the
    grouse were crying . . . / cry from the water's depths—it is the sigh of love. / O voice in the
    desert, like sound of a violin- / It is the song of love—only the people unaware took it / to be
    a woman's song.` (p. 331, folio 300), sourced by footnote 15 to `Shah c Abdu D l-Latif, "Sur
    Macdhurl," in Shdha jo risdld, ed. Kalyan B. Adwani (Bombay, 1958), i:lines 21-22.`
    [text-layer?: Sur Maʿdhūrī; Shāha jo risālo; `i:lines` = 1:lines] (p. 331, folio 300). Schimmel's
    gloss: `This mystery of transformation from chaste human love to the summit of divine love remains
    one of the central themes of great poetry in Iran and its neighboring countries` (p. 331,
    folio 300).
  - *Rūmī in his verse.* `Quotations from Rumi permeate the Sindhi verses of the eighteenth-century
    poet Shah cAbduDl-LatIf, and it would be easy to draw up an inventory of all the verses used in
    Indo-Pakistani literature written in the vernaculars that are inspired by the great mystic's
    work.` (p. 358, folio 327).
  - *Letter symbolism.* Quoted for a couplet on mīm and alif, introduced `says Shah ^bdu^l-Latif`:
    `Put a mlm` [text-layer?: mīm] `into your soul, / and an alif in front of it` (p. 450, folio 419),
    sourced to `Shah jo risdlo` [text-layer?: Shāh jo Risālo] and to Schimmel's Festschrift article
    (p. 450, folio 419). His name also carries the footnote for the alif-numerology of an unnamed
    `Sindhi Shia author` (p. 449, folio 418), cited to `Makhzan Shah ^Abdul Latlf Bhita?i` — **the
    chunk does not say whether that work is by him or about him** — (not stated) (p. 450, folio 419;
    and as a title-only bibliography entry, `Makhzan Shah CAbdul Latif Bhitefi. Hyderabad, Sind, 1954.
    In Sindhi.`, p. 485, folio 454).
  - *Generation.* Named as Bulleh Shah's Sindhi contemporary (p. 419, folio 388 — quoted under
    **mazar-of-bulleh-shah**).
  - *Works and reception, as the bibliography prints them.* `Shah c Abdul Latlf. Risdlo. Edited by
    Kalyan B. Adwani. Bombay, 195` / `In Sindhi.` — **the year is cut short at the right margin as
    `195` and the sentence-final period is missing; it is not completed** (p. 494, folio 463);
    `Kazi, Elsa, trans. "Risalo" of Shah Abdul Latif. Hyderabad, Sind, 1965.` (p. 483, folio 452);
    `Jotwani, Motilal. "Shah Latif: Man and Poet." Indian Literatures 6, no. 2 (1963).` (p. 482,
    folio 451); `Trumpp, Ernst. "Sorathi. Ein Sindhi-Gedichtaus dem grossen Divan des Sayyid` / `c` /
    `Abd-ul-Latif." Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenlandischen Gesell-` / `schaft 17 (1863).` —
    `Sindhi-Gedichtaus` printed without the space, the detached `c` an ʿayn (p. 496, folio 465);
    Schimmel, `"Schah Abdul Latif." Kairos, nos. 3-4 (1961).` and `"Shah cAbdul Latif's Beschreibung
    des wahren Sufi." In Fest-` / `schrift fur Fritz Meier, compiled by Richard Gramlich. Wiesbaden,`
    / `J` / `974-` — the year broken across two lines as `J` + `974-` (p. 493, folio 462). Also
    `An Urdu verse translation was made by Sheikh Ayaz (Hyderabad, Sind, 1963)` and `Elsa Kazi,
    Risalo of Shah Abdul Latif (Hyderabad, Sind, 1965)` (pp. 421–422, folios 390–391, footnote 75).
  - *Index apparatus.* The main entry's locator, quoted verbatim as the book's own printed folios:
    `113. l69- 195. 217, 234- 300, 327, 383, 387, 388, 389-93, 396, 397, 419` [text-layer?: 113, 169,
    195, 217, 234, 300, 327, 383, 387, 388, 389–93, 396, 397, 419] (p. 510, folio 479), with the
    sub-entry `Risdlo,` [text-layer?: Risālo] at `390-92` (p. 510, folio 479); the work is indexed
    again as `Shaha jo risalo ( c Abdu D l-Latif), 390-92` (p. 525, folio 494); and he stands in the
    cross-reference block `Shah. See cAbdu3l-Karim Bulrriwaro; cAbdu'l-Latif Bhita3!, Shah; c Abu
    cAli Qalandar, Shah; c lnayat Shahid, Shah; Ismail Shahid, Shah` (p. 525, folio 494). **These are
    the book's own index locators — printed folios, not PDF pages, and not converted.**
  - **A saying that must NOT be read as his.** The maxim closing "Saints and Miracles" — `It is
    better to restore one dead heart to eternal life than to restore to life a thousand dead bodies.`
    (p. 244, folio 213) — is introduced only as `the saint was right who said`, and its footnote gives
    Sorley's page, not an attribution. Schimmel does not say whose saying it is.
  - Earliest mention in the book is a bare footnote: footnote 23 at p. 49, folio 18, citing Schimmel's
    own article and nothing else.
  - The printed death date `(d. 1752)` is **identical to the one the index gives two pages later for
    `Bullhe Shah`** (p. 510, folio 479; p. 513, folio 482) — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

### shrine-of-sachal-sarmast — Shrine of Sachal Sarmast

Named `Sachal Sarmast` throughout, with no diacritic damage on the name itself; the densest treatment
is pp. 424–427, second only to Laṭīf in chapter 8 (pp. 414, 424–427, 383–399 in the index's folios,
491–492, 512–513, 524).

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of his death year stand side by side and are not reconciled.** The running text
    prints `Sachal Sarmast (d. 1826)` (p. 414, folio 383) and `(i 739-1826)` [text-layer?: 1739–1826]
    (p. 424, folio 393); the index prints `Sachal Sarmast (d. 1826), 383, 393-96, 397, 399` (p. 524,
    folio 493). **The archive row records 1827.** Both are kept.
  - The name explained, in the book's own words: `His name Sachal, a diminutive of sack
    [text-layer?: sach], "truth," seems to be an allusion to the word Haqq, "truth," as found in
    Hallaj's sentence anffl-Haqq [text-layer?: anāʾl-ḥaqq]; and the name Sarmast indicates that he was
    an "intoxicated" Sufi—and intoxicated he was indeed!` (p. 424, folio 393).
  - Life and languages: `Sachal Sarmast was a versatile mystic who led a solitary life; he wrote in
    Sindhi, Siraiki (the northern dialect of Sindhi), Urdu, and Persian, uttering whatever came into
    his heart with no thought for the consequences.` (p. 424, folio 393).
  - **No shrine, no Daraza, no Khairpur, no silsila anywhere in the book** — `(not stated)` at
    pp. 414, 424–427, 491–492, 524.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - The meeting-story is Laṭīf's, and Schimmel gives it a non-miraculous origin in the same paragraph
    (p. 424, folio 393) — quoted in full under **bhit-bhit-shah**.
  - His litany of the afflicted lovers, quoted at pp. 425–426, folios 394–395, names among the slain
    `Bullhe Shah`, `Jacfar`, `Sarmad`, `Mansur`, `Sheikh cAttar`, `Zakariya`, `Joseph`, `Shams`,
    `Sancan`, `Karmal` and `Bilawal`, and includes the line `hast killed / c / lnayat in the fighting
    arena` (p. 426, folio 395), which footnote 79 identifies as `c lnayat is the martyr-mystic of
    Jhok.` (p. 426, folio 395). The same footnote states flatly `Karmal and Bilawal have not yet been
    identified.` (p. 426, folio 395).
  - The self-riddle `Perhaps I am…` is quoted at p. 425, folio 394.
- **Significance**
  - Popularity in the present tense: `or the ardent poems of Sachal Sarmast (d. 1826), whose melodies
    and words are almost as popular today as they were 150 years ago.` (p. 414, folio 383).
  - Placed under Laṭīf: `destined to become a Sufi poet second only to Shah c Abdu D l-Latif.`
    (p. 424, folio 393).
  - His material and its unevenness: `Like his predecessors, he relied upon folk tales and made
    Sassul and Sohni his heroines; he did not, however, elaborate the subjects as much as Shah
    'Abdu^l-Latif did. His Persian and Urdu poetry is traditional and conventional in style and
    expression, but in his mother tongue his feeling overpowers him.` (p. 424, folio 393).
  - **Two epithets the book itself applies to him**, both reached only through index
    cross-references: `Attar of Sind. See Sachal Sarmast` — printed with a detached `c` (ʿayn) on the
    line above (p. 512, folio 481) — and `Ashikar. See Sachal Sarmast` (p. 513, folio 482); plus
    `Sarmast. See Sachal Sarmast` (p. 524, folio 493). The running text gives the first of these
    directly: `Sachal has been called "the cAttar of Sind," and in fact the influence of "Attar's
    imagery is clearly visible in his poetry.` (p. 426, folio 395).
  - Ḥallāj: `This implies his ardent love for Hallaj, whose name occurs hundreds of times in his
    verses.` (p. 427, folio 306 as printed — rule gives 396).
  - Schimmel's final estimate: `But none of the later mystical poets can match Sachal's intense glow;
    he is a vigorous poet, and the rhythms and energy, the intense yearning and love, give his verses
    an almost magical quality.` (p. 427, folio 306 as printed — rule gives 396).
  - Works as the bibliography prints them: `Sachal Sarmast. Dtwdn-i Ashikar. Edited by Makhdum Amir
    Ahmad.` / `Lahore, 1957. In Persian.` (p. 491, folio 460); `. Risdlo Sindhi. Edited by Othman Ali
    Ansari. Karachi, 1958. In` / `Sindhi.` (p. 491, folio 460); `. Siraiki kalam. Edited by Maulwi
    Hakim M. Sadiq Ranipuri.` / `Karachi, 1959. In Siraiki.` (p. 492, folio 461).
  - **He is not the `Sarmad` of this book.** `Hashmi, Bashir Ahmad. "Sarmad." Islamic Culture,
    1933-34.` (p. 479, folio 448) and the `Sarmad, the Jewish convert to Sufism (executed 1661) in
    Mogul India` of pp. 226 and 393–394 are a different man, and the Pass-1 workers refused the join
    at every occurrence.

### shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar — Shrine of Fariduddin Ganjshakar

Printed `Fariduddin`, `Fariduddm`, `Farld`, `Far id`, `Fartd`, `Farlduddln`, `Farid Ganj-i Shakar`,
`Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar of Pakpattan`, `Sheikh Farld Ganj-i Shakar` and `Shaikh Farid Ganj-i Shakar`
[text-layer?: Farīduddīn Ganj-i Shakar] (pp. 273, 310–311, 377–384, 411, 415, 461, 489, 514, 534). The
`t` of `Fartd` is **not** in the measured substitution table and was not repaired. **This is not the
`Farīduddīn ʿAṭṭār` of the false-friend list** — the book prints the distinguishing epithet
`Ganj-i Shakar`, and prints `cAttar` separately on the same pages.

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, kept side by side.** The running text prints
    `Bakhtiyar Kaki's successor was Fariduddm (d. 1265` [text-layer?: clean]`)` (p. 377, folio 346)
    and the index prints `Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar (d. 1265), 279,` / `346-48, 350, 352, 380, 384;
    mother of,` / `346, 43°` [text-layer?: 346, 430 — `43°` is **not resolved**: `°` has proved to
    stand for 0 and for 8 in this book] (p. 514, folio 483). **The archive row records 1266.**
  - Succession and move: `but because of the politically confused situation he left the capital and
    settled in the Punjab on the river Sutlej.` (p. 377, folio 346).
  - Formation: `Far id had been influenced in his early religious life by his pious mother.`
    (p. 377, folio 346). She is not named — **(not stated)**. Named again among the mothers who formed
    Sufi leaders: `Whether it be Farid Ganj-i Shakar's mother in India or cAbduDl-Qadir Gilam's`
    [text-layer?: ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Gīlānī's] `mother and aunt (N 628), there is no doubt that many elderly
    women in the families contributed to the spiritual formation of some of the great Sufi leaders.`
    (p. 461, folio 430).
  - Asceticism, with the book's own term: `He performed extremely difficult ascetic practices, among
    them the chilla ma'kusa, hanging upside down in a well and performing the prescribed prayers and
    recollections for forty days.` (p. 377, folio 346). The same practice under its other name is
    footnoted to him: footnote 19, `For the salat maqluba see Khaliq Ahmad Nizami, The Life and Times
    of Fartd Ganj-i Shakar (Aligarh, 1955), p. 179.` [text-layer?: ṣalāt maqlūba] (p. 273, folio 242)
    — **but the main text does not say Farīd himself performed it**, only that `some later Indo-Muslim
    saints` did (pp. 272–273, folios 241–242). `(not stated)`.
  - Family: `He did not care for his wives or children, eight of whom survived.` (p. 378, folio 347).
  - A judgement the book quotes without naming its source: `we may well believe that "no saint
    excelled him in his devotions and penances."` (p. 378, folio 347).
  - Named in the thirteenth-century roll of India: `the names of Mu c inuddm Chishti, BahaDuddin
    Zakariya Mul-` / `tani, Farld Ganj-i Shakar, Nizamuddin Auliya, Fakhruddin Iraqi, and many others
    tell of the spiritual glory of the thirteenth` (p. 310, folio 279) `century.` (p. 311, folio 280).
  - Contrasted with Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā: `the stern ascetic Farld, who gave no thought to any worldly
    needs and refused all governmental grants for his family and his disciples` (p. 383, folio 352).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - The place-name and its meaning: `His home has been known, ever since, as Pakpattan, "the ferry of
    the pure."` (p. 377, folio 346). The **toponym `Pakpattan` matches the single archive row of that
    place**, which is the same row the person rule gives; no second id attaches.
  - Pakpattan as a place of resort: `After his third visit to Pakpattan, he was appointed khalifa in
    Delhi.` (p. 379, folio 348) — of Nizāmuddīn.
  - The book gives **no** tomb, **no** ʿurs, **no** custodian, **no** building — `(not stated)`
    throughout (pp. 273, 310–311, 377–384, 411, 415, 461, 489, 514, 534).
- **Practices & Events**
  - The economy of his khānqāh is given by the book as the type case of early Chishti practice
    (p. 378, folio 347) — the detail sits in the Practices material, not here.
  - Succession: `Fariduddin invested seven khalifas with spiritual power; among his disciples, Jamal
    Hanswi and Nizamuddin must be counted as his special friends.` (p. 379, folio 348);
    `Farlduddln's outstanding khalifa, however, was Nizamuddin, whom he met in 1257` [text-layer?:
    clean]`, orHY a ^ew years before his death.` (p. 379, folio 348) — **the words after the date are
    badly damaged and are not reconstructed**; the date itself prints clean. The index records the
    office: `Khalifa ("vicegerent"): … of Fariduddm Ganj-i Shakar, 348` (p. 534, folio 503).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The surname legend**, with Schimmel's hedge kept: `His constant fasting was miraculously
    rewarded—even pebbles turned into sugar, hence his surname Ganj-i shakar, "sugar treasure."`
    (pp. 377–378, folios 346–347), followed by `Whether this charming legend is true or not, it shows
    the extreme importance Farld placed on ascetic exercises` (p. 378, folio 347).
  - **The dead-child tradition**, marked as legend by the book itself: `When he was informed that a
    child had died—so legend has it—he said: "If fate has so decreed and he dies, tie a rope around
    his feet and throw him out and come back!"` (p. 378, folio 347).
- **Significance**
  - *The vernacular claim the archive will want*, with Schimmel's own hedge: `Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar
    of Pakpattan is credited with having used a kind of Old Panjabi for his mystical songs.` (p. 415,
    folio 384); and, separately, `It is possible that Fariduddin composed a few lines of poetry in the
    local dialect, which would place him in the chain of those mystics who helped disseminate Sufi
    teachings in popular songs, thus influencing the population, particularly the women, who used to
    sing these simple verses while doing their daily work.` (p. 379, folio 348). **Both hedges —
    `is credited with`, `It is possible` — are the book's and are kept.**
  - *A living claim, stated in the present tense*: `A number of Punjabi tribes still claim to have
    been converted by Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar.` (p. 381, folio 350). No tribe is named —
    **(not stated)**.
  - *A proverb for patience.* In Mīr Dard's hagiography, `One of his biographers writes that "the
    mountain of his patience would have made Sheikh Farld Ganj-i Shakar bite his fingers from
    amazement like sugarcane,"` (p. 411, folio 380) — the praise is of Dard; Farīd is the measure, not
    the subject.
  - *As a subject of scholarship*: `Nizami, Khaliq Ahmad. The Life and Times of Shaikh Farid Ganj-i` /
    `Shakar. Aligarh, 1955.` (p. 489, folio printed `45°`).

### shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya — Shrine of Bahauddin Zakariya

Printed six ways in one chunk — `Baha'uddln Zakariya Multani`, `Baha^uddm Zakariya`, `BahaDuddin`,
`Baha'uddin`, `Baha/'uddm`, `BahjPuddin Zakariya` — and elsewhere `BahaDuddin Zakariya Mul-` / `tani`
and `Baha3uddm Zakariya MultanI` [all text-layer?: Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā Multānī]; the `D` and `^` are
the ʿayn/hamza damage class (pp. 310–311, 383–385, 512, 517, 521, 527).

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, both hedged by the book itself, kept side by side.** The running
    text gives `(d. ca. 1262` [text-layer?: clean]`)` (p. 383, folio 352) and the index gives
    `Baha^uddln Zakariya Multani (d. ca.` / `1267), 279, 352-54` (p. 512, folio 481). **The archive row
    records 1267.** The `ca.` is the book's own hedge in both places.
  - He is named as the figure through whom the Suhrawardiyya took hold: `An even greater Suhrawardiyya
    impact on Muslim religious life was made by Baha'uddln Zakariya Multani (d. ca. 1262), a
    contemporary of Farlduddin Ganj-i Shakar.` (p. 383, folio 352); the two men `were separated by only
    a few hundred miles` (p. 383, folio 352), and the book stages them as a deliberate contrast of
    styles of life.
  - Style of life: `the well-to-do landlord Baha/'uddm` [text-layer?: Bahāʾuddīn]`, who looked after
    the needs of his family and never failed to keep a supply of grain in his house.` (p. 383,
    folio 352).
  - Wealth and succession: `His accumulation of wealth was sufficient to make him the target of
    accusations by other Sufis, but his sons, unlike the sons of most of the early Chishti saints,
    followed in his path; the succession in the Suhrawardiyya became, generally, hereditary.` (p. 383,
    folio 352).
  - Affiliation as the book gives it, **inside the quoted Iraqi story and so in the story's wording,
    not Schimmel's own**: `this order followed the rule of Shihabuddin Suhrawardi whose favoured pupil
    Baha^uddm was, and Suhrawardi's rule was that the devotee should occupy himself only with the
    recitation of the Koran and the expounding of tradition.` (p. 384, folio 353).
  - Named in the thirteenth-century roll of India (p. 310, folio 279 – p. 311, folio 280 — quoted under
    **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar**), and paired with Muʿīnuddīn Chishtī as the second pole of the
    period: `the same period during which Muclnuddln Chishtl and BahjPuddin Zakariya were preaching
    divine love and union` (p. 385, folio 354).
  - His disciple founds the Uch line: `One of Zakariya's disciples, Sayyid Jalaluddin Surkhpush …
    settled in Ucch` (p. 385, folio 354).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **A living practice at the tomb, stated in the present tense**: `The tender and intoxicating love
    poems that the Persian poet composed are still being sung by Pakistani musicians at the door of the
    master's tomb in Multan.` (p. 384, folio 353) — of Fakhruddīn ʿIrāqī's poems. The book does not name
    the tomb, give its quarter, or date the practice — **(not stated)**.
  - No building date, no custodian, no ʿurs, no endowment anywhere — **(not stated)** (pp. 310–311,
    383–385, 512, 517, 521, 527).
- **Practices & Events**
  - Hospitality regime: `As opposed to the open table in the poor Chishti khanqahs, Baha'uddln was more
    formal and had fixed hours for visitors who were invited to partake in meals.` (p. 383, folio 352).
  - Relation to power: `And he was willing to mix freely with members of the ruling classes—just as Abu
    Hafs c Umar Suhrawardi` [text-layer?: Abū Ḥafṣ ʿUmar Suhrawardī] `himself had served the caliph
    an-Nasir.` (p. 383, folio 352).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The Iraqi cell story**, introduced by the book as `The story goes:` and quoted at length from
    Arberry's translation (footnote 11, `c lraqt, Song of the Lovers, no. 15; cf. N 602`): Iraqi sits
    ten days admitting nobody, sings the `sdqi's languorous eye` verse on the eleventh day, the
    brothers complain, the saint answers `that this was prohibited to them, but not to him`, and on
    hearing the poem sung in the bazaar and the taverns declares `"His affair is complete,"` and invests
    him with `the mystic robe` (pp. 383–384, folios 352–353). Schimmel's verdict on the episode:
    `Baha'uddin, with the spiritual insight of a true saint, acknowledged Iraqi's greatness and true
    love.` (p. 384, folio 353).
- **Significance**
  - The poet Fakhruddīn ʿIrāqī `lived in his entourage for nearly twenty-five years` (p. 383,
    folio 352), entering the order at Multan: `In Multan he attached himself to BahaDuddin, to whom he
    dedicated some impressive qasldas` [text-layer?: qaṣīdas] (p. 383, folio 352); and
    `Soon after Baha'uddln Zakariya's death, clraql left Multan for Kenya` [text-layer?: Konya]`, where
    he met Sadruddin Qonawi and perhaps Jalaluddin Rumi.` (p. 385, folio 354).
  - *Index apparatus.* Reached twice as a cross-reference target: `Multam. See Baha3uddm Zakariya
    MultanI` (p. 521, folio `49°` [text-layer?: 490]) — **the headword is the nisba `Multam`
    [text-layer?: Multānī], not the city `Multan`, which is a separate entry two lines above it** — and
    `Zakariya. See Baha'uddin Zakariya Multanl` (p. 527, folio 496). Neither cross-reference carries a
    locator, a date or a site.
  - A second index entry points inside his folio run: `lmaduddin (disciple of Multani), 353`
    [text-layer?: ʿImāduddīn] (p. 517, folio 486), whose locator `353` falls inside the `352-54` the
    main entry gives. **The book does not say which Multānī is meant** — (not stated).
  - **The nisba `Mul-` / `tani` was not expanded into Multan's eight rows** (p. 310, folio 279) — see
    `## REVIEW (worker A)` and the place-level run below.

### dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed — Dargah / Roza Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed

Printed `Shah clnayat Shahid of Jhok`, `Shah clnayat`, `c / lnayat`, `Shah c lnayat of Jhok` and, in
the index, `lnayat Shahid, Shah` with the ʿayn detached as a bare `c` on the line above [all
text-layer?: Shāh ʿInāyat Shahīd] (pp. 426, 428, 493, 517, 518, 525). This carries the fullest
shrine-political narrative in the book. **The book's own epithet `Shahid` in the index headword is
what separates him from the seventeenth-century Sindhi poet `Shah ʿInāt`** — see `## REVIEW
(worker A)`.

- **Saint**
  - Placed: `An interesting case in Sind is that of Shah clnayat Shahid of Jhok, which is not far
    from Hyderabad (his name occurs in Sachal's litany).` (p. 428, folio 397).
  - **Death year, agreeing across the book's two registers and with the archive**: the running text
    gives `Shah clnayat was executed in January 1718.` (p. 428, folio 397) — `1718` prints cleanly —
    and the index gives `lnayat Shahid, Shah (d. 1718), 395, 397` (p. 517, folio 486).
  - Reached again through the index cross-reference block `Shah. See … c lnayat Shahid, Shah …`
    (p. 525, folio 494), which carries no locator, date or site.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - The book names the **dargah at Jhok** as an institution and as an economic actor: `The Sufi
    sheikhs and the feudal lords in the country lost large numbers of disciples and workers, whereas
    the dargah in Jhok flourished.` (p. 428, folio 397).
  - No building, no tomb description, no ʿurs, no custodian and no later history of the site —
    **(not stated)** (pp. 426, 428, 493, 517, 525).
- **Practices & Events**
  - Who came to him: `His way of life, spirituality, and justice attracted a large number, not only of
    Sufi adepts, but also, according to the sources, of slaves and husbandmen from the neighboring
    villages.` (p. 428, folio 397).
  - Land: `A considerable amount of land was distributed among Shah clnayat's followers after an
    unjustified attack on Jhok, led by the Sayyids of Bulrri.` (p. 428, folio 397). **`Bulrri` was not
    joined to **shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri**** — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - **Schimmel's explicit refusal to decide the land-reform question, which must travel with the
    land sentence**: `To what extent Shah clnayat really aimed at something like a land reform in the
    modern sense of the word cannot be detected from the sources, which are mostly unfriendly to him;
    positive evaluations, on the other hand, are strongly mixed with legends.` (p. 428, folio 397).
  - The charge, the siege and the execution: `the pious mystic was accused of conspiracy against the
    Mogul throne, the easiest method of blackmail, since the throne was in constant danger after
    Aurangzeb's death. Large armies were sent to Jhok, which surrendered after four months' siege;
    Shah clnayat was executed in January 1718.` (p. 428, folio 397). `four months' siege` is the
    book's own figure.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **Songs of his martyrdom, Muslim and Hindu**: `Both Muslim and Hindu admirers have composed songs
    extolling him or describing his martyrdom; among them is the SuhrawardI mystic Janullah of Rohri,
    one of the outstanding Persian poets of Sind from the second half of the eighteenth century.`
    (p. 428, folio 397). Schimmel's caution on the whole hagiographic record is in the land-reform
    sentence above and should be read with it.
  - He is named inside Sachal's litany of the afflicted: `hast killed / c / lnayat in the fighting
    arena` (p. 426, folio 395), which footnote 79 glosses `c lnayat is the martyr-mystic of Jhok.`
    (p. 426, folio 395) — **the book's own identification, not an editorial one**.
- **Significance**
  - Schimmel devoted an article to him under his own place-name: `"Shah c lnayat of Jhok: A Sindhi
    Mystic of the Early i8th Cen-` / `tury." In Liber Amicorum in Honour of C. J. Bleeker. Leiden,
    1969.` — the century printed `i8th` [text-layer?: 18th], `i` for `1` (p. 493, folio 462).
  - Ruling (a) on the toponym `jhok` returns this same single row, so nothing is added by firing it
    separately; the index's bare `Jhok, 395, 397` (p. 518, folio 487) was recorded and declined.

### data-darbar — Data Darbar

The row's principal figure is the book's most-used single authority: named `Hujwiri`, `Hujwirl`,
`Hujwlri`, `HujwIri`, `Hujwm`, `Huj-` / `wirl`, `Hujwlri`, `Hujwirfs`, `A1I ibn c Uthman al-Hujwirl`,
`CAH ibn cUthman al-HujwIri` and, in the index, `Hujwiri, cAli ibn "-Uthman Data Ganj` / `Bakhsh`
[all text-layer?: Hujwīrī, ʿAlī ibn ʿUthmān Dātā Ganj Bakhsh], and cited far more often still by the
bare siglum `H` (pp. 22–24, 37, 45–48, 68, 73, 82, 89, 93, 100, 104, 118–122, 134, 141, 153–163,
177–186, 219–237, 321, 376–379, 480, 516, 519, 538). **`H` is Nicholson's translation of the
*Kashf al-Maḥjūb*, never a page of this book.**

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, kept side by side.** The running text gives `Hujwiri (d. circa
    1071)` (p. 37, folio 6) — `circa` is the book's own hedge — and `He later came to Lahore, the
    capital of the Ghaznawids in India, and he died there in 1071.` (p. 119, folio 88), where `1071`
    prints cleanly in four digits; the index prints `(d. ca. 1071)` (p. 516, folio 485). **The archive
    row records 1072.**
  - Origin and formation: `One of Qushayri's colleagues, who had attended his meetings and had visited
    almost every leading Sufi of his time, was Hujwiri from Ghazna.` (p. 119, folio 88). No birth
    date, no family, no silsila and no teacher other than this attendance on Qushayrī —
    `(not stated)`.
  - Dated only by century elsewhere: `Hujwirl, in the mid-eleventh century, summed up the discussion`
    (p. 45, folio 14); `Even Hujwlri, in a book written in the mid-eleventh century—unfortu- nately no
    longer extant—in spite of all his admiration for Hallaj, felt obliged to declare him "not firmly
    settled."` (p. 104, folio 73). **That lost book's title and the ground of the verdict are
    `(not stated)`.**
  - Contemporary of Anṣārī: `A contemporary of Qushayri and Hujwiri was cAbdullah-i Ansarl (1006-89),
    whose work, like that of Hujwiri, was written in part in his Persian mother tongue.` (p. 120,
    folio 89).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The shrine is named, placed and described as a living cult, in two sentences a chapter apart.**
    `His shrine, called that of Data Ganj Bakhsh, is still a popular place of pilgrimage in Lahore.`
    (p. 119, folio 88) — `still` is Schimmel's word and dates the claim to her own time of writing,
    which the book does not otherwise fix. And: `the name of Hujwiri, who composed his famous Persian
    treatise on Sufism in this town, has already been mentioned; his tomb still provides a place of
    pilgrimage for the Panjabis.` (p. 376, folio 345) — the town is Lahore, from the same sentence.
  - **That is the whole of the shrine material.** No building date, no custodianship, no ʿurs, no
    visitor number, no Auqaf, no architecture, no quarter — all `(not stated)` (explicitly noted at
    pp. 119, 376).
  - The `INDEX OF SUBJECTS` records pilgrimage to him as a topic of the book, set beside pilgrimage
    to Mecca: `Pilgrimage: to Mecca, 32, 66, 67, 71, 89, 106, 229, 230, 264, 388, 425, 430; to Data
    Ganj Bakhsh, 88; rewards of making seventy, 231; Abu Sacld without, 242; spiritual, 276` (p. 538,
    folio 507). `88` is **the book's own index locator** — a printed folio, quoted verbatim, not
    converted. The name `Data Ganj Bakhsh` occurs **once** in that whole chunk, at this entry.
  - **Promoted here by worker A under ruling (a).** The toponym key `data darbar` (2 rows:
    **darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz**, **peer-makki**) was declined by chunk 006 on the ground that the book
    prints `Data Ganj Bakhsh` and not `Data Darbar`. That is an orthographic objection of exactly the
    class that `Ucch`→`uch sharif` and `Sehwan`→`sehwan sharif` were fired through elsewhere in this
    same book, and both rows' `location_short` names this shrine as their own landmark. The two
    pilgrimage sentences above therefore attach to those two rows as well — see the place-level run
    below. Both rows are in any case inside the Lahore 35, so nothing is lost if Rauf reverses it.
- **Significance**
  - *The innovation the book credits him with*: `Hujwirfs important innovation is that he wrote his
    Kashf al-mahjub, "Unveiling of the Hidden," in Persian and thus ushered in a new period in
    mystical literature.` (p. 119, folio 88).
  - *Schimmel's judgement on the book, running across the page break*: `A monument of early Persian
    and noteworthy for its expressiveness, the Kashf, "which belongs to the valid and famous books"
    (N 316), contains much interesting information rarely found in other sources. Although the
    author's` (p. 119, folio 88) `inclination toward systematization sometimes goes too far, it is
    singularly valuable in its approach and its balanced discussion.` (p. 120, folio 89). The praise
    `which belongs to the valid and famous books` is **Jāmī's**, cited as `N 316`, not Schimmel's.
    She repeats the charge of over-systematising at p. 141, folio 110.
  - *His book in the Delhi book trade*, in Barani's list of what it became fashionable to buy in
    Nizāmuddīn's Delhi: `Hujwiri's Kashf al-mahjub` (p. 379, folio 348).
  - *Editions, from the book's own apparatus.* Abbreviations key: `H   A1I ibn c Uthman al-Hujwirl.
    The "Kashf al-Mahjub," the Oldest Persian Treatise on Sufism by al-Hujwiri. Translated by Reynold
    A. Nicholson. Gibb Memorial Series, no. 17. 1911. Reprint. London, 1959.` (p. 22, folio xix,
    continuing onto p. 23, folio XX) — the entry straddles the page break with a stray `C`, an ʿayn
    knocked out of `c Uthman` or `al-Hujwiri`, alone on its own line between the halves (p. 22,
    folio xix). Footnote 62: `CAH ibn cUthman al-HujwIri, Kashf al-mahjub, ed. V. A. Zukovskij
    (Leningrad, 1926; reprint ed., Tehran, 1336 sh./igsy` [text-layer?: 1336 sh./195 — **the final
    digit prints `y`, which is not in the measured substitution table and is not guessed**]`)`
    (p. 119, folio 88). Bibliography: `Hujwiri, CAH ibn c Uthman al-. Kashf al-mahjub. Edited by V. A.
    Zukov-` / `skij. Leningrad, 1926. Reprint. Tehran, 1336 sh./ig57.` [text-layer?: 1957] and
    `The "Kashf al-Mahjub," the Oldest Persian Treatise on Sufism` / `by al-Hujwiri. Translated by
    Reynold A. Nicholson. Gibb Me-` / `morial Series, no. 17. 1911. Reprint. London, 1959.` (p. 480,
    folio 449). `1926`, `1911` and `1959` print cleanly.
  - *Index apparatus.* The main entry, verbatim: `Hujwiri, cAli ibn "-Uthman Data Ganj` / `Bakhsh
    (d. ca. 1071), 6, 14, 15, 17, 51,` / `62, 73, 87, 88-89, 1O3- 11D' lll> 122 >` / `126, 129, 130,
    131, 132, 146, 147, 152,` / `199, 206, 290, 345; Kashf al-mahjub, 88,` / `34°` (p. 516, folio 485).
    **Four tokens in it are unreadable as printed**: `1O3-` [text-layer?: 103], `11D'` [text-layer?:
    110], `lll>` [text-layer?: 111], `34°` [text-layer?: 340 or 348 — **unresolved**]. The book is
    indexed again by its title, `Kashf al-mahjub (Hujwlri), 88, 348` (p. 519, folio 488).
  - *His own positions, as Schimmel explicitly ascribes them to him* — kept apart from the sayings he
    merely transmits, per the notes' own voice test:
    - On the name Sufi, four derivations summed up: `Some assert that the Sufi is so called because he
      wears a woollen garment (jama-i suf), others that he is so called because he is in the first
      rank (saff-i awwal), others say it is because the Sufis claim to belong to the ashab-i Suffa
      (the people of the Bench who gathered around the Prophet's mosque). Others, again, declare that
      the name is derived from safd` [text-layer?: ṣafā] `(purity).` (`H 30`) (p. 45, folio 14).
    - On discord and love: `"Love is concord, and the lover has but one duty in the world, namely to
      keep the commandment of the beloved, and if the object of desire is one, how can discord
      arise?"` (`H 38`) (pp. 46–47, folios 15–16; the sentence is split by the page break).
    - On learning: `"Knowledge is immense and life is short: therefore it is not obligatory to learn
      all the science . . . but only so much as bears upon the religious law"` (`H 11`) (p. 48,
      folio 17).
    - On God's two approaches: `There is a difference between one who is burned by His Majesty in the
      fire of love and one who is illuminated by His Beauty in the light of contemplation.` (`H 367`)
      and `There is a difference between one who meditates upon the Divine acts and one who is amazed
      at the Divine Majesty; the one is a follower of friendship, the other is a companion of love.`
      (`H 373`) (p. 37, folio 6).
    - On the Malāmatiyya, more critical than Sulamī: `"The ostentatious men purposely act in such a
      way as to win popularity, while the malamatl purposely acts in such a way that people reject
      him. Both have their thought fixed on mankind and do not pass beyond that sphere"` (`H 6`)
      (p. 118, folio 87).
    - On the dervish garb: `Its collar is annihilation of intercourse [with men], its two sleeves are
      observance and continence, its two gussets are poverty and purity, its belt is persistence in
      contemplation, its hem is tranquillity in [God's] presence, and its fringe is settlement in the
      abode of union.` (`H 56`) (p. 134, folio 103).
    - On the forty-day retreat: its origin is `derived, as Hujwiri says, from the forty-day fast of
      Moses, when he hoped for a vision from God, as related in Sura 7:138` (p. 134, folio 103).
    - On repentance: `"The penitent is a lover of God, and the lover of God is in contemplation of
      God: in contemplation it is wrong to remember sin, for recollection of sin is a veil between God
      and the contemplator"` (`H 296`) (p. 141, folio 110); and his threefold scheme, which Schimmel
      marks as unique to him — `tauba as return from great sins to obedience; indba` [text-layer?:
      ināba] `is the return from minor sins to love, and auba the return from one's self to God`
      (`H 295`)`. This tripartition is not found elsewhere.` (p. 141, folio 110).
    - On poverty, endorsed by Schimmel in her own voice: `"Its form is destitution and indigence, but
      its essence is fortune and free choice"` (`H 19`) (p. 153, folio 122); and the long description
      of dervishhood, `Dervishhood in all its meanings is a metaphorical poverty … Then he is the Way,
      not the wayfarer, i.e., the dervish is a place over which something is passing, not a wayfarer
      following his own will.` cited as `(Hag)` [text-layer?: **an `H` siglum whose number is damaged
      — not resolved**] (p. 154, folio 123).
    - On contentment: `Hujwirl is probably right in his statement that ridd is a result of love,
      inasmuch as the lover is content with what is done by the beloved (H 180).` (p. 157, folio 126)
      — `probably right` is Schimmel's own judgement, carried as printed.
    - On sobriety: `True sobriety is defined by Hujwiri as "reaching the goal" (H 230)` (p. 160,
      folio 129).
    - On *maʿrifa*: named among the systematisers, `like Hujwm (H 267)` (p. 161, folio 130).
    - His classification of lovers, `which Ghazzali adopted as well, in slightly elaborated form:
      "Those who regard the favor and beneficence of God toward them and are led by that regard to love
      the Benefactor, and those who are so enraptured by love that they reckon all favors as a veil and
      by regarding the Benefactor are led to [consciousness of] His favors"` (`H 308`) (p. 163,
      folio 132). **The square brackets are printed in the book.**
    - His threefold *tauḥīd*: `"God's unification of God, i.e. His knowledge of His Unity; God's
      unification of His creatures, i.e. His decree that a man shall pronounce Him to be one, and the
      creation of unification in his heart; man's unification of God, i.e. their knowledge of the Unity
      of God"` (`H 278`) (p. 177, folio 146).
    - His correction of a misattributed saying, endorsed by Schimmel: `But Hujwirl has given the more
      correct version—Muhammad ibn Wasic` [text-layer?: Wāsiʿ] `said: "I never saw anything without
      seeing God therein,"` (`H 91, 330`) (p. 178, folio 147).
    - On the *ṣalāt*: `Hujwiri has defined the various types of mystics by their attitudes toward the
      salat: some hold that it is a means of obtaining the presence of God; others regard it as a means
      of obtaining absence` (`H 301`) (p. 183, folio 152).
    - On saintship, which opens the book's whole discussion of it: `"You must know that the principle
      and foundation of Sufism and knowledge of God rest on saintship"` (`H 210`)`, says Hujwirl`
      (p. 230, folio 199). Schimmel pairs him with Jāmī: `and three centuries after him Jam! opened his
      discussions about the history of Sufism with a paragraph on saintship.` (p. 230, folio 199) —
      **the book does not say where or when Hujwīrī wrote** (not stated).
    - On the condemnation of `nazar Ha? l-ahddth` [text-layer?: naẓar ilā ʾl-aḥdāth], `the "looking
      toward the young men"`, to which he `devoted a weighty paragraph` (p. 321, folio 290): `Looking at
      youth and associating with them are forbidden practices and anyone who declares this to be
      allowable is an unbeliever. The traditions brought forward in this matter are vain and foolish. I
      have seen ignorant persons who suspected the Sufis of the crime in question and regarded them with
      abhorrence, and I observed that some have made it a religious rule. All the Sufi shaykhs, however,
      have recognized the wickedness of such practices, which the adherents of incarnation (hulull-` /
      `yat)` [text-layer?: ḥulūliyyat]`—may God curse them!—have left as a stigma on the saints of God
      and the aspirants to Sufism.` (`H 549`) (p. 321, folio 290).
  - *As a transmitter — the act of transmission is a fact about him, the words are not his*: a prayer
    of Sarī as-Saqaṭī `has been preserved by Hujwiri` (p. 162, folio 131); an unnamed predecessor's
    definition of unification is `what an earlier Sufi, quoted by Hujwirl, had said` (p. 177,
    folio 146); and `"To traverse distance is child's play: henceforth pay visits by means of thought;
    it is not worth while to visit any person, and there is no virtue in bodily presence"` (`H 235`)`,
    as one of Hujwiri's masters told his disciple.` — the master is not named, **(not stated)**
    (p. 237, folio 206).
  - *The sigla trap.* The chunk covering pp. 59–74 cites `H` three times — `(H 103)` for Junayd on
    Ibrāhīm ibn Adham (p. 68, folio 37) and `(H 100)` twice for Dhuʾn-Nūn (p. 73, folio 42) — **without
    printing the name `Hujwiri` anywhere in those sixteen pages**; the attachment there rests on the
    abbreviations key printed on PDF 22–24. A Pass-1 worker records this as a judgement a consolidator
    may reasonably reverse.
  - **The `cAli` collision was refused at every occurrence.** The book's `CA1I` / `CAH` is the
    Prophet's son-in-law — `the attribute given by the Shiites to CA1I, the wall Allah par excellence`
    (p. 230, folio 199), `Muhammad's descendants through CA1I and Fatima` (p. 113, folio 82),
    `there is no fata but CA1I` (p. 277, folio 246), `Hasan ibn CA1I the first, 374` (p. 539) — and is
    **not** the row's `Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri`. The row rests on `Data Ganj Bakhsh` and on
    `Hujwiri`, both unambiguous.

### mazar-of-bulleh-shah — Mazar of Bulleh Shah

Printed `Bullhe Shah` throughout [text-layer?: Bullhe Shāh] (pp. 419, 426, 474, 513, 524).

- **Saint**
  - Dates as printed: `Bullhe Shah (1680-1752) is considered the greatest of the Panjabi mystical
    poets; he lived near Lahore.` (p. 419, folio 388); the index prints `Bullhe Shah (d. 1752), 388,
    395` (p. 513, folio 482). **The identical printed date `(d. 1752)` is also given two pages earlier
    for `Abdu3l-Lat!f Bhita3!, Shah`** — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - **Residence is given only as proximity to Lahore**; the book does not say where he is buried and
    **never names Kasur** — `(not stated)` (p. 419, folio 388).
  - His generation: `Like his contemporaries Shah cAbduDl-Latif in Sind and Shah Walmllah
    [text-layer?: Shāh Walīullāh], Mir Dard, and Mazhar Janjanan in Delhi, he was a witness of the
    epoch of great political disasters that occurred, with only brief interruptions, in the
    northwestern part of the Subcontinent after Aurangzeb's death in 1707.` (p. 419, folio 388), and
    `Like these mystics, he found peace in the inner world of love and surrender, singing his mystical
    songs in order to console himself and his friends in the times of external sufferings and
    afflictions.` (p. 419, folio 388).
  - No tomb, no silsila anywhere — `(not stated)` (pp. 419, 426, 474, 513, 524).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **A martyrdom tradition, recorded and rejected in the same breath — the pairing is the material
    and should not be split.** Sachal's litany has `Thou hast made to be slaughtered Bullhe Shah,
    Jacfar to / be drowned in the sea,` (p. 426, folio 395) — Sachal's line, not Schimmel's claim —
    and footnote 79 on the same page answers it: `The traditional sources say nothing about the
    martyrdom of Bullhe Shah.` (p. 426, folio 395).
- **Significance**
  - **The epithet the book files to him**, twice: `His poetry was so highly esteemed that he was
    surnamed "the Rumi of Punjab."` (p. 419, folio 388), and, as an index cross-reference,
    `Rumi of Punjab. See Bullhe Shah` (p. 524, folio 493).
  - Output beyond Panjabi: `In addition to being the leading poet of Panjabi Sufism, he also composed
    a number of Persian prose treatises, comparable to those of Mir Dard.` (p. 419, folio 388).
  - The Hīr–Rāñjhā romance used mystically: `The theme of Hir Ranjhd [text-layer?: Hīr Rānjhā] was
    used by Bullhe Shah, as well as by his younger contemporary Hashim Shah of Amritsar, for their
    mystical meditations.` (p. 419, folio 388).
  - Edition, given twice: `Bullhe Shah, Diwan` [text-layer?: Dīwān]`, ed. Faqir M. Faqir (Lahore,
    1960).` (p. 419, folio 388, footnote 70), and in the bibliography `Bullhe Shah. Dlwdn. Edited by
    Faqir M. Faqir. Lahore, 1960. In Pan-` / `jabi.` (p. 474, folio 443).

### mausoleum-of-waris-shah — Mausoleum of Waris Shah

**The book's spelling is `Warith Shah` throughout, never `Waris`** [text-layer?: Wāriṯh Shāh]
(pp. 414, 419, 420, 527).

- **Saint**
  - **Dated only by period, and the two statements are kept as printed.** `the old romance of Hir
    Ranjha in the classical form given it by Warith Shah in the late eighteenth century.` (p. 414,
    folio 383); `It was given its classical form by Warith Shah (completed around 1794).` (p. 419,
    folio 388) — Schimmel's `around` is hers; and the index prints `Warith Shah (late i8th century)
    [text-layer?: late 18th century], 383, 388, 389; Hlr Ranjha, 383, 388` [text-layer?: Hīr Rānjhā]
    (p. 527, folio 496).
  - **The book gives no death year.** The archive row records 1798, which is consistent with but not
    stated by the book. No Jandiala Sher Khan, no tomb, no silsila — `(not stated)` (pp. 414, 419, 420,
    527).
- **Significance**
  - *His version is the one villagers sing*: `The visitor who stays in a Panjabi village will
    certainly find somebody to sing for him the old romance of Hir Ranjha in the classical form given
    it by Warith Shah in the late eighteenth century.` (p. 414, folio 383).
  - *Technique*: `Since the tale was known to everybody, the poet has the heroes recite short verses,
    which are commonly interpreted in a mystical sense.` (p. 419, folio 388).
  - *The mystical key to Hīr–Rāñjhā, quoted in the book from a translation*: `has translated the
    mystical interpretation given by Warith Shah:` (p. 420, folio 389), introducing a twenty-two-line
    allegorical key in which `Our soul is the tragic heroine Hlr [text-layer?: Hīr], / Our body is the
    lover Ranjha, / Our spiritual preceptor is the Yogi Balmath,` and, further down, `The secret
    chamber is the grave, / Saida Khera is the angel of death.` and `Reason is the flute / that to us
    the music of Divine wisdom conveys.` (p. 420, folio 389).
  - *His signature line, as printed*: `Warith Shah says: Those alone cross over / who have silently
    prayed to the Lord, / and ever devotedly recited His glorious Name.` (p. 420, folio 389).
  - *Schimmel's gloss on the whole*: `"Hir is our soul"—one of the characteristics of Sindhi and
    Panjabi poetry is that the longing soul is always depicted as a woman; this typical aspect of
    Indian Sufi poetry was inherited from Hinduism (see Appendix 2).` (p. 420, folio 389).
  - *References for the text*, footnote 71: `The best translation is Charles Frederick Usborne, Hir
    Ranjha, ed. Murntaz Hasan [text-layer?: Mumtaz Hasan] (Karachi, 1966). On the subject in
    Indo-Persian literature see Hafeez Hoshyarpurl, Mathnawiydt-i Hlr Ranjha (Karachi, 1957).`
    (p. 420, folio 389). **Neither bibliography entry for those two books names Waris Shah** (p. 480,
    folio 449; p. 496, folio 465), so neither was used to reach this row.
  - The poem is separately indexed through its characters: `Ranjha, 388, 389` (p. 523, folio `4Q2`
    [text-layer?: 492]); `Hir, 388, 389, 434` and `Hir Ranjha, 384, 388` (p. 516, folio 485).
  - **`Hir` alone was not used to reach this row.** Where the book names the heroine without the poet
    (p. 465, folio 434), the material stays under the folk-tale figures in the next section.

### madho-lal-hussain — Shrine of Hazrat Madho Lal Hussain (Shah Hussain Darbar)

Printed `Madho Lai Husayn` and `Madho Lai` [text-layer?: Mādho Lāl Ḥusayn] (pp. 415, 419, 520).

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, kept side by side.** The running text prints `This Madho Lai
    (1539-93) had sung of his love for a Hindu boy in simple Panjabi verses, and his poems contain the
    traditional mystical vocabulary of longing and hope for annihilation.` (p. 415, folio 384) —
    four-digit years survive intact here — and the index prints `Madho Lai Husayn (d. 1593), 384, 388`
    (p. 520, folio 489). **The archive row records 1599.**
  - Attestation in a seventeenth-century source: `The name of a sixteenth-century Panjabi Sufi, Madho
    Lai Husayn, is mentioned by Dara Shikoh in his Hasanat al-^drifin [text-layer?: *Ḥasanāt
    al-ʿārifīn*].` (p. 415, folio 384).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - The book locates his anniversary only as `near the Shalimar Garden`; it **does not name
    Baghbanpura, does not describe a shrine building, and gives no death place** — `(not stated)`
    (p. 415, folio 384).
- **Practices & Events**
  - **The book fixes an annual Lahore festival by place, date and name**: `Madho Lai's anniversary,
    still celebrated in Lahore near the Shalimar Garden on the last Saturday in March, is famous as
    Meld Chiraghdn` [text-layer?: Melā Chirāghān]`, the "Fair of Lamps."` (p. 415, folio 384). The book
    gives **no** attendance figure, **no** duration, **no** custodian and **no** year of founding — all
    `(not stated)`. The `INDEX OF SUBJECTS` carries it as a glossed entry: `Mela chiraghdn (festival in
    Lahore), 384` (p. 536, folio 505) — see `## REVIEW (worker A)` on that gloss.
- **Significance**
  - **He is credited with beginning the tradition chapter 8 is about**: `He also alluded to popular
    motifs, like the stories of Hir and of Sohni Mehanwal, and thus seems to inaugurate (or perhaps
    even continue from unknown beginnings) a tradition typical of the religious poetry of the two
    provinces: the insertion of folk tales into the context of mystical thought.` (p. 415, folio 384) —
    Schimmel's parenthetical hedge is hers and is kept. Named again as `The first known representative
    of this trend, Madho Lai Husayn, has already been mentioned.` (p. 419, folio 388).
  - The index locator `384, 388` runs over folios that also carry `Lahore` and `Panjabi` poetry
    entries; a Pass-1 worker records that as a locator coincidence and not a statement (p. 520,
    folio 489).

### lal-shahbaz-qalandar — Lal Shahbaz Qalandar

Printed `Lai Shahbaz` [text-layer?: Lal Shahbaz], `Lai Shahbaz Qalandar` [text-layer?: Lāl Shahbāz
Qalandar], and called `Qalandar` and `the saint of Sehwan` (pp. 385–386, 520, 523).

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, kept side by side.** The index prints `Lai Shahbaz Qalandar
    (d. 1262), 354-55` (p. 520, folio 489). **The archive row records 1274.** No other date is given
    in running text.
  - He is introduced at a chunk boundary, in a sentence that breaks off: `Another aspect of mystical
    life developed in the same period during which Muclnuddln Chishtl and BahjPuddin Zakariya were
    preaching divine love and union: its representative is Lai Shahbaz,` (p. 385, folio 354). So he is
    placed in the same period as Muʿīnuddīn Chishtī and Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā and announced as the
    representative of a **different** `aspect of mystical life`; the predicate falls on the next page.
  - The book never prints `Usman Marwandi` (not stated). A separate index entry `Marwand, 355` (p. 520,
    folio 489) stands on its own, with a locator adjacent to his; **the book does not join them.**
  - Cross-referenced at `Qalandar. See Abu CA1I Qalandar, Shah; Lai Shahbaz Qalandar` (p. 523,
    folio `4Q2` [text-layer?: 492]) — the other name in that cross-reference, `Abu CA1I Qalandar, Shah`
    [text-layer?: Shāh Abū ʿAlī Qalandar], is **not** an archive row and is not mapped.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The book's only statement that a Muslim shrine of this archive stands on a Hindu site**: the
    saint of Sehwan settled `at the site of the old Shiva sanctuary at the west bank of the lower
    Indus` (p. 386, folio 355); and `A lingam close to the actual tomb proves the Shivait background of
    some of the rites.` (p. 386, folio 355). **`proves` is the book's own word, and the claim is made
    without a source.**
  - The book never prints `Sehwan Sharif`; a separate index entry `Sehwan, 355` stands alone (p. 525,
    folio 494).
- **Practices & Events**
  - `Around his sanctuary strange groups of bl-shar^` [text-layer?: bī-sharʿ] `, "lawless," Sufis
    (malang) gathered, dressed in black, and the mystical current connected with Sehwan often shows
    Sufism in its least attractive aspects.` (p. 386, folio 355). **Schimmel's own valuation is part of
    the sentence and is kept rather than paraphrased away.**
  - **The fair, with its dates**: `The "fair" of Sehwan—celebrated between Shawwal 18 and 20—has become
    notorious for its rather illicit events.` (p. 386, folio 355). The book writes `"fair"` in
    quotation marks and **does not use the word *ʿurs***; `Shawwal 18 and 20` [text-layer?: clean] are
    days of a Muslim month, not years. No attendance figure, no organiser and no year — (not stated).
  - The `INDEX OF SUBJECTS` glosses the attendants: `Mdlang (dervish at the sanctuary in Sehwan), 355`
    (p. 536, folio 505) — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
- **Significance**
  - Footnote 14 at the foot of the same page carries Schimmel's own assessment of the standard English
    book on saint worship in Sind, and is worth carrying to any Sindhi entry: `Peter Mayne, Saints of
    Sind (London, 1956` [text-layer?: clean] `), gives a vivid and often ironical account of the
    customs of so-called saints; yet his brilliantly written book contains much valuable information
    about little-known facts of saint worship and mystical thought in the Indus Valley.` (p. 386,
    folio 355).

### shrine-of-mian-mir — Shrine of Mian Mir

Printed `Mian Mir` throughout (pp. 392, 394, 464, 521). **The book never uses the archive's form
`Mir Mohammad of Sind`** — but it independently supplies the Sind origin.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive**: the index prints `Mian Mir (d. 1635), 361, 433`
    (p. 521, folio `49°` [text-layer?: 490]). The running text gives him **no** date — `(not stated)`
    at pp. 392, 394, 464.
  - Origin, which independently matches the row's own parenthesis: `the Qadiriyya, represented in
    Lahore by Mian Mir, who had come there from Sind, together with his mystically inclined sister
    Bibi Jamal` (p. 392, folio 361).
  - Kinship: `The sister of Jihanara's and Dara's first mystical guide Mian Mir, Bibi Jamal Khatun
    (d. 1639), was one of the outstanding saints of the Qadiriyya order during its formative period in
    the Punjab.` (p. 464, folio 433). **His order is given only by implication, through his sister's
    Qadiriyya** — (not stated) in his own right.
  - He is `the first mystical guide` of `Shah Jihan's eldest daughter Jihanara` and of `her luckless
    brother Dara Shikoh` (p. 464, folio 433).
  - **No tomb, no shrine, no Lahore quarter, no Alam Ganj, no Dharampura anywhere** — `(not stated)`
    (pp. 392, 394, 464, 521).
- **Significance**
  - *The Mogul heir apparent's shaykh, at one remove*: `Mian Mir's disciple and successor, Molla Shah
    Badakhshi, introduced the prince to the saint.` (p. 392, folio 361).
  - *A seventeenth-century Qadiri biography of Lahore exists, written by the Mogul heir apparent*:
    Dara Shikoh wrote `a fine biography of Mian Mir and the Qadiriyya in Lahore`, the `Sakinat
    al-auliyff` [text-layer?: Sakīnat al-auliyāʾ] (p. 392, folio 361). The book gives the work no date
    — (not stated).
  - *The succession*: `Her master Molla Shah thought her worthy to be his successor, though this did
    not materialize` — of Jihanara, Dara's and Aurangzeb's eldest sister (p. 394, folio 363).

### rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine — Rahman Baba Mausoleum (Rehman Baba Shrine)

Printed `'AbduVRahman, lovingly called Rahman Baba`, also `"Abdu^r-Rahman`, `cAbduDr-Rahman` and
`c / AbduDr-Rahman` across a line break, and in the index `^bdu^r-Rahman (Rahman Baba)` [all
text-layer?: ʿAbduʾr-Raḥmān (Raḥmān Bābā)] (pp. 430–432, 510, 523).

- **Saint**
  - **The book's death year disagrees with the archive's, and both registers of the book agree with
    each other.** Running text: `a mystic of the Chishtl [text-layer?: Chishtī] order who lived during
    the Aurangzeb period (d. 1709) and is usually regarded as the best mystical poet in Pashto.`
    (p. 430, folio 399) — `1709` prints cleanly; index: `^bdu^r-Rahman (Rahman Baba)` / `(d. 1709),
    399-401` (p. 510, folio 479). **The archive row records 1711.** Kept side by side.
  - **The order is stated**: `of the Chishtl order` (p. 430, folio 399).
  - Reached again through the index cross-reference `Rahman Baba. See ^bdu'r-Rahman` (p. 523,
    folio `4Q2` [text-layer?: 492]), which carries no locator, date or place.
  - **No tomb, no Peshawar, no Hazarkhwani, no ʿurs** — `(not stated)` (pp. 430–432, 510, 523).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **Marked as legend by the author herself**: `As legend has it, he led a life of withdrawal and
    seclusion, and his poetry reflects a sadness and ascetic feeling that are not too frequent in the
    mystical poets of this period.` (p. 430, folio 399).
- **Significance**
  - Schimmel's ranking of him: `usually regarded as the best mystical poet in Pashto` (p. 430,
    folio 399); this is what `That is particularly clear in the case of 'AbduVRahman` is said of.
  - A fellow Pashto Chishtī is named against him: `a remarkable poet tried to express mystical
    teachings in Pashto. This poet was Khwaja Muhammad Bangash, like cAbdu3r-Rahman a member of the
    Chishti order` (p. 432, folio 401).

### garh-maharaja-shorkot — Garh Maharaja (Shorkot)

Printed `Sultan Bahu of the Punjab`, `Sultan Bahii`, `Sultan Bahu`, and in the bibliography
`Sultan Bahoo` [text-layer?: Sulṭān Bāhū] (pp. 199–200, 416–417, 496, 526).

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive.** `The first verse of Sultan Bahu's (d. 1691) poem goes:`
    (p. 416, folio 385); the index prints `Sultan Bahu (d. 1691), 169, 385, 386` (p. 526, folio 495).
  - Called the first of his tradition, with the surname explained: `the first great Panjabi mystical
    poet, Sultan Bahii (who got his surname because each line of his Slkarfl [text-layer?: **reading
    not recoverable**], "Golden Alpha-bet," ends with the exclamation hu, "He")` (p. 416, folio 385).
  - **No tomb, no Garh Maharaja, no Shorkot, no Jhang, no silsila anywhere** — `(not stated)` at
    pp. 200, 416–417, 496, 526. The place he is given is `of the Punjab` and nothing narrower.
- **Significance**
  - *The dhikr-watered tree.* The sentence runs across the page break: `it is the jasmine tree watered
    by the Id and ilia [text-layer?: lā and illā] (the two parts of the profession of faith, which was
    frequently used` (p. 199, folio 168) `in dhikr}` [text-layer?: `in dhikr)` — the closing
    parenthesis is printed as a brace]`, as Sultan Bahu of the Punjab sang.` (p. 200, folio 169).
  - *Schimmel's estimate of him*: his verses `do nothing but develop ideas well known to earlier
    Sufis`, yet `his approach is worth mentioning as typical of the way rural mystics spread the ideas
    of Sufism.` (p. 416, folio 385).
  - *The opening verse, in full as printed across the page break* (p. 416, folio 385 – p. 417,
    folio 386): `Alif: Allah is like the jasmine plant which the preceptor planted in my heart—o Hu! /
    By water and the gardener of negation and positive statement it remained near the jugular vein and
    everywhere—o Hu! / It spread fragrance inside when it approached the time of blossoming—o Hu! /
    May the efficient preceptor live long, says Bahu, who planted this plant—o Hu!`
  - *Schimmel's reading of it*: `These lines point to the recollection of the divine name, planted in
    the mystic's heart by his Pir. The water of negation and affirmation, the Id and ilia of the
    profession of faith in the dhikr, is combined with the allusion to Sura 50:16` (p. 417, folio 386).
  - *Edition*: `Sultan Bahoo, Abydt` [text-layer?: Abyāt]`, trans. Maqbool Elahi (Lahore, 1967).`
    (p. 416, folio 385, footnote 64), and in the bibliography `Sultan Bahoo. Abyat. Translated by
    Maqbool Elahi. Lahore, 1967.` — spelling `Bahoo` as printed (p. 496, folio 465).

### shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala — Shrine of Makhdoom Nooh (Hala)

Printed `Makhdum Nuh` [text-layer?: Makhdūm Nūḥ] (pp. 421, 520). Reached **both** ways — by the person
rule on the name and by ruling (a) on the bare toponym `Hala`, which `toponym_map.txt` gives as one
row.

- **Saint**
  - **A century-level disagreement with the archive, larger than any of the book's date
    contradictions.** The book places him in the seventeenth century twice: `where mystical leaders of
    the Suhrawardiyya had settled—the name of Makhdum Nuh in the seventeenth century is best known.`
    (p. 421, folio 390); and the index prints `Makhdum Nuh (i7th century) [text-layer?: 17th century],
    390` (p. 520, folio 489). **The archive row records `figure_died` 1590.** Both are kept.
  - **The order is stated at place level**: Hala is named as a Suhrawardī settlement (p. 421,
    folio 390), which agrees with the archive row's recorded Suhrawardi silsila.
  - No tomb, no ʿurs, no custodian, no dates beyond the century — `(not stated)` (pp. 421, 516, 520).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **A craft attribution to the place, not to this shrine**: Hala's tilework is named as the type for
    Bhit Shah's tomb — `covered with blue and white tiles (typical of Hala)` (p. 421, folio 390). The
    sentence is about Laṭīf's tomb; it is recorded here because `(typical of Hala)` is a statement
    about Hala's craft, and it is quoted in full under **bhit-bhit-shah**.
  - The index's bare place entry `Hala, 390` (p. 516, folio 485) was recorded and declined as a bare
    index entry — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
- **Significance**
  - Hala is the birthplace of Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf: `Shah ^bdu^l-Latif was born into a family of mystics
    in 1689 in Hala, near present-day Hyderabad-Sind,` (p. 421, folio 390) — the whole sentence is
    quoted under **bhit-bhit-shah**.

### shrine-of-shah-abdul-karim-bulri — Shrine of Shah Abdul Karim Bulri

Printed `Shah cAbduDl-Karim` and `Shdh cAbdul Karlm` [text-layer?: Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Karīm]; in the index
`Abdu :l l-Karim Bulrrrwaro`, with the ʿayn detached as a bare `c` on the line above [text-layer?:
ʿAbduʾl-Karīm Bulṛīwāro] (pp. 420–421, 482, 510, 513, 525).

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive.** `Abdu :l l-Karim Bulrrrwaro (d. 1623), 390` (p. 510,
    folio 479). The headword is badly mangled — `:l l-` for `ʾl-`, `Bulrrrwaro` for a nisba — and is
    flagged rather than reconstructed.
  - Kinship: he is `^bdu3!-Latif's ancestor` (pp. 420–421, folios 389–390; the sentence breaks across
    the page turn).
  - Reached again through the cross-reference block `Shah. See cAbdu3l-Karim Bulrriwaro; …` (p. 525,
    folio 494), which carries no locator, date or site.
  - **The running text never prints `Bulri`**; the bibliography entry likewise gives `no place, no
    dates and no epithet` — `(not stated)` (pp. 420–421, 482, folios 389–390, 451).
- **Significance**
  - `The poems of ^bdu3!-Latif's ancestor Shah cAbduDl-Karim72 or those o£ [text-layer?: of] Shah
    c lnat 73 in the seventeenth century are closely related to Latif's poetry.` (pp. 420–421,
    folios 389–390) — two facts: he is Laṭīf's ancestor, and his poems are closely related to Laṭīf's.
  - The book's own reference, footnote 72: `Motilal Jotwani, Shah ^Abdul Karlm (New Delhi, 1970).`
    (p. 421, folio 390), and as a bibliography entry, `Jotwani, Motilal. Shdh cAbdul Karlm. New Delhi,
    1970.` — a monograph filed under the subject's name (p. 482, folio 451).
  - The index carries the bare place entry `Bulrri, 397` [text-layer?: Bulṛī] (p. 513, folio 482),
    recorded and **not** used to fire ruling (a); and the book's `the Sayyids of Bulrri` who attacked
    Jhok (p. 428, folio 397) was likewise **not** joined to this row — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

### shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori — Shrine of Makhdoom Abdul Rahim Girhori

Printed `Girhorl`, `Girhori`, `cAbdu:>r-Rahim Girhori` and, in the index, `Girhori, ^bduVRahlm`
[all text-layer?: Girhōrī, ʿAbduʾr-Raḥīm] (pp. 147, 418, 475, 510, 515). **In three of the four
chunks the identification rests on the nisba alone**, inside a book title.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive.** The index prints `Girhori, ^bduVRahlm (d. 1778), 387`
    (p. 515, folio 484), with a cross-reference five pages earlier: `^bdu^r-Rahim. See Girhorl,` /
    `cAbdu3r-` / `Rahim` (p. 510, folio 479), which carries no locator of its own.
  - **The book's order for him CONFLICTS with the archive's.** The running text calls him `the
    eighteenth-century Suhrawardi mystic cAbdu:>r-Rahim Girhori` (p. 418, folio 387); **the archive
    row records Naqshbandi.** Kept side by side.
  - Manner of death, stated without detail: `who paid with his life for his missionary zeal.`
    (p. 418, folio 387).
  - **No death year in running text, no shrine, no Girhor Sharif, no Umarkot, no Pithoro** —
    `(not stated)` (pp. 147, 418, 475, folios 116, 387, 444).
- **Significance**
  - *The one substantive claim*: `Exalted descriptions of Muhammad as the Perfect Man, who is like the
    dawn between the divine light and the darkness of human existence, can be read in the theoretical
    works of some Sindhi Sufis, for example, the eighteenth-century Suhrawardi mystic cAbdu:>r-Rahim
    Girhori, who paid with his life for his missionary zeal.` (p. 418, folio 387).
  - *Cited for a practice of extreme asceticism.* Footnote 12 supports the statement that some
    ascetics `not only fasted and restricted their diet to the absolute minimum but, in addition to
    that, took a purgative on every third day.` — `Umar Muhammad Daudpota, Kaldm-i Girhorl (Hyderabad,
    Sind, 1956), p. 23.` [text-layer?: Kalām-i Girhōrī] (p. 147, folio 116). The same edition is cited
    again at p. 418, folio 387, footnote 67, and as a bibliography entry, `Daudpota, Umar Muhammad.
    Kalam-i Girhorl. Hyderabad, Sind, 1956. In Sindhi.` (p. 475, folio 444). **The bibliography entry
    nowhere spells out `Abdul Rahim` and nowhere says the man is a saint.**

### shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari — Shrine of Jalaluddin Surkh-Posh Bukhari (Jalaluddin Bukhari)

Printed `Sayyid Jalaluddin Surkhpush` and, in the index, `Jalaluddln Surkhpush` [text-layer?:
Jalāluddīn Surkhpūsh] (pp. 385, 518).

- **Saint**
  - **The book's death year disagrees with the archive's, and both of the book's registers agree with
    each other.** `("Red-dressed," d. 1292` [text-layer?: clean]`)` (p. 385, folio 354); index,
    `Jalaluddln Surkhpush (d. 1292), 354` (p. 518, folio 487). **The archive row records 1291.**
  - The whole of what the book gives: `One of Zakariya's disciples, Sayyid Jalaluddin Surkhpush
    ("Red-dressed," d. 1292) from Bukhara, settled in Ucch, northeast of Multan, and is the forefather
    of a long line of devout mystics and theologians.` (p. 385, folio 354) — i.e. **the epithet's
    meaning, his origin (Bukhara), his master (Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā), his settlement (Ucch) and his
    death year**, and nothing else: **no** tomb, **no** birth date, **no** named descendants,
    **no** silsila in his own right — **(not stated)**.
