  - The index's single locator `354` is the same folio given for `Jalaluddln Makhdum-i Jahaniyan` and
    for `Lai Shahbaz Qalandar` (p. 518, folio 487). A neighbouring entry `Jalaluddln Tabriz!
    (d. 1244), 351` (p. 518, folio 487) is a **third, different Jalāluddīn** and is not mapped.

### shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht — Shrine of Makhdoom Jahaniyan Jahangasht

Printed `Jalaluddin Makhdum-i Jahaniyan` and `Jalaluddln Makhdum-i Jahaniyan` [text-layer?:
Jalāluddīn Makhdūm-i Jahāniyān] (pp. 385, 518, 520).

- **Saint**
  - **Two readings of the death year, and the book disagrees with the archive.** Running text:
    `(d. 1383` [text-layer?: clean]`)` (p. 385, folio 354); index: `Jalaluddln Makhdum-i Jahaniyan
    (d. 1382-83), 354` (p. 518, folio 487). **The archive row records 1384.** All three are kept;
    none is resolved.
  - The whole of what the book gives: `the active and pious Jalaluddin Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, "whom all
    the inhabitants of the world serve" (d. 1383), a prolific writer in almost all religious fields.`
    (p. 385, folio 354) — **the gloss of the title is the book's own**.
  - He is given as the reason Ucch was `for a while, the center of the Suhrawardiyya` (p. 385,
    folio 354), which agrees with the archive row's recorded Suhrawardi silsila.
  - Cross-referenced at `Makhdum-i Jahaniyan. See Jalaluddin Makhdum-i Jahaniyan` (p. 520, folio 489).
    **No** shrine, **no** site, **no** tomb, **no** Uch building — (not stated).

### shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan — Shrine at Odero Lal (Udero Lal Teerath Asthan)

Printed `Sheikh Tahir`, `called Lai Uderd by the Hindus` [text-layer?: Lāl Uḍerō], and in the index
`Lai Udero` with the cross-reference `Tahir, Sheikh. See Lai Udero` (pp. 418, 520, 526). **The book
itself equates Sheikh Tāhir with Lāl Udero, which is exactly the double name this row carries in its
`principal_figure` field.**

- **Saint**
  - **Period only, and two statements of it.** `This sixteenth-century mystic was one of the most
    ardent defenders of the unity of being; he saw God "even in a camel."` (p. 418, folio 387); the
    index prints `Lai Udero (i7th century) [text-layer?: 17th century], 387` (p. 520, folio 489).
    **The two disagree by a century and are kept side by side.** No death year — (not stated).
  - **No shrine, no Odero Lal village, no Matiari, no ʿurs** — `(not stated)` (pp. 418, 520, 526).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The archive's shared-veneration claim, stated directly by Schimmel**: `A strange fact—more
    visible in Sind than in the Punjab—is the way Hindus shared in the Muslim orders. Some saints were
    claimed by both communities, like Sheikh Tahir, who was called Lai Uderd by the Hindus.` (p. 418,
    folio 387).
- **Significance**
  - His doctrine: one of the most ardent defenders of *waḥdat al-wujūd*, who `saw God "even in a
    camel."` (p. 418, folio 387).
  - Source for the identification, as cited in footnote 68: `Mir CA1I Shir Qanic [text-layer?: Mīr ʿAlī
    Shīr Qāniʿ], Tuhfat al-kiram (Hyderabad, Sind, 1957), p. 389.` (p. 418, folio 387) — `p. 389` is a
    page of the *Tuḥfat al-kirām*, **not of this book**.
  - **The book never prints the name `Jhulelal` in any spelling.** The two further ids a Pass-1 worker
    attached at p. 520, folio 489 — **jhollay-lal-mandir** and
    **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir** — rest on the *archive's* own parenthetical
    equations, not on the book. They are carried in the place-level run below, flagged. See
    `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

### shrine-of-akhund-darweza-baba — Shrine of Akhund Darweza Baba

Printed `Akhund Darwaza` [text-layer?: Akhūnd Darwēza] (pp. 429, 511).

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive.** `Akhund Darwaza (d. 1638), 398` (p. 511, folio 480) —
    the book's own index locator is the single folio `398`.
  - **No shrine, no Peshawar, no Hazarkhwani, no order** — (not stated) (pp. 429, 511, folios 398, 480).
- **Significance**
  - **One sentence, and it is a hostile one**: `Pir-i Roshan's mystical theories—a full-fledged
    pantheism—were vehemently attacked by Akhund Darwaza, the orthodox preacher, and Pir-i Roshan's
    sons were drowned in the Indus near Attock.` (p. 429, folio 398). So: named as **the orthodox
    preacher who attacked the Roshaniyya**, and nothing else. **Whether he had any part in the
    drowning is not stated**; the sentence joins the two clauses without asserting a connection and
    no connection has been supplied.
  - `Bayezld Ansari (d. 1585), 398, 399; Khayr` / `ul-bayan, 398` (p. 513, folio 482) is indexed at
    the same folios — his opponent, not an archive row.

### tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak — Tomb of Qutbuddin Aibak

Printed `Qutbuddln Aybek of Delhi` and, in the index, `Qutbuddln Aybek` [text-layer?: Quṭbuddīn Aybek]
(pp. 377, 512, 523).

- **Saint**
  - **The book's death year disagrees with the archive's.** `Qutbuddln Aybek (d. 1206), 346` (p. 523,
    folio `4Q2` [text-layer?: 492]). **The archive row records 1210.** Kept side by side.
  - Reached also through the cross-reference `Aybek. See Qutbuddln Aybek` (p. 512, folio 481), which
    carries no locator.
- **Significance**
  - **A single passing mention, as the builder of a mosque, and in a geography that is not this row's**:
    `When Mucinuddm died in 1236` [text-layer?: clean]`—not far from the beautiful mosque Qutbuddln
    Aybek of Delhi had erected in Ajmer—he was succeeded by a large number of khalifas` (p. 377,
    folio 346). **No** tomb, **no** Lahore, **no** Anarkali, **no** title beyond the implicit one —
    **(not stated)**.
  - **A real false friend stands nine lines later on the same page and must not be merged**:
    `Qutbuddln Bakhtiyar Kaki from Farghana`, who `died in 1235` and whose `rather modest compound in
    Merauli near the Qutb Minar in Delhi is usually filled with pious pilgrims` (p. 377, folio 346) —
    a different man, indexed separately as `Bakhtiyar Kaki, Qutbuddin (d. 1235), 346` (p. 513,
    folio 482).

### shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir — Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir

Reached through the book's own phrase `the sacred mountain Hinglaj in Balochistan` (pp. 421, 516).
**Note the join: the identification rests on the archive row's own *name*, not on a
`toponym_map.txt` key** — the map's keys for that area are `hingol national park` and
`hingol balochistan national park`.

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The book calls it a mountain, not a temple or a cave, and names no deity, no ritual, no
    pilgrimage season and no community** — `(not stated)` for all of these (pp. 421, 516, folios 390,
    485).
- **Significance**
  - **Two distinct claims in one clause, and the second is the more valuable**: `perhaps reached the
    sacred mountain Hinglaj in Balochistan, which is often mentioned as "the goal" in Sindhi mystical
    poetry.` (p. 421, folio 390). Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf **may** have gone there — Schimmel's own hedge
    `perhaps` — and, independently of that, **the site is a recurring topos of Sindhi mystical poetry
    as `"the goal"`**.
  - The index carries the bare place entry `Hinglaj, 390` (p. 516, folio 485), recorded and declined
    as a bare index entry. Its locator `390` sits in the same folio run as `Bhit Shah`, `Hala` and
    `Abdu :l l-Karim Bulrrrwaro` — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

### guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat — Guru Gurpat Mandir (DB-80 Sirey Ghat)

**Place-level only.** The single archive row in Hyderabad, Sindh, reached under ruling (a) on the
book's `Hyderabad, Sind` / `Hyderabad-Sind` (pp. 403, 418, 421–422, 428, 517). The book writes
`Hyderabad, Sind` explicitly wherever it matters, which rules out the Deccan Hyderabad of brief §7.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **An institution is located there**: `The Shah Waliul-lah Academy in Hyderabad, Sind, under the
    presidency of Maulana Ghulam Mustafa Qasimi, has published a number of Wallullah's works, as well
    as studies about him.` (p. 403, folio 372, footnote 41). The hyphen in `Waliul-lah` is a line
    break and is not the book's own.
- **Significance**
  - Used twice purely to locate other places: Laṭīf was born `in Hala, near present-day
    Hyderabad-Sind` (p. 421, folio 390), and Jhok is `not far from Hyderabad` (p. 428, folio 397).
  - It is the imprint city of several of the book's Sindhi sources (p. 418, folio 387, footnotes 67
    and 68; p. 421, folio 390, footnote 73, `ed. Dr. Nabi Bakhsh Baloch (Hyderabad, Sind, !963-)`
    [text-layer?: 1963]; pp. 421–422, folios 390–391, footnote 75). **Those imprints were declined;
    the row stands on the two body-text uses and on the Academy footnote.**
  - The index's bare place entry `Hyderabad Sind, 372 n, 390, 397` (p. 517, folio 486) — the `n` is
    the book's own note marker — was recorded and declined as a bare index entry. The separate entry
    `Hyderabad Deccan, 351` (p. 517, folio 486) is **a different place** and was refused outright.
  - **The attachment is place-level and thin**; the book says nothing about the city itself (pp. 403,
    418, 421–422, 428, 517).

### sial-sharif — Sial Sharif

**Place-level only.** The single archive row in the Sargodha district, reached under ruling (a)
(pp. 419, 524).

- **Significance**
  - The one occurrence is inside the plot of the Hīr–Rāñjhā romance: `Ranjha, the son of a landlord of
    the Sargodha district, falls in love with Hir, the daughter of the ruler of Djang.` (p. 419,
    folio 388). **Nothing is said about the district itself, and nothing about any shrine in it.**
  - The index's bare place entry `Sargodha, 388` (p. 524, folio 493) was recorded and declined as a
    bare index entry.
  - **This is the thinnest attachment in the section** — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

---

*The four rows below carry site- or person-specific material but are **not** fed by chapter 8; their
evidence lies in the formative-period chapters, the Epilogue and the back matter.*

### mazar-e-iqbal — Tomb of Allama Iqbal (Mazar-e-Iqbal)

By volume the second-largest archive presence in the book after **data-darbar**, and the most widely
spread: named on some thirty pages across nineteen chunks, as `Muhammad Iqbal`, `Iqbal`,
`Sir Muhammad Iqbal`, `Iqbal, Muhammad`, `Iqbal, Sir Muhammad` and, in one footnote,
`Muhammad Iqbil` [text-layer?: Iqbāl] (pp. 53, 81, 107, 148, 170, 186, 191, 224–235, 250–251,
258, 269–270, 294, 304, 312, 337–338, 353–359, 436–438, 448, 472, 481–493, 518, 523, 535–542).
**He is an author-and-doctrine mapping under the 18 September ruling, which names Iqbal as its own
worked example.**

- **Saint**
  - **Death year, agreeing with the archive, printed cleanly in three places**: `Muhammad Iqbal
    (d. 1938)` (p. 107, folio 76; p. 148, folio 117) and, in the index, `Iqbal, Sir Muhammad
    (d. 1938)` (p. 518, folio 487).
  - **No birth year anywhere.** The book gives `in our day`, `in the twentieth century`, `a modern
    thinker` — `(not stated)` for a birth date (pp. 170, 186, 231, 337, 518).
  - **The book never uses the title `Allama`** — (not stated) (pp. 81, 107, 448, 481, 518).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The burial place is named once, in a parenthesis inside a paragraph about *duplicate* tombs**:
    `Several maqdms (places) may bear the name of the same saint—a few years ago a maqam of Muhammad
    Iqbal (who is buried in Lahore) was erected in the garden of Jalaluddin Ruml's tomb in Konya.`
    (p. 270, folio 239). `maqdms` [text-layer?: maqāms]; `a few years ago` is the book's own dating
    and **no year is given** — `(not stated)`. The Lahore tomb is **not described** and its quarter is
    not named.
  - The index confirms that the only *site* it files for him is the foreign one: the sub-entry
    `maqdm of, in Konya, 239` [text-layer?: maqām] (p. 518, folio 487). **The index says nothing about
    his Lahore tomb.**
- **Significance**
  - *Against pirism, twice, in his own coinage.* `HijazI has put his finger on the danger of the
    exaggerated veneration of the spiritual master, the sheikh or plr [text-layer?: pīr] (see chapter
    5), what Muhammad Iqbal has called "pirism," which means the absolute sway of the leader over his
    followers and the attendant exploitation of ignorant peasants and villagers.` (p. 53, folio 22);
    `why, though mystically minded, a modernist like Iqbal considered Pirism one of the most dangerous
    aspects of Islam, a wall that closes off large groups of Muslims from a new and vital
    interpretation of Islamic values.` (p. 269, folio 238) — this sits directly beside Schimmel's
    remark that Atatürk abolished the dervish orders in Turkey `in 1925`; and `More than once Muhammad
    Iqbal attacked the "Pirism" and backwardness of the so-called "spiritual leaders" of the
    Indo-Pakistani Subcontinent;` (pp. 436–437, folios 405–406). Indexed as `attack on Pirism by, 22,
    238, 405` (p. 518, folio 487).
  - **Schimmel's counter-reading, which must travel with the attack**: `yet a close study of his work
    makes it clear that he himself follows quite closely the Sufi thought of the classical period.`
    (p. 437, folio 406); and `As a thinker, Iqbal turned from a Hegelian-Ibn cArabi pantheism to what
    seemed to him true Islam. In fact, he relied heavily upon the heritage of the great masters of
    early Sufism` (p. 437, folio 406).
  - *On Ḥallāj.* `Muhammad Iqbal (d. 1938), who in his youth described the great mystic as a
    pantheist—as he had seen him through hundreds of Persian, Urdu, and Panjabi poems—later recognized
    Hallaj's strong personal religious commitment and saw in him one of the few who had attained to an
    experience of the divine higher than that of ordinary people.` (p. 107, folio 76); `He recognized
    that Hallaj had called the slumbering Muslims to a personal realization of the truth, thus coming
    into conflict with the religious authorities who were afraid of any ardent witness for the living
    God.` (p. 107, folio 76); `In the scene in Jupiter-Heaven in the Jawldname [text-layer?:
    Jāvīdnāma] that depicts his spiritual journey through the spheres, Iqbal has even treated Hallaj
    as a sort of medieval forerunner of himself and has emphasized his dynamic concept of love and
    faith as an ideal for every free Muslim.` (p. 107, folio 76). **The change of mind is dated only
    as `in his youth` versus `later`; no year is given for either position** — `(not stated)`. And
    `his treatment of Hallaj, with whose work he became acquainted through Louis Massignon's books,
    shows an extraordinary insight into the phenomenon of the enthusiastic mystic` (p. 437,
    folio 406). Indexed as `relation of, with Hallaj, 406` (p. 518, folio 487).
  - *On Ḥāfiẓ.* `as much as he detested the kind of enthralling, "otherworldly" mysticism that he
    thought was hidden behind Hafiz's beautiful verses, he himself used the whole fabric of Sufi
    imagery in his poetry, though he often reinterpreted it.` (p. 437, folio 406).
  - *On Satan.* His Satan is weaker than the tradition's: `(Iqbal's Satan sadly complains that`
    (p. 224, folio 193) `it is much too easy for him to seduce people).` (p. 225, folio 194); he
    follows Rūmī on Iblīs's one-eyedness (`M 4:1402`) (p. 226, folio 195); `The manifold aspects of
    Satan developed throughout the centuries by the Sufis have been echoed, in our day, in the work of
    Muhammad Iqbal, whose multicolored picture of Satan is quite unusual.` (p. 227, folio 196); `He
    sees in him the lover as well as the intellectual, the monotheist and the spirit of evil who longs
    to be overcome by the perfect man (faithful to the Prophetic tradition, "My shaytan has become
    Muslim") and to be broken in order to find salvation. Thus, he will eventually prostrate himself
    before man, who proves stronger than he. On the other hand, Iqbal has also employed the more common
    image of Satan the seducer, the materialist, and the destroyer.` (p. 227, folio 196). Indexed as
    `satanology of , 193, 195, 196` (p. 518, folio 487).
  - *On predestination.* `the jabr mahmud, the "higher predestination," as Iqbal calls it, can be
    achieved not simply by` (p. 229, folio 198) `accepting a given situation but by actively conforming
    with God's will through love and prayer.` (p. 230, folio 199). **The same phrase appears earlier
    without him, glossed differently, as `the jabr mahmud, "agreeable constraint."` (p. 228,
    folio 197); the two glosses stand side by side and are not reconciled.**
  - *As a claimant to the quṭb's office.* Schimmel reads his best-known slogan that way, in a sentence
    split by a plate page: `the claim of a modern thinker like Iqbal that he would "call a people to
    spiritual resurrection" gains special significance; he assumes, in a sense,` (p. 231, folio 200)
    `the role of the qutb.` (p. 233, folio 202). `In fact, the three angelic figures Sarosh, Israfil,
    and Gabriel occur very frequently in his poetry.` (p. 233, folio 202).
  - *In a line of descent from the medieval saints.* `The difference between what Na-` `than Soderblom
    and Friedrich Heiler have denned [text-layer?: defined] as "mystical" and "prophetic" religiosity
    has been very clearly understood and expressed by the saints of medieval Islam, whom Iqbal follows
    in the fifth chapter of his Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam` `(iQS 0 )` **[text-layer?:
    a four-digit year, all four characters damaged; not repaired and not guessed]** (p. 235, folio 204);
    indexed as `Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam, 204` (p. 518, folio 487; also p. 523,
    folio `4Q2`).
  - *On the Prophet.* `Muhammad Iqbal stressed the importance of the Prophet in his daring statement:
    "You can deny God, but you cannot deny the Prophet."` (p. 258, folio 227), sourced in footnote 46
    to `Iqbal, Jawldname, verse 608` [text-layer?: *Jāvīdnāme*], with Schimmel's own references
    `"The Place of the Prophet in Iqbal's Thought," Islamic Studies \, no. 4 (1962); Rais Ahmad Ja c fri,
    Iqbal aur '•asliq-i rasul (Lahore, 1956).` — `Islamic Studies \` [text-layer?: **volume number
    lost; `\` is not in the measured table and is not reconstructed**]; `Ja c fri` [text-layer?:
    Jaʿfarī]; `'•asliq-i rasul` [text-layer?: ʿāshiq-i rasūl] (p. 258, folio 227).
  - *On prayer.* `True prayer is constant. It cannot be limited to a number of rak'as and portions of
    the Koran, but permeates man's whole being—an idea that in our day has been expressed once more by
    Muhammad Iqbal, who follows Rumi's example.` (p. 186, folio 155); `These ideas, so central to
    prayer theology, and thus to the whole religious life, were taken up once more by Muhammad Iqbal,
    who often speaks of the miraculous power of true prayer, which ends in a complete conformity of the
    human to the divine will, in a change and sublimation of man's hopes and anguish.` (p. 191,
    folio 160).
  - *On love and longing.* `He is, as Muhammad Iqbal has said in our day, "like us, a prisoner of
    Desire."` (p. 170, folio 139), sourced to `Muhammad Iqbal, Zabur-i fajam [text-layer?: Zabūr-i
    ʿAjam] (Lahore, 1927), part 2, no. 29; trans. A. J. Arberry as Persian Psalms (Lahore, 1948), part
    2, no. 29.` (p. 170, folio 139). And on the nightingale: `Iqbal has interpreted the song of the
    nightingale in the context of his philosophy of unfulfilled union and longing—only longing gives
    the soul bird the capacity to sing, inspiring- it to create lovely melodies. Longing is the highest
    state the soul can reach, for it results in creativity, whereas union brings about silence and
    annihilation.` (p. 338, folio 307). Indexed under `Longing`: `in Iqbal's work, 307` (p. 535,
    folio 504); and under `Unio mystica, 4; union`: `Iqbal critical of, 307; not substantial, 376`
    (p. 542, folio 511).
  - *On the restlessness of creation.* `in our time, it was Muhammad Iqbal, who never tired of
    describing the long way of development from a drop to the ocean, from sperm to man, and eventually
    to the Perfect Man, the crown and apex of creation.` (p. 337, folio 306) — the `1721` on that page
    is **Bedil's** date, not his.
  - *On the Perfect Man and light.* `even an orthodox mystic like Mir Dard in the eighteenth century
    equates God with light—an idea that finds a faint echo in some sentences of Iqbal as well.`
    (p. 294, folio 263); `Iqbal's ideas of the Perfect Man as he who has developed his individual
    possibilities to the fullest bloom are derived from these theories rather than from the Nietzschean
    superman.` (p. 304, folio 273).
  - *On Rūmī.* Listed among the readers who take the `I died as mineral` passage as evolutionary
    (p. 353, folio 322), alongside `Khalifa cAbdul Hakim`, `Afzal Iqbal` and `Abdulbaki Golpmarli`
    [text-layer?: Abdülbaki Golpınarlı] — **`Afzal Iqbal` is a different man and is indexed separately
    as `Iqbal, Afzal, 322` (p. 518, folio 487)**; `In the Muslim world, Muhammad Iqbal gave this idea
    of constant development a new interpretation in his poetical works, which appeared from 1915 until
    1935.` (p. 354, folio 323) — both years print cleanly; the Darwinian reading reached him through
    `the Indian scholar Maulana Shibll [text-layer?: Maulānā Shiblī] in his Urdu biography of Rumi
    (1903), which deeply influenced Iqbal's ideas` (p. 353, folio 322); `Iqbal was deeply influenced by
    Maulana and inserted many of his lines into his own works, mainly into his mathnawls, which he
    wrote in the same meter as that of his master's Mathnawi` (p. 358, folio 327); and Schimmel's
    verdict, `Muhammad Iqbal, no doubt, understood best the dynamic force behind Rumi's experiences,
    though he highlights only one of the numerous aspects of the great lover.` … `in many respects his
    interpretation appeals more to the modern mind than many of the traditional interpretations`
    (p. 359, folio 328). Indexed as `relation of, with RumI, 155, 322, 323, 327, 328` (p. 518,
    folio 487) and, in the subject index, `Mathnawl … by Iqbal, 327` (p. 536, folio 505).
  - *On Bāyezīd.* He takes over Rūmī's story of the Zoroastrian `in his Jdwldndme [text-layer?:
    Jāwīdnāme] (1932), where it serves to assert the spiritual strength of the Islamic religion and the
    true Muslims.` (p. 81, folio 50) — `1932` prints intact.
  - *On the miʿrāj.* `In the twentieth century, Muhammad Iqbal has chosen the type of the heavenly
    journey in the company of the spiritual preceptor (in his case the spirit of Rumi) for his most
    intriguing literary work, the Jawidname (igga)` **[text-layer?: 1932 — the same year prints cleanly
    in the footnote on the same page]** (p. 250, folio 219); `Iqbal, in his modern interpretation of
    mystical symbols, understands this tradition as a reference to the moment at which "the infidel's
    girdle," namely, "serial" time, is torn and the mystic establishes direct contact with God in a
    person-to-person encounter.` (p. 251, folio 220) — of the Prophet's `"I have a time with God in
    which no created being has access, not even Gabriel who is pure spirit."`. Indexed as
    `Jdwidndme, 50, 76, 219` and `Jdwidndme (Iqbal), 50, 76, 219` (p. 518, folio 487).
  - *On Ḥallāj's ṭawāsīn.* `the title has been imitated, in our time, by Muhammad Iqbal, who in his
    account of his spiritual journey, the Jdwldname, invented the "tawasin of the Prophets," dwelling
    places of the prophetic spirits in the Sphere of Moon.` (p. 448, folio 417).
  - *On Shabistarī.* `Its form and content inspired Muhammad Iqbal to compose his Gulshan-i raz-i
    jadid, "The New Rose Garden of Mystery," published in 1927; in this poem he follows Shabistari's
    notions and offers his own solutions to the questions of the Perfect Man (one of his central
    ideas), of God, and of destiny.` (p. 312, folio 281) — `1927` prints cleanly. Indexed as
    `Gulshan-i rdz-i jadid, 281` (p. 518, folio 487).
  - *On the belly.* `And Muhammad Iqbal (d. 1938) made the fake murld ex-plain that God, though
    "closer than the jugular vein" (Sura 50:16), is not closer to man than his stomach.` (p. 148,
    folio 117), in a run of poets criticising Sufi gluttony immediately after `Bedil (d. 1721)`.
  - *Works and scholarship, as the bibliography prints them, all on one page*: `The Development of
    Metaphysics in Iran. Cam-` / `bridge, 1908.`; `Jdwidndme. Lahore, 1932. In Persian. Translated into
    English` / `by Shaikh Mahmud Ahmad, Lahore, 1961, and by A. J. Arberry,` / `London, 1966; into
    Italian by Alessandro Bausani, 1955; into` / `French by Eva Meyerovitch, 1962; into German verse by
    Annemarie` / `Schimmel, 1957; and into Turkish prose by Annemarie Schimmel,` / `1958.`; `Six
    Lectures on the Reconstruction of Religious Thought in` / `Islam. Lahore, 1930.`; `Zabur-i cajam.
    Lahore, 1927. Part translated by A. J. Arberry as` / `Persian Psalms. Lahore, 1948.` (p. 481,
    folio 450). As subject: `Jacfrl, Rals Ahmad. Iqbal aur cashq-i rasiil. Lahore, 1956. In Urdu.`
    (p. 482, folio 451); `Malik, Hafeez, ed. Muhammad Iqbal: Poet-Philosopher of Pakistan,` / `New York,
    1971.` — the comma before `New York` is as printed (p. 485, folio 454); `. "II Gulsan-i raz-i gadid
    di Muhammad Iqbal." Annali del Isti- tuto Universitario Orientale di Napoli, n.s. 8 (1959).` and
    `-. "Satana nell'opera filosofico-poetica di Muhammad Iqbal." Ri- vista degli studi orientali 30
    (1957).` (p. 472, folio `44!` [text-layer?: 441]); and Schimmel's own `Gabriel's Wing: A Study into
    the Religious Ideas of Sir Mu-` / `hammad Iqbal. Leiden, 1963.`, `"Die Gestalt des Satan in Muhammad
    Iqbal's Werk." Kairos,` / `no. 2 (1963).`, `"The Idea of Prayer in the Thought of Iqbal." Moslem
    World` / `48, no. 3(1958).` (p. 492, folio 461) and `"The Place of the Prophet in Iqbal's Thought."
    Islamic Studies` / `i, no. 4 (1962).` — `i` for `1` in the volume number (p. 493, folio 462).
    Earlier citations of the same monograph print `Gabriel's Wing … (Leiden, 1963), pp. 353 ff. and
    bibliography.` (p. 359, folio 328) and `contains a detailed bibliography on prophetology and
    ascension.` (p. 250, folio 219); footnote 46 at p. 186, folio 155 prints the volume number of
    `Moslem World^S` **[text-layer?: not recoverable; not repaired]**.
  - *Index locator, verbatim and unconverted*: `117, 139, 160, 198, 200, 220, 227, 263, 273, 306, 307,
    406`, with the sub-entries listed above (p. 518, folio 487). **These are the book's own printed
    folios, not PDF pages.**

### shrine-of-pir-mangho — Shrine of Pir Mangho

Printed `Mango Pir near Karachi` and, in the index, `Mango Pir` (pp. 238, 520). **`Mango Pir` is not
a key in `toponym_map.txt`; the row is reached as its principal figure's alternative name.**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The one thing the book says, and it names a miracle type at this site**: `In wrath a saint can
    turn a flower into an alligator (as happened in Mango Pir near Karachi) or live among wild animals,
    who serve him` (p. 238, folio 207). **The saint is not named; no date, no ʿurs, no custodianship,
    no building, no crocodile pool as such** — all `(not stated)`. The archive row's principal figure is
    `Pir Mangho (Haji Syed Sakhi Sultan)`; **Schimmel names no person here at all.**
  - The sentence is embedded in Schimmel's general class of obedience miracles, whose maxim she gives as
    `Whosoever obeys God completely, everything created is bound to obey him—that is an old Sufi maxim
    that has been illustrated by miracle stories.` (p. 238, folio 207). The Mango Pir case is offered as
    her example of the **wrathful** variety, set against the benign variety (living among wild animals).
  - **Schimmel gives no source for the Mango Pir statement** — no siglum, no footnote attaches to it
    (p. 238, folio 207).
  - Related material on the same page, not attached to this site: `even the frogs may talk to him, and
    the crocodile, with tears in its eyes, is willing to surrender its prey to him.` (p. 239,
    folio 208).
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - The index entry is bare: `Mango Pir, 207` (p. 520, folio 489) — **no** date, **no** Karachi,
    **no** description (not stated).

### darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz — Darbar Malik Ahmad Ayaz

Printed only `Ayaz`, `his slave Ayaz` and `the Turkish officer Ayaz` — **the book never prints
`Malik Ahmad Ayaz`, `Malik Ayaz`, a governorship, a ministry, a tomb, Lahore or a death date for him**
(pp. 221, 323, 512). The identification rests on the one descriptor the book states explicitly and the
archive row also states: that he was **the slave of Maḥmūd of Ghazna**.

- **Saint**
  - `Mahmud of Ghazna, the warrior king of Afghanistan (d. 1030), famous in political history as the
    conqueror of northwest India, becomes in literary tradition the model of the lover, because of his
    inclination toward his slave Ayaz.` (p. 323, folio 292). **The death year `1030` prints cleanly in
    four digits and belongs to Maḥmūd, not to Ayāz; the book gives Ayāz no date** — `(not stated)`.
    The archive row's own `figure_died` of `8 August 1041` is **not** in this book, and neither date is
    carried onto the other.
  - **Promoted here by worker A.** The index entry `Ayaz, 190, 292` (p. 512, folio 481) was declined
    by chunk 027 for want of a gloss. Its two locators are exactly the two printed folios at which this
    book discusses Maḥmūd's Ayāz — p. 221 = folio 190 and p. 323 = folio 292 — so the entry is the
    same man the person rule already took, and the ordinary person rule covers it. **The separate entry
    `Ayaz, Sheikh, 390 n` four lines away (p. 512, folio 481) is a different man and is NOT mapped.**
- **Significance**
  - *The standing model of mystical love.* `The story of Mahmud and Ayaz is a typical expression of
    this kind of love and shows, at the same time, how the poets of Iran used historical facts to point
    out eternal truth.` (p. 323, folio 292); `The complete surrender of the Turkish officer Ayaz, for
    whom only Mahmud exists, results in perfect love of the king, who becomes, in a wonderful
    transformation, the slave of his slave. Ayaz is the symbol of the loving soul.` (p. 323,
    folio 292).
  - *The Hūmā bird.* `One day the Huma bird passed over Mahmud's army, and everybody rushed to be
    touched by the Huma's shade, which, in Persian tradition, conveys kingdom. Only Ayaz went into
    Mahmud's shade` (`U 176`) `, for here was his true kingdom: he is like the faithful, who does not
    seek glory and power from anything created but only from his eternally rich, beloved Lord.`
    (p. 323, folio 292).
  - *Rūmī's allegory of the worn shoes.* `One of the finest allegories ever written is by RumI, who
    tells the story of Ayaz, Mahmud of Ghazna's favorite slave, who looked every morning at the
    out-worn shoes and shabby clothes he wore before Mahmud elected him for himself; it was necessary
    for him to remember his former lowly state so that he could appreciate the bounty that his lord had
    bestowed upon him` (`M 5:2113`) (p. 221, folio 190), with Schimmel's gloss: `Thus, concludes RumI,
    man should likewise remember how weak he was created, and then he will recognize his nothingness
    before God, who gives him every- thing out of grace.` (p. 221, folio 190).
  - *The book's own reference on the theme*, footnote 5: `See Gertrud Spies, Mahmud von Ghazna bei
    Farid ud^din ^Attdr [text-layer?: Farīd ud-dīn ʿAṭṭār] (Basel, 1959); several epics have been
    devoted to this topic, and lyrics abound in allusions to Ayaz.` (p. 323, folio 292).
  - **Maḥmūd of Ghazna himself is NOT mapped to this row**; the row's principal figure is Ayāz. Maḥmūd
    is indexed separately as `Mahmud of Ghazna (d. 1030), 89, 190, 292` (p. 520, folio 489) and is
    carried in the next section.

### shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi — Shri Panchmukhi Hanuman Mandir (Karachi)

Reached through the single bare index headword `Hanuman` (p. 516). **The thinnest person-level
attachment in the section.**

- **Significance**
  - The entry as printed, entire: `Hanuman, 394` (p. 516, folio 485) — the book's own index locator is
    the single folio `394`.
  - **The chunk says nothing whatever about him beyond the headword and the locator: no temple, no
    Karachi, no context** — (not stated) (p. 516, folio 485). See `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

### Place-level and person-level attachments under ruling (a) and the many-row person rule

*Seventy-one further archive rows are reached only through a place name or through a many-row
principal figure. The material is recorded once here, with every id of each bullet head on a single
physical line; it is not repeated in the subsections above except where a row is independently
supported by its own person evidence, and it is **not** repeated in `## Figures and sites not in the
archive`. Every one of these attachments is **place-level or person-level, not site-specific** — the
book describes none of these sites. Ruling (a) is heavy by design (Rauf, 21 September 2026).*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's `Lahore` — all 35 rows in that place, ruling (a)): the city is given four
  substantive statements in the whole book, and one more inside an article title.
  - **The founding statement, at the moment of the Ghaznawid conquest**: `Lahore became the first
    center of Persian-inspired Muslim culture in the Subcontinent—the name of Hujwiri, who composed
    his famous Persian treatise on Sufism in this town, has already been mentioned; his tomb still
    provides a place of pilgrimage for the Panjabis.` (p. 376, folio 345). Its setting: `The second
    wave of Muslim conquest in India, that of the Ghaznawids about the year 1000` [text-layer?:
    clean]`, brought into the Subcontinent not only scholars like al-Blruni [text-layer?: al-Bīrūnī]
    (d. 1048 [text-layer?: clean]), who made a careful study of Hindu philosophy and life, but
    theologians and poets as well.` (p. 376, folio 345).
  - **Political status**: `Lahore, the capital of the Ghaznawids in India` (p. 119, folio 88), where
    Hujwīrī `died there in 1071` and where `His shrine, called that of Data Ganj Bakhsh, is still a
    popular place of pilgrimage in Lahore.` (p. 119, folio 88).
  - **The seat of the Qadiriyya in Mogul India, and the order is said to have arrived there from
    Sind**: `It was not the "official" Chishti order to which he was attracted, but rather the
    Qadiriyya, represented in Lahore by Mian Mir, who had come there from Sind, together with his
    mystically inclined sister Bibi Jamal.` (p. 392, folio 361); and a seventeenth-century Qadiri
    biography *of Lahore* exists, written by the Mogul heir apparent — `The Sakinat al-auliyff
    [text-layer?: Sakīnat al-auliyāʾ] is a fine biography of Mian Mir and the Qadiriyya in Lahore.`
    (p. 392, folio 361). The book gives that work no date — (not stated).
  - **An annual festival, fixed by place, date and name**: `Madho Lai's anniversary, still celebrated
    in Lahore near the Shalimar Garden on the last Saturday in March, is famous as Meld Chiraghdn
    [text-layer?: Melā Chirāghān], the "Fair of Lamps."` (p. 415, folio 384). No attendance figure,
    no duration, no custodian, no founding year — all (not stated).
  - **A book trade**: of al-Būṣīrī's *Burda*, `Copies are still being sold in the shops of Lahore and
    elsewhere.` (p. 256, folio 225). No shop, no bazaar, no date for the observation — (not stated).
  - **A burial**: `a few years ago a maqam of Muhammad Iqbal (who is buried in Lahore) was erected in
    the garden of Jalaluddin Ruml's tomb in Konya` (p. 270, folio 239). `a few years ago` is the
    book's own dating and no year is given — (not stated).
  - **Residence by proximity only**: `Bullhe Shah (1680-1752) … lived near Lahore.` (p. 419,
    folio 388). The book does not say where he is buried and does not name Kasur — (not stated).
  - **Inside an article title, and fired by chunk 026 under ruling (a) because §6(ii) exempts only
    imprints and index locators**: `Massignon, Louis, and Huart, Clemens. "Les entretiens de Lahore."`
    / `Journal asiatique 209 (1926).` (p. 486, folio 455; the same article cited at p. 391, folio 360,
    footnote 23). **The entire content is the entry as printed.** The Pass-1 worker recorded being
    uncomfortable with this firing — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - **Nothing else in the book is said of Lahore as a place.** No mosque, no other shrine, no quarter,
    no building date, no modern condition — **(not stated)**. Every other `Lahore` in the book (some
    fifty occurrences across chunks 007, 009, 013, 014, 015, 016, 017, 018, 019, 021, 022, 023, 025,
    026) is **a publisher's imprint city in a footnote or a bibliography entry**, or the index entry
    `Lahore, 88, 225, 239, 345, 361, 384, 388` (p. 520, folio 489), and all of those were declined.

- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's `Multan` — all 8 rows in that place, ruling (a)): the city is given four
  statements, two of them about the Arab conquest and the Carmathians.
  - **The northern limit of the first conquest**: `The western provinces of the Indo-Pakistan
    Subcontinent had become part of the Muslim Empire in 711` [text-layer?: clean]`, the year in which
    the Arabs conquered Sind and the adjacent provinces northward up to Multan.` (p. 375, folio 344).
  - **Carmathian territory, and Ḥallāj's contact there — two statements, kept side by side.** `An even
    greater cause for suspicion was his supposed relations with the Carmathians, who ruled not only
    Bahrain but also northern Sind and Multan—places that the mystic had just visited.` (p. 98,
    folio 67); and `Central Arabia had been conquered, in 930, by the Carmathians, an extreme Shia
    group whose capital was located in Bahrain and whose branches extended to the Indus valley; there,
    at Multan, Hallaj had been in touch with them, according to the tradition.` (p. 113, folio 82) —
    `930` prints cleanly, and Schimmel marks the claim as tradition and gives it no source. The
    consequence she draws is political and about Baghdad, not about Multan (p. 98, folio 67).
  - **Where Fakhruddīn ʿIrāqī entered the Suhrawardiyya**: `In Multan he attached himself to
    BahaDuddin, to whom he dedicated some impressive qasldas` (p. 383, folio 352); and
    `Soon after Baha'uddln Zakariya's death, clraql left Multan for Kenya [text-layer?: Konya]`
    (p. 385, folio 354).
  - **A living practice at a tomb in Multan, present tense**: `The tender and intoxicating love poems
    that the Persian poet composed are still being sung by Pakistani musicians at the door of the
    master's tomb in Multan.` (p. 384, folio 353).
  - **An unnamed small shrine Schimmel visited, and it is a women's shrine**: `I vividly remember my
    visit to one such small shrine in Multan, decorated with lovely blue and white tiles; the cheerful
    women in charge of the tomb were no less greedy than the guardians in many other sacred places.`
    (p. 464, folio 433), depending on the sentence immediately before it: `In all the provinces of
    Muslim India and Pakistan one may see shrines of women saints to which men are not admitted (which
    is true for other parts of the Islamic world as well).` (p. 464, folio 433). **The shrine is not
    named, its saint is not named, and no date, silsila or quarter is given** — (not stated). **No
    archive row in Multan is a women's shrine** — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
  - Multan is also used as the fixed point for locating Ucch: `settled in Ucch, northeast of Multan`
    (p. 385, folio 354).
  - Declined and recorded: the nisba `BahaDuddin Zakariya Mul-` / `tani` (p. 310, folio 279), the
    index entry `Multan, 67, 82, 344, 352, 353, 354, 433` (p. 521, folio `49°` [text-layer?: 490]),
    and the imprint `Multan, ca. 1976` in `Shackle, Christopher. Styles and Themes in the Siraiki
    Mystical Poetry of Sind. Multan, ca. 1976.` (p. 503, folio 472).

- **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (the book's `Karachi` — all 11 rows in that place, ruling (a)):
  - **The single place-level statement is the alligator miracle**: `(as happened in Mango Pir near
    Karachi)` (p. 238, folio 207) — quoted in full under **shrine-of-pir-mangho**.
  - **Nothing else in the book says anything about Karachi or about any of these eleven sites
    individually** — (not stated). Every other `Karachi` in the book (some forty occurrences across
    chunks 001, 006, 010, 011, 012, 019, 021, 022, 023, 024, 025, 026) is **a publisher's imprint
    city**, and the index entry `Karachi, 207` (p. 519, folio 488) is a bare pointer; all were
    declined.

- **dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee**, **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple**, **gurdwara-bhai-beba-singh**, **gurdwara-bhai-joga-singh**, **kali-bari-mandir**, **panj-tirath**, **rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine**, **shrine-of-akhund-darweza-baba**, **shrine-of-mian-umar-baba-chamkani**, **wadpagga-sharif** (the book's `Peshawari` — all 10 rows in that place, ruling (a)):

    book says nothing else about Peshawar or about any Peshawar site anywhere.

    edition cited at p. 445, folio 414, footnote 12) — and the index prints only the same demonym,
    `Peshawari, 386` (p. 522, folio 491). Both were declined.

    and is carried here rather than dropped — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.

- **amb-temples-amb-sharif**, **bhagnari-mandir**, **chandragup-baba-chandragup**, **katas-raj-temples**, **shahwala-teja-singh-mandir**, **shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (the book's `Shiva` / `Shivait` — all 8 rows whose principal figure is Shiva, per the
  22 September many-row person rule):

- **shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht**, **tomb-of-baha-al-halim-uch-sharif**, **tomb-of-javindi-bibi**, **tomb-of-ustad-nuriya** (the book's `Ucch` — all 5 Uch Sharif rows, ruling (a); the book prints `Ucch`, never

### Explicit negatives, checked rather than assumed

- **Guru Nanak is absent from the whole `N` run of the `INDEX OF NAMES AND PLACES`, and Ranjit Singh
  from the `R` run** (pp. 521–524, folios `49°`–493). **All eighteen gurdwara rows whose principal
  figure is Guru Nanak, and the samadhi row, are therefore unreachable from this book's own index of
  names**, and the 22 September many-row person rule never fires for him anywhere in the book. This is
  a real finding about the book, not an omission in these notes.
- The book names **no Guru, no gurdwara, no Goraknath, no Valmiki, no Jhulelal, no Durga, no Kali and
  no Tirthankara** anywhere. What it does carry are two bare community headwords, both recorded and
  neither mapped: `Sikh: fight against, 372, 396, 400; mysticism, 434` (p. 525, folio 494) — **the book
  names no gurdwara and no guru under it** — and `Jain, 358` (p. 518, folio 487). The one Sikh title in
  the bibliography, `Mohan Singh (Diwana). Sikh Mysticism. Amritsar, India, 1964.` (p. 487, folio 456),
  **does not name Guru Nanak**, so the many-row person rule does not fire on it either.
- **`toponym_map.txt` has no province key** — no `sind`, `sindh`, `punjab`, `balochistan` or `kashmir`
  — while roughly thirty archive rows carry Sindh in `location_short`. **Chunks 015 and 025 carry the
  densest Sind, Punjab and Balochistan material outside chapter 8 and reach zero archive ids because of
  it**, and so do the Sind passages at pp. 61, 78, 98, 106–107, 144, 226, 279, 358 and 465. No province
  join has been invented. What it costs is recorded in `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
- **`Kashmir` is a real place in the book** (`from Sind to Kashmir`, p. 465, folio 434;
  `Kashmir, 258, 305, 363, 434`, p. 519, folio 488) **but is not a key in `toponym_map.txt`** —
  `sharada-peeth` is indexed only under `sharda` and `neelum valley`. No join was made.
- Archive principal figures checked for and **not found in the book at all**: Sakhi Sarwar, Shah
  Daula Daryai, Shah Rukn-e-Alam, Shah Yusuf Gardez, Syed Musa Pak, Hafiz Muhammad Jamal Multani,
  Bibi Jawindi, Bahaʾal-Halim, Ustad Nuriya, Bari Imam, Meher Ali Shah, Sulaiman Taunsvi, Shams
  Sialvi, Ghulam Farid, Noushah Ganj Bakhsh, Pir Sher Muhammad Sharaqpuri, and every Nanakpanthi /
  Udasi and Hindu-temple principal figure except Shiva, Krishna and Hanuman.
- **Three men with archive-adjacent names were checked and refused**, each on the book's own evidence:
  `al-Hakim at-Tirmidhi`, who `died early in the tenth century in Mecca` (pp. 87–88, folios 56–57), is
  not the archive's `Syed Ali Tirmizi (Pir Baba)`; `Shah Kirmani` (p. 83, folio 52) and
  `Auhaduddin KirmanI` (p. 212, folio 181) are not `Daud Bandagi Kirmani`; and `Shams-i Tabriz`
  (pp. 106, 315, 344, 351, 358) is not `Shah Shams-ud-Din Sabzwari` of Multan.

## Province-level material

*Swept from the raw chunks (`chunk_001`–`chunk_029`, route `text_layer`) under ruling (c), not from the Pass-1 notes. Hits were heaviest in chapter 8, `SUFISM IN INDO-PAKISTAN` (PDF 375–433, chunks 020–023), and in Appendix 1 and Appendix 2 (chunks 024–025). Only statements about a province or region in general are kept. Statements really about one named town, site or person stay with that toponym or figure and are listed in the sweep notes, and so are adjectives that only describe a language or a poem. Register is marked on every bullet: **book's historical prose** (Schimmel's narrative of events), **author's argument** (her characterisation of the region's literature and piety), **author's anecdote** (her own visits), **quoted source** (named), or **ethnographic present** (what she says still holds "today"). Page references are PDF page indices; `folio = PDF − 31` on this book, checked against the running header or the foot folio on every page cited. Damaged tokens are given as printed, then `[text-layer?: reading]`. No siglum and no internal cross-reference has been turned into a page. Every bullet is province-level: weigh accordingly.*

### province: punjab

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: the Mogul period, "the Mogul emperors (especially Akbar)"; author's argument): interest in Rumi's work "seems to have been common to all classes of society—the Mogul emperors (especially Akbar) were as fond of him as the simple villagers in Sind and Punjab" (p. 358, folio 327).
- (book's word: "Punjabi"; refers to: present day ("still claim"), looking back to "Nizamuddln's time" [text-layer?: Niẓāmuddīn's]; ethnographic present): in Nizamuddin's time "Sufism became a mass movement in northwestern India", and "A number of Punjabi tribes still claim to have been converted by Fariduddin Ganj-i Shakar" (p. 381, folio 350; header printed `35O` [text-layer?: 350]).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Shah Ismacll [text-layer?: Ismāʿīl] Shahid" and "Sayyid Ahmad of Bareilly", no date given for the occupation; book's historical prose): both were "known for their brave fight against the Sikh, who had occupied the whole of the Punjab and part of the northwestern frontier" (p. 403, folio 372). The book's unit here is the Punjab as occupied by "the Sikh"; carried as printed.
- (book's word: "a Panjabi village", "The Punjab"; refers to: present day, with Warith Shah's form dated "in the late eighteenth century"; ethnographic present and author's argument): "The visitor who stays in a Panjabi village will certainly find somebody to sing for him the old romance of Hir Ranjha in the classical form given it by Warith Shah"; "The Punjab and the Indus valley have a common tradition of mystical folk literature" (p. 414, folio 383; the section heading `MYSTICAL POETRY IN THE REGIONAL LANGUAGES— SINDHI, PANJABI, PASHTO` begins mid-page under an ordinary header).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the two provinces"; refers to: "back to the fifteenth century", with Madho Lal Husayn as "sixteenth-century"; book's historical prose): "Reliable tradition traces the use of the native language for mystical poetry both in Sind and in the Punjab back to the fifteenth century"; Madho Lal's allusions to the stories of Hir and of Sohni Mehanwal "seems to inaugurate (or perhaps even continue from unknown beginnings) a tradition typical of the religious poetry of the two provinces: the insertion of folk tales into the context of mystical thought" (p. 415, folio 384).
- (book's word: "The Punjab"; refers to: period not stated (an eighteenth-century Panjabi poet is quoted); author's argument): "The Punjab and Sind show close similarities in the types of mystical literature that nourished there"; Panjabi and Sindhi "were excellent media to express mystical feelings, though not mystical theories"; the themes "were sung time and again in forms inherited from the Indian past—dhoras, kdfh [text-layer?: kāfī], way—not in the Persian form of ghazal", in forms "closely connected with indigenous musical modes"; the attack on the "molld" [text-layer?: mollā] was taken up "by the mystics in the northwest part of the Subcontinent", and such verses "appealed particularly to the peasants and the illiterate" (p. 416, folio 385).
- (book's word: "both provinces"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "In both provinces, a preference for the motif of spinning and weaving can be observed, a natural propensity in a cotton-growing country"; "Both provinces have also produced hymns extolling the great masters of the mystical orders, particularly cAbduDl-Qadir Gilanl [text-layer?: ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Gīlānī]"; the imagery "could easily be understood by everybody in the village" (p. 417, folio 386).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the example Sheikh Tahir is "sixteenth-century"; author's argument): "A strange fact—more visible in Sind than in the Punjab—is the way Hindus shared in the Muslim orders. Some saints were claimed by both communities" (p. 418, folio 387).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "after 1800" and "the twentieth century"; book's historical prose): "In the Punjab, the literary heritage of Panjabi was rarely continued by Muslims after 1800, though in the twentieth century a number of mystical treatises have been written and published in Panjabi, including a part translation of Rumi's Mathnawi" (printed "Rumi'sMathnawi"); the language "was used almost exclusively by the Sikhs as their literary medium, whereas the Panjabi Muslims resorted to Urdu", "though they enjoyed and still enjoy listening to the simple mystical verses and ballads in their spoken mother tongue" (p. 427, folio 396; header printed `306` [text-layer?: 396]).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, Appendix 1): the Ahmad bila mim tradition "has been repeated in high literature as much as in popular mystical poetry, be it among the Turkish Bektashis or among the mystical bards of Sind and the Punjab" (p. 451, folio 420; header printed `42O` [text-layer?: 420]).
- (book's word: "Punjab", "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, Sayyid Muhammad Jiw Jan is "in the seventeenth century"; author's argument, Appendix 2): "In the province of Sind, as well as in Punjab, a special aspect of mystical appreciation of women can be observed that is rarely encountered in other parts of the Muslim world, i.e., the representation of the longing soul in the form of a woman"; the bridal symbolism "is used mostly in the mystically interpreted folk tales of Sind and the Punjab"; "The tragic heroines of the Sindhi and Panjabi folk tales—like Hir, Sassui, Sohni, and many others—represent the human soul in search of the beloved" (p. 465, folio 434).

### province: sindh

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "711" and "present Pakistan"; book's historical prose): in 711 the Arabs "also reached Sind, the lower Indus valley, and laid the foundation of a Muslim rule that still continues in present Pakistan" (p. 61, folio 30; header printed `go` [text-layer?: 30]).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "711" and "the early centuries"; book's historical prose): the western provinces of the Subcontinent "had become part of the Muslim Empire in 711, the year in which the Arabs conquered Sind and the adjacent provinces northward up to Multan"; "The Muslim pious in these areas were, in the early centuries, apparently interested mainly in the collection of hadith and in the transmission to the central Muslim countries of scientific information from India" (p. 375, folio 344, printed at the foot of the chapter-opening page).
- (book's word: "Sind", "this province", "northern Sind"; refers to: Hallaj's journey "in 905", with "since 711" and "later centuries"; book's historical prose): "From Gujerat he wandered through Sind, the lower Indus valley, which had been a part of the Muslim Empire since 711. The seeds he sowed there grew in later centuries in the mystical poetry of this province"; the Carmathians "ruled not only Bahrain but also northern Sind and Multan" (p. 98, folio 67). Again: "In 905, a mystic like Hallaj traveled extensively throughout Sind and probably discussed theological problems with the sages of this country" (p. 376, folio 345).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: period not stated, in the discussion of Bayezid (d. 874); author's argument): on the claim that Bayezid's master was "Abu CAH [text-layer?: ʿAlī] as-Sindi, i.e., from Sind", even if he came "from the lower Indus valley and not from a village called Sind close to Bistam", "not every man from Sind could be expected to know all the intricacies of Hindu monistic philosophy" (p. 78, folio 47). The book itself raises a second "Sind" near Bistam; see sweep notes.
- (book's word: "the remote province of Sind"; refers to: the eighteenth century, Shah ʿAbduʾl-Latif's time; author's argument): "Even the poetry of the eighteenth-century mystic Shah cAbduDl-Latif in the remote province of Sind calls the reader to admire Iblis as the one true lover" (p. 226, folio 195; header printed `1Q5` [text-layer?: 195]).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "Sir Richard Burton, writing about 1850", and "Old Sindhi songs", undated; quoted source, Burton, *Sind and the Races That Inhabit the Valley of the Indus* (London, 1851), p. 177, as the footnote gives it): "Old Sindhi songs describe how his [ʿAbduʾl-Qadir Gilani's] spiritual realm extends from Istanbul to Delhi"; Burton "says that there were about a hundred large trees in Sind, all called after Gilani. Each had a pole and a flag hung upon it to fulfill some vow made in adversity or sickness. Sweetmeats were distributed to the poor in hono [text-layer?: honor, cut short at the margin] of the saint, and the fruit and leaves of the trees were not allowed to be touched even by cattle"; "Proper names like Ghauth Bakhsh, 'gift of the help,' in Sind and Balochistan point to his popularity" (p. 279, folio 248).
- (book's word: "Sind", "other provinces"; refers to: period not stated ("It is said"); reported tradition, author's prose): "It is said that some mystics in Sind—and it may be true for other provinces as well-kept only three books for spiritual nourishment: the Koran, the Mathnawi, and the Diwdn [text-layer?: Dīwān] of Hafiz" (p. 358, folio 327). Same page: the Mogul emperors "were as fond of him as the simple villagers in Sind and Punjab" (see `punjab`).
- (book's word: "Sind, to the lower Indus valley"; refers to: present day, "almost as popular today as they were 150 years ago"; ethnographic present): the visitor who goes on "to Sind, to the lower Indus valley, ... will enjoy listening to the moving mystical songs of Shah cAbduDl-Latif of Bhit (1689-1752) or the ardent poems of Sachal Sarmast (d. 1826)" (p. 414, folio 383).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindhi tradition"; refers to: "Also in the sixteenth century" and "From that time on"; book's historical prose): "a group of mystics migrated from Sind to Burhanpur in central India, where they founded a new branch of the Qadiriyya tariqa; they are reported to have used Sindhi verses during their samcf [text-layer?: samāʿ] sessions. From that time on the indigenous Sindhi tradition contains innumerable stories of saints, mainly of those who are buried on Makli Hill near Thatta" (p. 415, folio 384). The same page gives the fifteenth-century date for native-language mystical poetry "both in Sind and in the Punjab" (see `punjab`).
- (book's word: "Sind", "both provinces"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): the Punjab–Sind similarity of mystical literature, the spinning-and-weaving motif "in a cotton-growing country", and the hymns to Gilani, as under `punjab` (pp. 416–417, folios 385–386); "One of the first 'geographical' poems in Sindhi was composed in his honor" (p. 417, folio 386).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the Shia community of Sind"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): Hindu sharing in the Muslim orders is "more visible in Sind than in the Punjab"; "Hindu writers wrote mystical poems in which they used the imagery of the Muslims; they composed poems in honor of the Prophet and even wrote tcfziya [text-layer?: taʿziya] for the Muharram mourning of the Shia community of Sind; or they devoted ballads to the fate of famous Sufi martyrs" (p. 418, folio 387).
- (book's word: "all over Sind"; refers to: present day; ethnographic present): dervishes at Latif's tomb perform his music on Thursday nights, "and the melodies are well known all over Sind" (p. 421, folio 390).
- (book's word: "Sind", "The country", "The province"; refers to: "after the British occupation in 1843", "a new Arabic alphabet in 1852", and "Nadir Shah (1739)"; book's historical prose): "Sindhi continued to flourish after the British occupation in 1843. With the introduction of a new Arabic alphabet in 1852, a considerable literature grew up that included mystical poetry and prose. The country had always been proud of its mystical heritage. Did not Diwan Gidumal, the minister, give the invading Nadir Shah (1739) the most precious treasure Sind had to offer, some of the dust of its saints? The province continued to produce mystics who wrote either in Persian or in their mother tongue" (p. 427, folio 396; header printed `306` [text-layer?: 396]). The book marks the unit as Sind after "the British occupation in 1843"; carried as printed.
- (book's word: "the lower Indus valley", "Sind"; refers to: "the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries, notably between the two world wars", with "(1829-31)" and "1971"; book's historical prose): "The attitude of some mystical leaders in the lower Indus valley ... proved quite decisive in the political arena", e.g. "the hurr, the 'Free,' the loyal and absolutely obedient disciples of the Plr Pagaro [text-layer?: Pīr Pagaro] who were gathered first to support Ahmad Brelwi's struggle against the Sikh (1829-31) and later actively joined in the struggle against the British"; "The hurr even played a minor role in the Indian-Pakistani war in 1971"; "This was not the first time that mystical leaders in Sind, or in other parts of the Muslim world, were engaged in politics or in social reforms" (p. 427, folio 396; p. 428, folio 397).
- (book's word: "southern Sind", "the Indus valley"; refers to: "in the beginning of the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "Mystical speculation flourished in the Indus valley as it did elsewhere. A strong Naqshbandiyya influence in the beginning of the eighteenth century, however, changed the spiritual climate of southern Sind to a certain extent"; Makhdum Muhammad Hashim of Thatta and his relatives attacked the Sufis "who defended the emotional side of religion", and "this Naqshbandi group contributed to promoting a more sober mystical piety" (p. 428, folio 397).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: present day ("partly still continue", "modern Sindhi short stories"); author's argument): "the majority of the masses continued, and partly still continue, in the veneration of their saints, which is echoed in hundreds of simple folk songs and popular verses. The exaggerated veneration of a saint's tomb has been made the target of some of the best modern Sindhi short stories. The discrepancy between the wealth of the shrines and their guardians and the poverty of the masses indeed provides an impressive theme for social and religious criticism. That holds true not only for Sind, but for other parts of the Muslim world as well" (p. 429, folio 398).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the later Naqshbandiyya", period not stated further; author's argument): "In Bengal as in Sind, the Naqshbandiyya and some more militant mystically inspired groups tried to curb the overflowing emotionalism expressed in the lovely folk songs" (p. 432, folio 401; p. 433, folio 402, header printed `4O2` [text-layer?: 402]).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sind and the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, Appendix 1): in Golden Alphabets words with the same letters were combined in one verse, "since alliteration was a frequently used poetical device, particularly in Sind" (p. 454, folio 423); "In the folk poetry of Sind the heroines always complain that no letter from the beloved has reached them" (p. 455, folio 424); the Ahmad bila mim tradition among "the mystical bards of Sind and the Punjab" (p. 451, folio 420).
- (book's word: "The province of Sind", "Sind"; refers to: Burton "long ago", footnoted to *Sind and the Races* (London, 1851), p. 230, and the present; quoted source (Burton) and author's argument, Appendix 2): "The province of Sind, generally famous for its saint worship, is particularly rich in legends connected with women saints, as the critical observer Richard Burton long ago observed: 'To the credit of the Sindhi it must be said that they do not refuse to admit the religious merits of the softer sex'"; he speaks of the "faqlrdni [text-layer?: faqīrānī] (feminine for faqlr [text-layer?: faqīr])": "a few of them occasionally rise to the high rank of a murshid" (p. 464, folio 433). His remarks on "Blbi [text-layer?: Bībī] Fatima Hajram, a haftza [text-layer?: ḥāfiẓa]" are "corroborated by the numerous books devoted by Sindhi scholars to the religious life of their country. As elsewhere in the Muslim world, we find in Sind whole groups of women saints, like the haft cafifa [text-layer?: haft ʿafīfa], 'the Seven Chaste,' ... or the mukhaddardt-i abddliyya [text-layer?: mukhaddarāt-i abdāliyya], 'the veiled women in the rank of abddl [text-layer?: abdāl],' and countless others" (p. 465, folio 434). Same page: the longing soul as a woman "In the province of Sind, as well as in Punjab" (see `punjab`).

### province: balochistan

- (book's word: "Balochistan"; refers to: period not stated, beside Burton "writing about 1850"; author's argument): "Proper names like Ghauth Bakhsh, 'gift of the help,' in Sind and Balochistan point to his [Gilani's] popularity" (p. 279, folio 248).

### not attached

- "Kashmir" (p. 289, folio 258): "The Kubrawiyya became the most important order in Kashmir in the latter part of the fourteenth century", Sayyid ʿAli Hamadhani reaching "Kashmir in 1371"; "the Kashmir valley, which had only recently been Islamized". The book does not name the Pakistani part.
- "Kashmir" (p. 394, folio 363): "Kashmir, the lovely summer resort of the Moguls, had been a center of mystical poetry since the Kubrawl leader Sayyid CAH Hamadhani had settled there, with seven hundred followers, in 1371". The part is not stated.
- "from Sind to Kashmir" (p. 465, folio 434): "In the western part of Muslim India, however—from Sind to Kashmir—the poets followed the Hindu tradition, which describes the soul as the longing girl". This is a span across regions; the Sind half is carried under `sindh` through the same page's "province of Sind" sentence.
- "part of the northwestern frontier" (p. 403, folio 372): the Sikh occupation. This is not the book's name for a province ("frontier province" is the alias), so it does not take `khyber pakhtunkhwa`.
- "the frontier provinces of India" (p. 377, folio 346): "the type of the soldier-Sufi ... is sometimes found in the frontier provinces of India, as it is in other border areas between Islam and the land of the infidels". No province is named.

The heaviest chunks are 022 (Punjab/Panjab 22, Sind 13, Sindhi 15) and 023 (Sind 17, Sindhi 16). `Medina` does not fire, because `dina` is not in this sweep's pattern. Bibliography and index hits (chunks 025 from PDF 468, and 026–029) were skipped as carrying no substantive text: imprint cities `Hyderabad, Sind` (about 30 across the book), titles such as Burton's *Sind and the Races*, Mayne's *Saints of Sind*, Quddusi's *Tadhkira-yi sufiya-yi Sind/Panjab*, Parsram's *Sufis of Sind*, Sadarangani's *Persian Poets of Sind*, and index headwords (`Sind, 30, 47, …`, `Punjab, 169, 327, …`, `Balochistan, 248, 390, 391`, `Kashmir, 258, 305, 363, 434`, `Hurr (militant group in Sind)`, `Pir Pagaro (mystical leader in Sind)`). None of their locators was used as a page. Excluded as town or site statements (toponyms, ruling a): Hujwiri's tomb as "a place of pilgrimage for the Panjabis" (Lahore, p. 376); Farid settled "in the Punjab on the river Sutlej" (Pakpattan, p. 377); Lal Shahbaz "saint of Sehwan, who lived in the mid-thirteenth century in Sind" (p. 386); "one of the saints of Thatta in Sind" (p. 216); Makli Hill (p. 415); Hala "near present-day Hyderabad-Sind" and "the sacred mountain Hinglaj in Balochistan" (p. 421); "Punhun, the prince of Kecch in Balochistan" (folk tale, p. 422); Shah ʿInayat of Jhok, "An interesting case in Sind" (p. 428). Excluded as person epithets or single anecdotes: Sultan Bahu "of the Punjab" and Latif "in Sind" (p. 200; Latif "in Sind" also pp. 144, 248, 265, 331, 419); Mian Mir "had come there from Sind" (p. 392); Bullhe Shah "the Rumi of Punjab" (p. 419); Sachal "the cAttar of Sind" (p. 426); Janullah of Rohri among "the outstanding Persian poets of Sind" (p. 428); Mir ʿAli Shir Qaniʿ "historian of Lower Sind" (p. 429); Bibi Jamal Khatun of the Qadiriyya "during its formative period in the Punjab" (p. 464); "a seventeenth-century mystic from Sind" (p. 171); "a Sindhi saint" thrown into the Indus (p. 238); "In Sind, a Hindu physician was converted" (p. 240, one anecdote). Excluded as language or literature only, with no regional claim: chunk 001 (contents line and transliteration note), pp. 49, 64, 105, 106, 107 ("plaintive Sindhi folk songs in a remote corner of the Indus valley", which does not name Sind), 139, 255, 257, 271, 336, 357, 402, 419–420, 429 ("heyday of Sindhi and Panjabi Sufi poetry"), 430 (a Pashto poet resembling "the poets in Sind and Punjab"), 432 ("the Sindhi or Panjabi mystics" against "the Afghan hills"), 450, 451 ("a Punjabi Sufi"), 454 (Golden Alphabets list). Flags a human must check: (1) headers misread on p. 61 `go`, p. 226 `1Q5`, p. 381 `35O`, p. 427 `306` (read 396 by parity and PDF − 31; the printed `306` is a three-for-nine misread), p. 433 `4O2`, p. 451 `42O`; (2) the Sikh-occupation bullet (p. 403) gives no date, and p. 427 dates "Ahmad Brelwi's struggle against the Sikh (1829-31)". The two are kept apart, and the book's "Sayyid Ahmad of Bareilly" and "Ahmad Brelwi" are not equated here; (3) p. 78 is the book's own doubt, a "Sind" in the lower Indus valley or "a village called Sind close to Bistam"; the bullet keeps only the general clause; (4) "Bulrri" (p. 428, "the Sayyids of Bulrri") is unresolved and not used; (5) every "Indus valley"/"lower Indus valley" bullet is attached to `sindh` only where the book itself equates it with Sind or names Sind in the same passage (pp. 61, 98, 414, 427–428).

## Cross-cutting material

*Practices, institutions and economy first, then the orders, then the author's own arguments, then the apparatus. Page references are reproduced exactly as the chunk notes give them; every `[text-layer?]` flag is kept on the word it was attached to. Index locators printed inside backticks are the book's own damaged folios and are **never** converted into PDF pages; the `(p. N, folio N)` after each is the page the entry is printed on.*

### The khānqāh and its vocabulary

The book's own account of how the Sufi centre came into being, and of the five words for it, sits at pp. 262–263, folios 231–232, and it describes the form every shrine in this archive took.

- Why a building became necessary: `At the time that the fraternities came into existence, the center of mystical activity was no longer the private house or shop of the master. A more institutional structure proved to be necessary to cope with the growing number of disciples and adepts.` (p. 262, folio 231).
- **Khānqāh**: `These new centers were usually called khanqah in the eastern Islamic world; the same term was used in medieval Egypt, where the Sufi khdnqahs formed cultural and theological centers and were su-` / `sidized by the government or endowed by influential benefactors.` (pp. 262–263, folios 231–232). `su-`/`sidized` [text-layer?: subsidized — the word is broken at the right margin and the `b` is absent; **not** reconstructed as a printed form].
- **Zāwiya** and *tekke* (vocabulary heads, not archive ids): `The word zawiya, literally "corner," was used for smaller units, like the solitary dwelling place of a sheikh. The Turks would call the Sufi convent tekke.` (p. 262, folio 231).
- **Ribāṭ** and **dargāh**: `The term ribdt, essentially connected with the frontier castles of the soldiers who defend Islam and expand it, could also be used to refer to the center of a fraternity. Often the expression dargdh "door, court," is used.` (pp. 262–263, folios 231–232). `ribdt` [text-layer?: ribāṭ]; `dargdh` [text-layer?: dargāh].
- The index confirms each word with its own gloss, locators unconverted: `Khdnqdh, 210, 231, 232, 235, 238, 433; Chishti, 345, 347, 352, 356` (p. 534, folio 503); `Ribat (convent), 231` (p. 540, folio 509); `Tekhe (convent), 231, 330; Mevlevi, 324` (p. 542, folio 511); `Zawiya ("corner," dwelling place of a Sufi), 231` (p. 543, folio 512); `Dargdh (dervish convent), 232, 238, 360, 397` [text-layer?: Dargāh] — **the book's own working definition of the word this archive is built on** — (p. 530, folio 499); and `Qubba (small sanctuary), 407` (p. 539, folio 508).
- The institution is dated: `The basic rules of mystical education were elaborated during the eleventh century, and in a comparatively short span of time—beginning in the early twelfth century—mystical fraternities that included adepts from all strata of society were emerging.` (p. 262, folio 231). Sanāʾī could already call it `The khanqdh is the nest for the bird "purity," / it is the rosegarden of pleasure and the garden of faithfulness,` `shortly after noo` [text-layer?: 1100 — reading rests on the measured `11 → n` and `0 → o` classes] (p. 263, folio 232; footnote 5 cites `Abu 3 l-Majd Majdud Sana3!, Sana/'Pabad, line 289.` [text-layer?: Sanāʾīʾābād]). The index keeps the image: `Rose garden, 166, 380; khanqdh as, 232; of Abraham, 119` (p. 540, folio 509).
- An older and smaller form of the same thing: the `ahl as-suffa, "the People of the Bench," poor and pious members "of the community who lived in the mosque of Medina` (p. 59, folio 28 — the stray opening quotation mark before `of the community` is as printed), and the first named ascetic settlement, founded by one of Ḥasan al-Baṣrī's disciples, `a settlement of ascetically inclined people in Abbadan on the Persian Gulf` (p. 62, folio 31). Abū Saʿīd ibn Abīʾl-Khair is `the first to draw up a simple monastic rule for his Sufi community` (p. 119, folio 88).

### Architecture: the compound built around a grave

- The compound and its parts: `The history of Islamic art can contribute more to the history of the construction of such Sufi buildings, which were sometimes isolated but more frequently were connected with a mosque, a large kitchen for both the disciples and the guests, and sometimes a school.` (p. 263, folio 232).
- **The grave is the centre, and can come first**: `The tomb of the founder was usually located in the same compound—unless the compound was built later around the sacred place where the first saint of the order or subgroup was buried.` (p. 263, folio 232).
- Two internal plans: `In some khanqdhs, the dervishes lived in small cells—the Mevlana Miizesi in Konya is a fine example of this type; other convents had only one large room in which all the dervishes lived, studied, and worked.` (p. 263, folio 232).
- The sheikh's own quarter: `The sheikh himself would live, with his family, in one quarter of the compound and see his disciples at fixed hours to supervise their spiritual progress; he would generally lead the five prayers in the congregation.` (p. 263, folio 232).
- The retreat cell as a building: `Sometimes they would construct a special chillakhdna` [text-layer?: chillakhāna]`, a room for seclusion, which might even be subterranean (N 325), close to the center of the fraternity or in a blessed place nearby.` (p. 136, folio 105) — no site, date or patron is given, `(not stated)`. Indexed as `Chillakhdna (solitary place to perform seclusion), 105` [text-layer?: Chillakhāna] (p. 530, folio 499).
- The concert hall as a building: `Samcfkhanas` [text-layer?: samāʿkhānas]`, houses in which the Sufis could listen to music and let themselves be drawn into ecstatic states, were founded in Baghdad as early as the second half of the ninth century. The orthodox were scandalized by the sight of what took place there.` (p. 212, folio 181). Indexed as `Samackhana (room for sama*), 181` (p. 540, folio 509).
- Architecture read as doctrine: `The equation of faqr with annihilation, and the emphasis on the negative, nonexistent aspect of things is expressed, in Islamic art, by the large empty hall of the mosque, which inspires the visitor with numinous grandeur. It is also reflected in the negative space in the arabesques or in calligraphy.` (p. 154, folio 123). Calligraphy on buildings: `the artists created very intricate patterns of floriated and lacelike plaited Run script` [text-layer?: unreadable] `on domes, minarets, and objects of minor art` (p. 455, folio 424), and `Arabic calligraphy is the typical expression of a culture that outlawed the representation of living beings` (p. 455, folio 424).
- The only dated Subcontinental building programme in this material is Mogul: Akbar `built a magnificent tomb` for Muhammad Ghauth Gwaliori (p. 386, folio 355); `In gratitude for the blessings of this saint, Akbar erected a wonderful Sufi dargah in his new capital Fathpur Sikri.` and `When Jihangir grew up, he adorned Ajmer, Mucinuddm Chishti's city, with beautiful buildings of white marble.` (p. 391, folio 360).
- For contrast the book gives exact Anatolian building dates where it has them — `the Ince Minareli (1258) and the Karatay Medrese (1251)`, with the Konya mosque built by `Sultan 'Ala^uddin Kaykobad` and Bahāʾuddīn Walad called to Konya `in 1228` (p. 343, folio 312; all four dates print cleanly) — and a tomb recovered by excavation: Shams's grave `was discovered a few years ago by the then director of the Mevlana Miizesi` [text-layer?: Mevlânâ Müzesi]`, Mehmet Onder; it is located beneath the memorial erected for Shams some time after the event.` (p. 344, folio 313); `a few years ago` is relative to the book and is not dated, `(not stated)`.

### Endowment, economy and the trade in blessing

- **The two economies of a khānqāh, stated as the basic alternative**: `The organization of the khanqdh was not everywhere alike. Some khanqdhs lived on futuh, unsolicited gifts or donations, whereas others enjoyed regular stipends.` (p. 263, folio 232). Indexed as `Futuh ("unsolicited gifts"), 232, 347` (p. 531, folio 500).
- **Waqf, defined by the book**: `Others put their inherite` / `property into a waqf, a tax-exempt endowment, bequeathed for the living expenses of their disciples.` (p. 269, folio 238). `inherite` [text-layer?: inherited — the final `d` is lost at the right margin]. Indexed as `Waqf (tax-exempt endowment), 238` (p. 543, folio 512).
- **Endowment of land, glossed by the index in its Indian form**: `Jagir (India) (endowment of land), 347` (p. 534, folio 503).
- Schimmel states the problem as an enigma and does not solve it: `And one enigma remains to be resolved: how so many of those who preached poverty as their pride became wealthy landlords and fitted perfectly into the feudal system, amassing wealth laid at their feet by poor, ignorant followers.` (p. 269, folio 238). The one figure she gives: `It seems that by the early thirteenth century some sheikhs were spending enormous amounts of money for the maintenance of their followers: Jam! speaks of 20,000 gold dinar a year in the case of Majduddin Baghdad! (N 442).` (p. 269, folio 238; `20,000` prints cleanly).
- **Amulets as the sheikh's trade**: `People would flock around the khanqdh or dargdh, waiting for help for all their needs, hoping that the sheikh, or his khalifa, would give them some amulets or teach them some useful prayer formulae.` and `Indeed, the fabrication of amulets was one of the major occupations of mystical leaders in later times.` (p. 269, folio 238).
- **State endowment measured on Mamluk Egypt** — the book's only fully costed convent: `We have exact accounts of the Sufi khdnqdhs in Egypt during the Mamluk period. The khanqdh Scfid as-Su^ada*` [text-layer?: Saʿīd as-Suʿadāʾ]`, founded by the Ayyubid Sultan Saladin in 1173, was most revered, and "in order to get blessings," people liked to watch the three hundred dervishes who lived there when they went out for the Friday prayer.` (p. 263, folio 232; `1173` and `three hundred` print cleanly). The ration list: `The sultans gave rich endownments` [text-layer?: endowments] `to the inhabitants of "their" khan-` / `qdh—daily rations of meat, bread, and sometimes sweetmeat, soap, new clothing for the two Muslim festivals, and some cash. The khdnqahs also enjoyed some tax privileges.` (p. 263, folio 232).
