And the supervising office: `They were controlled by the amir majlis, one of the highest ranking members of the military government (comparable to the Home Minister).` (p. 265, folio 234; footnote 6 adds Schimmel's own two studies and `Virginia Vacca, "Aspetti politici e sociali dei 'sufi' mu-` / `sulmani," Oriente moderno 35, no. i (1955)` [text-layer?: no. 1]).
- **The Chishti type case, refusing both land and grants** — **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** (the book's `Farlduddln`, named as its outstanding example): `The maintenance of Farlduddln's khdnqdh—and he is mentioned here as an outstanding example of the early Chishti way of life—was difficult, since the sheikh relied exclusively upon gifts (futuh), and the khdnqdh did not own or cultivate land from which the dervishes might draw their living.` (p. 378, folio 347); `No jtigirs` [text-layer?: jāgīrs] `(endowments of land) or grants from the rulers were accepted by the Chishtis, for they refused to deal with the worldly government.` (p. 378, folio 347); `these Chishti saints, who considered everything in the hands of the rulers to be unlawful` (p. 378, folio 347). Life inside it: `The dervishes in the khdnqdh regarded themselves as "guests of God," living and working in one large room; visitors were always welcome, and the table always spread for unexpected guests—if there was any food available. The residents had to serve the sheikh and the community, and their main occupations were prayer, worship, and the study of books of devotion and the biographies of saints.` (p. 378, folio 347). And the social consequence: `The fact that the Chishti khanqdhs avoided any discrimination between the disciples and practiced a classless society attracted many people into their fold.` (p. 376, folio 345).
- **The Suhrawardi counter-model**, all at p. 383, folio 352: hereditary succession; fixed visiting hours instead of the open table; the sheikh as a `well-to-do landlord` keeping grain in the house; willingness to `mix freely with members of the ruling classes`; and `This contrasting attitude toward the world, and toward its most dangerous representative, the government, has survived through the centuries.`
- **A dargāh economy and its social effect, with a land redistribution** — **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** (the book's Jhok): `The Sufi sheikhs and the feudal lords in the country lost large numbers of disciples and workers, whereas the dargah in Jhok flourished.` and `A considerable amount of land was distributed among Shah clnayat's followers after an unjustified attack on Jhok` (p. 428, folio 397), with a siege of `four months'` and an execution `in January 1718` (p. 428, folio 397).
- **Shrine wealth as a target of criticism, and the archive's own subject named as such**: `The exaggerated veneration of a saint's tomb has been made the target of some of the best modern Sindhi short stories.` and `The discrepancy between the wealth of the shrines and their guardians and the poverty of the masses indeed provides an impressive theme for social and religious criticism. That holds true not only for Sind, but for other parts of the Muslim world as well.` (p. 429, folio 398). The footnote identifying the story is printed with its number missing from the transcription and names `Jamal Abro, Munhun kdro`, translated by Schimmel as `"Geschwarzten Gesichtes"` (p. 429, folio 398).
- **Custodians described as greedy, at a named place** — **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan", ruling (a), place-level): `the cheerful women in charge of the tomb were no less greedy than the guardians in many other sacred places` (p. 464, folio 433). This is the only remark on shrine income in that chunk and it is a complaint, not a figure — no sums, no endowment, no Auqaf, `(not stated)`.
- **Women as the endowing class**: `From the earliest times on, wealthy women are mentioned as benefactors of Sufi masters or institutions or of whole groups of dervishes. They would endow the khdnqdhs` [text-layer?: khānqāhs] `with food and money` (p. 464, folio 433), with two named instances on the same page — `Bibi Fatima supported Abu Sacid ibn Abi'l-Khayr and his disciples in the early eleventh century` and `one of Aurangzeb's daughters offered a whole complex of buildings to Mir Dard in eighteenth-century Delhi` (p. 464, folio 433) — generalised as women `could do much good in social service, in founding khdnqdhs, or in contributing to the growth of extant facilities for the dervish community`, activities that `can still be witnessed in some parts of the Muslim world.` (p. 464, folio 433). The same imperial gift is recorded from the other side: Dard `apparently did not even leave the compound that had been given to him and his father by one of Aurangzeb's daughters` (p. 411, folio 380) — the book names neither the daughter nor the compound, `(not stated)`.
- **Sufi trades and the redistribution of earnings**: the surnames `saqati, "huckster"; halldj` [text-layer?: ḥallāj]`, "cotton carder"; nassaj, "weaver"; warrdq` [text-layer?: warrāq]`, "bookseller" or "copyist"; qawdrln` [text-layer?: qawārīrī — printed `qawdrln`, reading uncertain]`, "glassmaker"; hadddd` [text-layer?: ḥaddād]`, "blacksmith"` (p. 115, folio 84); `Some would work regularly and use a trifle of their gain for themselves, distributing the main part to the Sufis, so that it could be said, for example, that a person "veiled his saintliness under the modest shape of a cupper"` (`N 572`) (p. 115, folio 84); `Some left their original professions after they had gained fame as mystical leaders and attracted a few disciples. Others were trained as theologians, traditionalists, or jurists in one of the four schools of law.` (p. 115, folio 84). Salmān `was later regarded as the patron saint of small artisans, just as some of the ninth- and tenth-century Sufis were to become patrons of the artisan groups whose professions they shared` (p. 59, folio 28; `MM 5`).
- **Money refused, and the economy that would follow**: `To store money or goods was regarded as a major sin` (p. 150, folio 119); `Even mystics who cannot be regarded as typical representatives of strict tawakkul often distributed all their money in the evening or gave away everything they had on Friday. They also refused any medical treatment.` (p. 151, folio 120); and the macro-judgement, `If everybody had lived according to the ideals promoted by some of the early ascetics, the whole economic and social fabric of the Muslim Empire would have collapsed.` (p. 151, folio 120).
- **Money from the state is polluting**: `Food or anything belonging to or coming from the ruling classes was regarded as suspect. It is well known that in the old days the most pious scholars refused to accept government offices; even in later periods relations between the Sufis and the rulers were often cold, if not strained` (p. 142, folio 111), with the extreme of scruple — `Pious women would not spin in the light of a neighbor's candle; a mystic whose only sheep had grazed on someone else's lawn by mistake would no longer drink its milk` (p. 142, folio 111).
- **Begging, and the dervish's one possession**: `One of the aspects of true faqr is that the mystic must not ask anything of anyone` (p. 152, folio 121), because `to receive would burden the soul with gratitude toward the giver`, `minnat, "gratitude,"` having `a negative value for the faithful.` (p. 152, folio 121); indexed as `Minnat ("gratitude" as a burden and obligation), 121` (p. 536, folio 505) and `Independence (not needing anything, istighna), 123` (p. 533, folio 502). Against the ideal: `it was certainly easier to beg food at the doors of the rich and give a blessing in exchange than to pursue a normal profession` (p. 52, folio 21). The mat: `The reed mat on which the mystic slept, and which often constituted his only worldly possession, became in later Persian poetry a symbol of spiritual wealth` (p. 152, folio 121). A rich dervish is nonetheless possible: `The dervish, the Sufi, may be rich if he has the right attitude` (p. 153, folio 122).
- **Common property inside the convent**: `A dervish must never say "my shoes" or "my so-and-so"—he should have no private property (N 216, 109). If one possesses anything, he should give it to the brethren; otherwise he will lose his spiritual rank (N 169.` (p. 261, folio 230 — the citation `(N 169.` is printed with a period where the closing bracket belongs); Schimmel calls this `a kind of communism comparable to that of the first Christians`.
- **"Shopkeeper sheikhs"** is the book's own term for the commercial end of the miracle trade: miracle-mongering `constituted part of the activities of the "shopkeeper sheikhs" and pseudo-mystics` (p. 243, folio 212).
- The index's economic vocabulary, locators unconverted: `Faqr ("poverty"), 120-24. See also Dervishes; Poverty`, `Faqlr ("poor"), 20, 28, 121, 122, 124`, `Faqlranl (feminine of faqir), 433` (p. 531, folio 500); `Poverty, 28, 35, 37, 58, 60, 95, 100, in, 120-24, 126, 238, 242, 322, 429; as quality of Muhammad, 222, 315; cAttar on, 304. See also Faqr` — the `in` inside the locator string is a damaged digit and is **not resolved** (p. 538, folio 507); `Tul al-amal ("extended hope"), 120` (p. 542, folio 511).

### Hospitality, langar and the travelling dervish

- The kitchen is part of the compound's definition — `a large kitchen for both the disciples and the guests` (p. 263, folio 232) — but **the word *langar* is not used anywhere in this material**, `(not stated)`.
- The open and the closed house: `Orders like the Chishtis in India were extremely hospitable, and foreign visitors were always admitted; in others, strict rules were enforced about visiting times and the types of visitors who were allowed to see the master. Arrangements for occasional and for long-term visitors were found in almost every khanqdh.` (p. 263, folio 232).
- The ideal: `This feeling and the proverbial oriental hospitality have worked together in shaping the ideal of the Sufi who will offer everything to his guests, leaving nothing for himself or even for his family.` (p. 261, folio 230). Abū Saʿīd's rules require `The Sufi should welcome the poor and needy, and look after them.` and `He should not eat without another person participating` (p. 274, folio 243), on which Schimmel glosses `to eat alone is considered unpleasant and unlawful; to offer even the smallest morsel to a brother who may be hungrier than the host is highly appreciated.` (p. 274, folio 243).
- **The catered khānqāh, counted by portion** — from the fifteenth-century Indian cat story: `When travellers were about to come to the khanqah, it used to indicate their number by mewing; from this the servitors of the khanqah would know for how many guests to lay the cloth for the meal. At the time when food was served, the cat also was given a portion equal to that of others who were present` (p. 241, folio 210). The khanqah has `servitors`; a party of `dervishes` arrives and is fed by portion count (pp. 241–242, folios 210–211). When the cat dies saving them, `The Sufi remarked that the cat had sacrificed his own life for the sake of the dervishes, and a tomb should be built for him.` (p. 242, folio 211).
- **What a host owes a travelling dervish**: `When a Sufi decided to wander through the Muslim lands to visit other communities of friends, or to find a master who would instruct him further and perhaps invest him with the khirqa-yi tabarruk, the "frock of blessing," he was to take with him a stick and a beggar's bowl. Other Sufis were supposed to receive him well, feed him, bring him to the hot bath, and, if possible, provide him with new clothes, or at least wash his frock—according to the legends, people who neglected these duties were severely punished.` (p. 266, folio 235).
- Convent cats as guardians: `Cats, especially, are often associated with miracles, and some convents had cats as guardians.` (p. 240, folio 209); the Heddawa `explicitly venerates cats` and `even the novices are called quetat, "little tomcats."` (p. 241, folio 210), indexed as `Quetat ("little torn cats," the novices in the Heddawa order), 210` (p. 539, folio 508).

### Adab, admission and training

- Admission and probation: `The newcomer was sometimes made to wait for days at the sheikh's door, and sometimes as a first test was treated very rudely. Usually three years of service were required before the adept could be formally accepted in a master's group—one year in the service of the people, one in the service of God, and one year in watching over his own heart.` (p. 132, folio 101).
- The three bonds: `The relation of the novice to the master is threefold: by the khirqa, by being instructed in the formula of dhikr (recollection), and by company (suhbat), service, and education (N 560).` (p. 133, folio 102).
- Training scales with numbers: `The formerly more individual regulations became stricter, the larger the number of adepts was, but the main object remained the same for most of the orders—to break and train the lower soul. Only a few orders laid more emphasis upon the purification of the heart than upon ascetic preliminaries.` (p. 265, folio 234).
- A measured curriculum: `The Mevlevis, to mention a prominent example, trained the novice in different kitchen functions; at the same time he had to study Rumi's Mathnawl and its correct recitation and interpretation, as well as the technique of the whirling dance. This training continued for 1001 days.` (p. 265, folio 234; `1001` prints cleanly).
- Service and etiquette: `Service to men has always been one of the first stages in the preparatory steps of the Path, but it remains the true Sufi's duty throughout his life.` with `"whoever excuses himself from the service of his brethren, God will give him a humiliation from which he cannot be rescued" (N 268)` (p. 260, folio 229); `The manuals of Sufism are replete with stories illustrating correct behavior (adab) in the presence of the sheikh and the brethren. Whole books were devoted to this topic` and `"Everything has a servant, and correct behavior is the servant of religion" (N 91)` (p. 261, folio 230); `The rules of behavior among the Sufis became more and more detailed—every limb had its own etiquette.` (p. 266, folio 235), footnote 9 citing `c Abdur Rahman as-SuIamI, Kitab (idab as-suhba` [text-layer?: *Kitāb ādāb aṣ-ṣuḥba*]`, ed. Meir J. Kister (Jerusalem, 19(54), p. 85.` — `19(54)` [text-layer?: year not recoverable; the third digit is overwritten by a bracket and is **not** guessed].
- And the exemptions: `In spite of the numerous rules for correct behavior, a considerable number of indulgences, rukhas, existed along with them; these are exemptions from duties under certain circumstances.` (p. 266, folio 235).
- **Abū Saʿīd's ten rules for a convent**, cited by Schimmel as `(NS 46)` and printed complete on one page (p. 274, folio 243), in the book's wording: `1) The disciple should keep his garments clean and be always in a state of ritual purity.` · `2) One should not sit in a holy place for gossiping.` · `3) The prayer should be performed in the congregation, particularly by the beginner.` · `4) Much night prayer is recommended` · `5) At dawn, the disciple should pray for forgiveness.` · `6) Then, in the early morning, he should read the Koran, abstaining from talk till sunrise.` · `7) Between the two evening prayers, he should be occupied with his recollection (dhikr) and the specially litany (wird) which is given to him by his master.` (`specially` is as printed) · `8) The Sufi should welcome the poor and needy, and look after them.` · `9) He should not eat without another person participating` · `10) The disciple should not absent himself without permission.` Schimmel's gloss on rule 2 records graveyard sociability: `the womanfolk love to go to the graveyards not only to perform pious acts but to sit there and chat.` (p. 274, folio 243).
- Obedience: Ghazzālī `also maintained that complete and absolute obedience is necessary, even if the sheikh should be wrong` (p. 134, folio 103); `the same applies to the disciple who has to honor and obey his sheikh, even if the sheikh is a veritable satan` (p. 224, folio 193); the system that produced it is traced to Abū ʿUthmān al-Ḥīrī, `one of the first to introduce a strict system of education for his disciples, a trend that developed finally into the perinde ac cadaver obedience that distinguishes later Sufi education.` (p. 84, folio 53), indexed as `Perinde ac cadaver, 53, 103` (p. 538, folio 507). Schimmel adds the cost: `This attitude in later times lent itself to dangerous consequences; indeed, it is one of the reasons for modernist Muslims' aversion to Sufism. But the original intent was genuine: the master should act like a physician, diagnosing and healing the illnesses and defects of the human soul.` (p. 135, folio 104).
- Visiting the master is itself a duty: `Visiting his master is a religious duty of the disciple (N 115), for he will find from him what he will not find elsewhere.` and `Even to have met a leading sheikh at once endow` [text-layer?: endows — word truncated at the right margin] `a man with a higher rank (N 115).` (p. 135, folio 104). The great ablution `before every prayer or before visiting their spiritual director` (`N 292`) `was, for them, a religious duty comparable to prayer.` (p. 180, folio 149).
- The tie between the two: `The strong relationship between sheikh and murid is exemplified in the technique of tawajjuh, concentration upon the sheikh, which later orders, mainly the Naqshbandiyya, considered necessary for the successful performance of the dhikr. One speaks in Turkish of rabita kurmak, "to establish a tie" between master and disciple.` and `The sheikh, too, would practice tawajjuh and thus "enter the door of the disciple's heart" to watch him and to guard him every moment.` (p. 268, folio 237). `tawajjuh, the concentration of two partners on` (p. 236, folio 205) `each other, is necessary to produce such an effect; it is taken for granted that the sheikh and his disciple are, so to speak, on the same wave length.` (p. 237, folio 206). Indexed as `Tawajjuh (strong concentration of master and disciple on each other), 205, 237, •afifi 3°°` — the locator carries two `°` and is **not resolved** (p. 542, folio 511) — and `Rabita kurmak ("establish a tie" between master and disciple), 237` (p. 539, folio 508).

### Initiation: the khirqa, the bayʿa and the tāj

- **The ceremony, in one sentence**: `During the initiation ceremony—a festive day in the dervish community—the adept had to pronounce the bay'a, the oath of allegiance, and was invested with the khirqa, the Sufi frock. An essential part of the ceremony consists of the novice's putting his hand into the sheikh's hand so that the baraka is properly transmitted.` (p. 265, folio 234). Indexed as `Bay'-a (oath of allegiance), 234` [text-layer?: Bayʿa] (p. 530, folio 499).
- **The cap**: `Another important act is the bestowing of the taj, the dervish cap. Headdresses differed from order to order both in shape and in color, and the number of their parts—twelve, according to the twelve imams, or nine, or seven—has symbolic meaning.` (p. 265, folio 234). Indexed as `Taj ("crown"), dervish cap, 234` (p. 542, folio 511).
- **The khirqa carries baraka by contact**: `by donning a garment that has been worn, or even touched, by the blessed hands of a master, the disciple acquires some of the baraka, the mystico-magical power of the sheikh.` Two kinds: `the khirqa-yi irdda` [text-layer?: khirqa-yi irāda]`, which the aspirant gets from the master to whom he has sworn allegiance, and the khirqa-yi tabarruk, the "frock of blessing," which he may obtain from different masters with whom he has lived or whom he has visited during his journeys` (p. 133, folio 102). Both indexed with glosses: `Khirqa-yi irdda (from the educating master), 102` and `Khirqa-yi tabarruk ("for the sake of blessing"), 102, 235` (p. 534, folio 503), under the main entry `Khirqa (patched frock, sign of the der-vish), 102, 103, 234, 325; from Khidr, 106, 264; as mantle of the Prophet, 222; bestowed on Ahmad-i Jam, 244; bestowed on cAbduDl-Qadir GllanI, 247. See also Initiation` (p. 534, folio 503).
- Colour and its meaning: `The khirqa was usually dark blue. It was practical for travel, since dirt was not easily visible on it, and at the same time it was the color of mourning and distress`; later `in certain circles mystics would choose for their frocks a color that corresponded to their mystical station (CL 166), and sabzpush, "he who wears green," has always been an epithet for those who live on the highest possible spiritual level` (`cf. V 52`) (p. 133, folio 102); indexed as `Sabzpush ("wearing green," the highest saints, prophets, and angels), 102` (p. 540, folio 509).
- The frock disputed and the frock ridiculed: `Some of the Sufis invented complicated rules for the sewing and stitching of their patched frocks, disputing whether to use thin or coarse thread in putting the patches together` (p. 133, folio 102), until `others might accuse them of worshiping the khirqa like an idol` (p. 134, folio 103); against it, `"This patched frock must have been sewn in pre-eternity"` (`U 70-71`) (p. 51, folio 20) and `"I looked into Hell, and I saw that most of its inhabitants were those donning a patched frock and carrying a food-bowl"` (`B 309`), attributed to `a saint of the eleventh century` (p. 52, folio 21). Indexed as `Patched frock, 20, 21. See also Kkirqa` — the cross-reference headword is damaged (p. 538, folio 507).
- The khirqa read as the Prophet's mantle: `the Sufi khirqa was sometimes interpreted as inspired by the mantle in which the Prophet had wrapped his family members, and the Sufis thus became "members of his family."` (p. 253, folio 222).
- **The woollen garment is the origin of the name**: `the coarse woolen garment of the first generation of Muslim ascetics was their distinguishing mark` (p. 45, folio 14); `It is even possible that the first Sufis adopted from the Christian ascetics the woolen garment from which their name derives.` (p. 66, folio 35); `the derivation from Greek sophos, "wise," is philologically impossible. The derivation from suf, "wool," is now generally accepted` (p. 45, folio 14). Indexed as `Suf ("wool"), 14, 102` and `Safa ("Purity"), 14, 16, 102, 232` (pp. 540–541, folios 509 and `51O` [text-layer?: 510]).
- **Initiation without a living master**, three routes: the *uwaysī* line, initiation `by a nonvisible master or by a saint who had died long ago`, and investiture by Khiḍr, `which was accepted as valid in the tradition of Sufi initiation` (pp. 136–137, folios 105–106); indexed as `Uwaysl (without formal initiation), 28, 89, 105` (p. 542, folio 511) and `Initiation, 7, 9 n, 105, 106, 169, 204, 364, 411; chain of, 28, 57, 95; ceremony, 234; for Bektashiyya, 340, 341, 432; through death, 411` (p. 533, folio 502).
- **The silsila is compulsory knowledge**: `Each adept, of course, had to learn the silsila, the spiritual lineage that leads from his master back through past generations to the Prophet; the pivotal figure in most silsilas is Junayd. The proper knowledge of the affiliations, difficult as it may be for an uninitiated person, is absolutely necessary for an understanding of the mystical tradition.` (p. 265, folio 234). Of Junayd: `the initiation chains of later Sufi orders almost invariably go back to him.` (p. 88, folio 57). Indexed as `Silsila ("spiritual chain"), 27, 88, 169, 234, 297. 3!3> 339. 351. 356, 364, 372` — the locator string is digit-damaged and **not resolved** (p. 541, folio `51O` [text-layer?: 510]).
- The forty-day retreat as an institution: `arbcfin, chilld` became `very early, a regular institution in the Sufi path` (p. 134, folio 103); the sheikh `might exempt a disciple for a time from the forty-day seclusion, for instance, because he was spiritually too weak, or because his spiritual ecstasy might overwhelm him` (p. 135, folio 104); mystics `were praised if, at the time of their death, they had completed thirty or forty chilla` (p. 136, folio 105). Adam was forty days `"in seclusion" (like the novice at the beginning of the Path)` (p. 47, folio 16). Indexed as `Chilla ("forty days' seclusion"), 103, 105. See also Forty` and `Chilla-yi mackusa ("inverted chilla," hanging upside down for forty days), 346, 358` [text-layer?: Chilla-yi maʿkūsa] (p. 530, folio 499), with `Salat maqliiba (performing the prayer hanging upside down), 241` (p. 540, folio 509) and the composite entry `Forty (number of trial), 94, 306, 340, 419; days, 16, 35, 103, 104, 305, 336, 346, 419; hadlth, 94; saints, 202; and letter m, 224, 419; stages in development, 224, 280, 341, 419; age of, 242; years, 115, 329` (p. 531, folio 500).

### Succession, custodianship and the sajjāda-nishīn

This is the material directly under every custodianship claim in the archive (pp. 266–267, folios 235–236).

- Offices inside the house: `The dervish would be invested with different offices in the khdnqdh according to his progress on the spiritual path; the hierarchical chain in such offices was carefully maintained, but it was a hierarchy of virtue, not of power.` (p. 266, folio 235).
- **The khalīfa**: `The most sincere dervish could gain the rank of khalifa, "successor"; he would either stay in the monastery to direct it after the sheikh's death or would be sent to foreign countries to preach and extend the order.` (pp. 266–267, folios 235–236).
- **The deed and its territory**: `In the khild-` / `fatname, the document given to him at the occasion of his investiture, he was sometimes assigned a special area in which his spiritual influence was needed.` (p. 267, folio 236). `khild-`/`fatname` [text-layer?: khilāfatnāme]. Indexed as `Khilafatndme (letter of investiture for the successor), 236` (p. 534, folio 503). In India the practice is named as characteristic: `the sphere of influence of a khalifa was designated by the master, who determined the areas where the baraka of this or that disciple should become active—a territorial distribution of spiritual power frequently found in Indian Sufism.` (p. 377, folio 346).
- **Transfer of qualities at death**: `In cases in which a successor was elected who had not previously excelled in spiritual qualities, the sheikh could transfer his lofty qualities to his successor at the moment of his death (intiqal-i nisbaf) and thus endow him with the necessary spiritual power.` (p. 267, folio 236). `intiqal-i nisbaf` [text-layer?: intiqāl-i nisbat]. Indexed as `Intiqal-i nisbat (transfer of spiritual qualities), 236` (p. 533, folio 502).
- **The sajjāda-nishīn, defined**: `The khalifa inherited the carpet (sajjada) or the deerskin or sheepskin (post), which was the sheikh's ritual place—hence the expression post-nishin or sajjada-nishln, "he who sits on the carpet," for the successor.` (p. 267, folio 236). `sajjada-nishln` [text-layer?: sajjāda-nishīn]. The index carries all four terms separately: `Sajjada-nishln ("successor"), 236`, `Sajjdda ("prayer rug"), 236` (p. 540, folio 509), `Postnishln (successor), 236` and `Post (sitting place for the sheikh), 236` (p. 538, folio 507).
- **Hereditary succession and the pīr families** — the single sentence that governs most modern custodianship disputes: `Later, the office of khalifa in the main convent often became hereditary. This led to a deterioration of the office and to an accumulation of power and wealth in the hands of certain pir families, in whom, in the course of time, not too many traces of true spirituality were left.` (p. 267, folio 236). The Suhrawardi model in India is hereditary from the start (p. 383, folio 352, above).
- **Pirism** is the book's word for the abuse: `the absolute sway of the leader over his followers and the attendant exploitation of ignorant peasants and villagers` (p. 53, folio 22) — Iqbal's term as Schimmel reports it, with the master named `the sheikh or plr` [text-layer?: pīr] and the reader referred to `chapter 5`; carried here for **mazar-e-iqbal** (Iqbal named, 18 September ruling). Indexed as a headword in its own right, `Pirism, 22, 236, 405, 406`, and `Pir, 22; Bektashi, 341. See also Sheikh` (p. 538, folio 507).
- One investiture described in full, from Samiha Ayverdi's Rifāʿī account: `All friends of the order were invited, candles were lit according to a prescribed ritual, recitation of the Koran and mystical music alternated, and after the candidate had kissed the sheikh's hand four dervishes held a veil over the two persons so that the master might introduce the khalifa into the secrets of the office. The whole night was spent in recitations, music, and prayer.` (p. 267, folio 236), with Schimmel's own extrapolation, `One can assume that the ceremony was not very different in other orders.`
- Succession by a test of the disciples, in `the Khalvati order in Istanbul`: the sheikh `sent his disciples forth to get flowers to adorn the convent` (p. 77, folio 46). Succession by chair, not by shrine: `Baha'uddln Walad died after a few years of teaching, and his son Jalaluddm succeeded him in his chair.` (p. 343, folio 312).
- The *abdāl* title is itself explained as a succession practice: `Later, the word was used to designate a saint who after his death would be "substituted" for (badal) by another person.` (p. 233, folio 202).
- The index's custodianship run, locators unconverted: `Khalifa ("vicegerent"): of God, 188, 223, 377' 37^; as successor in the order, 235, 236, 238; of Shadhill, 251; of Mucmud-dln Chishti, 346; of Fariduddm Ganj-i Shakar, 348; of Hamadhanl, 364` (p. 534, folio 503); `Ishan ("They," honorific title of Naqshbandi leaders), 365` (p. 533, folio 502); `Murshid ("spiritual guide"), 433` and `Mystical leader, 335; suhbat with, 366; letters by, 367. See also Sheikh` (p. 537, folio 506); `Shaykha (female spiritual leader), 432` and `Sheikh at-tarblya, 101` (p. 541, folio `51O` [text-layer?: 510]); `Qayyum (the highest spiritual guide in later Indian Naqshbandi theories), 203, 369. 37°. 374` — `37°` contains a `°` and is **not resolved** (p. 539, folio 508); `Tasarruf (power of the sheikh to bring about events), 257` and `Kibrlt ahmar ("red sulphur," symbol of the alchemistic activity of the sheikh), 236, 237` (pp. 542 and 534, folios 511 and 503); `Kafir ("infidel," "who denies the sheikh"), 200` (p. 534, folio 503); and the long `Sheikh (spiritual guide), 101-3, 12 5> '61, 210, 230, 231, 232, 234-36, 241, 247, 255 264, 283, 290, 330, 332, 386, 397, 428, 432; miracles performed by, 17, 205; veneration of, 22, 193; … questionable, 212, 238, 405; … of the Mevlevis, 325, 326 of the Chishti, 347; suhbat with, 366; letters of, 367; as father figure, 375, 376` — the locator string is damaged in several places (`12 5>`, `'61`, `255 264`) and is **left as printed** (p. 541, folio `51O` [text-layer?: 510]).
- The office above the quṭb, claimed dynastically: Sirhindī `claimed for himself and three of his descendants the rank of the qayyum` (p. 234, folio 203); `It is through the qayyum that the world is kept in order; he is even higher than the mystical leader, the qutb, and keeps in motion everything created.` and `The qayyum has been elected by God, who bestowed upon him special grace` (p. 400, folio 369). A public office of a different kind: Abū Ḥafṣ ʿUmar `became shaykh ash-shuyukh, the official Sufi master of Baghdad, under the caliph an-Nasir` and served as his `ambassador` (p. 276, folio 245).

### The tomb cult, ziyārat and what pilgrims do

The passage at pp. 269–270, folios 238–239 is the most transferable in the book for this archive.

- **The basic fact**: `Even greater than the veneration offered to the living sheikh was that shown to the deceased masters. All over the Muslim world small sanctuaries indicate the places where holy people are buried.` (pp. 269–270, folios 238–239).
- **Earlier sacred sites taken over** — this bears directly on the archive's Hindu and Sikh rows, and the book names no particular place, so no id is attached: `In many cases, sacred places of pre-Islamic religions were taken over, and, according to the assertion that people always pray at the same places, Christian or Hindu places of worship were transformed into Muslim sanctuaries, and legends connected with the former place were often applied to the new saint.` (p. 270, folio 239).
- **A village needs a tomb**, with Pakistan named: `Many stories—often rather awkward—are told, especially in Pakistan, in the Baloch and Pathan areas, about the necessity of having at least one saint's tomb in the village so that life can properly continue with his blessing.` (p. 270, folio 239). Footnote 12 cites `Sir Thomas Arnold, "Saints, Muhammadan, in India," in Encyclopedia of Religion and Ethics, ed. James Hastings, 13 vols. (1908-27), 11:67-73.`
- **Where tombs sit, and the pseudotomb**: `These tombs are often found close to strangely formed stones, to wells, fountains, or caves; in many cases they are pseudotombs. Several maqdms (places) may bear the name of the same saint` (p. 270, folio 239). The index makes the double sense explicit: `Maqdm ("station" on the mystical path), 98, 99, 100; definitions of, 109-30; as memorial place, 239` (p. 536, folio 505).
- **What pilgrims do — the paragraph in full**: `At these tombs, or sacred enclosures (which give sanctuary to the persecuted), people take their vows, make the circumambulation three or seven times, hang pieces of cloth around the windows or on nearby trees. Women praying for children will go to one place (and, incidentally, make of such a visit at a ziyara, "visiting place," a pleasant occasion of a rare outing); men longing for success in worldly matters will go to see another saint; children will visit a certain shrine before their examinations—all of them sure that the baraka, the spiritual power of the saint, will help them.` (p. 270, folio 239). Footnote 13 names `Edward William Lane, The Manners and Customs of the Modern Egyptians (London, 1860); Rudolf Kriss and Hubert Kriss-Heinrich, Volksglaube im Bereich des Islam, 2 vols. (Wies-` / `baden, 1960-61)` and `Cesar E. Dubler, (Jber islami-` / `schen Grab- und Heiligenkult (Zurich, 1960).` [text-layer?: Über]. The index glosses the practice-words separately: `Ziydra, ziyaret ("visiting place"), 239` (p. 543, folio 512); `Tawaf (circumambulation), 27` (p. 542, folio 511); `Baraka ("blessing," power of sanctity), 82, 102, 217, 234, 239, 241, 346` (p. 530, folio 499).
- **Vows for children, stated as worldwide and explicitly not only Islamic**: `the hopes of barren women are expressed all over the world—not only in Islamic lands—by the same magical means: by binding little cradles at the gate of the saint's abode, by taking vows, by "selling" the child to the saint (in Turkey many boys are called Satilniish` [text-layer?: the Turkish word for "sold"; the printed form is damaged and is not reconstructed here]`, "sold," or girls` — the sentence is cut off at the chunk boundary (p. 237, folio 206) and resumes `Sati, because they were granted by a living or dead saint to whose threshold they were "sold" by the parents).` (p. 238, folio 207). **A living or a dead saint grants the child, and the transaction is with the threshold.**
- **Sweeping the threshold**, at ʿAbduʾl-Qādir's tomb: `These pilgrims, who often stay for weeks, silently walk around with little brooms, cleaning the sanctuary—to sweep the threshold of a saint is considered very rewarding work.` (p. 278, folio 247).
- **Praying for rain at a grave**: of Maʿrūf al-Karkhī, `his prayers were heard, and after his death people from Baghdad would cross the Tigris to the section of Karkh and pray for rain at his tomb.` (p. 84, folio 53).
- **A blessing-tank far from the saint's own country**: `There are still sacred places dedicated to the memory of the lonely mystic of Bistam in the remotest corners of the Islamic world: in Zousfana in the Maghreb and in Chittagong in Bangla Desh, where huge, whitish turtles inhabit a tank to which people come to obtain blessings for themselves and for their children.` (pp. 81–82, folios 50–51). No dates, custodians, numbers or endowments, `(not stated)`.
- **Tomb visitation as a technique, not only a devotion**: `The Naqshbandi masters considered it useful to visit the tombs of deceased saints and to concentrate upon the spirit of particular departed Sufis in order to increase their spiritual strength. By directing one's thought to the spirit of this or that mystic—instead of concentrating upon one's sheikh exclusively—a mystic might achieve a greater sublimation of the spirit, according to the power of the saint in question.` (p. 206, folio 175). No shrine, region or order-house is named, no ʿurs, no offering, no custodian, no endowment — all `(not stated)`.
- **Women-only shrines, South Asia**: `In all the provinces of Muslim India and Pakistan one may see shrines of women saints to which men are not admitted` (p. 464, folio 433); and in Anatolia and Iran the small shrines of women `are visited by women to express special wishes connected with conjugal life, children, and similar problems. The same is true in Iran.` (p. 464, folio 433).
- **Trance at a shrine, recorded by the book's own plate caption**: `Woman in trance at a saint's tomb in Pakistan.` (p. 182, folio not stated).
- **Pilgrimage in the present tense**, all in chapter 8: **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (the book's "Lahore", ruling (a), place-level — of which **data-darbar** also takes it at person level, Hujwiri being that row's principal figure): Hujwiri's tomb `still provides a place of pilgrimage for the Panjabis` (p. 376, folio 345), and the index records the destination as a headword sub-entry, `Pilgrimage: to Mecca, 32, 66, 67, 71, 89, 106, 229, 230, 264, 388, 425, 430; to Data Ganj Bakhsh, 88; rewards of making seventy, 231; Abu Sacld without, 242; spiritual, 276` (p. 538, folio 507).
- The same present-tense run continues outside this archive's geography and is recorded for the pattern: Bakhtiyar Kaki's compound in Merauli `is usually filled with pious pilgrims, who recite and sing their devotional poetry` (p. 377, folio 346); Muʿinuddin's marble sanctuary at Ajmer, `erected by the Mogul Emperor Jihangir`, `is also still a center of religious inspiration for thousands of faithful Muslims` (p. 377, folio 346); Nizamuddin's tomb is `still the most frequently visited sanctuary in Delhi` (p. 381, folio 350); Ibn ʿArabī's tomb at the place `where he died in 1240` is `a goal for pious pilgrims` (p. 295, folio 264) — location, custodianship, ʿurs, endowment and visitor numbers all **(not stated)**; and at Konya `the thousands of visitors who come to pay their respect at his shrine … and the large crowds that throng there for the festivities in commemoration of his death in December` (p. 357, folio 326). A tomb described as poor: Bedil's `humble tomb in Delhi does not reflect the influence his poetry had upon Afghan and Central Asian, mainly Tajik, literature` (p. 402, folio 371).
- **Poems sung at the door of a tomb** — **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan", ruling (a), place-level): Iraqi's poems `are still being sung by Pakistani musicians at the door of the master's tomb in Multan` (p. 384, folio 353). The book does not name the master of that tomb here — `(not stated)`.
- **Vows, flags and sweetmeats at the Sind trees** (p. 279, folio 248): the practices extract carries the pointer only, the quotation itself sitting with the shrine-and-figure material. **`toponym_map.txt` has no `sind` or `sindh` key**, so this reaches **zero archive ids** although roughly thirty archive rows carry Sindh in `location_short`. Recorded as a cost, not fixed (open question 2).
- Saint veneration as the majority practice, against reform: `With or without the Naqshbandi reform, however, the majority of the masses continued, and partly still continue, in the veneration of their saints, which is echoed in hundreds of simple folk songs and popular verses.` (p. 429, folio 398). And the private survival of what is publicly denied: `a modern man may violently deny miracles and saint worship in rational debate and is highly critical of mysticism in any form, but he will show veneration at a sacred place or respect to a person who is reported to be a saint, or who comes from a noted family of mystics.` (p. 436, folio 405).
- A defence of the village sanctuary against reform: `And a modern French interpreter of Sufism concludes that it would be cruel "to destroy the qubba, the sanctuary of the village," where so many simple souls have found spiritual help.` (p. 438, folio 407), citing `fimile Derraenghem [text-layer?: Émile Dermenghem], Le culte des saints dans I'Islam Maghrebin (Paris, 1954)`.
- And the modernist attack it answers: `It is because of these darker sides of Muslim saint worship—which are scarcely compatible with the basic teaching of Islam—that Sufism has come into disrepute among many Muslim modernists, who see only the negative aspects as they are manifested at the lower levels of popular piety.` (p. 236, folio 205). The shrine-pilgrimage satire of al-Badr al-Ḥijāzī, translated by Arberry and quoted by Schimmel: `When he dies, they make him the object of pilgrimage, and hasten to his shrine, Arabs and foreigners alike; / Some kiss his grave, and some the threshold of his door, and the d u s t . . . .` (p. 53, folio 22; the satire's first three lines are on p. 52, folio 21).
- Place-guardianship by a group: `The Seven are mentioned in some towns as the spiritual guardians of a sacred place… Often, seven saintly persons or virgins are treated as a single unit in veneration (that can be the case with the Forty as well).` (p. 233, folio 202).

### ʿUrs, maulid, mela and the calendar the dates sit in

- **ʿUrs, defined by the book**, inside the lay-membership sentence: `The tanqas usually had a large number of lay members. One may even speak of a kind of "third order." Such persons would stay in the convent for a couple of days every year to participate in exercises and in the festivities of the order, like the maulid, the birthday of the Prophet and the saint, or the curs, "wedding," the anniversary of the saint's death.` (p. 271, folio 240). `tanqas` [text-layer?: ṭarīqas]; `curs` [text-layer?: ʿurs]. Footnote 14 cites `Joseph Williams McPherson, The Moulid of Egypt (Cairo, 1941).` The index repeats the gloss on its own: `^Vrs ("wedding," anniversary of a saint's death), 240` [text-layer?: ʿUrs] (p. 542, folio 511), beside `Maulid (celebration of a saint's or the Prophet's birthday), 216, 240, 401. See also Siileyman Celebi` (p. 536, folio 505).
- **Melā Chirāghān, the one named Pakistani festival** — **madho-lal-hussain** (Madho Lal named, person rule): `Madho Lai's anniversary, still celebrated in Lahore near the Shalimar Garden on the last Saturday in March, is famous as Meld Chiraghdn, the "Fair of Lamps."` (p. 415, folio 384). A movable date, a place by landmark, and a name; **no attendance, no custodian, no origin date** — `(not stated)`. The index carries it as its own glossed headword: `Mela chiraghdn (festival in Lahore), 384` (p. 536, folio 505).
- At place level the same festival takes every Lahore row under ruling (a), on one line: **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** — the attachment is place-level, not site-specific.
- **The Sehwan "fair" and the malangs** — **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (the book's "Sehwan"; `toponym_map.txt`'s key is `sehwan sharif`, see `## REVIEW`): `The "fair" of Sehwan—celebrated between Shawwal 18 and 20` [text-layer?: clean] `—has become notorious for its rather illicit events.` (p. 386, folio 355). **The book uses `"fair"` in quotation marks and never the word *ʿurs* here**; no year, no attendance, no organiser — `(not stated)`. The resident dervishes: `strange groups of bl-shar^` [text-layer?: bī-sharʿ] `, "lawless," Sufis (malang) gathered, dressed in black` around the sanctuary (p. 386, folio 355) — an institution described by dress and by legal status. The index glosses the word as a place-specific office: `Mdlang (dervish at the sanctuary in Sehwan), 355` (p. 536, folio 505), beside `Bi-shar'- ("without law," dervishes who do not cling to the shan^a), 20, 355` [text-layer?: Bī-sharʿ … sharīʿa] (p. 530, folio 499). **And a lingam beside the Muslim tomb**: `A lingam close to the actual tomb proves the Shivait background of some of the rites.` (p. 386, folio 355).
- **An anniversary in April** — **rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine** (Raḥmān Bābā): `His anniversary at the end of April is usually celebrated in a solemn manner.` (p. 430, folio 399). **The book does not use the word *ʿurs* anywhere in that chunk.**
- **An anniversary given only by lunar month**: `The poems in honor of cAbdu3l-Qadir, which are sung at his anniversary in the fourth month of the Muslim lunar year, reveal the tremendous admiration of the people for this mystical leader` (p. 279, folio 248). The month is given by number only; no name, no date — `(not stated)`. The book's calendar table names that month's observance independently: `Rabi^ ath-tham: n, anniversary of 'Abdu'l-Qadir Gildni.` [text-layer?: Rabīʿ ath-thānī: 11, anniversary of ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Gīlānī] — **the day-number prints as the letter `n` and is not resolved** (p. 30, folio xxvn [text-layer?: xxvii]).
- **The book's own festival calendar**, transcribed complete at p. 30, folio xxvn [text-layer?: xxvii], because it is the frame every ʿurs and mela date in this book sits in: `The Muslim year is a lunar year of 354 days, 12 months of 29 and 30 days. The calendar begins with Muhammad's emigration from Mecca to Medina in 622; thus the year 300 h.=912-13 A.D., 600 h.= 1203-4 A.D., 1000 h.=1591-92 A.D., and 1300 h.=1822-23 A.D.` — all six years print cleanly here, unusually for this book. Then: `Muharram: 10, 'Ashura, the memorial of Husayn ibn 'All's death at Kerbela on 10 Muharram 680 A.D.` [text-layer?: ʿĀshūrā; ʿAlī's]; `Safar` — no observance attached; `Rablc ul-awwal: 12, birthday of the Prophet.` [text-layer?: Rabīʿ ul-awwal]; `Rabi^ ath-tham: n, anniversary of 'Abdu'l-Qadir Gildni.`; `Jumada al-ula` and `Jumada al-akhira` — no observance attached; `Rajab: at the beginning, raga'ib-m'g/zte (conception of the Prophet). 27, micraj, the Prophet's ascension to Heaven.` [text-layer?: ragāʾib-nights; miʿrāj]; `Shacban: 14-15, shab-i barat, when the destinies are fixed for the coming year.` [text-layer?: Shaʿbān; shab-i barāt]; `Ramadan: the month of fasting. In one of the last three odd nights—generally thought to be the twenty-seventh—the laylat ul-qadr, during which the Koran was revealed for the first time.`; `Shawwal: begins with the cld ul-fitr, the feast of breaking the fasting.` [text-layer?: ʿīd ul-fiṭr]; `Dhu3l-Qacda` [text-layer?: Dhūʾl-Qaʿda] — no observance attached; `Dhu'l-Hijja: the month of Pilgrimage to Mecca (hajj). From the tenth to the twelfth, the cld ul-adha, Feast of Sacrifices.` [text-layer?: ʿīd ul-aḍḥā].
- The index repeats the calendar as glossed headwords: `^Ashura (memorial day of Husayn's martyrdom at Kerbela, 10 Muharram 680), 340` [text-layer?: ʿĀshūrā] (p. 530, folio 499); `Muharram, 75, 340, 341 , 38` — the locator is cut off at the right margin (p. 537, folio 506); `Ramadan, 193, 208, 430` and `Laylat al-qadr ("Night of Might," when the Koran was first revealed, in the last days of Ramadan), 193, 430` (pp. 539 and 535, folios 508 and 504); `Ha//, 106. See also Pilgrimage to Mecca` — the headword is damaged and **not resolved** — and `Hijra (hegira), 222` (p. 532, folio `50!` [text-layer?: 501]); `Kiswa (black, gold-embroidered veil for the Kaaba), 276, 277` (p. 535, folio 504).
- **The maulid dated and its spread**: `As early as the late eleventh century, and generally from the twelfth century on, the veneration of the Prophet assumed a visible form in the celebration of the maulid, his birthday, on 12 Rab? ul-awwal` [text-layer?: Rabīʿ ul-awwal]`, the third month of the Muslim lunar year. This day is still celebrated in the Muslim world.` (p. 247, folio 216); `From the eastern end of the Muslim world to the west the maulid is a wonderful occasion for the pious to show their warm love of the Prophet in songs, poems, and prayers.` (p. 247, folio 216).
- **Death anniversaries, the fortieth day, and vows**, of Süleyman Çelebi's *Mevlud*: `This poem is still recited on special occasions in many Turkish families: at the anniversaries, or at the fortieth day, of a bereavement or in fulfilment of a vow.` (p. 247, folio 216).
- **A festival on a solar calendar, and why the order stayed local**: `during the fifteenth century the festivals in Tanta were often attended by Mamluk officers and soldiers (which sometimes led to disturbances when the excitement grew).` (p. 280, folio 249); `The Badawiyya is a rustic order that has adopted a considerable number of pre-Islamic customs. Its festivities are held according to the solar Coptic calendar and thus are connected with the Nile and its flood; signs of old fertility rites are therefore assimilated into the cult. That is why the Badawiyya never crossed the borders of its homeland` (p. 280, folio 249).
- **A state-permitted anniversary, dated exactly**: `since 1954 they have been allowed to perform the samff on the occasion of the anniversary of Rumi's death on December 17 in Konya, though not in the central sanctuary` (p. 216, folio 185); `1925`, `1954` and `December 17` print cleanly.
- **The festival as the mass movement's attraction**: `the rank and file of the faithful have been given an emotional outlet for their feelings of veneration for the holy man and the opportunity for participation in festivities with music and often with whirling dancing. The corporate pursuit of the Way was easier for most people than the lonely spiritual struggle of the mystical seeker; the common prayer meetings gave them strength and warmed their faith.` (p. 270, folio 239).
- And a cooked dish that outlives its order: the ʿĀshūrā dish `^d^ure`, still cooked `on the tenth of Muharram` by `many Turkish families—most of them non-Bektashis` (pp. 371–372, folios 340–341).

### Samāʿ, music and dance

- **Only one order institutionalised the whirl**: `This mystical dance was noted by the first European visitors to the convents of the Mevlevis, the Whirling Dervishes. For the Mevlevi tarlqa is the only order in which this whirling movement has been institutionalized, though it has been practiced throughout the world of Islam from early times.` (p. 210, folio 179).
- **The orders split over it, and the index says so in its own sub-entries**: `Orthodoxy would ban every musical and rhythmical movement, and some of the sober orders, like the Naqshbandiyya, followed their example. Others saw in samcf an outlet for the religious feeling of the pious—and it was this musical side of some of the fraternities that attracted large masses.` (p. 211, folio 180); `In India, samcf was advocated by the Chishtl order.` (p. 216, folio 185); `The Naqshbandiyya is a sober order, eschewing artistic performance—mainly music and samcf` [text-layer?: samāʿ] (p. 396, folio 365). Indexed: `Samac ("mystical concert and dance"), 178-86, 243, 350, 384; Abu Sacid on, 244; Ahmad Ghazzali on, 295; Mevlevi, 325; Naqshbandiyya against, 365; Dard on, 380, 381. See also Dance; Music` (p. 540, folio 509); `Music, 179-86, 236, 239, 240, 241, 353, 371, 384, 385, 389, 390, 401; in Ruml's work, 317, 318, 324, 325; Chishtiyya in favor of, 348, 350; Naqshbandiyya against, 365, 366; Dard's love of, 373, 374, 380, 381, 403. See also Dance; Samd1-` (p. 537, folio 506); `Dance, 179-86, 239, 240, 277, 309, 384, 397; of the Mevlevis, 234, 315, 325; Rum! on, 315-18, 323, 414; Chishtiyya for, 350. See also Sama'` (p. 530, folio 499); `Mystical concert, 20. See also Dance; Music; Sama1-` (p. 537, folio 506).
- **The orthodox objections, itemised**: `They very much objected, among other things, to the rending of the garments that frequently happened in samaf.` (p. 212, folio 181) — indexed as `Rending of garments, 181` (p. 539, folio 508) — and to the participation of `charming young boys` in the later period (p. 212, folio 181). Novices are restricted: `It is more desirable that beginners should not be allowed to attend musical concerts lest their natures become depraved. (H 416, 430)` (p. 212, folio 181), on which Schimmel adds `This last sentence would be subscribed to by all spiritual directors in the moderate orders` (p. 213, folio 182). Rūzbihān's three requisites for perfect enjoyment: `fine scents, a beautiful face to look at, and a lovely voice (N 256)—the beauty of the singer is a prerequisite for spiritual happiness.` (p. 213, folio 182).
- **Samāʿ as discipline rather than ecstasy**, Abū Saʿīd's use of it: `In his time, the practice of sama' to incite ecstasy was apparently very popular, but for him the samcf was mainly a practical device to dissipate the lust of the dervishes, which might otherwise find other, more dangerous ways of distraction.` (pp. 274–275, folios 243–244).
- **The Mevlevi ritual described in full** (p. 356, folio 325): timing and dress — `At the occasion of the ceremonial samac` [text-layer?: samāʿ]`, usually held on Friday at noon after the congregational prayer, the dervishes used to wear their typical dress: a tennure, a white sleeveless frock, the destegiil` [text-layer?: destegül]`, a jacket with long sleeves, a belt, and a black khirqa, used as an overcoat but cast aside before the ritual dance started.` The head was covered by `a high felt cap around which a turban cloth might be wound`; `The cap, sikke, became the typical sign of the Mevlevis, and many inscriptions composed of words of prayers or blessings have been written in the shape of the dervish hat.` Choreography and discipline: `The samcf is regulated by very strict rules. The sheikh stands in the most honored corner of the dancing place, and the dervishes pass by him three times, each time exchanging greetings, until the circling movement starts. This is to be performed on the right foot, with accelerating speed. If a dervish should become too enraptured, another Sufi, who is in charge of the orderly performance, will gently touch his frock in order to curb his movement.` The music begins `with the great hymn in honor of the Prophet (na't-i sharif, written by Jalaluddm himself) and ending with short, enthusiastic songs, sometimes sung in Turkish`. Indexed as `Sikke (Mevlevi cap), 325` (p. 541, folio `51O` [text-layer?: 510]), `Tennure (white sleeveless frock of the Mevlevis), 325` and `Destegiil (jacket with long sleeves in the Mevlevi order), 325` (pp. 542 and 529, folios 511 and 498), `Na'-t (poem in honor of Muhammad), 226, 325` (p. 537, folio 506).
- **The order as patron of the arts**: `the best pieces of Turkish classical music, such as those by cltri (seventeenth century), were composed by artists who were either members of, or at least loosely connected with, the order. The same is true of the calligraphers and miniaturists, many of whom belonged to the Mevlevis.` (p. 356, folio 325); and in Turkey `the calligraphers—who were often members of the Mevlevi order—liked to shape the basmala as stork` (p. 456, folio 425).
- **Samāʿ on a schedule, with the emperor attending**: `The samcf meetings that he arranged twice a month were famous in Delhi, and even the ruler, Shah cAlam II, with the pen name Aftab, sometimes attended them.` (p. 411, folio 380) — twice monthly, with the reigning emperor as an occasional attender.
- **Samāʿ contested inside an order, with a treatise written in defence**: Dard `had written a book on music (Hurmat-i ghina)` and `must have been attacked by other Sufis for his love of music` (pp. 411–412, folios 380–381); his own defence: `I have not given a fatwa to my friends that this should be licit; and I have not built the mystical path upon sama?` [text-layer?: samāʿ] (p. 412, folio 381).
- **Music at a shrine threshold, weekly and resident** — **bhit-bhit-shah**: `Some dervishes who live at the threshold of his tomb perform this music in their gatherings on Thursday nights, and the melodies are well known all over Sind.` (p. 421, folio 390). Three separate institutional facts: dervishes **resident at the threshold**, a **Thursday-night** cycle, and a repertoire **transmitted province-wide**. The performance form: `Each group of poems, which rhyme according to particular rules of popular Indian prosody, usually contains an alternation between the recitation of the main verses by a solo voice and the repetition of a so-called kafl [text-layer?: kāfī] or way by a chorus; the way usually sums up the contents of the whole group of verses.` (p. 421, folio 390) — a description of Sindhi performance practice generally, not of one shrine.
- **A graveyard as a performance site**: Makli Hill is `where mystical concerts and dances were performed on almost any day of the week` (p. 415, folio 384). `toponym_map.txt` has no Makli or Thatta key, so this reaches no archive row.
- **Samāʿ in Sindhi outside Sind**: the sixteenth-century Sindhi migrants to Burhanpur `are reported to have used Sindhi verses during their samcf sessions.` (p. 415, folio 384).
- **Verse forms tied to musical modes** — the technical ground under every shrine-poetry entry: `These themes were sung time and again in forms inherited from the Indian past—dhoras, kdfh, way—not in the Persian form of ghazal in quantitative meters, but in Indian poetical forms that were closely connected with indigenous musical modes. The tunes were partly adapted from the Indian tradition, partly invented by the mystical poets themselves on the basis of classical and folk music.` (p. 416, folio 385). The three form names are printed `dhoras, kdfh, way` and are flagged. Indexed with glosses: `Kafi (Sindhi verse form), 385, 390`; `Way (end form in Sindhi poetry), 385, 390`; `Sur (chapter in Sindhi poetry), 390, 391`; `Dhora (Indian verse form), 385` (pp. 534, 543, 541, 531).
- **Living repertoire** — **bhit-bhit-shah**, **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** (Shāh ʿAbduʾl-Laṭīf and Sachal Sarmast named): their `melodies and words are almost as popular today as they were 150 years ago` (p. 414, folio 383); and `The visitor who stays in a Panjabi village will certainly find somebody to sing for him the old romance of Hir Ranjha` (p. 414, folio 383).
- **The tekke song as a genre**: `The genre of ildhi` [text-layer?: ilāhī]`, a mystical poem in popular meter meant to be sung in the meetings of the dervishes, is the typical expression of this tradition` — of `poems sung in the tekkes, the centers of Sufi life` (p. 361, folio 330); indexed as `Ildhl (Turkish mystical song), 330` (p. 533, folio 502), with `Marifati (mystical folk song in Bengal), 401` (p. 536, folio 505) for the Bengali equivalent — `here once more the folk songs—so-called marifati [text-layer?: maʿrifatī]—are of exquisite beauty, delicate and full of music. They were sung on the rivers, and Muhammad, the mystical leader, appears as the boatsman who brings the soul safely to the other shore.` (p. 432, folio 401).
- **Poets' gatherings in a house**: `Dard instructed a number of Urdu poets, who would gather in his house for mushffiras` [text-layer?: mushāʿiras] (p. 405, folio 374); indexed as `Mushd'-ira (meeting of poets, particularly in Indo-Pakistan), 374` (p. 537, folio 506).
- **Samāʿ as a dated institution and its early opponents**: samāʿkhānas at Baghdad in the second half of the ninth century (p. 212, folio 181, above); `The mystical concerts in which the Sufis might become enraptured and begin to spin around their axis were taken, by many, for the essence,of Sufism.` (p. 51, folio 20; the comma-for-space is as printed). Ecstasy words indexed: `TawSjud (trying to reach ecstasy), 179` and `Wajd ("finding," ecstasy), 178, 179. Se also Ecstasy` — `Se` is cut off at the right margin (pp. 542 and 543, folios 511 and 512).
- **Samāʿ triggered by trade noise**: `It is said that the hammering of the goldsmiths in the bazaar of Konya inspired him to dance and to recite verses, as did the sound of the watermills in the gardens of Meram.` (p. 347, folio 316).
- **Naqshbandī suppression of emotional practice, twice.** In Sind, the Thatta group `undertook to attack all those Sufis who defended the emotional side of religion as exemplified in the dancing practices on Makli Hill. Through their literary activities, this Naqshbandi group contributed to promoting a more sober mystical piety.` (p. 428, folio 397). In Bengal: `In Bengal as in Sind, the Naqshbandiyya and some more militant mystically inspired groups tried to curb the overflowing emotionalism expressed in the lovely folk songs. As a defense against syncretism, the later Naqshbandiyya has played a remarkable role in all parts of Muslim India.` (pp. 432–433, folios 401–402).

### Dhikr, litany and prayer as institutions

- **The dhikr is the Sufi's distinctive worship**: `But the distinctive worship of the Sufi is the dhikr (theomnemie, as Nwyia calls it with a term borrowed from Byzantine Christianity), the remembrance or recollection of God—a recollection that can be performed either silently or aloud` (`cf. K 95-97`) (p. 198, folio 167), founded on `"and recollect God often" (Sura 33:40)` and `"the recollection of God makes the heart calm" (Sura 13:28)` (p. 198, folio 167), and `"Dhikr is a strong pillar in the path toward God, nay rather the most important pillar"` (`Q 35`) (p. 198, folio 167). `The genuine mystical practices, like the performance of the dhikr, the constant recollection of God, occupied only part of their time.` (p. 115, folio 84). Its portability: `in its primary form (not in its later, highly developed phase)—it is permitted in any place and at any time; its practice is restricted neither to the exact hours of ritual prayer nor to a ritually clean place.` (p. 198, folio 167).
- **The dhikr must be transmitted, and that transmission is the initiation**: `the talqln adh-dhikr, the teaching of the formula of recollection, was one of the most important aspects of mystical initiation and was later developed into a complicated art.` (p. 200, folio 169); `Only a dhikr properly inspired by the spiritual director, and constantly controlled by him, was effective` (p. 200, folio 169); `The formula has to be transmitted through the chain of spiritual leaders that goes back to the Prophet himself or to an angelic inspirer of Muhammad.` (p. 200, folio 169), though `The dhikr could be learned, if not from a living master, then from Khidr`. `The mystical leader had to decide, first, which kind of dhikr was fitting for the spiritual stage of his disciple.` (p. 201, folio 170). Indexed as `T-alqln adh-dhikr (teaching the dhikr), 169` (p. 542, folio 511).
- **Equipment and posture**: `Since the ninth century the rosary (tasblh, subha) has been used to count the repetitions of the formulae, yet a true mystic would regard this medium as a toy "to attract heedlessness" (N 79)` (p. 200, folio 169); `The rosary, with its thirty-three or ninety-nine beads, is used for counting the names.` (p. 208, folio 177) — indexed as `Rosary, 114, 154, 169, 172, 177; exchange of, for the infidels' girdle, 305, 432. See also Tasblh`; `Tasblh ("rosary"), 169. See also Rosary`; `Subha. See Tasblh` (pp. 540, 541, 542). Posture, per `Simnani (d. 1336)`: `the mystic should sit cross-legged, the right hand on the left, and the left hand keeping the right leg in position on top of the left leg (CL i is)`, and `Even if the sitting style might vary according to the orders, the correct position was considered of great importance for the success of the dhikr, and the manuals carefully explain the relevant rules.` (p. 201, folio 170). The cell: `The adept must not only purify himself inwardly and outwardly before entering the cell to perform his dhikr, but should keep the image of his sheikh before his eyes for spiritual help during the recollection. This practice is, apparently, not of very early origin, but was elaborated by the orders.` (p. 201, folio 170). A later handbook `orders the Sufi to drink water after performing the dhikr to cool down the internal heat that he has produced` (p. 201, folio 170).
- **Loud versus silent, and its political consequences**: `dhikr is generally divided into two branches: recollection with the tongue (dhikr jail, jahrl, calanlya, lisani)` [text-layer?: jalī, jahrī, ʿalāniya, lisānī] `and recollection in the heart (dhikr khafi, qalbT)` [text-layer?: khafī, qalbī] (p. 202, folio 171); `The problem of whether the true dhikr should be hidden or spoken aloud caused a major split in the Naqshbandi order in Central Asia in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries; it had grave political consequences resulting in religious wars in Sinkiang.` (p. 206, folio 175); the fourteenth-century defence of the loud dhikr by the example of `the talqin, the loud recitation of the profession of faith in a dying person's ear, "and for the dervish, every breath is his last breath" (N 391)` (p. 206, folio 175); and against it, `the loud dhikr pronounced by a small group could disturb the people in the mosque who were absorbed in silent meditation.` (pp. 206–207, folios 175–176). Indexed as `Dhikr-i jail, jahri, ^alawiya, lisam ("with loud voice"), 171, 366` and `Dhikr-i khafi, qalbl ("silent" recollec-tion), 171, 366` (p. 531, folio 500), with `Talqln (recitation of the shahada in a dying person's ear), 175` (p. 542, folio 511).
- **The halqa**: `Often the Sufis would join in a halqa, a circle formed around a qutb, "pivot"; they would put right hands on the left hands of their neighbors and with closed eyes repeat the shahada until it consisted merely of the last h.` (p. 207, folio 176; the footnote marker after `h.` is printed `7S` [text-layer?: 78]). Indexed as `Halqa ("circle"), 176` (p. 532, folio `50!` [text-layer?: 501]).
- **The sawing dhikr, and tourism**: `the dhikr-i arra, the "sawing dhikr," practiced primarily among Central Asian and Turkish orders. The heavy sounds that come from the larynx are indeed reminiscent of a crosscut saw.` (p. 207, folio 176) — indexed as `Dhikr-i arra (loud repetitions that sound like a saw), 176` (p. 530, folio 499) — followed at once by `Unfortunately, in some Muslim countries dhikr sessions of this type have been used lately as tourist attractions.` (p. 207, folio 176).
- **Breath control**: `Dhikr in its developed forms is usually connected with some sort of breath control.` (p. 204, folio 173); Jāmī's record of the early Naqshbandi practice from `Khwaja cAttar`, `"The purpose in dhikr is not to speak much; one says in one breath three times la Hah ilia Allah` [text-layer?: lā ilāh illā Allāh — `ilāh` is printed `Hah`]`, beginning from the right side, and brings it down to the heart, and brings forth Muhammad rasul Allah from the left side. A ninefold or eighteenfold repetition in one breath is also possible" (N 390-91)` (p. 205, folio 174); and `In the later Middle Ages, especially in Afghanistan and India, the habs-i dam, holding the breath for a very long time, was practiced—a disputed technique that may show influence from Indian asceticism.` (p. 205, folio 174). Indexed as `Habs-i dam (keeping one's breath in rec-ollection), 174. See also Breath; Dhikr` (p. 532, folio `50!` [text-layer?: 501]).
- **The five subtle points**: `The Naqshbandi tradition has taught the dhikr of the five latffif` [text-layer?: laṭāʾif]`, five subtle points in the body upon which the mystic has to concentrate his recollection until his whole being is transformed.` (p. 205, folio 174); Mir Dard's five points and the terminal `dhikr sultani, the royal recollection` are set out at pp. 205–206, folios 174–175. Indexed as `Dhikr al-khafiy (khafawl), dhikr akhfd al-khafi (akhfawi), 174`, `Dhikr an-nafs (nafsi), 174, 175`, `Dhikr-i qalbl, dhikr-i ruhl, dhikr as-sirr (sirrl), 174` and `Dhikr sultanl, 175` (pp. 530–531, folios 499–500), under the main entry `Dhikr ("recollection," repetition of divine names or religious formulae), 84, 117, 141, 167-78, 243, 250, 277, 332, 358, 376, 401, 404`, with sub-entries `instruction in, 102`, `loud, 248`, `concentration on the sheikh during, 237` (p. 530, folio 499).
- **Prescribed counts, and the reward attached**: `Manuals of piety, like Ghazzali's Ihyff 'uliim ad-din` [text-layer?: *Iḥyāʾ ʿulūm ad-dīn*]`, teach a number of dhikr formulae, along with exact prescriptions as to how often each of these formulae should be repeated in order to bear fruit in Paradise (G 1:265, 302 ff).` (p. 207, folio 176); `Reciting the profession of faith seven thousand times can save the mystic himself` (p. 207, folio 176) `or any other person from hellfire (N 531). These ideas were, and still are, widely accepted by the masses.` (p. 208, folio 177); `Many a pious old woman will never get up from her ritual prayer without adding to it a number of dhikr formulae` (p. 208, folio 177). Footnote 63 locates them: `The main dhikr formulae are found in G 1:302 ff.` (p. 198, folio 167). The repetition numbers in free prayer: `Three, seven, fifteen, fifty, seventy, and one thousand are the most common numbers for the repetitions. These particular numbers are in accordance with the belief in magical numbers all over the world, but in Islam these rules are regarded as inherited from the Prophet and his companions.` (p. 188, folio 157) — all six numerals print as words, not digits.
- **The divine names as an operational system**: `The usual collection of these names of glorification comprises ninety-nine names. The Greatest Name is hidden, but many a mystic has claimed that he possesses it and that it enables him to perform every kind of miracle.` (p. 208, folio 177); named rules per name — `al-Hadl, "the Guiding One," must work upon the adept when he turns to God in his dhikr (N 410)`, `al-Latlf` in seclusion, `Al-Hdfiz` to preserve a state, `Al-Wadud` to be loved by all creatures, and `The name al-Fffiq` [text-layer?: al-Fāʾiq]`, "The Overpowering," should never be used by the beginner, but only by the gnostic in a high station.` (pp. 208–209, folios 177–178), with `A wrong application of a divine name was considered to produce grave consequences for the person who used it or for those close to him.` (p. 209, folio 178). Counting by numerical value: `the number of repetitions required to make these names efficient is dependent upon the numerical value of the name (thus, rahman, "the Merciful," has the numerical value of 299 and should be repeated that many times).` (p. 192, folio 161; `299` prints cleanly).
- **The wird and the protective litany**: `the master could also give his disciples, or those who called upon him, rather long formulae to be recited after the ritual prayer or in case of special need (wird, aurdd, or hizb). Many of these prayers are credited with supernatural powers, and the most famous one is probably the hizb al-bahr, attributed to ash-Shadhill and used mainly during travel as a protective charm.` (p. 209, folio 178). Indexed as `Wird (pi. aurad, litany), 178, 243` (p. 543, folio 512), `Hizb (litany, special prayer formula), 178` and `Hizb al-bahr. by Shadhili, 178, 25` — the second locator is cut off at the right margin (p. 533, folio 502).
- **Prayer that shades into incantation**: `Some of the prayers practiced by certain Sufis—mainly in later periods—are in fact very close to magical incantation: the master played with the letters of the Arabic alphabet, he composed prayers by deriving every possible word from a particular Arabic root—for example, the root underlying a specific divine name—or wrote prayers in alphabetical order, filling them with certain words and letters that are considered powerful and impressive.` (p. 192, folio 161); `Illness could be cured by applying the appropriate divine name; and to strengthen the power of prayers, strange-sounding words of non-Arabic origin and intricate names of angelic beings were added.` (p. 192, folio 161).
- **Saints whose prayers are heard, and prayer as power over others**: `we read and hear many legends about Sufi masters who were mustajab ad-difa,` [text-layer?: mustajāb ad-duʿā] `"whose prayers were heard" so that "if he would ask for something God would certainly grant it."` (pp. 191–192, folios 160–161); `In illness and danger, in times of dearth and need, they would utter an effectual prayer for themselves or their fellow townsmen, and they were capable of punishing those who disagreed with them.` (p. 192, folio 161); and the contemporary note, `Similar words can be heard even today when some village "saint" feels advanced enough to threaten God in order to gain his desired end—an act often approximating magic.` (p. 190, folio 159). Indexed as `Mustajdb ad-du^a ("whose prayers are answered"), 160, 205` (p. 537, folio 506).
- **Approved prayers as the channel by which mysticism reached the masses** — Schimmel's strongest institutional claim in that chunk: `The concept of the du^d ma^thur,` [text-layer?: duʿā maʾthūr] `the prayer that is effective because it was first uttered by some pious person or inspired by the Prophet through a dream, has greatly influenced Muslim popular piety, as Constance Padwick has shown in her fine book, Muslim Devotions. Through this channel of "approved prayers" and "effective prayers," mystical piety was infused into the large masses and did more to form their religious consciousness than the formulae used in the prescribed ritual.` (p. 188, folio 157). Indexed as `Du'-d ma^thur (traditional prayer that is thought to bring good results), 157. See also Prayer` (p. 531, folio 500).
- **Ritual prayer as the frame the rest sits in**: `One of the five pillars of Islam is the ritual prayer (saldt; in Persian and in Turkish, namaz) to be performed five times a day at prescribed hours` (p. 179, folio 148); ritual purity `(tahdra)` [text-layer?: ṭahāra] as its prerequisite (p. 179, folio 148); the *niya* and the `rak^as` [text-layer?: rakʿas] (p. 180, folio 149); prolonging and minimising it according to temperament (p. 183, folio 152); and the exemption, `When a Sufi was completely lost in ecstasy for a period of days, or even weeks, he was exempt from praying.` (p. 183, folio 152). The prayer for others and the epic colophon: `Thus, many Persian, Turkish, and Urdu epic poems and numerous prose works end with an invitation to the reader to recite a prayer for the author and for the scribe.` (p. 191, folio 160). Indexed as `Salat, 148-55`, `Namaz ("ritual prayer"), 148; interpreta-tion of letters of, 422`, `Rak'-a (unit of movements and formulae in the ritual prayer), 149, 152, 155`, `Ruku^ (genuflection in ritual prayer), '53. "54` — the locator is damaged and **not resolved** — `Qibla (prayer direction), 195, 29` — cut off at the right margin — `Night vigils and prayers (tahajjud), 27, 40, 114, 115, 148, 155, 196, 243, 254`, `Tahara ("ritual purity"), 37, 148, 149, 243» 255. 4i6` — locator damaged, **not resolved** — `Ghusl (complete purification), 149`, `Mundjdt ("intimate conversation," prayer, orison), 155`, `Duca ("free prayer"), 155`, `Istighfdr (formula to ask God for forgive-ness), 156, 243`, `Isqat al-fara^id (substitution of pious works for ritual duties), 71`, `Shahada ("profession of faith"), 256; use of in dhikr, 170, 176`, `Hafiz ("who knows the Koran by heart"), 180, 434`, `Gulbang (a Turkish prayer in the Bektashiyya), 340`, `Terceman (Turkish prayer in the Bektashiyya), 340` and `Mim duasi (Bektashi prayer connected with Muhammad's name), 420` (pp. 528–543, folios 497–512 as printed on each page).
- **Koran recitation as practice**: `the recitation of the Koran was an important means of leading the spirit into a meditative state, or even of producing a mystical rapture. Recited in beautiful tones, the rhythmic and musical wording of the holy book carried the minds of the devout into higher spheres` (p. 57, folio 26); `"A disciple who does not know the Koran by heart is like a lemon without scent" (N 131)` (p. 138, folio 107).
- **Substituting one duty for another** — the doctrine that made Ḥallāj unacceptable to the legalists: `it even led him to the doctrine of the isqat al-jarffid` [text-layer?: the second word is damaged past confident reading]`, i.e., that certain religious duties can be exchanged for other acts that are more useful at the moment. Instead of performing the pilgrimage, he advised people to invite orphans and to feed and dress them and make them happy for the day of the Great Feast. Such ideas, of course, were not acceptable to the legalists.` (p. 102, folio 71).

### Healing, miracle and the powers attributed to a shrine

- **Healing by formula, against biomedicine**: `That illnesses are cured by means of religious formulae is well known—the story of the deaf girl who was cured by the saint's whispering the call to prayer into her ear is only one example from a long list of miracles in which the Sufis used prayer and dhikr formulae for healing purposes.` (p. 239, folio 208); `Even today the recitation of a Fdtiha` [text-layer?: Fātiḥa] `or similar prayer, together with "breathing upon" (damidan, uflemek) the sick, is common in the Muslim world (cf.
