N 352)—and the faith in a religious formula pronounced by an alleged saint is often, in rural areas, much greater than the trust in a Western-trained physician.` (p. 239, folio 208). Note the book's own hedge, `an alleged saint`. Indexed as `Damidan ("to breathe" on a sick person), 208` [text-layer?: Damīdan] and `Uflemek ("breathing" on someone for healing purposes), 208` (pp. 530 and 542, folios 499 and 511), with `Call to prayer (ad.ha.ri), 48, 108, 149, 152; … for healing purposes, 208` (p. 530, folio 499).
- **Contests between saints as an institution**: `In connection with the manifestation of spiritual power, contests (munaqara) were held to prove which saint was more powerful and could work more exciting miracles. These contests—which go back at least to Shibli—are usually ascribed to the popular saints, who could easily turn their enemies into stones or wither trees with their curses.` (p. 242, folio 211); Schimmel calls the field `otherwise rather unpleasant and, basically, un-Sufi`. Indexed as `Munaqara (contest of saints), 79, 211` (p. 537, folio 506).
- **Visitation itself named as the miracle**, in Abū Saʿīd's answer: `Is it not the greatest miracle that a butcher, who was also a butcher's son, should be attracted to the mystical Path, talk to Khidr, and be visited by innumerable people who want to obtain his blessings (N 288)?` (p. 243, folio 212).
- **The sheikh's power over events**: `Faith in these powers of the mystical leader—which, however, often belong to the magical sphere rather than to the mystical—is still strong. But the implications of such a faith are dangerous in times of degeneration.` (p. 268, folio 237); Kubrā `held that the mystic is capable of bringing certain events to pass by directing his spiritual energy toward the sphere of possibilities, just as his spiritual energy allows him to work upon other human beings, forming their hearts according to the exigencies of the mystical Path.` (p. 288, folio 257).
- **Bodily feats, by order**: the Rifāʿiyya, `known as the Howling Dervishes because of their loud dhikr. They are notorious for performing strange miracles, like eating live snakes, cutting themselves with swords and lances without being hurt, and taking out their eyes.` (p. 279, folio 248); `the Aissawiyya dervishes in North Africa dance with burning coals in their hands without feeling any pain.` (p. 240, folio 209); `One may think, on a lower level, of the Rifaci dervishes and many pretenders to Sufism who injure themselves by gashing themselves with a sword or by eating glass.` (p. 240, folio 209); and the travellers' report that first shaped the European picture, `both the ritual dance of the Whirling Dervishes (Mevlevis) and the strange performances of the Howling Dervishes (Rifaci's) attracting casual visitors.` [text-layer?: Rifāʿīs] (p. 39, folio 8) — reported as the travellers' account, not as Schimmel's own description.
- The index's miracle vocabulary: `Karamat (miracles of saints), 206, 243. See also Miracles`, `Khdriq ul-cdda ("what breaks the cus-tom," miracle), 206`, `Mu^jizat (miracle of a prophet), 206`, `Tayy al-makan (to change one's place, ubiquity), 205`, `Saklna ("divine presence"), 209`, `Jafr (telling the future from the letters of the Koran), 412`, and, under `Miracles`, `of the yogis, 357, 358` (pp. 534–543, folios 503–512 as printed on each page). From the Index of Prophetic Traditions, the four traditions that bear on shrines and shaykhly authority, each with its own locator and `AM` number — **`AM no. N` is a hadith number in Furūzānfar's collection, never a page of this book**: `My saints are under My domes, 203` / `(AM no. 131)`, listed under `HADrrH QUDSI` [text-layer?: ḤADĪTH QUDSĪ]; `The sheikh in his group is like the` / `prophet in his people, 101, 237 (AM` / `no. 224)`; `When someone has no sheikh, Satan` / `becomes his sheikh, 103 (AM no. 73)`; `Who wants to sit with God should sit with` / `the Sufis, 235 (AM no. 635)` (p. 509, folio 478); and, printed **without** an `AM` number, `Miracles are the menstruation of men,` / `212` (p. 508, folio 477).

### Asceticism, diet, dress and the daily regime

- `The chief means for taming and training the nafs were, and still are, fasting and sleeplessness.` (p. 145, folio 114); `qa'im al-lail wa sffim ad-dahr, "spending their nights upright in prayer and maintaining a perpetual fast by day."` [text-layer?: ṣāʾim] (p. 145, folio 114); and the threefold rule, `qillat at-ta^dm, qillat al-mandm wa qillat al-kaldm, "little food, little sleep, little talk" (to which often "loneliness, keeping away from men," was added) is still as valid as it was a thousand years ago.` (p. 145, folio 114) — each of the three indexed separately, `Qillat al-kaldm ("little speech"), 114, 334`, `Qillat al-mandm ("little sleep"), 114`, `Qillat at-ta^dm ("little food"), 114, 115` (p. 539, folio 508), with `QjPim al-layl wa slPim ad-dahr ("who stays awake during the night and fasts constantly"), 114` (p. 539, folio 508) and `Sleeplessness: as ascetic means, 114, 115` (p. 541, folio `51O` [text-layer?: 510]).
- The alternating fast: `they invented the so-called sawn dcfudi, which meant that they would eat one day and fast one day, so that their bodies would not become accustomed to either of the two states.` [text-layer?: ṣawm dāwūdī] (p. 146, folio 115), indexed as `Saum dcPudi (fasting one day, eating one day), "5` — the locator is damaged and **not resolved** (p. 540, folio 509). Also `Others not only fasted and restricted their diet to the absolute minimum but, in addition to that, took a purgative on every third day.` (p. 147, folio 116).
- The moderate counter-rule: `Many later orders taught that the middle way, between excessive hunger and excessive eating, was safest for the disciple's progress (N 395).` (p. 148, folio 117); and, `According to the later Naqshbandiyya and other moderate orders, the people who truly fast are those who keep their minds free from the food of satanic suggestions` — `"Such people's sleep is worship. Their walking and going and resting are glorification of God, and their breathing praise of God."` (p. 148, folio 117).
- Sweets, and the joke made of them: `as early as the tenth century, the fondness of the Sufis for halvah, sweetmeat, is mentioned; and in the eleventh and later centuries poets often speak in derogatory terms of the Sufi with milk-white hair who has made the recollection (dhikr) of sugar, rice, and milk his special litany (SD 149).` (p. 148, folio 117). Coffee: `The Shadhiliyya mystics are credited with having invented the use of coffee as a way of staying awake during their litanies and vigils. Many mystics of the sober orders still rejoice in coffee as a useful means of attaining spiritual wakefulness and strictly refuse alcoholic beverages or drugs that would produce a spiritual intoxication and thus preclude clear contemplation.` (p. 285, folio 254). Against which the index records `Drugs, 254, 336; opium, 335; hashish, 336` and `Kaygusuz ("sans souci," catchword for hashish), 336` (pp. 531 and 534, folios 500 and 503), and the `abdalan-i Rum`, `dervishes who wandered through the Ottoman Empire covered with animal skins and wearing a silver earring`, who `were also notorious for their use of opium.` (p. 366, folio 335).
- Material poverty as practice: `they might use only a brick for a pillow (N 49) and a worn-out mat of straw for a bed (if they did not prefer to sleep in a seated position or refrain from sleep at all)`; `although they strictly observed the ritual purity required for prayer, Ibn Adham was proud of the huge number of lice living in his coat`; `as late as 900, a maidservant of a Sufi from Baghdad exclaimed: "O God, how dirty are Thy friends—not a single one among them is clean!" (N 621)` (p. 68, folio 37).
- **Vegetarianism and the fire-sitting, with their provenance argued**: `the strict vegetarianism of some of the Indian Sufis, like Hamiduddin Nagori and others` (p. 389, folio 358); `Some ascetics among the Indian Sufis added to the difficulty of their task by sitting in front of a fire on a summer day so that the heat was, in fact, intolerable for anybody—a custom inherited from Hindu ascetics.` (p. 201, folio 170), which Schimmel also calls `an exaggeration of dhikr practices`, comparing `the tapas, the ascetic "heat" of the Hindu ascetics` (p. 201, folio 170; indexed as `Tapas (ascetic heat), 170`, p. 542, folio 511). She supplies a counter-example against the Hindu-origin reading of vegetarianism — the Rābiʿa story `makes it clear` the trait is older, and it is `found in the Maghreb as well, where no Hindu or Jain influence can possibly have been present` (p. 389, folio 358).
- **The qalandar and the malāmatī**: `the Malamatis deliberately tried to draw the contempt of the world upon themselves by committing unseemly, even unlawful, actions, but they preserved perfect purity of heart` (p. 117, folio 86); Anṣārī's complaint that later claimants brought forth `licentiousness and treating lightly of the Divine law and heresy and lack of etiquette and respectlessness` (`N 61`) (p. 118, folio 87); and `the qalandar, the wandering dervish who performs only the absolute minimum in religious duties` (p. 118, folio 87), indexed as `Qalandar (dervish who does not care for outward forms), 87, 88` (p. 539, folio 508). Beside them `The type of the majdhub, the "enraptured one" who, under the shock of a mystical vision o` [text-layer?: text lost at line end] `any psychological experience, is bereft of his senses and walks around in a fashion prohibited by the religious law (i.e., stark naked) belongs to the darker side of the Sufi world.` (p. 50, folio 19), and `the degeneration of the wandering dervishes or faqlrs` [text-layer?: faqīrs]`, the "poor," who performed miracles and were beyond the law (bl shar*` [text-layer?: bī sharʿ]`), has done much to bring Sufism into discredit.` (p. 51, folio 20).
- Marriage against celibacy: `Among the early ascetics, a preference for celibacy was common in spite of the Prophet's example of married life and his advice to raise a family.`; `the sorrows of family life might be regarded as "punishment for the execution of legally permitted lusts" (N 185)`; against which `many of the great Sufis and founders of mystical fraternities were married and had large families` (pp. 67–68, folios 36–37). `even now some spiritual directors will tell their disciples to get married, for "the afflictions that wait for you will certainly lead you on the way toward God"` (p. 459, folio 428); some mystics `would not touch food cooked by a woman (N 576)` (p. 459, folio 428). Indexed as `Celibacy, 36, 111, 252, 340, 428` (p. 530, folio 499).
- Book-destruction as a first step: `"To break the ink-pots and to tear the books" was considered by some mystics the first step in Sufism` (p. 48, folio 17); `other Sufis were urged by dreams to cast their precious collections of books into a river (N 432)` (p. 49, folio 18). The minimum learning a Sufi needs, as Schimmel glosses Hujwīrī: `enough astronomy to find the direction of Mecca as required for the correct performance of prayer, enough mathematics to figure out the legal amount of alms one has to pay` (p. 48, folio 17).
- A forbidden devotional practice, named and condemned: `nazar Ha? l-ahddth` [text-layer?: naẓar ilā ʾl-aḥdāth], `the "looking toward the young men"`, which Hujwīrī calls a forbidden practice and its defender `an unbeliever` (`H 549`) (p. 321, folio 290), set by Schimmel in a society `in which the feminine element was largely excluded from communal life` (p. 320, folio 289), with `That many Sufis could not keep their feeling in check is understood from the complaints about such tendencies in Sufi circles.` (pp. 320–321, folios 289–290).

### The orders: formation, social base, politics and suppression

- **Why they formed, with Schimmel's candour about not knowing**: `How the crystallization process itself worked is difficult to explain; it must have been a response to an inner need of the community that was not being met spiritually by the scholasticism of orthodox theologians; people craved a more intimate and personal relationship with God and with the Prophet.` (p. 262, folio 231). A counter-Ismaili hypothesis is offered as a possibility only: `One cannot exclude the possibility that the orders came into existence as a movement to counter the strong Ismaili-Batini influence against which Ghazzali had fought so relentlessly. The esoteric interpretation of Islam, which threatened its very structure, was, thus, r-` / `placed by an interiorization of orthodox Muslim teaching.` (p. 262, folio 231). `r-`/`placed` [text-layer?: replaced — broken at the right margin with the `e` absent; **not** reconstructed].
- **From elite to mass religion**: `Out of this social activity of the expanding Sufi groups, a new attitude seems to have developed that transformed Sufism from the religion of the elite into a mass movement, spreading its tenets to all levels of the population.` (p. 262, folio 231), restated as `The orders have contributed to converting Sufism into a mass movement—a movement in which the high ambitions of the classical Sufis were considerably watered down.` (p. 270, folio 239).
- **Schimmel's verdict on their degeneration, unhedged**: `The mystical fraternities that grew out of a need for spiritualizing Islam became, in the course of time, the very cause contributing to the stagnation of the Islamic religion.` (p. 269, folio 238). Her qualified use of Birge: `Even if one does not fully agree with John K. Birge's statement about the Bektashi order—"social progress, even the highest moral progress of the individual, was impossible under the dervish system" (BO 202)—one has to admit that "even if the sheikh is well meaning but ignorant, his influence is certain to count for evil."` (p. 269, folio 238) — the second quotation is given without its own citation, source `(not stated)`.
- **An explicit gap she flags**: `The history of the political role assumed by mystical leaders in Islamic countries has still to be written.` (p. 269, folio 238).
- **Lay membership and the third order**: `The tanqas usually had a large number of lay members. One may even speak of a kind of "third order."` (p. 271, folio 240); `In many cases the orders assumed, thus, a function similar to that of associations (Vereiri) or sodalities in the modern world; many are the interrelations of the dervish orders proper and the artisans' lodges, the guilds, and the futuwwa groups.` (p. 271, folio 240). `Vereiri` [text-layer?: Verein]. A Persian instance of segregated third-order instruction: Shabistarī's `Gulshan-i rat` [text-layer?: Gulshan-i rāz] with Lāhījī's commentary `"served (and perhaps still serves) as a traditional manual for all those men or women (instructed in separate groups) who came under their direction in a kind of Third Order."` (p. 312, folio 281).
- **Social composition, order by order** (p. 272, folio 241): `Most of the Sufi orders are identified with certain strata of the population. Even in modern Turkey after the prohibition of religious activities in 1925, the old allegiances can still be felt: the Shadhiliyya order was attractive primarily to the middle classes; the Mevleviyya—the order of the Whirling Dervishes—was close to the house of the Ottoman emperors and, at the same time, the order of artists, inspiring music, poetry, and the fine arts; whereas the rustic Bektashis were connected with the Janissaries and produced a typically Turkish folk literature (see chapter 7).` The Shādhilī social base again at p. 285, folio 254: `This teaching appealed neither to the lower classes, who needed more exciting means of getting onto the Path, nor to the poets, but is primarily connected with the middle class, the officials and civil servants who were trained in the Shadhiliyya method to fulfill their duties carefully.`
- **Suborders**: `Suborders and small fractions of orders are found everywhere—J. Spencer Trimingham speaks of the tcPija sys-` / `tem; each of these suborders jealously tried to preserve the special teaching and the baraka of the founder sheikh or the family baraka.` (p. 272, folio 241). `tcPija` [text-layer?: ṭāʾifa]; indexed as `Td^ifa (suborder, fraction of an order), 241` (p. 542, folio 511), beside `Tariqa ("Path," order, fraternity; second stage in the threefold Path), 16, 98, 99, 153. 203, 334, 340; order, 239-41; Shadh-iliyya, 250; Bektashiyya, 339; Qadir-iyya, 384; letter speculations about, 422` and `Tarlqa-yi khwdjagdn: the Naqshbandiyya order, 364` (p. 542, folio 511).
- **A rule without a convent** — the Shādhilī model: `Unlike other mystical leaders (and similar to the later Naqshbandiyya), Shadhill did not emphasize the necessity of monastic or solitary life, nor did he encourage specific forms of vocal dhikr. Every member of the tarlqa was supposed to realize the spirit of the order in his own life and his own environment, in the midst of his duties. The Shadhiliyya Sufis were not expected to beg or espouse poverty; on the contrary, the Egyptian sources of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries tell how members of this order excelled in their tidy attire, quite in contrast to some of the other Sufis who filled the streets of Cairo.` (p. 281, folio 250), with membership itself predestined: `a Shadhill is sure to be predestined as a member of this tarlqa from preeternity and believes that the qutb, the head of the spiritual hierarchy, will always be a member of this order.` (p. 281, folio 250).
- **Kubrāwī discipline** as the opposite pole: `such experiences can be realized only after the strictest adherence to the rules of the tenfold Path, some conditions of which are to abstain from food as much as possible and to give complete surrender to the sheikh by giving up one's own will completely. Included in the path, too, is the so-called "way of Junayd": constant ritual purity, constant fasting, constant silence, constant retreat, constant recollection of God, and constant direction of a sheikh who explains the meaning of one's dreams and visions.` (p. 286, folio 255); their dhikr is the profession of faith, which `should work not only on the breath but even on the blood and permeate the disciple's entire being.` (p. 287, folio 256).
- **The eight Naqshbandi principles, as printed** (p. 395, folio 364) — the list numerals are damaged and are given as printed: `i)` [text-layer?: 1] `hush dar dam, "awareness in breathing,"` · `a)` [text-layer?: 2] `nazar bar qadam, "watching over one's steps,"` · `3) safar dar watan, "internal mystical journey,"` · `4) khalwat dar anjuman, "solitude in the crowd,"` · `5) ydd hard, "recollection,"` · `6) bdz gard, "restraining one's thoughts,"` · `7) nigdh dasht, "to watch one's thought,"` and · `8) ydd ddsht, "concentration upon God."` The index gives seven of the eight as separate glossed entries (pp. 533–543, folios 502–512 as printed). The fourth is also defined in the running text: `This ideal was defined later by the Naqshbandi mystics as khalwat daranjuman, complete absorption in proximity to God in the midst of a crowd; the perfect mystic's concentration can no longer be disturbed or altered by any external event or occupation.` [text-layer?: khalwat dar anjuman — inter-word space lost at the line break] (p. 274, folio 243).
- **Naqshbandi ritual practice, summarised**: `The center of Naqshbandl education is the silent dhikr, as opposed to the loud dhikr, with musical accompaniment, that attracted the masses to the other orders.` `The second noteworthy characteristic is suhbat, the intimate conversation between master and disciple conducted on a very high spiritual level (cf. N 387).` `The close relation between master and disciple reveals itself in tawajjuh, the concentration of the two partners upon each other that results in experiences of spiritual unity, faith healing, and many other phenomena (cf. N 403).` (p. 397, folio 366). Indexed as `Suhbat (spiritual conversation between master and disciple), 102, 366` (p. 541, folio `51O` [text-layer?: 510]).
- **Order economy and political power**: `His order established connections with the trade guilds and merchants, and his wealth grew parallel to the growth of his spiritual influence, so that he and his followers and friends controlled the Timurid court and carefully watched over religious practices there. At almost the same time the order became highly politicized.` (p. 396, folio 365); `It was the Khwaja's conviction that "to serve the world it is necessary to exercise political power" and to bring the rulers under control so that the divine law can be implemented in every part of life.` (pp. 396–397, folios 365–366); `Their leaders were, and still are, known as Ishan, "they."` (p. 396, folio 365).
- **Letters as an institution and as politics**: `The collections of the letters of Muslim Indian Sufis from the thirteenth century on are an extremely important source` and, `as in the case of Ahmad Sirhindl, have played a remarkable role even in politics.` (p. 387, folio 356). Sirhindi's `fame rests chiefly upon his 534 letters, of which 70 are addressed to Mogul officials. They were, like many letters by mystical leaders, intended for circulation, with only a few of them meant for his closest friends.` `The letters have been translated into Arabic, Turkish, and Urdu from the original Persian` (p. 398, folio 367). More generally, `Even today mystical instruction through letters is practiced by some Sufi leaders.` (p. 454, folio 423).
- **Futuwwa, akhi and the guilds**: `For all practical purposes, the caliph had institutionalized the so-called futuwwa movement, which was closely connected with Sufism. He took the investiture ceremony of neighboring Muslim rulers with the "futuwwa trousers" and headgear as a sign of their allegiance to him.` (p. 276, folio 245); `Sulami, who had composed a treatise about the Malamatiyya, devoted a treatise to the futuwwa as well, in which he enumerated 212 definitions of the true fata. Qushayri followed him by treating the futuwwa in a special chapter of his Risala.` (p. 277, folio 246); `In the early thirteenth century, an-Nasir tried once more to revive these ideas, but without much success. Yet futuwwa groups existed in several parts of the Near East, where they constituted an important factor in the social life.` (p. 277, folio 246); in Turkey `the related groups of the akhi, in which only blameless men of respectable profession were accepted as members, maintained the futuwwa ideals throughout the next centuries` (p. 277, folio 246), and `the relations between futuwwa groups and the guilds have been studied several times, though with different conclusions.` (p. 277, folio 246). Indexed as `Futuwwa ("virtue," "manliness," mystico-social movement), 240, 245, 246`, `Fata ("young man," the ideal mystic), 246, 426`, `Jawanmard ("virtuous young man"), 246, 426` and `Akhi (member of a religious sodality in medieval Turkey), 246` (pp. 528–534, folios 497–503 as printed).
- **The Bektashi structure, recorded compactly** (pp. 371–372, folios 340–341): the four gateways `sharfa` / `tarlqa` / `mcfrifa` / `haqlqa` with their adherents `cdbid`, `zdhid`, `carz'/`, `muhibb`, each `founded upon ten obligations` so that `the sacred number of forty is attained once more`; the hierarchy `dede, "grandfather," in the village of Hacci Bektas`, the `Celebi`, the `baba` `in charge of teaching and of the pastoral work`, the dervish `who, like the baba, may be married or lead a celibate life`, and the initiated `muhibb`; initiation on the `ddr-i Mansur`; lay `cdshiq` who `have not taken their "part" (nasib]`; the prayer formulae `gulbdng` and `tercemdn`; and Muharram and New Year `celebrated in good Shia and Persian form`. The order's initiation place is named for Ḥallāj: `In the Bektashi order, his name is connected with the central place of initiation, which is called ddr-e Mansiir` [text-layer?: dār-e Manṣūr]`, "the gallows of Mansur"` (p. 105, folio 74). **Women in the order**: `One of the outstanding features of the Bektashi order—unique in Islam—is that women are treated as equals; they participate in all ceremonies and freely converse with the men.` (p. 372, folio 341).
- **Order and army, and the state that ended both**: `From the fifteenth century on, Bektashi leaders (bdbd) lived close to the Janissary barracks to give spiritual guidance to the soldiers`, and they were `involved in the fall of the Janissaries in 1826` [text-layer?: clean] (p. 370, folio 339). `The activities of the Mevlevi dervishes in Turkey, along with those of the other orders, were banned by Atatiirk in 1925` (p. 216, folio 185); `the closing of the dervish orders in 1925` [text-layer?: clean] (p. 368, folio 337), repeated with its rationale at p. 374, folio 343; `The fact that the orders were banned in Turkey in 1925 has curbed only their external activities; they were, and are, very much alive beneath the surface.` (pp. 435–436, folios 404–405). **A second, undated statement of the same closing stands beside these**: `even forty-five years after Atatiirk` [text-layer?: Atatürk] `closed the dervish lodges and strictly prohibited any activity of mystical fraternities.` (p. 357, folio 326) — **the chunk gives no year there and no visitor figure**, `(not stated)`; `forty-five years` is counted from an unstated base.
- **The Mevlevi order and the Ottoman state**: `the head of the Mevlevi order was so closely connected with the Ottoman court that he had the privilege of girding the Sultan with the sword.` (p. 355, folio 324); `The center of the order was always in Konya; the leader was called by the honorific titles Molla Hunkar and Qelebi` [text-layer?: Çelebi]`. Many smaller tekkes of the Mevlevis were found all over the Ottoman Empire, as far away as Egypt and Syria, although the order never crossed the Ottoman borders.` (p. 355, folio 324). Its foundation is dated and attributed, **and not to Rūmī**: `the formation of the order of the Whirling Dervishes, the organization of the dancing ritual and the establishment of a true hierarchy, was left to Sultan Walad (d. 1312), Rumi's son, who took over his office after Husamuddm's death in 1284.` (p. 346, folio 315).
- **Orders counted in the twentieth century**: `There are still many orders alive—the High Sufi Council of Egypt enumerated not less than sixty orders a few years ago; they were, however, losing followers because of urbanization, though some of them were able to attract new members.` (p. 435, folio 404); `It is revealing that one of the few modern orders that still attracts new disciples in Egypt, and gives them a thorough training in spiritual education, is an offshoot of the Shadhiliyya` (p. 285, folio 254), named in footnote 41 as `the contemporary Hamidiyya-Shadhiliyya`. And the instrumental reading: `the use of technical devices (like common dhikr] by the orders to gain control over larger circles of the community.` (p. 435, folio 404 — the bracket is printed as `]`, a misprint for `)`).
- **The state's earlier interest**: a trial, `Ghulam Khalil brought charges against the Sufis of Baghdad in 885. Accused of heresy, and likely to be sentenced to capital punishment, Nuri offered his life to save his companions; the caliph, touched by such magnanimity, investigated the case, found the Sufis to be good Muslims, and set them free.` (p. 91, folio 60); and a madrasa system built by a vizier, `Nizamulmulk, the vizier, appointed Ghazzali professor at the Nizamiyya madrasa in Baghdad… the vizier had founded colleges (madrasa) all over the Seljuk territories.` (p. 122, folio 91), which `served as training institutions for theologians and proved to be models for later colleges in the Muslim world.` (p. 123, folio 92).
- The index's order run, headwords only, locators unconverted: `Chishtiyya, 185, 232, 258, 345, 351, 352,` / `356' 359. 3Sl- 399. 4°i> saints, 36, 213 n,` / `224, 296, 327, 345, 347, 348, 350, 360,` / `37°` [text-layer?: 356, 359, 381, 399, 401 … 370] — **`3Sl`, `4°i` and `37°` are unresolved** — (p. 513, folio 482); `Bektashiyya, 202, 238, 241, 280, 335, 337-` / `41, 364, 432; poets and poetry, 74, 153,` / `159, 189, 333, 395; letter mysticism, 413,` / `420, 422, 425` (p. 513, folio 482); `Badawiyya, 249, 280` (p. 512, folio 481); `Aissawiyya, 209` (p. 511, folio 480); `Heddawa, 210, 241, 250`, `Hamidiyya-Shadhiliyya, 254 n` and `Howling Dervishes. See Rifaciyya` (p. 516, folio 485); and the two Suhrawardī handbooks, `Adah al-muridin (SuhrawardI), 122, 245,` / `35 1` [text-layer?: Ādāb al-murīdīn; 351] and `'Awarif al-ma^iirif (Suhraward!, Abu` / `Hats), 122, 245, 348` [text-layer?: ʿAwārif al-maʿārif; Abū Ḥafṣ] — **both indexed at the same opening locator `122, 245`** (pp. 511–512, folios 480–481).
- The Suhrawardī handbook is named as the standard Indian text: `Suhrawardi's 'Awarif al-macdrif, the guidebook of almost all the Indo-Muslim mystics in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries` (p. 379, folio 348). And the devotional book market that carried them, attributed to Barani: `It became almost fashionable, if we are to believe Barani, to purchase copies of the following books: Makki's Qut al-qulub … Ghazzali's Ihyd" ^ulum ad-din; Suhrawardi's cAwdrif al-macdnf and Hujwiri's Kashf al-mahjub; Kalabadhi's Kitdb at-ta'arruf and its commentary; and the Risdla of Qushayri` (p. 379, folio 348), with `Najmuddin Daya's Mirsad ul-cibad`, `the letters of ^ynu^l-Qudat Hamadhani` and `Hamiduddin Nagori's Lawdmf`; `No wine, gambling, or usury was to be found in Delhi, and people even refrained from telling lies.` (p. 379, folio 348). A Sindhi devotional book-list against it: `It is said that some mystics in Sind—and it may be true for other provinces as well-kept only three books for spiritual nourishment: the Koran, the Mathnawi, and the Diwdn of Hafiz.` (p. 358, folio 327).
- **Two Indian genres named as institutions**: `In India, a special literary genre became very popular—the so-called malfuzdt` [text-layer?: malfūẓāt]`, collections of sayings of the spiritual preceptors.` `The Indian Sufis, however, carefully collected the dicta of their masters from day to day`, though `In later times, collections of malfuzdt were fabricated with more or less pious intent.` (p. 387, folio 356) — indexed as `Malfuzat (sayings of the Sufi masters), 356` (p. 536, folio 505) — and the order history, of which Mir Khurd's `Siyar al-auliyff` is `still the most trustworthy account of the Chishti silsila and of medieval Chishti khdnqdh` [text-layer?: khānqāh] `life` (p. 387, folio 356). Indexed also: `Vilayetname ("book on sainthood," hagiographical works), 339` (p. 542, folio 511).

### Saints, the hierarchy, and sayyid status

- **The hierarchy as an office-holding structure**: `The highest spiritual authority is the, qutb, "axis, pole," or ghauth, "help." He is surrounded by three nuqabff` [text-layer?: nuqabāʾ]`, "substitutes," four autad, "pillars," seven abrdr` [text-layer?: abrār]`, "pious," forty abddl` [text-layer?: abdāl]`, "substitutes," three hundred akhydr` [text-layer?: akhyār]`, "good," and four thousand hidden saints.` (p. 231, folio 200). **A competing count is recorded beside it and not reconciled**: `Some authorities, e.g., Ibn cArabi, claim that there are seven abddl, one for each of the seven climates` (p. 231, folio 200), footnoted to `Muhyiuddm Ibn cArab!, Al-futuhdt al-makkiyya, 4 vols. (Cairo, 1329 h./ign)` [text-layer?: 1911]`, chap. 198, fasl 31.` The earlier statement of the same structure: `The leader of the Sufi hierarchy is the qutb, "pole" or "pivot," or ghauth, "help." The saints govern the universe, certain groups of three, seven, forty, or three hundred saints being entrusted with various duties in maintaining the world order (see Chapter 4).` (p. 88, folio 57) — the `(see Chapter 4)` is the book's own internal cross-reference and is **not** converted.
- Claimants: `Not a few mystics claimed to be the qutb of their time, and quite a number of them assumed the role of the Mahdi, the manifestation of the hidden imam at the end of time.` (p. 231, folio 200).
- **Sayyid status and baraka, stated as a living fact of the author's own day**: `Widespread, too, was the veneration of the sayyids, Muhammad's descendants through CA1I` [text-layer?: ʿAlī] `and Fatima. Even in our day some of the sayyid families in countries like Muslim India or Pakistan consider themselves exalted above the common Muslim, surrounded by a sanctity or transmitting a baraka (spiritual power, blessing) that gives them a peculiar status. This veneration shown to the ahl al-bayt, the Prophet's family, constitutes in later times a very important aspect of popular Sufism.` (p. 113, folio 82) — the only explicit mention of Pakistan in that chunk. Repeated at `The veneration of the Prophet's family, common in Shia Islam, can be found in Sufi circles as well, and the reverence shown to the sayyids, his descendants, is part of this veneration.` (p. 254, folio 223). Indexed as `Sayyid (descendant of Muhammad through CA15 and Fatima), 82, 223` (p. 540, folio 509).
- **The three-stage annihilation that grounds pīr-veneration**: `first he has to experience annihilation in the spiritual guide, who functions as the representative of the Prophet, then the fand ffr-rasul` [text-layer?: fanā fi'r-rasūl]`, "annihilation in the Prophet," before he can hope to reach, if he ever does, fand fi Allah.` (p. 247, folio 216). Indexed as `Fana ft Allah (annihilation in God), 216`; `Fana ffr-rasul (annihilation in the Prophet), 216, 234, 375`; `Fana fpsh-shaykh (annihilation in the spiritual guide), 216, 237, 375` — the headwords carry `ff`/`fp` damage and are **not resolved** (p. 531, folio 500).
- **The saint's licence and its limit**: `A saying like hasandt al-abrdr sayyfat al-muqarrabln` [text-layer?: ḥasanāt al-abrār sayyiʾāt al-muqarrabīn] (`L 44`) `could lead, and in fact led, to the "saint" abolishing the religiously prescribed norms. He might consider himself jenseits von Gut und Base` [text-layer?: Böse]`, beyond good and evil` (p. 235, folio 204). Against which the tradition settles the prophet/saint ranking: `the traditional Sufi schools have always agreed upon the superiority of the prophet. The end of saintship is only the beginning of prophethood.` (p. 234, folio 203), with a dissenting `some mystics of the intoxicated type` (p. 235, folio 204).
- A comparative caution worth carrying into any entry that calls a figure a "saint": `The role of the wall` [text-layer?: walī] `in Islamic piety does not correspond exactly to that of the saint in the Christian faith. It is closely connected with the mystery of initiation and progress on the spiritual path` (p. 235, folio 204); and the etymology from Qushayrī, `a wall is one whose affairs are led (tuwulliya) by God and who performs (tawalla) worship and obedience` (`cf. N 6`) (p. 230, folio 199), with `The wilayat cdmma, the "general saintship" common to all the sincere faithful… is usually distinguished from the wildya khdssa, that of the advanced mystics` (p. 230, folio 199).
- The index's saint run — **the sub-entry `worship of` is the one a shrine article would want**: `Saints, n, 21, 72, 73, 86, 182, 199-213, 232, 239, 241, 243, 246, 247, 268, 270, 273, 290, 296, 308, 353, 357, 389, 405, 407, 427, 428; female, 8, 36, 38, 40, 264, 358, 426, 430, 431, 433; questionable, 20, 159, 238; worship of, 22, 205, 355 n, 398, 405, 433; Chishti, 36, 345, 347, 348, 351, 352, 360; … birthday celebration o£, 240; Indo-Muslim, 242, 355; GilanI as proto-type of, 248; North African, 358; pa-tron, 364; and intoxication, 369; Sindhi, 384, 396` — the leading `n` is a damaged digit and is **not resolved** (p. 540, folio 509); with `Saintship, sanctity, 83, 199-213, 255; hier-archical, 41, 57; books on, 55-57`, `Wall (pi. auliyiP, "saint"), 199`, `Wall minnat Allah (saint through love), 203`, `Wall sidq Allah (saint through works), 203`, `Wilayat ("saintship, sanctity"), 199, 200, 421; *dmma ("general"), 199; khassa ("special"), 199`, `Qutb ("pole, axis," highest member in the hierarchy of the saints), 57, 200, 302, 203, 369; in a Sufi circle, 176; alway. from the Shadhiliyya, 250; Hasan ibn CA1I the first, 374` — the locator runs `302, 203` out of ascending order and `alway.` is cut at the margin, **neither is resolved** — `Ghauth ("Help," highest mystical leader), 57, 200`, `Nuqaba? (three "substitutes" in the hierarchy of saints), 200`, `Abddl (the forty, or seven, saints), 200, 202; women, 431, 434`, `Akhyar (300 "good" in the hierarchy of saints), 200`, `Abrar (the seven "pious"), 200`, `Autad (the four "pillars" of the spiritual hierarchy), 200, 203`, `Khatam al-auliya* ("Seal of the saints"), 57` and `Khatm al-wildya ("Seal of saintship"), 203` (pp. 528–543, folios 497–512 as printed on each page).

### Women: convents, endowment, access and the bridal soul

- **Women's convents with a female head**: `In the later Middle Ages, the chronicles tell about convents where women could gather in pursuit of the mystical path or of religious life in general. In Mamluk Egypt, these convents had a shaykha who led the congregation in service and prayer. One of those convents was a refuge for divorced women, who could stay there until they found an opportunity for remarrying.` (p. 463, folio 432).
- **Lay attachment, and exclusion from the sanctuary**: `A number of Sufi orders had women attached to them as lay members. Although some of them do not allow women inside the sanctuaries, the love and enthusiasm of the female adherents is, as a rule, very ardent.` (p. 463, folio 432). Against that, the Bektashi exception: `The order that granted the greatest opportunities to women was the Bektashi in Ottoman Turkey. Here, they were absolutely equal with men; they had to undergo the same ceremony of initiation and joined in the common festive meals and gatherings—a custom that has lent itself to accusations of immorality against the Bektashis.` (p. 463, folio 432). `Apparently women were allowed to attend the meetings of Sufi preachers.` (p. 458, folio 427).
- Schimmel's general claim: `Sufism, more than stern orthodoxy, offered women a certain amount of possibilities to participate actively in the religious and social life.` (p. 463, folio 432); and, on the ambivalence, `The attitude of Sufism toward the fair sex was ambivalent, and it can even be said that Sufism was more favorable to the development of feminine activities than were other branches of Islam.` (p. 457, folio 426), while noting that the praise of the pious woman is double-edged — she `can be praised in the most glowing terms (just because of her difference from the ordinary representatives of her sex!)` — the exclamation mark is the book's own (p. 457, folio 426).
- **Where women saints are thickest**: `Names of women saints are found throughout the world of Islam, though only few of them have been entered into the official annals. But the popular imagination often invented tender legends that eventually resulted in the emergence of a new woman saint.` … `the area in which women saints flourished most is probably Muslim India.` (p. 464, folio 433); `The province of Sind, generally famous for its saint worship, is particularly rich in legends connected with women saints` (p. 464, folio 433), where women `occasionally rise to the high rank of a murshid` (p. 464, folio 433).
- **Women teaching now**: `some of the most genuine representatives of mystical tradition, directors of souls, in Istanbul and Delhi (and probably in other places as well) are women, who exert a remarkable influence upon smaller or larger groups of seekers` (p. 466, folio 435); `It is remarkable that in modern times Sufi teaching is, to a large extent, carried on by women again.` (p. 466, folio 435).
- Two research programmes Schimmel proposes outright: `It would be worthwhile to study the role of the mothers in the biographies of the Sufis.` (p. 461, folio 430) and `The role of women sponsoring Sufi activities deserves special attention, and its importance should be underlined` (p. 464, folio 433).
- **The bridal soul — the South Asian argument, and the archive's whole shrine-poetry register** (p. 465, folio 434): `In the province of Sind, as well as in Punjab, a special aspect of mystical appreciation of women can be observed that is rarely encountered in other parts of the Muslim world, i.e., the representation of the longing soul in the form of a woman.` Its source is Hindu, not Persian: `In the western part of Muslim India, however—from Sind to Kashmir—the poets followed the Hindu tradition, which describes the soul as the longing girl, a faithful wife, or a loving bride.`, the classical example given being `the legend of Krishna and the gopis, the cowherd-girls`, with `the whole complex of Shakti mysticism in Indian tradition` set aside in parentheses. Its spread across communities: `Sikh mysticism, too, has adopted this imagery. The ginan—sacred songs of the Ismaili community of Indo-Pakistan—also depict the soul as a loving female.` Its vehicles: `the earliest Urdu-writing Sufi poets—like Sayyid Muhammad Jiw Jan in the seventeenth century—knew this bridal symbolism; but it is used mostly in the mystically interpreted folk tales of Sind and the Punjab.` Its heroines: `The tragic heroines of the Sindhi and Panjabi folk tales—like Hir, Sassui, Sohni, and many others—represent the human soul in search of the beloved, a beloved to whom she can be united only by endless suffering and eventually through death on the Path.` And two further genres: `the popular poetical genre of the baramasa, poems dealing with the peculiarities of the twelve months and the feeling of the lover in each of these months`, and `the "Golden Alphabets" composed by Sindhi and Panjabi folk poets`; in both, `the soul is the bride and God—or the Prophet Muhammad—the longed-for bridegroom`. Restated in chapter 8: `one of the characteristics of Sindhi and Panjabi poetry is that the longing soul is always depicted as a woman; this typical aspect of Indian Sufi poetry was inherited from Hinduism (see Appendix 2).` (p. 420, folio 389) — a causal claim about origin whose evidence is deferred to an appendix.
- The Golden Alphabet as a teaching form: `a poetical form known in Ancient Eastern literature and throughout the Muslim world, mainly in later times`, `in which each verse begins with the following letter of the Arabic alphabet`; `There is no rule for the verse form`; `It was a form that could easily be memorized by anybody who tried to learn the alphabet itself, and that is why Turkish, Sindhi, Panjabi, and Pashto poets loved to explain their mystical doctrines and the mysteries of faith in the form of Golden Alphabets` (p. 454, folio 423), with `alliteration was a frequently used poetical device, particularly in Sind` and the Sindhi Golden Alphabets `collected by Dr. Nabi Bakhsh Baloch in Tih akaryun` (p. 454, folio 423, footnote 24).
- Literacy as the background: `Even in an environment in which illiteracy prevailed, particularly among women, the motif of letter-writing and waiting for letters is used with amazing frequency. In the folk poetry of Sind the heroines always complain that no letter from the beloved has reached them` (p. 455, folio 424).

### The author's arguments about Indo-Muslim Sufism

- **How the shrine landscape of present Pakistan came to be** — the book's own origin sentence for this archive: `At approximately the same time, in the thirteenth century, the fertile areas of present Pakistan became the seat of mystics who had migrated from the central lands of the Muslim world, partly because of the threat of the approaching Mongols.` (p. 415, folio 384). The wider frame: `It is as if the tremendous political changes of the thirteenth century—when the Mongols overrode most of Asia and penetrated deep into Europe… —produced, in marked contrast to this destruction, an upsurge of mystical activity, ideas, feeling, and poetry` (p. 310, folio 279); `The orders acquired a firm footing in India` (p. 310, folio 279).
- **Which orders, in what order**: the Kubrāwiyya `was eventually superseded by orders like the Chishtiyya, Suhrawardiyya, Qadiriyya, and, since about 1600, the Naqshbandiyya in India. Still, the works of Kubrawi saints have deeply influenced Indo-Muslim mysticism.` (p. 289, folio 258). `The Chishtiyya remained for centuries the most influential order in the Subcontinent, but it never went beyond its borders.` (p. 382, folio 351). `By that time, the first real Sufi fraternities had come into existence—namely, the Suhrawardiyya and the Qadiriyya, which even today are influential in large areas of the Muslim world.` (p. 275, folio 244). `the Sufi orders—Qadiriyya and Naqshbandiyya—made it known as far as Malaysia.` (p. 154, folio 123).
- **Islamisation by preaching, not by the sword**: `Although the type of the soldier-Sufi, the fighter for the true religion, is sometimes found in the frontier provinces of India, as it is in other border areas between Islam and the land of the infidels, the Islamization of the country was achieved largely by the preaching of the dervishes, not by the sword.` (pp. 376–377, folios 345–346); `conversions in India during that period were due mainly to the untiring activity of the Chishti saints, whose simple and unsophisticated preaching and practice of love of God and one's neighbor impressed many Hindus, particularly those from the lower castes, and even members of the scheduled castes.` (p. 376, folio 345); and the general claim, `It is a well-established fact that large parts of India, Indonesia, and Black Africa were Islamicized by the untiring activity of Sufi preachers who manifested in their lives the basic obligations of Islam` (p. 271, folio 240).
- **The orders made the vernaculars** — the single argument that underwrites most of this archive's poetry: `These preachers also used the local languages instead of the Arabic of the learned and are, thus, largely re` / `sponsible for the early development of languages like Turkish, Urdu, Sindhi, and Panjabi as literary vehicles.` (p. 271, folio 240). `re`/`sponsible` [text-layer?: responsible — broken at the right margin without a hyphen]. Restated earlier: `Likewise the Indo-Muslim languages (Sindhi, Panjabi, and to a great extent Urdu and Pashto) are in large part the result of the speech and song of mystical leaders who could not address their simple disciples in high-flown theological Arabic or poetical Persian` (p. 64, folio 33) — **no individual poet, order or place is named in support there**, `(not stated)`.
- **Sufism as a mass movement in the northwest**: `During Nizamuddln's time, Sufism became a mass movement in northwestern India, and the moral principles laid down by the early Chishti saints did much to shape the ideals of the Muslim society in that part of the Subcontinent.` (p. 381, folio 350).
- **Punjab and Sind as one literary region**: `The Punjab and the Indus valley have a common tradition of mystical folk literature.` (p. 414, folio 383); `The Punjab and Sind show close similarities in the types of mystical literature that nourished there.` (p. 416, folio 385); `The more rustic, very idiomatic Panjabi and the complicated, musical Sindhi—both strong, expressive languages—were excellent media to express mystical feelings, though not mystical theories.` (p. 416, folio 385). Dating: `Reliable tradition traces the use of the native language for mystical poetry both in Sind and in the Punjab back to the fifteenth century.` (p. 415, folio 384), offered as tradition, with Farīduddīn `credited with` Old Panjabi songs earlier still (p. 415, folio 384). For Sindhi specifically: `Attempts at expressing mystical experiences in the Sindhi tongue go back at least to the sixteenth century.` (p. 420, folio 389), with Qāḍī Qādan's verses as evidence — `they distinctly show, however, all the peculiarities of later poetry.` And a grammatical fact behind the style: `Their language is extremely compact, since the Sindhi grammar allows very intricate combinations of words.` (p. 421, folio 390).
- **Spinning imagery and the cotton economy**: `In both provinces, a preference for the motif of spinning and weaving can be observed, a natural propensity in a cotton-growing country; the dhikr could therefore be compared to the act of spinning` (p. 417, folio 386).
- **The anti-mullā motif is inherited, not local**: `The old accusations against the dry-as-dust theologians, the molld` [text-layer?: mullā]`, which form a standard motif of Persian and Turkish mystical and quasi-mystical poetry, were taken up again by the mystics in the northwest part of the Subcontinent.` (p. 416, folio 385), and such verses `appealed particularly to the peasants and the illiterate` (p. 416, folio 385).
- **Schimmel's central polemic: against the advaita reading of Indo-Muslim mysticism.** Certain audacious verses `have led a number of authors, particularly the Hindus who studied this aspect of Indo-Muslim religious life, to believe that here Indian advaita mysticism gained a complete victory over Islamic monotheism. The love for the sheikh and the attempt at becoming annihilated in him … has also been explained as the typical Hindu love for the guru.` (pp. 417–418, folios 386–387). Her rebuttal: `There is no doubt that the Indian Sufis were inclined to take over images and forms from their Hindu neighbors; however, none of the Panjabi or Sindhi mystics neglected the love of the Prophet` (p. 418, folio 387); and the charge against that scholarship, repeated — they `usually lacked a true understanding of the Koran and the foundations of Muslim faith and tried to explain everything in the light of Hindu philosophy` (pp. 418–419, folios 387–388), with Johann Fück's criticism of Ramakrishna's *Panjabi Sufi Poets* endorsed as `rightly criticized the overall Hindu interpretation` (p. 415, folio 384, footnote 60 continued). Earlier European derivation theories are likewise rejected: `earlier European theories that tried to explain Sufism as an Islamized form of Vedanta philosophy or of Yoga have now been discarded.` (p. 376, folio 345).
- **But the borrowing question is left open where the evidence is thin.** `The question of the probability and extent of mutual influences of Muslim and Hindu mysticism has been discussed often by Oriental and Western scholars.` (p. 388, folio 357); on breath control, `To what extent the breath control advocated by early Sufis in their dhikr developed under the influence of Indian practices cannot be judged properly.` (p. 389, folio 358), with the counter-evidence that it `existed, like the "inverted chillah," among the Sufis of eastern Iran before Sufism spread to India` (p. 389, folio 358); her limited concession, `For the later period, however, we may assume that a deepened interest in the ascetic practices of their Hindu neighbors might have colored some aspects of Indian Sufi life.` (p. 389, folio 358); and on the inverted prayer, `This practice was performed by some later Indo-Muslim saints as well, which has led to the suggestion of a yoga origin; but as early a witness as Abu Sacid in Khurasan seems to contradict such an idea.` (p. 273, folio 242). Indexed as `Yoga, 242, 345, 358, 374` (p. 543, folio 512) and `Guru (Hindu spiritual leader), 387` (p. 532, folio `50!` [text-layer?: 501]).
- **On the durability of the boundary between the two communities** — directly relevant to the archive's Hindu and Sikh rows: `On the whole, the boundaries between Muslim and Hindu saints remained quite well defined during the first five centuries of Muslim rule over large parts of India, though there were syncretistic movements, like that of Kabir in the fifteenth century.` (p. 389, folio 358). Against which stands the transformation of earlier sacred places quoted above (p. 270, folio 239), and **Hindu participation in Muslim orders in Sind**: Hindus `composed poems in honor of the Prophet and even wrote tcfziya` [text-layer?: taʿziya] `for the Muharram mourning of the Shia community of Sind; or they devoted ballads to the fate of famous Sufi martyrs.` (p. 418, folio 387). **The two statements are kept side by side and not reconciled.**
- **Akbar, and the limits of his settlement**: a possible Mahdawi influence is offered and held open, `thus an influence cannot be completely excluded.` (p. 390, folio 359); `Akbar's religious tolerance was, in its practical consequences, scarcely compatible with Muslim law` (p. 390, folio 359); `Akbar's attempts could not bring forth a perfect solution of the problems confronting a large, multireligious country; on the contrary, they evoked much resistance in all segments of the population. Yet they are reflected in the poetry and the fine arts of his time.` (p. 391, folio 360); `The close association of the Chishtiyya with the Moguls, so alien to their previous antigovernment attitude, is reflected in many stories about Akbar and his successors.` (p. 391, folio 360). Indexed as `Dln-i ilahl (Akbar's syncretistic religion), 359` (p. 531, folio 500).
- **Sirhindi's modern image, dated to 1919** — the sharpest historiographical argument in this material: `Yohanan Friedman has tried to show that this image developed only after 1919` [text-layer?: clean] `, in Abu0! Kalam Azad's` [text-layer?: Abūʾl Kalām Āzād] `work, and that it has been eagerly elaborated by many Indo-Muslim, particularly Pakistani, scholars.` Schimmel's own position: `It may be true that Sirhindl's direct influence upon Mogul religious life was not as strong as his modern adherents claim, but the fact remains that the Naqshbandiyya, though a mystical order, has always been interested in politics, regarding the education of the ruling classes as absolutely incumbent upon them.` `We may safely accept the notion that Ahmad's successor in the order had a hand in the political development that followed his death and that this situation continued until about 1740` [text-layer?: clean] `.` (pp. 398–399, folios 367–368). On the source: `The Raudat al-qayyumiyya is certainly not a solid historical work. Written at the time of the breakdown of the Mogul Empire (after 1739` [text-layer?: clean] `), it reflects the admiration of a certain Naqshbandl faction for the master and his family; yet it must certainly rely upon some true statements of Sirhindl and shed light, if not on Naqshbandl history, at least on mystical psychology.` (p. 401, folio 370).
- **The two tauḥīds, and the refusal to simplify them**: `Ahmad Sirhindi has been praised primarily as the restorer of the classical theology of wahdat ash-shuhud, "unity of vision," or "testimonial monism," as opposed to the "degenerate"—as the orthodox would call it—system of wahdat al-wujud. However, the problem can scarcely be seen in such oversimplified terms.` (p. 399, folio 368), Schimmel adopting Molé's reading `(MM 108-10)` — a siglum, not a page of this book. And the bridge: `It was Waliullah who tried to bridge the gap between the tauhid-i shuhudi and the tauhid-i wujudi, just as he tried to explain the differences between the law schools as historical facts with no basic difference of importance.` (p. 403, folio 372), with `That he was attacked by a disciple of Mazhar shows the manifold currents inside eighteenth-century Naqshbandi theology.` (p. 403, folio 372). Earlier, on the terms themselves: `the terms wahdat al-wujud and wahdat ash-shuhud, which were so intensely discussed by later mystics, especially in India, are sometimes even interchangeable (MM 61; cf. N 408).` (p. 298, folio 267) — **Schimmel there names no Indian order, no author of *waḥdat ash-shuhūd* and no date for the controversy**, all `(not stated)`. Indexed as `Wahdat al-wujud ("unity of Being"), 267, 273, 284, 376, 401; Simnani on, 257; blurring the borders between religions, 286, 357; Ahmad Sirhindl on, 36` — the last locator is cut off at the right margin — and `Wahdat ash-shuhud, 267, 368, 401`; `Tauhid-i shuhudi, 368, 37`; `Taufyid-i wujudi, 368, 372` — the second locator is cut off at the right margin (pp. 542–543, folios 511–512).
- **The dangerous popular form of the doctrine**: `Has the easy formulation of "unity of being" contributed to the` `decline of Islam, especially in its simplified and dangerous condensation as hama ust, "everything is He," which left no room for the subtle distinctions maintained by Ibn cArabI himself? Was the transformation of classical Sufism with its stress on Carnal, "religious work," "activity," not completely superseded by ^irfan, "gnosis," theosophical knowledge as provided by an ingenious system?` (pp. 304–305, folios 273–274). `Carnal` [text-layer?: ʿamal — the printed word is an ordinary English word and the reading rests on the book's own gloss `"religious work," "activity,"`; flagged, not silently repaired]. **Schimmel puts both as questions and answers neither**, closing that `the discussion about his role—positive or negative—will probably never end as long as there are two different approaches to the mystical goal—by activity and conformity of will, or by contemplation and gnosis.` (p. 305, folio 274). Dard's Indian version of the same objection: he `frequently attacks those "imperfect Sufis" who claim "in their immature minds" to be confessors of unity, but are entangled in a sort of heresy, namely, believing in wahdat al-wujud`, holding instead `"everything is from Him"` (p. 407, folio 376).
- **Where Rūmī mattered most, and how he was read there**: `Rumi's work had its largest influence in the Indo-Pakistan Subcontinent, where his poetry has been popular since the early fourteenth century.` (p. 355, folio 324); `Most of the poets of the Subcontinent—as in Turkey—saw in Rumi a representative of the pantheistic trend that had colored most Sufi expressions after Ibn cArabi's time`, and `only toward the beginning of the twentieth century were new approaches made toward a better understanding` (p. 359, folio 328). Translation practice: `in later times we find similar attempts in the Subcontinent, where Urdu, Panjabi, and Sindhi translations have been produced in the same style.` (p. 357, folio 326).
- **Ḥallāj in the vernaculars — the archive's region named as the strongest case**: `The poets in Iran and Afghanistan were outdone in their devotion to the Hallajian tradition by the Indian poets writing in Persian who made use of the figure of Hallaj from the eleventh century on`, whose `images and forms … are almost identical with those found in classical Persian poetry`; but `An even stronger predilection for Mansur is to be found in the mystical folk songs composed in the vernaculars of Muslim India and adjacent areas.` (p. 106, folio 75). Footnote 44 self-cites `Schimmel, "The Martyr-Mystic Hallaj in Sindhi Folk-Poetry," Numen 9 (1962): 3` (p. 104, folio 73). And the one order that stands outside it — **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast** (Sachal named): `The generations of mystics who followed Sachal took over this practice, except for the members of the Naqshbandiyya, who have little sympathy for the martyr-mystic.` (p. 427, folio 306 as printed — **the folio rule gives 396; the two readings are kept side by side**).
- **Sachal's litany read as evidence about provincial knowledge, and partly discounted** — **shrine-of-sachal-sarmast**: `This poem gives a good idea of the historical information on which a mystic in a distant province of the Muslim world could rely, though some of the charges cannot be verified.` (p. 426, folio 395); Schimmel then places the litany in a genre going back to Rūzbihān Baqlī's enumeration of `the afflictions of the Sufis` (`B 37`, a paragraph in Baqlī, **not** a page of this book) and ʿAṭṭār's lists of suffering prophets (`MT 12; V 6`) (p. 426, folio 395), its function being that `the poets established their spiritual genealogy with all the lovers who had been afflicted or tried by God throughout the ages.`
- **Laṭīf read against the existing scholarship** — **bhit-bhit-shah**: `Shah "Abdu'l-Latif did use the inherited language of the Sufis; he himself acknowledged his indebtedness to Rumi, and his work cannot be fully appreciated without a thorough knowledge of the different currents of Sufism in Iran and India. This is the point at which most of the Hindu interpreters, and even an excellent British scholar like Herbert Tower Sorley, have not done full justice to him.` (p. 424, folio 393). **Footnote 74 three pages earlier praises the same book without that reservation** — `H. T. Sorley, Shah Abdul Latif of Bhit: His Poetry, Life, and Times (1940; reprint ed., Oxford, 1966), is an excellent study, although it underscores the political and social background slightly too much.` (p. 421, folio 390) — **two judgements on Sorley, three pages apart, which do not agree in tone; both are kept.** His technique: `Everybody knew the contents of these tales, so that the poet did not need to dwell on the preliminaries; he could begin his poems with the most dramatic moment and then elaborate the feeling of the heroine and the different shades of the soul's movements—hope, longing, fear, annihilation.` (p. 422, folio 391). And Trumpp is treated as a distorting witness: `Some critics have considered them rather unpolished in terms of classical (i.e., "Persianized") metrics and vocabulary, or thought they were too full of jingling rhymes and puns (thus Trumpp, who thoroughly disliked Sufism).` (pp. 423–424, folios 392–393).
- **The sources on Shāh ʿInāyat are unusable in both directions** — **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed**: the truth `cannot be detected from the sources, which are mostly unfriendly to him; positive evaluations, on the other hand, are strongly mixed with legends.` (p. 428, folio 397). Political engagement by Sufi leaders is presented as recurrent, not exceptional: `This was not the first time that mystical leaders in Sind, or in other parts of the Muslim world, were engaged in politics or in social reforms.` (p. 428, folio 397); and of the *hurr*, `by acts that cannot be called mystical or even religious.` (p. 427, folio 306 as printed — the rule gives 396) — Schimmel's own disqualification.
- **Pashto mysticism, by contrast, sober** — flagged by Schimmel herself as an impression needing study: `A deeper study of Pashto mystical poetry would be worthwhile, and it would probably strengthen the impression that the inhabitants of the Afghan hills rarely indulged in the exuberant, fanciful love songs and poetical sighs that the Sindhi or Panjabi mystics did, but followed, on the whole, a more sober line in Sufism that was fully consistent with the tradition developed earlier in Ghazna.` (p. 432, folio 401).
- **Language, print and the confessional split of Panjabi** — **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed** is not at issue here, but the two sentences govern how every Punjab and Sindh row's literature is dated: `Sindhi continued to flourish after the British occupation in 1843. With the introduction of a new Arabic alphabet in 1852, a considerable literature grew up that included mystical poetry and prose.` and `In the Punjab, the literary heritage of Panjabi was rarely continued by Muslims after 1800… The language was used almost exclusively by the Sikhs as their literary medium, whereas the Panjabi Muslims resorted to Urdu to express their more sophisticated thoughts, though they enjoyed and still enjoy listening to the simple mystical verses and ballads in their spoken mother tongue.` (both p. 427, folio 306 as printed — the rule gives 396). Upstream of that: `The breakdown of the Mogul Empire was accompanied by a breakdown of poetical language. Not only did Urdu grow in the capital, but Sindhi, Panjabi, and Pashto developed literatures of their own during the last decades of Aurangzeb's reign and continued to flourish during the eighteenth century.` (p. 402, folio 371).
- **Prophet-veneration in Sindhi poetry eclipses the historical Prophet**: `The popular veneration of the Prophet was grounded theoretically in such a way that the historical person of the Prophet was almost totally eclipsed behind the veil of light that the Sindhi Sufis wove about him.` (p. 418, folio 387). And the nineteenth-century turn: `It is symptomatic of the development of Sunsm [text-layer?: Sufism] in the nineteenth century that the emphasis was placed once more upon the person of Muhammad.` (p. 434, folio 403) — a `fi`-ligature word that reads as an ordinary English word. Related: `in a time when Islam was defeated everywhere in the political field, and when the Western powers encroached practically and spiritually upon the Muslim world, those mystics who founded new orders and fraternities called them tarlqa Muhammadiyya, "the Muhammadari path."` (p. 258, folio 227). `Muhammadari` [text-layer?: Muhammadan].
- **A modern publishing institution named with its place and its president** — **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat** (the book's "Hyderabad, Sind"; see `## REVIEW`, this sits close to the imprint-city decline): `The Shah Waliul-lah Academy in Hyderabad, Sind, under the presidency of Maulana Ghulam Mustafa Qasimi, has published a number of Wallullah's works, as well as studies about him.` (p. 403, folio 372, footnote 41).
- **Research gaps Schimmel states outright**, each a warning about how thin the 1975 base was: `A scholarly study of Muclnuddin is still needed.` (p. 376, folio 345, footnote 3); Waliullah's `works still await serious study in the West` and, of Dard, `Scarcely anyone has studied his numerous Persian prose works` (pp. 403–404, folios 372–373); Muhammad Ghauth's `Al-jawahir al-khamsa` `deserves a detailed study both in its original Persian and in its widely read Arabic translation` (p. 386, folio 355); Gangohi's `Rushdname` `still deserves careful study` (p. 388, folio 357, footnote 17); `The theories about the qayyumiyya have only begun to be explored by the scholars of Sufism. The role of the four qayyums in Mogul politics has still to be studied in detail.` (p. 401, folio 370); and `a truly comprehensive study of Rumi in a Western language is still lacking.` (p. 342, folio 311). Her characterisation of the two standard surveys is worth carrying with any citation of them: `Ishtiaq Husain Qureshi, The Muslim Community of the Indo-Pakistan Subcontinent (The Hague, 1962` [text-layer?: clean]`), from a strict Pakistani viewpoint; Mohammed Mujeeb, The Indian Muslims (Montreal and London, 1969` [text-layer?: clean]`), from an Indian standpoint` (p. 375, folio 344, footnote 1). On *malfūẓāt* as evidence she endorses Nizami: `these "diaries" constitute a valuable source for our knowledge of life outside the court circles. They are a necessary corrective of the official historiography; they allow us interesting glimpses into social and cultural problems that the official authors wittingly or unwittingly overlooked.` (p. 387, folio 356), against which she sets a decline: `As time passed, the mystics became ever more preoccupied with collecting biographical notes and making compilations of second- and often third-hand information.` (p. 386, folio 355).

### The author's general arguments and her stated limits

- **On the origins of Sufism, she surveys and mostly deflates** (pp. 41–43, folios 10–12): the Iranian thesis (`certain important Iranian elements have survived through the ages beneath its surface, as both Henri Corbin and Seyyed H. Nasr have recently emphasized`); the Neoplatonic (Nicholson called it `"in the air"`, yet held that `the original form of Sufism is "a native product of Islam itself."`); the Christian (`The best studies in this field have been written by the Swedish Bishop Tor Andrae`); the Indian, `beginning with Alfred von Kremer (1868) and Reinhart P. Dozy (1869). But even Max Morten's numerous articles in this field could not bring any stringent proof of such influences in the early period; for later times, the situation is slightly different.`; the Turkestani (`For the earliest period, influences from Turkestan are much more important, as Richard Hartmann has shown`), with a warning against a nationalist reading — `But such generalizations are dangerous.`; and the Chinese/Taoist, `even the rather far-fetched possibility`. Footnote judgements: Horten's book `should be used with caution`; Zaehner's `Hindu and Muslim Mysticism` `overstresses the Indian elements` (p. 42, folio 11).
- **Her declared bias, in her own words**: `Even now there is so much material available in the different languages of Islam that any generalization seems impossible. That is why this book can give only a glimpse of a few aspects of Sufism; even this will, probably, be tinged by a personal predilection for mystical poetry derived from the large area of Iranian cultural influence.` (p. 43, folio 12).
- **A methodological warning that should travel with every quotation in this archive**: `many sayings to which we give a deep theological or philosophical meaning may have been intended to be suggestive wordplay`; some definitions were uttered `as a sort of ko^an` [text-layer?: kōan]`, a paradox meant to shock the hearer`; she endorses Gairdner's question `"Do we not take their language too seriously? It parades as scientific; it is really poetico-rhetorical."` (p. 44, folio 13). On poetry specifically: `One cannot derive a mystical system out of Persian or Turkish poetry or see in it an expression of experiences to be taken at face value.` and `The opalescence of Persian poetry has caused much misunderstanding in the West` (p. 319, folio 288); `One has to distinguish carefully between poetic language and metaphysical systems.` and `one should not draw far-reaching consequences from their constant use in poetry` (p. 317, folio 286).
- **Decline is coeval with the thing itself**: `the complaint about the decline of Sufism almost coincides with its beginning` (p. 51, folio 20); the distinction `between the true Sufi, the mutasawwif who aspires at reaching a higher spiritual level, and the mustawif, the man who pretends to be a mystic but is a useless, even dangerous, intruder` (p. 51, folio 20); the eleventh-century formula `"Today Sufism is a name without reality, but formerly it was a reality without name. . . . The pretence is known and the practice unknown"` (`H 44`) (p. 51, folio 20); `The word Sufi became a pejorative expression` (p. 52, folio 21). And her explanation of why later Sufism is understudied: `The complaint about the decay of mysticism becomes stronger from century to century. This may be one reason why the later forms of Sufism have attracted comparatively little interest on the part of the scholars. Another reason is certainly the indebtedness of the majority of later Sufis to the system of Ibn cArabi` (p. 434, folio 403).
- **The modern verdict on the orders, reported not endorsed**: `The general view is that most of the orders, in their present forms, appeal to the lower instincts of the people and can be classified as either un-Islamic or antimodern.` (p. 435, folio 404), with the Shādhilī subbranch offered as counter-evidence.
- **Correction of a European misreading that still shapes shrine writing**: `westerners hav` [text-layer?: have — word truncated at the right margin] `often regarded the Sufis as representatives of a movement that has freed itself from the legal prescriptions of Islam, no longer caring for religion and infidelity. Sufi became almost an equivalent for "free thinker" with many Europeans. That is, however, not correct.` Her counter: `They did not abolish the rites but rather interiorized them` (p. 137, folio 106). Likewise `The Sufis always remained inside the fold of Islam` (p. 48, folio 17) and `They did not reject the religious law but rather added to it—additions that made more exacting demands on their personal lives.` (p. 115, folio 84); the Path is derived from the law — `no mystical experience can be realized if the binding injunctions of the sharfa are not followed faithfully first.` (p. 129, folio 98).
- **On Ibn ʿArabī's tolerance verses, against the usual shrine-tolerance use of them**: after quoting `My heart is capable of every form, / a cloister of the monk, a temple for idols,` she writes `But this seemingly tolerant statement contains, rather, a statement about the author's own lofty spiritual rank... It is highest self-praise, acknowledgment of an il-` `lumination that is far beyond the "illumination of the names," but not tolerance preached to the rank and file.` (p. 303, folio 272). Beside it she does record the softening of boundaries: `as the Sufis gained more knowledge of God (or thought they had), their religious prejudices diminished, and they understood that "the ways to God are as many as the breaths of men."` Yet `they would formally cling to their own religion as the last and most comprehensive revelation of divine truth, and even though some of them might allegorize the rites of Islam, they did not abolish them.` (p. 317, folio 286). **Both stand; neither is resolved.**
- **Ghazzālī's effect is stated twice, in tension with itself, and the question is left open**: `This teaching—a marriage between mysticism and law—has made Ghazzali the most influential theologian of medieval Islam.` and `The moderate Sufi outlook began to color the life of most average Muslims.` (pp. 126–127, folios 95–96); against which, in Anawati's words she puts as an open question, `"Though so brilliant, his contribution did not succeed in preventing the anchylosis that two or three centuries later was to congeal Muslim religious thought"?` — and then in her own, `Or was his greatness itself the reason for the congealing of moderate Islam?` (p. 128, folio 97).
- **The book's closing thesis, and its refusal to settle the modernist question**: `The mysticism of love and suffering—which teaches man to live and to die for a goal outside himself—is perhaps the most important message of Sufism today.` (p. 438, folio 407), followed by `One may agree with this idea or reject it in favor of a modernized world view without the whole firmament of saints, which, like the stars at dawn, is naughted when the sun of reality appears.` (p. 438, folio 407). And the reason any of this matters for a literary archive: `Even the work of those who are often considered to be frivolous, or pagan, or unreligious, cannot be properly interpreted without a thorough knowledge of the Sufi heritage.` (p. 437, folio 406).
- A source-criticism run worth carrying whenever a hagiography is cited: of ʿAṭṭār's *Tadhkirat al-auliyāʾ*, `Hagiography was, for him, another medium for telling stories about his venerated masters` … `For factual details, this book should be supplemented with other, more sober sources.` (p. 335, folio 304); of Abū Nuʿaym, `his book is a storehouse of information, which, however, must be used with caution.` (p. 116, folio 85); on ḥadīth, `it cannot be denied that many of the traditions that are favorites with the later Sufis have no basis in the "canonical" collections of hadlth completed in the last third of the ninth century.` (p. 252, folio 221); and **`Idries Shah, The Sufis, as well as his other books, should be avoided by serious students.`** (p. 39, folio 8).

### The book's apparatus, and what it forbids

- **The sigla key is the thing that stops a later `(H 293)` or `(M 1:1720)` being read as a page of this book.** Its preface, in full: `Since this book is intended primarily for the general reader, we have—though reluctantly—refrained from having too many footnotes or too large a bibliography. The reader will find much additional information about personalities, terminology, and historical facts in the Encyclopedia of Islam (1913-36; sd ed., 1960-); the relevant articles have not been mentioned in the footnotes.` [text-layer?: 2d ed.] (p. 22, folio xix), then `The following frequently cited works are abbreviated in the text:`. **As printed the key holds 31 sigla, not 34.** In printed order, with the damaged years flagged: `A` = Abū Nuʿaym, *Ḥilyat ul-auliyāʾ*, Cairo 1932; `AD` = ʿAṭṭār, *Dīwān-i qaṣāʾid wa ghazaliyāt*, `1339 sh./igGo` [text-layer?: 1339 sh./1960], **number of poem cited**; `AP` = *Armaghān-i Pāk*, ed. Sheikh Muhammad Ikram, Karachi 1953; `B` = Rūzbihān Baqlī, *Sharḥ-i shaṭḥiyāt*, ed. Corbin, Tehran and Paris 1966, **paragraphs cited**; `BA` = Baqlī, *ʿAbhar al-ʿāshiqīn*, 1958, **paragraphs cited**; `BO` = Birge, *The Bektashi Order of Dervishes*, 1937/1965; `CL` = Corbin, *L'Homme de lumière dans le soufisme iranien*, 1971; `D` = Rūmī, *Dīwān-i kabīr*, `1336 sh./ig57` [text-layer?: 1957], **number of poem cited**; `G` = Ghazzālī, *Iḥyāʾ ʿulūm ad-dīn*, 1289 h./1872-73; `H` = Hujwīrī, *Kashf al-Maḥjūb*, trans. Nicholson, 1911/1959 (pp. 22–23, folios xix–XX; **a stray `C` stands alone on one line inside this entry — a detached ʿayn, not a siglum**); `IK` = Mīr Dard, *ʿIlm ul-kitāb*, `1309 h./i8gi-92` [text-layer?: 1891-92]; `K` = Kalābādhī, trans. Arberry, 1935; `L` = Sarrāj, *Kitāb al-lumaʿ*, 1914; `M` = Rūmī, *Mathnawī*, ed. Nicholson, 1925-40, **volume and line cited**; `MC` = Nicholson's commentary; `MM` = Molé, *Les mystiques musulmans*, 1965; `MT` = ʿAṭṭār, *Manṭiq aṭ-ṭayr*, 1962; `N` = Jāmī, *Nafaḥāt al-uns*, `1336 sh./ig57`; `NS` = Nicholson, *Studies in Islamic Mysticism*, 1921/1967; `P` = Massignon, *La passion d'al-Hosayn ibn Mansour Al-Hallāj*, 1922; `Q` = Qushayrī, *Ar-risāla*, `I33oh./igi2` [text-layer?: 1330 h./1912]; `QR` = Qushayrī, *Ar-rasāʾil al-qushayriyya*, Karachi 1964; `R` = Ritter, *Das Meer der Seele*, `J955-` [text-layer?: 1955; the trailing `-` is damage, not a date range]; `S` = Sanāʾī, *Ḥadīqat al-ḥaqīqat*, `1329 sh./igso` [text-layer?: 1950]; `SD` = Sanāʾī, *Dīwān*, `1341 sh./ig62`; `T` = ʿAṭṭār, *Tadhkirat al-auliyāʾ*, 1905-7/1959; `U` = ʿAṭṭār, *Muṣībatnāme*, `1338 sh./ig5g`; `V` = ʿAṭṭār, *Ushturnāme*, `1339 sh./ig6o`; `W` = Nwyia, *Exégèse coranique et langage mystique*, 1970; `X` = ad-Daylamī, *Sīrat-i Ibn al-Ḥafīf ash-Shīrāzī*, ed. Schimmel, Ankara 1955; `Y` = Yunus Emre, *Divan*, ed. Gölpınarlı, Istanbul 1943 (pp. 22–24, folios xix–XXI).
- **The sigla in use are always another book's locator.** Observed in chapter 1: `(M 3:12 59-68)` [text-layer?: M 3:1259-68 — a space splits the line number] (p. 34, folio 3); `(N 34)`, `(H 367)`, `(H 373)`, `(N 8-9)` (pp. 37–38, folios 6–7). The running text identifies several of them by naming the works: `L` = Sarrāj (p. 115, folio 84), `K` = Kalābādhī (p. 116, folio 85), `A` = Abū Nuʿaym (p. 116, folio 85), `N` = Jāmī (p. 117, folio 86), `Q` = Qushayrī and `H` = Hujwīrī (p. 119, folio 88), `T` = ʿAṭṭār (p. 120, folio 89), `B` = Baqlī (p. 110, folio 79), `W` = Nwyia (pp. 111–112, folios 80–81), `MM` = Molé (p. 117, folio 86) — **all agreeing with the key**. **One siglum used in the text is not in the key at all**: `(CV 135)`, for Corbin on Baqlī's *iltibās* (p. 330, folio 299) — **not expanded here**.
- **`AM` is resolved by the book itself**, in the headnote to the `INDEX OF PROPHETIC TRADITIONS`: `(The AM citation gives the location of the full Arabic text of the tradition in Badicuz-Zaman Furuzanfar's Aftadlth-i Mathnawl [Tehran, 1334 sh./ig55].)` [text-layer?: Aḥādīth-i Mathnawī; 1955] (p. 508, folio 477). The bibliography prints the same work independently as `Furuzanfar, Badicuz-Zaman. Ahadlth-i Mathnawl. Tehran, 1334 sh./` / `1955. In Persian.` (p. 477, folio 446). **`AM no. N` is therefore a hadith number, never a page and never a year.**
