- **The book's own transcription policy explains the whole ʿayn damage class.** `There is no single, commonly accepted system of transliterating languages written in Arabic characters.` and `Throughout this book the generally accepted American transcription system has been used.` (p. 28, folio XXV); **the table's line for the letter reads `'ayn: c, or unnoticed`** (p. 29, folio XXVI) — so a `c` in `cAttar` or `c Uthman` is the book's own superscript ʿayn flattened to the baseline by the text layer, not a transcriber's error, and Schimmel warns the book sometimes leaves ʿayn `unnoticed` altogether. The alif line reads `alif: a, D, carrier of the initial vowel.` (p. 28, folio XXV) [text-layer?: `a, ʾ`]. She adds that `Urdu, Sindhi, Panjabi, and Pashto, as well as other Islamic languages oi non-Arabic origin, have added a number oi letters and diacritical marks to secure the correct pronounciation.` (p. 29, folio XXVI) — `oi` = `of`, twice; `pronounciation` is printed thus and is left as printed.
- **The abjad values are printed out**, which matters because the book computes dates from them: alif `i` [text-layer?: 1], `bff` 2, `to?` 400, `thff` 500, `jim` 3, `ha` 8, `khff` 600, `dal` 4, `dhal` 700, `ra"` 200, `zdy` 7, `sin` 60, `shin` 300, `sad` `9°` [text-layer?: 90], `dad` 800, `to"` 9, `za1` 900, `'ayn` 70, `ghayn` `1OOO` [text-layer?: 1000], `/5s` [text-layer?: fāʾ] 80, `qdf` 100, `kaf` 20, `/am` [text-layer?: lām] 30, `mlm` 40, `niin` 50, `hff` 5, `waw` 6, `yff` 10 (pp. 28–29, folios XXV–XXVI). Schimmel notes the practice: `other authors may give, by the numerical value of the title, the date of its composition.` (p. 45, folio 14) — the chunk gives no example.
- **The edition's own account of the book's standing and its gaps**, by Carl W. Ernst, `Foreword © 2 0 1 1` (p. 7): `Mystical Dimensions of Islam, from its first appearance in 1975, has become the standard English-language handbook on the subject of Sufism or Islamic mysticism.` (p. 16, folio xiii); the author's dates and training, `Annemarie Schimmel (1922—2003), formerly professor of Indo-Muslim studies at Harvard University`, two doctorates, `the first from Berlin in Arabic and Islamic studies at the age of nineteen and the second, ten years later, from Marburg in the history of religion`, `over eighty books` (p. 16, folio xiii; all four dates print clean). What it is strong in: `particularly rich in its discussion of the spirituality of the Prophet Muhammad, the poetry of Rumi and other Persian Sufis, the feminine element in Sufism, and the extensive presence of Sufism in the Indo-Pakistan subcontinent.` (p. 17, folio XIV). Her method: `She relied upon the formidable tools of philology and history for research, and she used the comparative language of the phenomenology of religion to explain her insights thematically.` and `Unlike armchair scholars, throughout her career Schimmel traveled extensively and had numerous close friendships in Muslim countries.` (p. 17, folio XIV). **Its limits, to be quoted whenever this book is used as evidence about a shrine**: `a number of features in Mystical Dimensions of Islam are doubtless subject to improvement.` · `Characteristically, Schimmel remarked that her work avoided "sociology," perhaps acknowledging that she focused on the poetic and the ideal rather than the realm of society and politics where Sufism has in fact been contested and transformed; her adoption of Evelyn Underbill's highly personal approach to mysticism gave little consideration to mysticism's social history.` [text-layer?: Underhill] · `Reviewers have noted that the book did not cover the extensive history of Sufism in East and West Africa, it provided more attention to Eastern areas (Turkish, Persian, and Indian) than Arab regions, and it did not reflect on problems of Orientalism in Sufi studies or contemporary Sufi groups moving outside of traditional Islamic identifications.` (all p. 20, folio XVII). And `Her writings do not address terrorism or the conflicts that followed the end of the Cold War.` (p. 19, folio XVI).
- **The English text in hand is not the fullest version the author produced — and the book gives two different accounts of the fuller one. Both are kept.** Ernst: `(Schimmel herself produced an expanded German translation of the book in 1985.)`, footnoted to `Annemarie Schimmel, Mystische Dimensionen des Islam: Die Geschichte des Sufismus (Munich: Eugen DiederichsVerlag, 1985; reprinted., Baden-Baden: Insel Verlag, 1995).` (p. 20, folio XVII). Schimmel herself, in the Addendum: `A somewhat enlarged German edition of this book, Mystische Dimensionen des Islam (Cologne, 1986), appeared in my own translation.` (p. 500, folio 469) — **a different year and a different city; neither is resolved here.**
- **How the book was funded**: `With special gratitude I acknowledge a generous subsidy from the Ozai-Durrani Funds, Harvard University, which was given in support of the exploration of Indo-Muslim culture contained in this book.` (p. 27, folio XXIV).
- **The Addendum's stated purpose**, in Schimmel's own voice: `Since this book first appeared in 1975, many new works about various aspects of Sufism have been published, particularly in connection with Ibn 'Arabl.` [text-layer?: Ibn ʿArabī] (p. 500, folio 469) — the only first-person sentence in that chunk, and borne out by the Addendum's own contents (Addas 1988, Chittick 1989, Chodkiewicz 1986, Austin 1980, Deladrière 1978, pp. 500–501, folios 469–470). It also notes a project rather than a book, under `Nurbakhsh`: `A Sufi encyclopedia in many volumes is currently be-` / `ing translated from Persian into English.` (p. 502, folio 471).
- **The Index of Subjects is itself content, not a pointer**, because its entries carry the book's own parenthetical glosses — roughly 120 of them, distributed through the runs above. Its locators are the book's own damaged folios and are never converted. Damaged headwords and locators left unresolved include `Ha//, 106` (p. 532), `Saum dcPudi … "5` (p. 540), `Ruku^ … '53. "54` (p. 540), `Tahara … 243» 255. 4i6` (p. 541), `Silsila … 297. 3!3> 339. 351. 356, 364, 372` (p. 541), `Qutb … 302, 203, 369 … alway.` (p. 539), `Tawajjuh … •afifi 3°°` (p. 542), `Qayyum … 369. 37°. 374` (p. 539), `Muhibb … 34°` (p. 537), `Zuhd … 37, no` (p. 543), `Zdhid-i zahirparast … in` (p. 543), `Poverty … 95, 100, in, 120-24` (p. 538), `Murld … unity o with sheikh` (p. 537), `Wajd … Se also Ecstasy` (p. 543), `Qibla … 195, 29` (p. 539), `Muharram, 75, 340, 341 , 38` (p. 537), `Hizb al-bahr. by Shadhili, 178, 25` (p. 533), `Wahdat al-wujud … Ahmad Sirhindl on, 36` (p. 543) and `Tauhid-i shuhudi, 368, 37` (p. 542). ### The bibliography and the addendum

*The Bibliography runs pp. 468–498 and the Addendum pp. 500–504. Both are apparatus: they are recorded because the titles are useful to this project, not because they are statements about places. **Ruling (a) is NOT fired on any publisher's imprint city here — that decline is standing precedent across 23 independent Pass-1 readings, and the question is Rauf's, not a worker's.** Where a bibliography entry names a person who is an archive row's principal figure, the 18 September person ruling does apply, and the id is carried.*

- **What the bibliography covers.** It is South Asian to an unusual degree for a general Sufism handbook: by hand count of the entries' own printed language-notes it marks **eight entries `In Sindhi`** or as Sindhi translations (pp. 482, 485, 487, 489, 490, 491, 494), **one `In Siraiki`** (p. 492), **three `In Pashto`** (pp. 479 ×2, 483) and **fourteen `In Urdu`** (pp. 476, 481, 482, 486, 488, 489 ×3, 490 ×5, 490). **Schimmel's own entries occupy pp. 492–494 (folios 461–463) continuously**, and a substantial run of them is South Asian — the Sindhi Adabi Board, Sindhi folk-poetry, Sindhi literature, Sindhi Qurʾan translations, the veneration of the Prophet in Sindhi poetry, `Volkskunde von Sind`, Shah ʿAbdul Latif (twice), Shah ʿInayat of Jhok, Mir Dard, Indo-Muslim poetry and Islamic literatures of India (pp. 492–494, folios 461–463).
- **The stretch of the Bibliography the notes transcribe (pp. 468–475, folios 437–444) bears on this archive in four ways.** *On Sind and Sindhi*: `cAbdul Qadir Tattawi, Sayyid. Hadlqat al-auliya. Edited by Sayyid Hus- samuddin Rashdi. Hyderabad, Sind, 1967. In Persian.` (p. 468, folio 437); `Abro, Jamal. Munhun kdro. In Sindhi. Translated by Annemarie Schimmel as "Geschwarzten Gesichts."` [text-layer?: Geschwärzten] … `Tubingen` [text-layer?: Tübingen]`, 1966.` (p. 468, folio 437) — the same story named in the p. 429 footnote on shrine wealth, **there titled `"Geschwarzten Gesichtes"`, here `"Geschwarzten Gesichts"`; both forms kept**; `Aczam Tattawi, Muhammad. Tuhfat at-tahirln. Edited by Badr-i Alam Durrani. Karachi, 1956. In Persian.` (p. 471, folio `44°` [text-layer?: 440]), **cited in the appendix's footnote 5 with a different title spelling and a different form of the editor's name**, `Aczam Tattawi, Tuhfat at-tdhirin, ed. B. A. Durrani (Karachi, 1956), pp. 91, 138, 175` (p. 465, folio 434); `Baloch, Dr. Nabi Bakhsh, ed. Madahun ayn mundjdtun. Karachi, 1959. In Sindhi.` and `. Tih akaryun. Hyderabad, Sind, 1962. In Sindhi.` (p. 472, folio `44!` [text-layer?: 441]) — the second being the Golden Alphabet collection cited at p. 454; `Bedil Rohriwaro. Dlwdn. Edited by 'Abdul Husayn Musawl. Karachi, 1954. In Sindhi.` (p. 472, folio `44!`); `Bhatti, M. Salih. Mansur Hallaj. Hyderabad, Sind, 1952. In Sindhi.` (p. 473, folio 442); `Burhanpuri, Rashid. Burhdnpur ke Sindhi auliyd. Karachi, 1957. In Urdu.` (p. 474, folio 443) — the Burhanpur migrants of p. 415; `Burton, Sir Richard. Sind and the Races That Inhabit the Valley of the Indus.'London, 1851.` (p. 474, folio 443 — note the stray apostrophe before `London`), cited in the appendix's footnote 4 with a page, `p. 230` (p. 464, folio 433).
- **One Sindhi entry carries an archive id under the person ruling** — **shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori** (Girhōrī named in the title): `Daudpota, Umar Muhammad. Kalam-i Girhorl. Hyderabad, Sind, 1956. In Sindhi.` (p. 475, folio 444). **Evidence base: a title, nothing more** — the entry says nothing about the man, his shrine or his dates, and the attachment should be read as "his kalām was edited and printed in Sindhi by 1956", not as the book describing him.
- **A work cited in the appendix that has no entry in the visible stretch of the bibliography**: `I c jazul Haqq QuddusI, Tadhkira-yi sufiya-yi Sind (Karachi, 1959), p. 334` [text-layer?: ʿIjāzuʾl-Ḥaqq Quddūsī] (p. 465, folio 434).
- *On the Punjab and Panjabi*: `cAqueel, Shafic. Punjab rang. Lahore, 1968. In Urdu.` (p. 470, folio 439); `Barnett, Lionel D. Punjabi: Printed Books in the British Museum. Lon- don, 1961.` (p. 472, folio `44!`); and, carrying an id under the person ruling — **mazar-of-bulleh-shah** (the book's `Bullhe Shah`, named as the author of his own *Dīwān*) — `Bullhe Shah. Dlwdn. Edited by Faqir M. Faqir. Lahore, 1960. In Pan- jabi.` (p. 474, folio 443). **Evidence base: a bibliography headword.** The imprint `Lahore` on that same line is **not** fired.
- *On Indian and Indo-Pakistani Sufism, saints and shrines*, the entries most likely to be wanted by an entry writer: Aziz Ahmad's *An Intellectual History of Islam in India* (`i969-` [text-layer?: 1969]), his Waliullah and Sirhindi articles, `"The Sufi and the Sultan in Pre-Mughal Muslim India." Der Islam 38 (1963).` and `"Sufismus und Hindumystik." Saeculum 15, no. i (1964).` (pp. 468–469, folios 437–438); `Amir Khurd. Siyar al-auliya?. Delhi, 1309 h./1891-92. In Persian.` and `Amir Khusrau. Dlwdn-i kdmil` (p. 469, folio 438); `cAndalib, Muhammad Nasir. Nala-yi ^Andallb. 2 vols. Bhopal, 1309 h./ 1891-92. In Persian.` (p. 469, folio 438) — the work discussed at p. 466; `Ali, Meer Hassan. Observations on the Mussulmauns of India. 2 vols. London, 1833.` (p. 469, folio 438); the four Dard titles including `c/Zm ul-kitdb. Bhopal, 1309 h./1891-92` [text-layer?: ʿIlm ul-kitāb — **this is the work the sigla key calls `IK`**] (p. 475, folio 444); `Chiragh-i Dehlawl. Khayr al-majdlis` (p. 474, folio 443); `Begg, Mirza Wahiduddin. The Holy Biography of Hazrat Khwaja Mu- inaddin Chishti. Ajmer, 1960.` (p. 473, folio 442); `Digby, Simon. "Encounters with Jogis in Indian Sufi Hagiography." Mimeographed. London: School of Oriental and African Studies, January 1970.` and `Eaton, Richard M. "Sufis of Bijapur: Some Social Roles of Medieval Muslim Saints." Paper read at the Conference of South East Asian Studies, 29 March 1972, New'York.` (p. 475, folio 444).
- **A page-range contradiction inside the book's own apparatus, kept side by side.** Arnold's *Encyclopedia of Religion and Ethics* article is cited in footnote 12 as `"Saints, Muhammadan, in India," … 11:67-73.` (p. 270, folio 239) and in the bibliography as `"Saints, Muhammadan, India." In Encylopedia` [text-layer?: Encyclopedia] `of Religion and Ethics, edited by James Hastings, vol. 11, pp. 68-73. 1908-27.` (p. 470, folio 439) — **different start page and a different title form.** On the same bibliography page, `Arnold, Sir Thomas. The Preaching of Islam. 1896. Reprint. Lahor. 1956-` [text-layer?: Lahore, 1956 — **the imprint city is truncated at the right margin**].
- *Shrine and saint-cult studies outside South Asia, recorded because the genre is this project's*: `Dermenghem, fimile` [text-layer?: Émile] `. Le culte des saints dans I'Islam Maghrebin. Paris, 1954-` and `. Vies des saints musulmans. Algiers, 1942.` (p. 475, folio 444); `Dubler, Cesar E. Uber` [text-layer?: Über] `islamischen Grab- und Heiligenkult. Zurich, 1960.` (p. 475, folio 444); `Abd el-Haqq el-Badisi. El-Maqsad (Vies des saints du Rif). Translated by Gabriel S. Colin. Paris, 1926.` (p. 468, folio 437). **The first author's surname prints three different ways across the book** — `fimile Dermenghem` (p. 233, folio 202, where Schimmel calls the book `the best study in this field, written with a deep understanding of mystical Islam`), `fimile Derraenghem` (p. 438, folio 407) and `Dermenghem, fimile` (p. 475, folio 444) — **all three kept; the first name's initial is damaged throughout and is not reconstructed.**
- **The Addendum (pp. 500–504, folios 469–473) is post-1975 scholarship Schimmel added herself**, and the South Asian run is the part this project wants: `Baljon, J. M. S. Religion and Thought of Shah Wall Allah Dihlawl (1703-` / `1762). Leiden, 1986.`; `Eaton, Richard. Sufis of Bijapur. Princeton, 1978.`; `Hussaini, Syed Shah Khusro. Sayyid Muhammad al-Husayni Gisu-` / `dardz: On Sufism. Delhi, 1983.` [text-layer?: Gīsūdarāz]; `Jackson, Paul. The Way of a Sufi. Delhi, 1984.`; `Jalbani, Ghulam Nabi, trans. Sufism and the Islamic Tradition: The` / `Lamahat and Sataat of Shah Waliullah. London, 1980.`; `Manerl, Sharafuddin. The Hundred Letters. Translated by Paul Jack-` / `son. New York, 1980.` and `Khwdn-i pur Ni'mat: A Table Laden with Good Things. Translated` / `by Paul Jackson. Delhi, 1986.` [text-layer?: Manērī, Sharafuddīn; Khwān-i pur Niʿmat]; **`Rizvi, Syed Athar Abbas Rizvi. A History of Sufism in India. 2 vols.` / `Delhi, 1978.`** — the surname is printed twice and quoted as it stands; `Roy, Asim. The Islamic Syncretistic Tradition in Bengal. Princeton, 1983.`; and Schimmel's own `As through a Veil: Mystical Poetry in Islam. New` / `York, 1982.`, `"The Golden Chain of Sincere Muhammadans."`, `Islam in the Indian Subcontinent. Leiden, 1980.`, `Liebe zu dem Einen: Texte aus dem indischen Sufismus. Zurich,` / `1987.`, `Pain and Grace: A Study of Two Eighteenth-Century Indo-Muslim` / `Mystical Poets. Leiden, 1976.` and `"Zur Verwendung des Halladsch-Motivs in der indo-persisch-` / `en Poesie."` — note the text layer's hyphenated break across the line (pp. 500–503, folios 469–472). Two entries whose subject the notes cannot state: `Lawrence, Bruce B. Notes from a Distant Flute. London and Tehran,` / `1978.` and `Lambrick, H. T. The Terrorist. London, 1972.` — **(not stated)** in both cases; the second is listed only because it stands inside a Sufism addendum.
- **The one Addendum entry whose imprint city is a `toponym_map.txt` key — DECLINED, not attached.** `Shackle, Christopher. Styles and Themes in the Siraiki Mystical Poetry of` / `Sind. Multan, ca. 1976.` (p. 503, folio 472). Ruling (a) is not fired on a publisher's imprint city. Were Rauf to reverse that decline, this line would take, in one step: **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak**. The same decline stands over every `Lahore`, `Karachi` and `Hyderabad, Sind` imprint quoted above and over `AP` and `QR` in the sigla key, both `Karachi`.

## Figures and sites not in the archive

*Compact, one line each, ordered for use: first the Subcontinental figures and sites that could
plausibly become archive rows because a tomb, dargah, ʿurs or standing building is named; then the
rest of the Subcontinental cast; then the wider Islamic world in regional runs; then folk-tale
figures, offices, communities, places and the book's apparatus. Legends and miracles are folded into
the entry of the figure they belong to, attributed exactly as the book attributes them, and the
un-owned miracle types are gathered at the end. Nothing in this section is also carried in
`## Shrines in the archive this book informs`.*

### A. Subcontinental figures and sites with a tomb, shrine, dargah or building named

- **Muʿīnuddīn Chishtī of Ajmer** (`Mucmuddin Chishti`, `Muclnuddln Chishtl`) — born in Sistan,
  part-time disciple of Abū Najīb Suhrawardī, reached Delhi `in i igs` [text-layer?: `119_`, **last
  digit unreadable**], settled in Ajmer, died 1236; `The beautiful marble sanctuary of Mucmuddin
  (erected by the Mogul Emperor Jihangir) is also still a center of religious inspiration for thousands
  of faithful Muslims.` (pp. 310, 376–377, 385, 391; index `(d. 1236), 279, 345-46, 351, 360`, p. 521,
  folio `49°`; `Khalifa … of Mucmud-dln Chishti, 346`, p. 534, folio 503).
- **Nizāmuddīn Auliyā of Delhi** (`Nizamuddin Auliyff`) — `(d. 1325)`, `tenderly called by his
  followers Mahbub-i ildhl, "God's beloved"`, Farīd's khalīfa in Delhi, `outlived seven kings`; his tomb
  `still the most frequently visited sanctuary in Delhi`; his conversations the `Fawffid al-fifad`,
  compiled 1307–22 by Amīr Ḥasan Sijzī Dihlawī, who concealed the scheme for a year until
  `Nizamuddin himself helped him in filling the lacunae in the manuscript` (pp. 310, 358, 379–381, 387;
  index `(d. 1325), 279, 327, 348. 35°. 35 1. 356`, p. 522, folio 491).
- **Quṭbuddīn Bakhtiyār Kākī** — from Farghana, came to India with Muʿīnuddīn, died 1235, `highly
  venerated by Iltutmish`; his `rather modest compound in Merauli near the Qutb Minar in Delhi is
  usually filled with pious pilgrims, who recite and sing their devotional poetry` (p. 377, folio 346;
  index p. 513, folio 482). **Not the archive's Qutbuddin Aibak.**
- **Gīsūdarāz / Muhammad Bandanawaz** — `"he with the long tresses"`, `The south Indian Chishtl saint`
  who migrated to the Deccan under Bahmani patronage, `died in 1422 and is buried in Gulbarga near
  present-day Hyderabad`, the first Sufi to use Dakhni; commented on the *Tamhīdāt* about 1400 (pp. 255,
  321, 327, 382, 389, 401, 478; index p. 515, folio 484).
- **Jalāluddīn Tabrīzī** — a disciple of Abū Ḥafṣ ʿUmar Suhrawardī `who went to Bengal, where he died
  in 1244`; `The order still flourishes in this area` (pp. 382–383, folios 351–352; index
  `(d. 1244), 351`, p. 518, folio 487).
- **Sayyid ʿAlī Hamadhānī** — the Kubrāwī leader who, `according to the legend`, `migrated to that
  country with seven hundred followers`, reached Kashmir in 1371 and `died in 1385 in Swat`, his order
  remaining active in the Subcontinent (pp. 289, 394, 395; `Swat, 258`, p. 526).
- **Bāyezīd Anṣārī, Pīr-i Roshan, and the Roshaniyya** — `(d. 1585)`, `"Radiant Master"`, founder of a
  movement `in the days of Humayun and Akbar`, first to apply Persian metrics to Pashto, his
  `Khayr ul-bayan` `the beginning of Pashto literature proper`; `Pir-i Roshan's sons were drowned in the
  Indus near Attock` (p. 429, folio 398; index p. 513, folio 482; `Roshaniyya, 398`, p. 524).
- **Bedil of Rohri / Bedil Rohriwaro** — `(d. 1872)`, `a prolific, though not very outstanding, writer
  in five languages`, the languages not enumerated; his `Diwdn`, ed. cAbdul Husayn Musawi (p. 427,
  folio 306 as printed — rule gives 396; p. 216, folio 185, footnote 93; index p. 513, folio 482).
  **Carried here as a person; the key `rohri` fires only inside his nisba.**
- **Janullah of Rohri** — `the SuhrawardI mystic Janullah of Rohri, one of the outstanding Persian
  poets of Sind from the second half of the eighteenth century`, a composer of songs on Shāh ʿInāyat's
  martyrdom (p. 428, folio 397).
- **Makli Hill, near Thatta** — `one of the most fascinating sites of Indo-Muslim architecture, is the
  last resting place for 125,000 saints, according to the tradition`, a graveyard `which stretches over
  miles, where mystical concerts and dances were performed on almost any day of the week`; named again
  as the site of `the dancing practices` the Naqshbandīs attacked (pp. 415, 428; index
  `Makli Hill, 384, 397`, p. 520). **Schimmel attributes the figure to tradition, not to herself.**
- **Makhdūm Muhammad Hāshim of Thatta and the Naqshbandi families of Thatta** — `undertook to attack
  all those Sufis who defended the emotional side of religion as exemplified in the dancing practices on
  Makli Hill`; Sindhi Koran commentaries and Prophet-legends `were being written in the Sindhi language
  by the learned Naqshbandi families of Thatta` (pp. 428–429, folios 397–398; index `(d. 1760), 397`,
  p. 520, folio 489).
- **ʿĪsā Jund Allāh of Thatta** — `one of the saints of Thatta in Sind, clsa Jund Allah, in the
  seventeenth century even dared to equate samcf with ritual prayer.` (p. 216, folio 185).
- **The trees of Sind called after Gīlānī** — a site-class rather than a site: Burton, `writing about
  1850, says that there were about a hundred large trees in Sind, all called after Gilani. Each had a
  pole and a flag hung upon it to fulfill some vow made in adversity or sickness. Sweetmeats were
  distributed to the poor in hono` [text-layer?: **right-margin truncation of `honor`, not
  reconstructed**] `of the saint, and the fruit and leaves of the trees were not allowed to be touched
  even by cattle.` (p. 279, folio 248, with footnote 30).
- **Bībī Fāṭima Hajrānī of Sind** — `Blbi Fatima Haj-` / `ram` [text-layer?: **the final element is
  broken across a line end and is not resolved**], `one of the most famous saints in the province` of
  Sind, `a haftza` [text-layer?: ḥāfiẓa] `who performed many miracles`, Burton's remarks on her
  `corroborated by the numerous books devoted by Sindhi scholars to the religious life of their
  country` (p. 465, folio 434; index `BIbi Fatima Hajrani, 434`, p. 513, folio 482). **Not the
  eleventh-century `Bibi Fatima` of p. 464.**
- **The `haft ʿafīfa`, "the Seven Chaste"** — women saints of Sind `who escaped a group of attacking
  soldiers and were swallowed by the earth before their virtue could be touched` (p. 465, folio 434;
  index `Haft ca/j/<z ("Seven Chaste Women," group of saints), 434` — **the headword is heavily damaged
  and is not resolved** — p. 532, folio `50!` [text-layer?: 501]).
- **The `mukhaddarāt-i abdāliyya`** — `"the veiled women in the rank of abddl,"` in Sind, `and countless
  others` (p. 465, folio 434; index p. 537, folio 506).
- **Bibi Jamal Khatun** — `(d. 1639)`, Mian Mir's `mystically inclined sister`, who came with him from
  Sind to Lahore, `one of the outstanding saints of the Qadiriyya order during its formative period in
  the Punjab`; **no tomb, no date beyond the year, no site** (pp. 392, 464; index `(d. 1639), 361, 433`,
  p. 513, folio 482).
- **Molla Shah Badakhshi** — Mian Mir's `disciple and successor`, who introduced Dārā to the saint and
  was Jihanara's master; `Her master Molla Shah thought her worthy to be his successor, though this did
  not materialize` (pp. 392, 394, folios 361, 363). **No shrine, no date** — (not stated).
- **Muhammad Ghauth Gwaliori** — `(d. 1562)`, `main representative` of the Shattariyya, `for whom Akbar
  built a magnificent tomb`; author of `Al-jawahir al-khamsa, "The Five Jewels,"` connecting `the
  meditation of the most beautiful names with astrological ideas` (p. 386, folio 355).
- **Ḥajjī Bayram of Ankara** — `a modern Turkish woman, modernizing and simplifying the original act of
  sweeping, may offer a broom at the door of Ankara's local saint Hajji Bayram.` (p. 278, folio 247) —
  a living votive practice at a named shrine, outside the archive.
- **An unnamed saint's tomb in Pakistan** — the plate caption, the one direct shrine reference in its
  chunk: `Woman in trance at a saint's tomb in Pakistan.`, credit `Photograph by Dr. Jan Marek, Prague`
  (p. 182, folio not stated). **Neither the tomb nor the town is named.**
- **A Sindhi saint, unnamed** — `Such a story is told of a Sindhi saint who fell in love with a
  beautiful boy and was thrown, by the boy's father, into the Indus, a millstone fastened around his
  neck; he was seen, later, calmly riding on the stone on the waves of the river.` (p. 238, folio 207).
  **No name, no place, no date, no source.**
- **A Hindu physician in Sind, unnamed** — `In Sind, a Hindu physician was converted when he examined a
  saint's urine.` (p. 240, folio 209). The saint is unnamed.

### B. Further Subcontinental figures, poets and movements

- **Khwāja Mīr Dard of Delhi** — `(1721-85)`, the subject of nine pages of chapter 8: `the lyrical poet
  of Delhi whose mystical poetry is sweeter than anything written in Urdu`, `the master of short, moving
  Urdu poems`, first initiate of the `tarlqa Muhammadiyya`, succeeded his father in 1758 and `never left
  Delhi`; he foreknew his death — `since his father had died at that age and God had promised him that
  he would resemble his father in every respect, he was sure that he would die very soon. In fact, he
  died shortly afterward, on 11 January 1785.`; ninety-nine names bestowed on him, on which Schimmel
  withholds judgement; `one of Aurangzeb's daughters offered a whole complex of buildings` to him
  (pp. 52–53, 67, 112, 176, 192, 205–206, 216, 244, 254, 294, 302, 309, 397, 401, 403–412, 434, 452,
  464, 466).
- **Muhammad Nāṣir ʿAndalīb** — `(1697-1758)`, Dard's father and sheikh, `a sayyid from Turkish lands
  and a descendant of BahJPuddm Naqsh-band` whose mother `claimed descent from cAbduDl-Qadir Gilani`;
  he `gave up the military service to become a dervish`; **his founding vision**, `blessed about 1734 by
  a vision of the Prophet's grandson Hasan ibn CAH, who had introduced him to the secret of the true
  Muhammadan Path (at-tariqa al-Muhammadiyya)`; **the father's knife** — `his father put the knife to
  his throat in one of the states of being drawn near to God in the beginning of his way, with the
  intention of slaughtering him for God`, and `"Whoever has not seen a dead person wandering around on
  earth may look at this son of mine who lives through me and who moves through me!"` (pp. 401, 405,
  408–409, 434, 452, 466).
- **Shāh Walīullāh of Delhi** — `(1703-62)`, spelled `Waliullah`, `Wallullah` and `Walmllah`, `the
  defender of the true faith, initiated into both Qadiriyya and Naqshbandiyya, a scholar and mystic
  whose works still await serious study in the West`; his blind-men-and-the-tree image, his
  ice-in-the-kettle symbol, his `Lamahdt` and `Sata^dt`, and his political impact surviving `in the
  school of Deoband to our day, though without the strong mystical bias of Shah Wallullah's original
  thought` (pp. 34, 165, 200, 204, 218, 400, 403, 497; index `(d. 1762), 3 n, 134, 169, 372, 373, 381,
  388; on qayyum, 369; Salavat, 169`, p. 527, folio 496).
- **Ahmad Sirhindī** — `Ahmad Faruqi Sirhindl`, `(1564-1624)`, `the great Naqshbandi reformer … who
  claimed for himself and three of his descendants the rank of the qayyum, which is even superior to
  that of the qutb`; `usually considered Ibn cArabi's antagonist` yet admitting his debt; `"My hand is a
  substitute for the hand of God"`; his lettrist prophetology of the two mīms; **he had studied in
  Sialkot** (pp. 234, 256, 294, 356, 373, 398–400, 404–405, 451; index p. 511, folio 480).
- **Pir Muhammad Zubayr** — `the fourth and last qayyum`, `(Ahmad's great-grandson)`, `died in 1740,
  only a few months after Nadir Shah's attack on Delhi`; Schimmel calls the pairing `a strange
  coincidence` (pp. 401, 405).
- **Muhammad Maʿṣūm** — Sirhindī's son, first of the three successors said to have held the rank of
  `qayyum` (p. 400, folio 369).
- **Mazhar Janjanan** — `the militant adversary of the Shiites, who was killed by a Shia at the age of
  eighty-one in i^Ss` [text-layer?: **year unreadable; only the leading `i` is recoverable, and no digit
  is guessed**]; `one of the two great Naqshbandi leaders of Delhi` alongside Shāh Walīullāh (pp. 403,
  412, 419).
- **Shāh Saʿdullāh Gulshan** — `"rose garden,"` the Naqshbandi master, ʿAndalīb's master, who died in
  ʿAndalīb's house `in 1728`; `a prolific poet in Persian who was fond of music and was apparently a
  lovable and soft-hearted person who played a definite role in the poetry- and music-loving society of
  Delhi`; his own master `cAbdu3l-Ahad Gul, "rose," a member of Ahmad Sirhindi's family` (pp. 402, 405).
- **Walī** — `the greatest lyrical poet of the South`, who `went to Delhi in 1707` and attached himself
  to Gulshan, after which `it became fashionable in Delhi to use the previously despised vernacular,
  rekhta or Urdu, for poetry` (pp. 401–402, folios 370–371).
- **Bedil** — `(d. 1721)`, `the lonely poet whose humble tomb in Delhi does not reflect the influence his
  poetry had upon Afghan and Central Asian, mainly Tajik, literature`; `though not a practicing member of
  any order`, `steeped in the traditions of mystical Islam`; `His favorite word is shikast, "broken,
  break"`; compares human life to the movements of ritual prayer (pp. 148, 184, 337, 402, 444).
- **Nāṣir ʿAlī Sirhindī** — `(d. 1697)`, Bedil's compatriot, `who later became a member of the
  Naqshbandiyya` (p. 402, folio 371).
- **Dārā Shikōh** — `(1615-59)`, `the heir apparent of the Mogul Empire`, `the first-born son of Shah
  Jihan and Mumtaz Mahal`, `Widely read in classical works of Sufism, including Ruzbihan Baqli's
  writings`; works `Hasanat al-'arifin`, `Sakinat al-auliyff`, `Safinat al-auliyff`,
  `Risdla-yi haqnuma` and the Upanishad translation `Sirr-i akbar, "The Greatest Mystery,"`; his
  disputations with the Hindu sage **Bābā Lāl Dās**; `Dara was eventually betrayed to Aurangzeb, who
  had him executed in 1659` (pp. 204, 315, 391–394, 415; index p. 514, folio 483).
- **Jihanara** — `Dara's and Aurangzeb's eldest sister`, `also an outstanding mystic and a renowned
  author of mystical works`, who `together with her luckless brother Dara Shikoh, joined the Qadiriyya
  and was highly praised by her master Molla Shah` (pp. 394, 464).
- **Sarmad** — `a highly intellectual Persian Jew`, `the Jewish convert to Sufism (executed 1661) in
  Mogul India`, who `came as a merchant to India, fell in love with a Hindu boy in Thatta, became a
  dervish, and eventually joined Dara's circle`, `walked around stark naked`, `one of the outstanding
  masters of the Persian quatrain`, who `followed the tradition of Hallaj, longing for execution`; his
  Satan quatrain, `Go, learn the method of servantship from Satan: / Choose one qibla and do not
  prostrate yourself before anything else.` (`AP 238`) (pp. 226, 393–394, 426, 479). **Not Sachal
  Sarmast.**
- **Akbar** — `(1556-1605)`, who `tried to establish a religious eclecticism in which the best elements
  of all religions known to him Would be contained`, the `dln-i ildhi`; `It was in the house of a
  Chishti saint that Akbar's heir apparent Salim, whose throne name was Jihangir, was born.`, for which
  Akbar `erected a wonderful Sufi dargah` (pp. 386, 390–391).
- **Jihāngīr** — born `Salim`, reign `1605—27`, who `adorned Ajmer, Mucinuddm Chishti's city, with
  beautiful buildings of white marble`; his wife `Nur Jihan` `herself Shia` (pp. 391, 398).
- **Bābur** — `from the dynasty of Timur`, who established Mogul rule `with the decisive Battle of
  Panipat in 1526`, translated the `Risdla-yi walidiyya` of `cUbaydullah Ahrar` into Turkish and
  `in one of his poems called himself "the servant of the dervishes"` (pp. 389–390, 323).
- **Aurangzeb ʿĀlamgīr** — `the last great ruler of the Mogul Empire (1659—1707)`, whose death year
  `marks the end of the glory of the Moguls`; `Aurangzeb shed tears when listening to a beautiful
  recitation of the Mathnawi` (pp. 358, 394, 401, 411, 419).
- **Shah Jihan, Mumtaz Mahal and the Taj Mahal** — `whose monumental tomb, the Taj Mahal, symbolizes the
  ruler's deep love for the mother of his fourteen children`; Aurangzeb `took advantage of Shah Jihan's
  illness to imprison his father` (pp. 392, 394).
- **Shāh ʿĀlam II, pen name Aftab** — the ruler who `sometimes attended` Dard's samāʿ meetings (p. 411,
  folio 380).
- **Mīr ʿAlī Shīr Qāniʿ** — `(d. 1789)`, `who was to become the leading historian of Lower Sind, left
  Persian works, mostly still unexamined, that are mines of information about the mystical and cultural
  life of his homeland`; his `Makllname` [text-layer?: Maklīnāme — **unresolved**], `Tuhfat al-kiram` and
  `Maqalat ash-shucara` (pp. 415, 418, 429, 444, 489–490).
- **Qāḍī Qādan** — `A prominent master was Qadi Qadan (d. 1546), of whose work only a few verses have
  been preserved; they distinctly show, however, all the peculiarities of later poetry.` (p. 420,
  folio 389). **The index prints a different year — `Qadl Qadan (d. 1551), 389` (p. 523, folio `4Q2`
  [text-layer?: 492]). Both are kept.**
- **Shāh ʿInāt / Mian Shah ʿInat** — `a seventeenth-century Sindhi poet whose poems are closely related
  to Latif's poetry` (p. 421, folio 390, footnote 73); indexed `lnat, Mian Shah (d. early i8th century),`
  / `39°` (p. 517, folio 486); his `Miyen Shah ^Indt jo kaldm`, ed. Nabi Bakhsh Baloch (p. 487,
  folio 456). **Deliberately kept apart from Shāh ʿInāyat Shahīd of Jhok** — see `## REVIEW (worker A)`.
- **Jaman Charan** — `The author … lived in the eighteenth century`, composed one of the first
  "geographical" poems in Sindhi in honour of ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Gīlānī; `His poem was often imitated.`
  (p. 417, folio 386, footnote 65).
- **Pīr Pagāro and the *hurr*** — `the activities of the hurr, the "Free," the loyal and absolutely
  obedient disciples of the Plr Pagaro who were gathered first to support Ahmad Brelwi's struggle against
  the Sikh (1829-31) and later actively joined in the struggle against the British by acts that cannot be
  called mystical or even religious.`; `hurr even played a minor role in the Indian-Pakistani war in
  1971.` (pp. 427–428; index `Pir Pagaro (mystical leader in Sind), 396`, p. 538, folio 507;
  `Hurr (militant group in Sind), 396, 39` — **the locator is cut off at the right margin** — p. 533).
- **Dīwān Gīdūmal and Nādir Shāh** — `Did not Diwan Gidumal, the minister, give the invading Nadir Shah
  (1739) the most precious treasure Sind had to offer, some of the dust of its saints?` (p. 427,
  folio 306 as printed — rule gives 396). **Put by Schimmel as a rhetorical question, not a verified
  event.**
- **Sayyid Ahmad Brelwi and Shāh Ismāʿīl Shahīd** — `both known for their brave fight against the Sikh,
  who had occupied the whole of the Punjab and part of the northwestern frontier`; Brelwī `had taken
  pledge from the classical Sufi orders and also in the Muhammadan way as founded by Nasir Muhammad
  cAndalib` (pp. 403, 434; index `Ahmad Brelwl, Sayyid (d. 1831), 227, 372,` / `396. 4°3`, p. 511;
  `Ismacil Shahid, Shah (d. 1831), 372`, p. 518).
- **Khushhāl Khān Khattak** — `(d. 1689)`, `the great warrior poet of the Pathans`, who `sometimes wrote
  lovely verses of mystical flavor`, quoted for `The true men of God are those / who already in this
  world / read the script of the heart / from the tablet of the forehead` (pp. 431, 445; index
  `(d. 1689), 400, 414`, p. 519).
- **ʿAbduʾl-Qādir Khattak** — who `translated some of the works of Sacdl and Jam! into his mother
  tongue`, after which `The Persianization of Pashto poetry became even more conspicuous` (p. 431,
  folio 400).
- **Mirza Khan Ansari** — `one of the leading mystical poets of the later Aurangzeb period in the
  beginning of the eighteenth century`, `a descendant of Bayezid Ansari`, who `used the slharfi
  [text-layer?: si-ḥarfī], "Golden Alphabet," to teach his theories` (pp. 429–430, folios 398–399).
- **Khwāja Muhammad Bangash** — `a remarkable poet tried to express mystical teachings in Pashto … like
  cAbdu3r-Rahman a member of the Chishti order`, who `alluded to the dhikr and the breath regulation
  that is so important for the advanced Sufi` (p. 432, folio 401).
- **Ahmad Shah Abdali Durrani** — `The other Afghan ruler known as a poet`, `The successor of Nadir Shah
  of Iran`, who `invaded northwestern India several times, called in to help against the Sikhs and
  Mahrattas`; Walīullāh was `instrumental in inviting` him (pp. 403, 431).
- **Hāshim Shāh of Amritsar** — Bulleh Shah's `younger contemporary`, who also used Hīr–Rāñjhā for
  mystical meditation (p. 419, folio 388).
- **Mohan Singh Diwana** — introduced as `the best authority` on Panjabi mysticism, the sentence cut by
  a chunk boundary (pp. 419, 487; index `Mohan Singh Diwana, 388-89`, p. 521).
- **ʿAbduʾl-Quddūs Gangohī** — `(d. 1538)`, `a leading saint and prolific writer`, `an outstanding
  example of Sufism tinged by the theories of the wahdat al-wujud`, who nevertheless `strictly observed
  the Muslim law`; his `Rushdname, in which Hindi poetry is inserted and which seems to be influenced by
  Hindu thought, still deserves careful study` (p. 388, folio 357).
- **ʿAbduʾl-Ḥaqq Dihlawī** — `(d. 1642)`, `the great traditionist`, `founder of the traditionist school
  in Indian Islam`, a Qadiri who wrote `a famous letter` against Sirhindī's claims (p. 394, folio 363).
- **Hamīduddīn Nagorī** — `(d. 1274)`, `the only book written by an Indian Muslim` in Barani's list, the
  `Lawdmf`; `noted for his poverty and his vegetarianism`; his grandson `compiled his ancestor's
  sentences (Surur as-sudur)` (pp. 379, 387, 389).
- **Jamāluddīn Hānswī** — `the Chishti saint of the late thirteenth century`, Farīd's disciple, `wrote
  moving mystical songs in Persian`, credited with the classification `talib al-maula, talib al-akhira,
  and talib ad-dunya`, which `was used by almost every Indo-Muslim mystic.` (pp. 244, 379).
- **Chirāgh-i Dihlī** — `Shaykh Nasir al-Din Mahmud Chiragh-i Dihli (d. 1356)`, `the "Lamp of Dehli"`,
  whose discourses (`Khayr al-majalis`) are a fourteenth-century *malfūẓāt* (pp. 19, 382, 387).
- **Mīr Khurd** — disciple of Nizāmuddīn, `forced by Muhammad Tughluq to leave Delhi for the Deccan in
  1327`, wrote the `Siyar al-auliyff` as expiation for deserting his master's sanctuary; `still the most
  trustworthy account of the Chishti silsila and of medieval Chishti khdnqdh life` (p. 387, folio 356).
- **Amīr Khosrau** — `(1254-1325)`, buried close to Nizāmuddīn's tomb in Delhi, `the best-known poet of
  the early Muslim period in India`, `founder of the Indo-Muslim musical tradition`, styled
  `"the parrot of India"` and `"God's Turk"` (p. 381, folio 350).
- **Ḥasan Sijzī Dihlawī** — `(d. 1328)`, the disciple who collected Nizāmuddīn's sayings (pp. 381, 387).
- **Bū ʿAlī Qalandar Panipati** — `(d. 1323)`, whose poem in honour of the Prophet was `the first of the
  many eulogies for the Prophet written in the Subcontinent`; wrote `remarkable letters to the Delhi
  kings criticizing their way of life`; in the fourteenth century greeted the new-born Prophet with
  `Welcome, o Nightingale of the Ancient Garden!` (`AP 109`) (pp. 248, 381–382; the index's
  `Abu CA1I Qalandar, Shah` at p. 523, folio `4Q2`, and p. 525, folio 494).
- **Barani** — `the Indo-Muslim historiographer of the early fourteenth century`, the source for the
  devotional book trade of Delhi (p. 379, folio 348).
- **Jamālī Kanboh** — `(d. 1535)`, `Iskandar Lodi's court poet`, quoted for a couplet on Moses and the
  Prophet (`AP 158`) `As Jamali Kanboh put it in fifteenth-century India` (pp. 252, 388).
- **Shaikh Ashraf Jahangir**, his disciple `Qadi Rabfaddm` [text-layer?: **name uncertain, not
  expanded**] `by name and an inhabitant of Awadh`, and `Kamal the Jogi` — the fifteenth-century Indian
  **cat story**: the cat listens to the discourse and `fell down unconscious`, rubs the shaikh's feet,
  thereafter counts arriving travellers by mewing, exposes a concealed `dahriyya [materialist]` who then
  says `"Today I accept Islam . . . ."`, and finally dies in the boiling pot to save the dervishes from a
  black snake; `The story itself goes back to earlier sources (cf. N i48)` [text-layer?: N 148]
  (pp. 241–242, folios 210–211; index `Kamal the Jogi, 210`, p. 519).
- **Muhammad of Jaunpur and the Mahdawiyya** — `the chiliastic movement` whose founder `at the beginning
  of the sixteenth century had declared himself to be the promised Mahdi` (p. 390, folio 359).
- **Muhammad Ghauthī** — `(d. after 1633)`, another Shattari, author of a voluminous work on `the
  Indo-Muslim, especially Gujrati, saints that contains biographies of 575 Sufis` (p. 386, folio 355).
- **Mīrān Ḥusain Shāh of Bijapur** — `(d. 1669)`, who `translated the book into Dakhni Urdu, an idiom
  that was just beginning to emerge as a literary language` (p. 327, folio 296; index p. 521).
- **Ghālib** — `(d. 1869)`, `usually labelled as a free thinker or "half-Muslim" (according to his own
  joking words)`, `deeply steeped in the traditions of the Delhi school of Sufism`, `not a mystic but …
  deeply influenced by the mystical traditions of the Subcontinent`; the gallows couplet, the
  `alif-i sayqal`, and the paper-shirt complaint opening his Urdu *Dīwān* (pp. 107, 255, 325, 437–438,
  446, 450).
- **Sir Sayyid Ahmad Khan** — `the reformer of Indian Islam, d. 1898`, likewise `deeply steeped in the
  traditions of the Delhi school of Sufism` (p. 437, folio 406).
- **Momin** — `(1801-51)`, Urdu poet, `related through marriage with Mir Dard's family`, who sided with
  the two reformers (p. 403, folio 372).
- **Maulana Ilyas and the faith movement** — `An interesting example is the faith movement in India,
  founded by Maulana Ilyas in the igaos [text-layer?: 1920s], which combines strict fundamentalism with
  mystical practices.` (p. 436, folio 405; index `Ilyas, Maulana (2Oth century), 405`, p. 517).
- **Hajji Muhammad of Bengal** — `a mystic called Hajji Muhammad described, in his Nurnama, "The Book of
  Light," the problems of wahdat al-wujud and wahdat ash-shuhud and the different types of light
  mysticism.`, dated by the book `as early as the seventeenth century` (p. 432, folio 401).
- **Kabir** — named twice only: `syncretistic movements, like that of Kabir in the fifteenth century`
  (p. 389, folio 358), and the proverb `"When the fish climbs on the poplar tree"` `also found in the
  mystical poetry of Kabir in India` (p. 365, folio 334).
- **Sayyid Sultan** — author of `the Ibllsname` [text-layer?: Iblīsnāme]`, a Bengali Muslim poem of the
  early seventeenth century`, in which `the angels were ordered to honor Iblis even after God had cursed
  him, since he had been their teacher` (p. 224, folio 193).
- **Sayyid Muhammad Jiw Jan** — of `the earliest Urdu-writing Sufi poets—like Sayyid Muhammad Jiw Jan in
  the seventeenth century—knew this bridal symbolism` (p. 465, folio 434).
- **Sadiquain** — `the young Pakistani artist Sadiquain`, `a recent painting`, part of `his new
  calligraphic representation of the Koran`, writing `kun fayakun—"Re\` [text-layer?: Be!] `and it
  becomes"` `in the shape of a spiral nebula of great spiritual power` (p. 456, folio 425).
- **Fayzī, Abūʾl-Fażl and Badāʾūnī** — Akbar's `two most faithful friends`, `the sons of a mystic
  connected with the Mahdi movement`, `disliked by the orthodox because of their "heretical" views`; and
  Badāʾūnī, whose `chronicle reflects the unalloyed hatred of the orthodox theologian` who was `forced to
  translate into Persian "the books of the infidels," i.e., the Sanskrit epics` (pp. 390, 398, 438).
- **Khwaja Baqi billah** — `a sober but inspiring teacher` through whom the Naqshbandiyya `gained a firm
  footing shortly before 1600` in India (p. 398, folio 367).
- **Burhanpur** — a group of Sindhi mystics migrated there in the sixteenth century and `founded a new
  branch of the Qadiriyya tariqa` (p. 415, folio 384).

### C. Founders and eponyms of the orders

- **ʿAbduʾl-Qādir al-Gīlānī** — `(1088-1166)`, `from the Caspian Sea, probably the most popular saint in
  the Islamic world, whose name is surrounded by innumerable legends that scarcely fit the image of the
  stern, sober representative of contrition and mystical fear`; `a Hanbalite preacher in Baghdad`;
  received the khirqa from `al-Mukharrimi, the builder of the first Hanbalite madrasa, which his
  disciple inherited from him`; **`"Abdu'l-Qadir's tomb in Baghdad is still a place of pilgrimage for
  pious Muslims, mainly from the Indo-Pakistani Subcontinent, where the order was introduced in the late
  fourteenth century.`**; `He is called Muhyi ud-dln, "the reviver of religion."`, `the ghauth-i cfzam`
  and `the pir-i dast-glr`; `he had forty-nine sons!`; `Old Sindhi songs describe how his spiritual
  realm extends from Istanbul to Delhi`; `Proper names like Ghauth Bakhsh, "gift of the help," in Sind
  and Balochistan point to his popularity.`; `in folk piety Gilani has become the master of the jinn,
  and many haunted caves and sacred places in the Maghreb are devoted to his cult`; `He has also had a
  decisive influence in the Islamization of West Africa.`; Schimmel's own open question — `A
  satisfactory explanation of the transition from the sober Hanbalite preacher … to the prototype of
  saintliness venerated all over the Muslim world is still lacking.`; **legends**: he washed away the
  text of a philosophy book (`N 517`), the months appeared to him as youths (`N 512`), the
  `Muhyi ud-din` legend (`N 519`), and `"My foot is on the neck of every saint"` (`N 527`); his
  anniversary placed at `Rabi^ ath-tham: n` [text-layer?: Rabīʿ ath-thānī: 11] (pp. 30, 49, 67, 120,
  190, 239, 277–279, 395, 417, 461, 534, 536, 540).
- **Abū Najīb as-Suhrawardī** — `cAbduDl-Qahir Abu Najlb as-Suhrawardl (d. n68)` [text-layer?: 1168 —
  `n` = `11`; **flagged, not treated as certain**]`, the founder of the Suhrawardiyya, was a disciple of
  Ahmad Ghazzali, Imam Ghazzali's younger brother.`; author of `Addb al-murldin`, `"The Manners of the
  Adepts," a classic that has been translated into the different Islamic languages and has often been
  imitated` (pp. 153, 275–278, 283).
- **Abū Ḥafṣ ʿUmar as-Suhrawardī** — `Shihabuddin Abu Hafs c Umar as-Suhrawardi (1145-1234)`, author of
  `'Awdrif al-ma^drif`, `one of the standard works taught in Indian madrasas in courses on Sufism`;
  `It certainly enhanced the influence of the Suhrawardiyya order, which soon spread into India.` —
  **the whole of what the book says about the order reaching India: no Multan, no Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā,
  no date, no route** (not stated); `shaykh ash-shuyukh, the official Sufi master of Baghdad, under the
  caliph an-Nasir`; his embassies to Egypt, Syria and Konya; `his friendly relations with the ruling
  classes also determined the attitude of his followers in India in later centuries`; blessed by a saint
  who `made me forget all he had studied` (`N 515`) (pp. 48, 153, 213, 276, 383).
- **Ahmad ar-Rifāʿī and the Rifāʿiyya** — `founder of an order that appears more eccentric than the
  Qadiriyya—the Rifaciyya dervishes, known as the Howling Dervishes because of their loud dhikr.`; Jāmī
  refuses their feats — `"But this is something the sheikh did not know, nor did his pious companions—we
  seek refuge from Satan with God!"` (`N 532`); a khalīfa's investiture in the Rifāʿī ṭarīqa in Istanbul
  `in the early part of this century` described by Samiha Ayverdi (pp. 207, 240, 267, 279–280).
  **No dates for Rifāʿī** — (not stated).
- **Ahmad al-Badawī and the Badawiyya of Ṭanṭā** — `(d. layS)` — **the death year prints as four
  characters of which the measured table supports only `l`=1, `a`=2 and `S`=8; the third is not in the
  table and is not guessed** [text-layer?: 1 2 ? 8]; his fraternity `drew most of its adherents from the
  rural population of Egypt`; `The wife of Sultan Khushqadam was buried, in 1466, covered with the red
  flag of the Badawiyya` (p. 280, folio 249).
- **Ahmad ad-Dasūqī** — named only as the founder of `an order founded by Ahmad's contemporary Ahmad
  ad-Dasuqi in the same country`, which likewise `never crossed the borders of its homeland` (p. 280,
  folio 249).
- **Abūʾl-Ḥasan ash-Shādhilī and the Shādhiliyya** — a disciple of `cAbduVSalam ibn Mashish`, through
  whom `the tradition goes back to Abu Madyan, the patron saint of Tlemcen (d. 1126)`; `went from Spain,
  via Tunis, to Alexandria, where he settled and eventually died in 1258, the year of the destruction of
  Baghdad and the end of the Abbasid caliphate`; his `Hizb al-bahr` `one of the most popular devotional
  texts`, used by Ibn Baṭṭūṭa at sea `apparently with success`; his dream vision of the Prophet
  extolling himself with Ghazzālī (`N 373`) (pp. 122, 128, 209, 281–283).
- **Abūʾl-ʿAbbās al-Mursī and Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh** — `When AbuDl-Hasan ash-Shadhili died, AbuDl-cAbbas
  al-Mursi became his khalifa (A. 1287)` — **the `A.` before `1287` is flagged, not resolved** — `he was
  succeeded by Tajuddin Ibn 'Ata' Allah (d. 1309)`, `the great teacher of the Shadhiliyya order in Egypt
  (d. igog)` [text-layer?: **the year is unrecovered**], who `provided the Shadhiliyya with their
  classical literature, the Latcfif al-minan and, even more, the Hikam, a collection of 262 short
  sayings` (pp. 195, 209, 282).
- **Ibn ʿAbbād of Ronda** — `born in Ronda in Spain in 1332`, studied in Tlemcen and Fez, meditated
  under Ibn ʿĀshir in Salé, `became a preacher and imam in the famous Qara-wiyin madrasa in Fez. There he
  died in 1390.`; his `Shark al-hikam` made Ibn ʿAṭāʾ Allāh's work known in the west; his letters number
  `fifty-four in all` (pp. 283–284).
- **Najmuddīn Kubrā and the Kubrāwiyya** — `AbuDl-Jannab Ahmad, surnamed at-tdmmat al-kubrd, "the
  greatest affliction"`; `He was born in 1145 in Khiva in Central Asia … returned to Khwarizm about 1185,
  and was killed during the Mongol invasion in 1220. His tomb is located in Urgenj.` — **no cult, no
  ʿurs and no later history of that tomb** (not stated); `Kubra implanted his mystical theories in the
  Central Asian areas, from which they spread to Turkey and India.`; the `shdhid al-ghaib` and the
  colour-visions of the Path (pp. 183, 285–289, 310).
- **Najmuddīn Dāya Rāzī** — `left Khwarizm before the Mongols invaded it and migrated to Anatolia; in
  Sivas he wrote his Mirsdd ul-^ibdd, which he dedicated to the Seljukid ruler of Konya, ''Ala'uddin
  Kaykobad`; `It was frequently used in India` (pp. 288, 310, 343).
- **ʿAlāʾuddaula Simnānī** — `(d. 1336)`, `an excellent mystical psychologist and philosopher`, who
  `question[ed] the truth of Ibn cArabi's concept of wahdat al-wujud` and `thus became a favorite saint
  of the later Naqshbandiyya`; on the correct dhikr posture (`CL i is` — **damaged siglum**) (pp. 201,
  234–235, 288, 399, 405, 452).
- **Bahāʾuddīn Naqshband** — `(d. 1390)`, eponym of the order, `belonged to the Central Asian tradition`,
  `blessed with an immediate spiritual succession from Ghijduwam`; `His main activities were first
  connected with Bukhara, of which he became the patron saint` (pp. 395–396, 405).
- **Yūsuf Hamadhānī** — `(d. 1140)`, the lineage's head, `"the imam of his time, the one who knew the
  secrets of the soul, who saw the work" (MT 219)`; his affiliation `went back to Kharaqani and Bayezid
  Bistami`; `it was Hamadhani who encouraged ^bdu^l-Qadir Gilani to preach in public`, `According to the
  tradition` (pp. 395, folio 364).
- **ʿAbduʾl-Khāliq Ghijduwānī** — `(d. 1220)`, Hamadhānī's `most successful khalifa`, teaching
  `primarily in Transoxania`; his way became the `tanqa-yi Khwdjagdn`, `"the way of the Khojas, or
  teachers"`; credited with the eight principles (p. 395, folio 364).
- **Ahmad Yasawī and the Yasawiyya** — `(d. 1166)`, `a disciple of Yusuf Hamadhani, thus springing from
  the same spiritual root as the Naqshbandiyya`; his `Hikam` are `the first known sentences of mystical
  wisdom written in the Turkic language of the area of Yassi in present Uzbekistan`; the `dhikr-i arra`,
  the `"sawing dhikr,"` `began, allegedly` with him (pp. 207, 360, 395).
- **Khwāja Ahrār / ʿUbaydullāh Ahrār** — `(1404-90)`, under whom `Central Asia was virtually dominated
  by the Naqshbandiyya`; relations `with the Timurid prince Abu Sacid, as well as with the Shibanid
  Uzbegs`; `Yunus Khan Moghul, Babur's maternal uncle, belonged to the order` (pp. 390, 396).
- **Abū Saʿīd ibn Abīʾl-Khayr** — `The first Sufi to draw up a preliminary monastic rule for his
  disciples`, `born in 967 in Mayhana (Mihna) in Khurasan, a place peopled with Sufi saints`; `Abu Sacid
  passed away in 1049.`; **`Abu Sard's tomb was destroyed when the wild Ghuzz tribes conquered Mayhana
  about 1180.`** — no rebuilding, no cult, no later history (not stated); `at the legendary age of forty
  he reached perfect illumination`; deflated miracles — `"Frogs and waterfowl do that as well!"`,
  `"So do birds and insects!"`, `"Satan goes in one moment from the East to the West!"` (`NS 67`) — and
  `"whosoever belongs entirely to the Giver (karlm), all his acts are karamat, 'gifts of grace' "`
  (`NS 66`); the Kaaba said to circumambulate above his head; `It is said that` on his deathbed he
  bestowed his khirqa on Ahmad-i Jām Zandapīl, `who was just about to be born`; `We can be quite sure
  that none of the quatrains formerly attributed to him are actually his` (pp. 119, 147, 243, 272–275).
- **Ahmad-i Jām Zandapīl** — `a Persian saint who was the opposite of Abu Sacld in almost every respect:
  stern, proud of his mystical power, drawing people to repentance, not to love, and often using his
  spiritual strength for revenge and punishment.`; `had forty-two children (N 357)`; **the year pair
  `1040-1141` appears only inside Moayyad's book title, not as Schimmel's statement** (pp. 67, 275, 487).
- **Ḥajjī Bektash and the Bektashiyya** — `the alleged founder of the order`, said to have come to
  Anatolia in the thirteenth century from Khurasan; **his dates are derived by letter-numerology and the
  book says so**: `The date of his birth is given—typical of Bektashi thought—by the numerical value of
  the word murawwat, "virtue," which is 648/1247, and his death is dated, by a similar device, in
  738/1338.`; `the vilayetnames, books on saintliness that favor miraculous evidence, speak of some of
  the miracles he performed` — **the miracles themselves are not described** (pp. 369–370).
- **Balim Sultan** — `the order claims as its second master a certain Balim Sultan, who was born about
  1500 in Rumeli`; the order `is still very much alive today in Albania, where it was introduced prior to
  the seventeenth century` (p. 370, folio 339).
- **Faḍlullāh Astarābādī and the Ḥurūfīs** — `Fadlullah of Asterabad in Khurasan`, founder of the Hurufi
  letter-speculation, `executed for his heretical ideas in 1398`; taught that Adam was given nine
  letters, Abraham fourteen, Muhammad twenty-eight, and himself 32 (pp. 370, 443).
- **The Kāzarūniyya** — founded by Ibn Khafīf's `spiritual pupil Kazarum (d. 1035)` [text-layer?:
  al-Kāzarūnī — **the final `-ī` is absent in both occurrences and is not reconstructed**], `who was
  noted for his social activities` (pp. 189, 195, 260, 487).
- **The Shattariyya** — `a suborder emerging in the sixteenth century`, `which is restricted to India and
  Indonesia` (p. 386, folio 355; index `Shattariyya, 355`, p. 525).
- **Orders indexed but not otherwise described** — `Naqshbandi`/`Naqshbandiyya`, `Qadiriyya`,
  `Suhrawardiyya`, `Shadhiliyya`, `Mevleviyya`, `Kubrawiyya`, `Rifa c iyya`, `Malamatiyya`, `Roshaniyya`,
  `Yasawiyya`, `Khalvetiyya`, `Mahdawiyya`, `Nicmatullahi order`, `Nurbakhshiyya`, `Sanusiyya`,
  `Tijaniyya`, `Salimiyya`, and the **Heddawa**, `a beggars' order in Morocco, which claims descent from
  the Qadiriyya and practices absolute poverty` (pp. 241, 272, 519–526).

### D. The formative period: Iraq, Khurasan, Egypt and Iran

- **Ḥasan al-Baṣrī** — `(d. 728)`, `the patriarch of Muslim mysticism` and `the name that stands for the
  early ascetic, antigovernmental attitude`; `"O son of Adam, you will die alone and enter the tomb alone
  and be resurrected alone, and it is with you alone that the reckoning will be made!"` and `"Be with
  this world as if you had never been there, and with the Otherworld as if you would never leave it."`;
  `"It was as if Hellfire had been created exclusively for him and for c Umar II," says one historian` —
  **the historian is unnamed** (pp. 61–62, 143–160).
- **Rābiʿa al-ʿAdawiyya** — `the great woman saint of the eighth century`, `the saint of Basra`, `a slave
  girl, set free by her master`, who `died only a few years before him (801)`; `generally regarded as the
  person who introduced the element of selfless love into the austere teachings of the early ascetics and
  gave Sufism the hue of true mysticism`; `She did not marry, nor did she give the Prophet a special place
  in her piety.`; `seems to have been the first Sufi to speak about the jealous God`; **legends**: the
  torch and the ewer — `"I want to throw fire into Paradise and pour water into Hell so that these two
  veils disappear"`, `The most famous story illustrative of the singlemindedness of her devotion`; she
  `would shut the windows in spring without looking at the flowers`; and `complete abstinence from animal
  products so that the animals no longer fled from her` (`T i: 64`); `the first figure from the history of
  Sufism to be introduced into European literature`, brought to Europe `by Joinville, the chancellor of
  Louis IX, in the late thirteenth century` (pp. 39, 69–71, 76, 84, 109, 389, 457, 495).
- **Dhuʾn-Nūn the Egyptian** — `(d. 859)`, `Thauban ibn Ibrahim, surnamed Dhu'n-Nun, "he with the fish,"
  was born of Nubian parents in Ikhmim in Upper Egypt`; imprisoned in the Muʿtazilite persecution and
  freed by the caliph Mutawakkil; `accused of being a philosopher and an alchemist`; `formulated for the
  first time a theory of mcfrifa`; **legends**: `Many miracles are ascribed to him, and in strange legends
  he figures as a kind of magician whom men and jinn obeyed`, and `when he died, legend asserts, "it was
  written on his forehead: 'This is the friend of God, he died in love of God, slain by God' " (H 100)`;
  the Greatest Name exchange — `"Show me the smallest one!"—and scolded him` — and the woman at the sea
  shore, `'What is the end of love?' And she answered: 'O simpleton, love has no end.'` (`T i: 123`)
  (pp. 37, 46, 73–78, 131, 162, 187, 193, 292, 447).
- **Bāyezīd Bisṭāmī** — `(d. 874)`, `this ascetic from the little place known as Bistam (Bastam) in
  northwestern Iran`, `A strange figure of dark fire`, `almost completely transformed into a kind of Sufi
  symbol`; `His name occurs, in poetry, more frequently than that of any other mystic, with the exception
  of "Mansur" Hallaj`; `"Bayezld of his time" has become an honorary epithet`; `the first to describe the
  mystical experience in terms of the image of the mfraj`; **legends**: the flight beyond space, the
  twelve years as `the blacksmith of his self until he made of himself a pure mirror (T 1:139)`, the
  disciples whose knives wounded themselves (`M 4:2102-40`), the faint at the call to prayer, and God's
  word `"Everybody wants something from Me, only Bayezld wants Me Myself"` (`T 1:152`); `the saints are
  God's brides whom only the close relatives can behold` (`MC 1:43`) (pp. 78–81, 120, 136, 176, 192, 234,
  237, 250, 292, 346, 458).
- **Junayd** — `Abu'l-Qasim al-Junayd`, `(d. 910)`, `the undisputed leader of the Iraqian school of
  mysticism`, `the pivot in the history of early Sufism` and `the pivotal figure in most silsilas`; `born
  in Niha-wand, he settled in Baghdad and studied law according to the Shafi-ite rite. In Sufism he was
  educated by his uncle Sari as-Saqatl`; `"Love between two is not right until the one addresses the
  other, 'O Thou I'"` (`T 2:29`); `"Junayd's letters are a famous example of the cryptic density of early
  Sufi writing."`; he `had rejected Hallaj` and `foretold—according to the legends—an evil end for his
  former disciple` (pp. 45, 68, 78, 88–90, 97, 135, 141, 161–177, 242, 346, 454).
- **Shiblī** — `(d. 945)`, `the mystic of Baghdad whose paradoxes are famous in Sufi history`; `When he
  died in 945 at the age of eighty-six, he left behind a considerable number of sayings and paradoxes upon
  which following generations often pondered.`; `His strange behavior resulted from time to time in his
  confinement in an asylum; thus he was spared, according to his own statement, the fate of Hal-la]`;
  Junayd called him `"the crown of these people"` (`N 180`), others `held that he was not a proper
  interpreter of tauhid` (`N 145`); **legends**: the *munāqara* in the Tigris (`B 494`), `"fire of Hell
  will not touch me, and I can easily extinguish it"`, his rebuke at a funeral (`T 2:172`), and the rose
  he threw at Ḥallāj (pp. 47, 71, 99, 109–111, 167, 178, 243, 328, 443, 455).
- **Ḥallāj** — `Husayn ibn Mansur al-Hallaj`, `the "martyr of divine love,"` born `in the province of Fars
  in 858`, `On 26 March 922 he was put to death`, `hands and feet were cut off, and he was put on the
  cross or, more probably, on the gallows, then decapitated; his body was burned and the ashes thrown in
  the Tigris`; **`in 905, Hallaj took a boat to India` and `From Gujerat he wandered through Sind, the
  lower Indus valley`**; `The seeds he sowed there grew in later centuries in the mystical poetry of this
  province.`; `Hallaj is mentioned just as frequently in Sindhi poetry`; `The Pathans knew the name of the
  martyr of love (who is even mentioned in their proverbs), as did the Panjabis.`; `India had, indeed,
  been known as the country of magical practices— Hallaj even went there "in order to learn magic,"
  according to his detractors.`; `people still stood on the banks of the Tigris awaiting Hallaj's return`
  around the year 1000; his `Kitdb at-tawasln` `probably written during his imprisonment`, in `eight
  chapters, each of them called tdsin` (pp. 64, 91–108, 166–176, 225, 238, 246, 336, 351, 369, 385, 417,
  437, 443, 447–455).
- **Sarī as-Saqaṭī** — `(d. circa 867)`, `"the huckster"`, `Ma c rufs disciple`, `the first to discuss the
  various mystical states (ahwal)` and `apparently the first to define` mystical love as `"real mutual
  love between man and God"—a scandal to the orthodox`; his scrupulosity, `he was afraid that his nose
  might turn black because of his sins`; `"when God created the letters, the b prostrated itself"`
  (pp. 84, 162, 451).
- **Maʿrūf al-Karkhī** — `(d. 815)`, `a leading Sufi of the early ninth century in Baghdad`,
  `RabiVs younger contemporary`; `"When God loves His servant, He opens for him the door of actions … and
  closes the door of theological disputations."`; `Legends speak of his strong mystical power; his prayers
  were heard` (pp. 63, 84).
- **al-Ḥārith al-Muḥāsibī** — `(d. 857)`, `Born in Basra in 781`, `it is he who gave Sufism a highly
  developed technical language`, his surname `derived from muhasaba`; `Muhasibi claimed to have a nerve in
  his finger that would warn him whenever food was not perfectly clean legally (N 572)` (pp. 69, 85, 139,
  180, 448).
- **Sahl at-Tustarī** — `(d. 896)`, `praised by Junayd as "the proof of the Sufis" (QR 59)`, spent years
  in the Abbadan settlement and `was eventually exiled to Basra`, wrote `an extensive commentary on the
  Koran in which he discussed the fourfold meaning of each verse`; his disciple trained to unceasing
  dhikr whose `drops of blood which trickled to the ground bore the legend 'Allah! Allah! Allah!"`
  (`H 195`) (pp. 46, 87, 199–204, 230, 292, 448).
- **Ibn Sālim and the Salimiyya** — `Sahl's teaching was continued by his disciple Ibn Salim (d. 909);
  hence their school was known as the Salimiyya.` (p. 87, folio 56).
- **Nūrī** — `(d. 907)`, `the greatest representative of that pure love that had been introduced by
  Rabica`, held a heretic (`zindlq`) for calling himself a lover of God, author of `Maqdmat al-qulub`;
  **his death**: `enraptured by the recitation of a verse, he ran into a nearby reedbed where the reeds
  had just been cut; the razor-sharp edges of the stumps hurt his feet without his being aware of the
  pain; soon afterward he died from the wounds.`; `was afraid of lions and therefore stayed in the
  lion-infested forests along the Tigris to conquer his fear` (pp. 91–92, 168, 223, 242, 458).
- **Sumnūn the Lover** — `whose sobriquet was al-Muhibb, "the lover" (d. after 900)`, who `called himself
  "the Liar"`, tried and imprisoned with Nūrī; `the lamps in the mosque shattered when Sumnun began
  preaching about disinterested and selfless love, and birds killed themselves while listening to his
  heart-rending sermons`; `Later, Jam! related that men and women gave up their spirits during his
  preaching.` (pp. 91–93, 171, 529).
- **al-Ḥakīm at-Tirmidhī** — `Sahl's younger contemporary`, `surnamed al-Hakim, "the philosopher,"`,
  author of `Khatm al-auliya>`, `"The Seal of Saints"`; `Tirmidhi died early in the tenth century in
  Mecca, where he lived after the study of Shafiite law in Balkh and a prolonged stay in Iraq.`;
  distinguished `the wall sidq Allah and the wall minnat Allah` (`MM 16`) (pp. 86–88, 230–234).
- **Ibrāhīm ibn Adham** — `(d. circa 790)`, `he renounced the princely life in Balkh, the old Buddhist
  capital where he was born (a story embellished with echoes of the Buddha legend)`, `A romance was even
  composed about his adventures and was known particularly in the Malayan archipelago`; `is credited with
  making the first classification of the stages of zuhd`; `Ibn Adham was proud of the huge number of lice
  living in his coat`; the man on the palace roof searching for a lost camel, after which `Ibrahim repented
  and repudiated all his possessions.` (`Cf. M 6:829*1.`) (pp. 67–68, 140, 479).
- **Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ** — `a highwayman, albeit a magnanimous one, between the cities of Abiward and
  Sarakhs`, converted on hearing a Koranic verse; `He died in Mecca in 803.`; `"and when he died, sadness
  was taken away from the world" (Q 9)`; `he was seen smiling only once in thirty years—when his son died`,
  which he read as `a sign of divine grace` (pp. 66–67).
- **Bishr al-Ḥāfī** — `"the barefooted one"`, a disciple of Fuḍayl `who considered even shoes to be a
  "veil"`; `came from Merw`; `He settled in Baghdad, where he died, after a perfectly scrupulous life, in
  841.`; converted by finding a paper bearing God's name on the road (pp. 68–69).
- **Shaqīq al-Balkhī** — `(d. 809)`, `the former merchant`, `an expert on tawakkul`, `the first to discuss
  the "mystical states"`, `The first mystic to speak of the "alchemy of hunger"` (`W 216`) (pp. 69, 146,
  148).
- **Yaḥyā ibn Muʿādh ar-Rāzī** — `(d. 871)`, `The preacher from Rayy`, `he is the only one to be
  distinguished by the title al-wdciz, "the preacher."`; `lived for a while in Balkh, and died, in 871, in
  Nishapur, "and he spoke constantly about hope" (Q 16)`; `"When fate meets me with a ruse of affliction, I
  meet it with a ruse of prayer"` (`A 10:53`); credited with `one of the earliest known examples` of
  `"spiritual dancing verses"` (pp. 51, 82–83, 131, 187, 214).
- **Abū Bakr al-Kharrāz** — `(d. 899)`, `known in the West through Arberry's translation of his Kitdb
  as-sidq`; `Tradition credits him with having been the first to discuss the theory of fand … and baqa`;
  `he can be regarded as one of Junayd's masters`; Satan's dream-boast of `"love for the unbearded"`
  (`Q g` — **the numeral is unresolved**) (pp. 86, 181, 222, 230, 245, 321).
- **Abū Ṭālib al-Makkī** — `(d. 996 in Baghdad)`, `The author of the first comprehensive manual of Sufism`,
  the `Qut al-qulub, "The Food of the Hearts."`, `considered a follower of the Salimiyya school`; `knew
  nine aspects of love` (pp. 87, 116, 162–163).
- **ʿAmr al-Makkī** — `(d. 909)`, `probably the first to compose a systematic treatise on the degrees of
  love, intimacy, and proximity`; `has summed up some of the early Sufi ideas in a lovely myth` of hearts,
  spirits and consciences created seven thousand years apart (pp. 88, 223).
- **Ruwaym** — `(d. 915)`, `One of Junayd's friends in Baghdad`; `"Sufism is to sacrifice one's soul—but do
  not occupy yourself with the small-talk of the Sufis!"` (`X 90`); `Ansari admitted 150 years later that
  he "would prefer one hair of Ruwaym to a hundred of Junayd" (N 95)` (pp. 46, 90, 141).
- **Abū Nasr as-Sarrāj** — `from Tus in eastern Iran (d. 988)`, whose `Kitdb al-lumcf fft-tasawwuf`
  [text-layer?: **printed in four different damaged forms**] `is an excellent exposition of the doctrines of
  the Sufis`; `for a while a disciple of Ibn Khafif`; in ecstasy `put his face on the fireplace and
  prostrated himself before God in the midst of the fire without being hurt` (`N 283`) (pp. 80, 115).
- **al-Kalābādhī** — `(d. ca. 990)`, `who died in Bukhara about 990`, wrote `Kitdb at-ta'arruf` `in an
  effort to find a middle ground between orthodoxy and Sufism`; `A commentary of 222 Prophetic traditions
  is also ascribed to Kalabadhi.` (pp. 45, 116).
- **Sulamī** — `(d. 1021)`, author of `Tabaqdt as-sufiyya`, who `dealt not only with the biographies of the
  mystics, but also with the different strands of Sufism`, and `devoted a special treatise` to the
  Malāmatiyya (pp. 102, 116–117).
- **Abū Nuʿaym al-Iṣfahānī** — `(d. 1037)`, who `chose for his ten-volume work the more romantic title
  Hilyat al-auliya3, "The Ornament of the Saints."` (p. 116, folio 85).
- **ʿAbdullāh-i Anṣārī** — `(1006-89)`, `the leading mystic and hagiographer of Herat` and `"the patron
  saint of Herat"`; detained from the ḥajj by `the untimely death in 1030 of Mahmud of Ghazna`; changed by
  `his meeting with the distinguished mystic Kharaqani in 1034, shortly before the master died at the age
  of eighty`; persecuted after `The advent of the Seljuks in eastern Iran in 1041`; `The grand vizier
  Nizamulmulk even exiled him from Herat in 1066, but he was soon called back.`; `Eventually Ansari lost
  his sight and spent the last eight years of his life in darkness`; `He died in Herat on 8 March 1089.` —
  **the one full calendar date in its chunk**; works `Manazil as-sffirln` and `the Munajat, "Orisons"`
  (pp. 86, 90, 120–121, 152, 332).
- **Kharaqānī** — `This illiterate peasant, who could not pronounce Arabic correctly (N 336, 353), was a
  typical uwaysi, initiated not by a living master but by the powerful spirit of Bayezld Bistami`;
  **legends**: `the scent of Kharaqani reached Bayezid long before his spiritual disciple was born`
  (`M 4:1802-50`), the *ṭayy al-arḍ* between Kharaqan and Bistam (`T 2:201`), his oath not to give his soul
  to the angel of death (`T 2:212`), God's `"for We have loved thee already in the pre-eternity of
  eternities"` (`T 2:253`), and `riding on a lion and using a snake as a whip` as the reward for his
  patience under `his railing wife's constant pressures` (`M 6:2044-129`) (pp. 47, 62, 120–121, 136, 172,
  253, 292, 459).
- **Qushayrī** — `al-Qushayri (d. 1074)`, whose `Risala—written in 1046—is probably the most widely read
  summary of early Sufism`; reached through his father-in-law `Abu CA1I ad-Daqqaq`, who `classified prayer
  in the usual threefold way: "There are three degrees: to ask, to pray, to praise."` (`T 2:195`) (pp. 84,
  119, 189, 192–193, 490).
- **Ḥamdūn al-Qaṣṣār** — `one of the first among the Khurasanian Sufis to adopt this way of life`, to whom
  `Al-malama tark as-salama, "blame is to give up well-being"` is attributed, **but cited to `H 67`, i.e.
  reported by Hujwīrī** (p. 118, folio 87).
- **Ibn Khafīf of Shiraz** — `(d. 982, at about 100 years of age)`, `the Shirazi Sufi leader`, `the master
  of Shiraz`, `who collected traditions from more than thirty masters-yet the Hanbalite theologian Ibn
  al-Jauzi (d. 1200) calls him "not trustworthy."`; he `had visited [Ḥallāj] in prison and defended him,
  even to the extent of calling him an 'alim rabbani`; `N 600 mentions that the famous poet Sacdi (d. 1292)
  lived close to Ibn Khafif's tomb (which is now in a rather dark quarter of Shiraz, close to the charcoal
  sellers).` — **the one tomb-topography note in its chunk**; the dog's-head legend (`X 44`) and his
  mother's vision of the `laylat ul-qadr` (`X 205`) (pp. 90, 105, 114, 143, 201, 252, 260–261, 328, 461).
- **Niffarī** — `This Iraqian mystic, who died in 965, left writings, called Mawdqif and Mukhatabdt, that
  seem to have been studied carefully by later Sufis.`; `Even Ibn c Arabi may have been inspired by the
  daring ideas of this mystical thinker.`; `"unmasked the idolatry of the letter"` (`W 370`), the
  `hijdb al-mcfrifa` (`W 380`), `the greatest theologian of prayer in the tenth century` (pp. 111–112, 185,
  196, 315, 442, 448).
- **Abū Bakr al-Wāsiṭī** — `who came from Farghana, settled for a while in Baghdad, and then returned to
  Khurasan`; Anṣārī praises him as `"leader of tauhid"` and `"the master of the East in the science of
  subtle allusions"` (`N 175`) (p. 111, folio 80).
- **Ghazzālī** — born `in Tus, near present-day Meshed, in 1058`, taught by `al-Juwaini, surnamed the imam
  al-haramayn (d. 1083)`, appointed by Nizāmulmulk `professor at the Nizamiyya madrasa in Baghdad`, `after
  a breakdown in 1095, left his teaching position to enter the spiritual life`, `There he died in December
  1111`; the `Ihyff c ulum ad-din` `a comprehensive work of forty chapters`; `This teaching—a marriage
  between mysticism and law—has made Ghazzali the most influential theologian of medieval Islam.`; his
  `Al-munqidh min ad-dalal` `has even been compared to Augustine's Confessions` (pp. 86, 122–128, 134, 161,
  166, 187–188, 246, 325).
- **Ahmad Ghazzālī** — `(d. 1126)`, `the classical representative of Satan's rehabilitation, who dared to
  say: "Who does not learn tauhld from Satan, is an infidel"`, author of the `Sawanih, "Aphorisms on
  Love"`; his brother `is reported to have acknowledged his brother's superiority in the path of love`
  (pp. 226, 275, 322, 325–326).
- **ʿAynuʾl-Quḍāt Hamadhānī** — `(executed 1132)`, author of the `Tamhiddt`, `The first classic to be
  translated into Dakhni Urdu in the seventeenth century`, and `one of the favorite books` of `the Delhi
  Chishtl mystics in the late thirteenth century`; Jāmī on him — `"Only few people made such disclosures of
  realities and explanations of subtle points as he did, and miracles like killing and reviving became
  manifest in him"` (`N 414-15`) (pp. 325–327, 394, 401).
- **Rūzbihān Baqlī of Shiraz** — `(d. 1209)`, `who died at an advanced age in 1209 in his hometown of
  Shiraz`, `the mystic of Shiraz who stands in the Ibn Khaflf tradition`, whose commentaries are `the most
  valuable source for our understanding of large parts of Hallaj's theology`; `himself … a lover of samd*`
  who `even symbolized the state of mystical union as "a dance with God"`; the rose tradition — `It was
  Ruzbihan Baqli who highlighted the prophetic tradition according to which Muhammad declared the red rose
  to be the manifestation of God's glory` (`B 265`) — and `his vision of God is a vision of clouds of
  roses, the divine presence fulgent as a marvelous red rose.`; the theory of 360 saints (pp. 52, 105,
  110, 147, 155, 158, 213, 227, 233–234, 257, 325–332, 336, 338, 344, 449–451).

### E. Poets, theorists and later figures of the Persian, Arabic and Turkish worlds

- **Sanāʾī** — `Abul-Majd Majdud Sanal (d. ii3i)` [text-layer?: 1131], court poet of the later Ghaznawids
  in Ghazna who `visited several places in Khurasan, most importantly Balkh and Sarakhs` and then turned
  to mystical poetry; `Although there is a charming anecdote about his repentance, the true reason for this
  change of attitude is not clear.`; his *Ḥadīqat al-ḥaqīqa* `dedicated to Sultan Bahramshah`, the
  *Kārnāma-yi Balkh*, the *Sayr al-ʿibād*, the `"Lament of Satan"` (`SD 871`) and the `"Litany of the
  Birds"` (pp. 49, 81, 107, 175, 225–226, 247, 332–339, 447, 451, 454, 457). **The text layer's
  `Sanaa's` for `Sanāʾī's` reads as the name of a city and must not be taken as one.**
- **Farīduddīn ʿAṭṭār** — `Fariduddin was born in Nishapur and died there, most likely in 1220 at an old
  age.`, `sitting in his druggist's shop—as his surnam.` [text-layer?: surname — **right-margin
  truncation**] `c Attar indicates`; `had accepted Hallaj as his spiritual guide in a vision`;
  `Later mystical tradition includes him in the list of the martyrs of love, slain by the orthodox or, in
  his case, by the infidels because of his excessive divine love. Whether he was indeed killed by the
  invading Mongols is uncertain, but the story indicates the rank he was given`; `cAttar and Najmuddin
  Kubra both died about 1220`; **it is probably legend that he blessed the child Rūmī about 1219**
  (pp. 50, 105, 136, 165, 172, 187, 190, 291, 303–310, 334, 342, 352, 447). **Not Bābā Farīd.**
- **Jalāluddīn Rūmī** — `born in 1207 in the city of Balkh`, died `on 17 December 1273`; `the "country of
  the Romans" (Rum, hence his surname Rumi'), Anatolia`; his shrine in Konya and `on whose mausoleum in
  Kenya [text-layer?: Konya] the famous lines are written: Come back, come back, even if you have broken
  your repentance a thousand times.`; `his death is commemorated in December`; `the Mogul emperors
  (especially Akbar) were as fond of him as the simple villagers in Sind and Punjab`; `According to legend,
  Rumi attended seventeen parties at one time and wrote a poem at each one!` (pp. 48, 91, 141, 176, 189,
  236, 342–359, 445–447).
- **Shamsuddīn Tabrīzī** — `an overpowering person of strange behavior`, `This dervish, whose spiritual
  affiliation is as unknown as his family`; **his murder**, related with the chronicles cited: `One night
  they called him out of Jalaluddm's house … and, after stabbing him, they threw him into the nearby well.`;
  `Shams-i Tabriz became a legendary figure in India and was often counted among the martyrs of love.`
  (pp. 106, 315, 344, 351, 358). **Not Shāh Shams of Multan.**
- **Burhānuddīn Muḥaqqiq** — `Burhanuddin left Konya about 1240 and is buried in Kaisari, where his modest
  tomb is still venerated.` (p. 343, folio 312).
- **Bahāʾuddīn Walad, Sulṭān Walad, Ḥusāmuddīn Chelebi, Ṣalāḥuddīn Zarkūb** — Rūmī's father, `a noted
  theologian with mystical inclinations`, author of a `Macdrif`, `called to Konya in 1228`; his son
  (d. 1312), who `institutionalized` the Mevlevi order and used Turkish `very skillfully in his Dlwdn`;
