  Ḥusāmuddīn (d. 1284); and `the modest goldsmith`, whose daughter became Sultan Walad's wife (pp. 342–346,
  355, 360).
- **Ibn ʿArabī** — `ash-shaykh al-akbar, "the greatest master"`, `born in 1165 in Murcia, Spain, and was
  educated by two women saints, one of them Fatima of Cordova`; `In 1201 Ibn cArabi was inspired to perform
  the pilgrimage to Mecca`, where he met `a highly accomplished young Persian lady` and composed the
  `Tarjuman al-ashwaq`; `settled in Damascus, where he died in 1240`; `Al-futuhdt al-makkiyya, "Meccan
  Revelations," in 560 chapters` and `the Fusiis al-hikam`; `claimed to have received the khirqa from
  Khidr`; `is said to have experienced the vision of the highest divine essence in the shape of the word
  hu` (pp. 137, 185, 197, 240, 295–304, 331–332, 343, 451–453).
- **Ibn al-Fāriḍ** — `the great Egyptian mystic Ibn al-Farid (d. 1235)`, `the delicate poet and scholar
  from Cairo, who lived a solitary life first on the Muqattam Hill and then, for fifteen years, in the
  Hijaz, eventually returning to his hometown and dying there in 1235`; the `Khamriyya`, and the
  `Tffiyyat al-kubra`, `a poem of 756 verses`, which the Prophet in a dream bade him call
  `Nazm as-suluk, "Order of the Progress,"` (`N 542`) (pp. 39, 185, 305–308).
- **Ṣadruddīn Qonawī** — `Young Sadr-` `uddm Qonawi was to become his foremost interpreter (d.i274).`;
  in Anatolia `mystics like Sadruddm Qonawl transmitted and publicized Ibn cArabi's ideas`; his reply on
  Rūmī after his death — `"If Bayezid and Junayd had lived in this time, they would have seized his mantle"`
  (`N 464`) (pp. 295, 310, 346, 349).
- **Jīlī** — `(d. after 1408)`, `a descendant of the founder of the Qadiriyya order`, author of
  `Al-insan al-kdmil, "The Perfect Man,"` analyzed by Nicholson; `in Jili's theosophy the amr … is equated
  with the haqiqa muhammadiyya` (pp. 254, 311–313, 453).
- **Shabistarī** — `In 1311 Shabistari, who lived not far from Tabriz, wrote a Persian poem called
  Gulshan-i rdz, "Rose Garden of Mystery," as a reply to eighteen questions posed to him by a Sufi friend.`,
  its spread helped by `Lahiji's excellent commentary, composed in the second half of the fifteenth
  century` (pp. 311–312, 322).
- **Jāmī** — `(d. 1492)`, Naqshbandi, who revised Anṣārī's translation `in his Nafahat al-uns`; his
  `Lawcfih` written `in the same city of Herat 400 years later` and `has become one of the most widely used
  manuals of later Sufi teachings`; `called` the *Mathnawī* `"the Koran in Pahlavi"`; dedicated
  `Tuhfat al-ahrdr` to Ahrār (pp. 37, 51, 69, 117–122, 296, 314–316, 346, 395–396, 446).
- **Suhrawardī Maqtūl, the *shaykh al-ishrāq*** — `born in 1153 in the same place in northwestern Iran from
  which the founder of the Suhrawardiyya order came`, drawn to Aleppo by `al-Malik az-Zahir`, imprisoned at
  the lawyers' urging with Saladin's help, `he died in prison at the age of thirty-eight in 1191`, `That is
  why he is often called al-maqtiil, "he who was killed," to avoid confusion with the two other Suhrawardis`;
  `nearly fifty Arabic and Persian books`, culminating in the `Hikmat al-ishraq`; the `Haydkil cm-nur`
  `has been read in Muslim India throughout the centuries` (pp. 290–294, 442). **A third, different
  Suhrawardī.**
- **Molla Sadra of Shiraz** — `(d. 1640)`, `deeply influenced by Suhrawardian ideas` (p. 294, folio 263).
- **Shaʿrānī** — `Dhu D n-Nun's compatriot`, `the last great mystic of Egypt in the sixteenth century`,
  `(d. 1565)`, `whose literary legacy is interesting as a typical expression of later Sufi thought`, `a good
  interpreter of Sufism in the Near East in the sixteenth century`; `has described, in touching words, the
  dark night of the soul through which he had to pass for a month without being able to pray.`; heard `the
  voices of the creatures in the praise of God` (pp. 75–77, 188, 205, 280, 405, 434).
- **ʿAbduʾl-Ghanī an-Nabulusī** — `(d. 1728)`, `the Syrian scholar and mystic` whose contributions `still
  lack` a full evaluation (pp. 434–435).
- **Ghālib Dede, Gulshani, ʿItrī and the three Turkish Mathnawī commentators** — `Ghalib Dede (d. 1799)`,
  `the sheikh of the convent in Galata`, `perhaps the last true master of classical Turkish poetry`;
  `Gulshani, in the fifteenth century`; `cltri (seventeenth century)` among the best composers of Turkish
  classical music; `Ismacil Riisuhi Ankarawi (d. 1631)`, whose commentary `is still considered the best
  commentary available`; `Ismacil Haqqi Bursali, himself a fine mystical poet (d. 1724)`; and
  `Suleyman Nahifi (d. 1738)`, who `translated the entire work into Turkish verse, faithfully preserving
  the meter and the form` (pp. 291, 356–357).
- **Yunus Emre** — `(d. circa 1321)`, `Sultan Walad's contemporary`, `in central Anatolia`, who
  `transformed his Turkish mother tongue into a vehicle for mystical expression`; `Not much is known about
  Yunus Emre's life.`; `According to the most recent research, he died in 1321 at the age of 72; numerous
  places in Turkey claim to house his real tomb.`; `a disciple of a mystic called Tapduq Emre for forty
  years (the number of perseverance)` (pp. 64, 77, 137, 190, 310, 360–366).
- **Kaygusuz Abdal** — `a Bektashi mystic who lived in the fifteenth century, probably in the European
  provinces of the Ottoman Empire`; `He is said to have settled in Cairo to found a Bektashi convent;
  according to tradition, his tomb is on the Muqattam Hill, where many saints of Cairo are buried.`;
  `kaygusuz, "sans souci," has been used as a secret name for hashish, as Golpmarh has shown` (pp. 233,
  364–368).
- **Pir Sultan Abdal** — `executed in Sivas about 1560 because of alleged treasonous relations with the
  Persian Safawids`; alludes to `the rose that made Hallaj sigh` (pp. 233, 369).
- **Nesīmī** — `the Hurufi poet (executed in 1417) who considered himself "a new Mansur" and reenacted in
  his own life the passion and death of Hallaj`; `flayed alive in Aleppo in 1417` (pp. 105, 370, 443).
- **Barak Baba** — `a certain Barak Baba in the fourteenth century, who went around shouting like a bear
  and dancing like a monkey, according to the sources` (p. 366, folio 335).
- **Niyazi Misri, Eshrefoglu Rumi, Aq Shamsuddin, Ibrahim Haqqi Erzerumlu, Ismail Emre** — `Niyazi Misri
  (d. 1697)`, `a leading member of the influential Khalvetiyya order`; `Eshrefoglu RumI`, `a Qadirl mystic
  who lived in Iznik in the fifteenth century`; `Aq Shamsuddin` in the life of `Mehmet the Conqueror`;
  `Ibrahim Haqqi Erzerumlu, the eighteenth-century Sufi writer`; and `Ismail Emre` (`Yeni Yunus Emre`,
  `d. 1973`), `a blacksmith from Adana who can read no more than a few words`, producing mystical songs in
  trance `Since 1940` (pp. 244, 365–373).
- **Merkez Efendi and Sünbül Efendi** — `The sheikh of the Khalvati order in Istanbul, Sunbiil Efendi, in
  looking for a successor` sent his disciples for flowers; Merkez alone brought back `one flower` that
  `had finished its recollection`; `It was he who became the successor of Siinbiil Efendi, and one of the
  cemeteries along the Byzantine wall of Istanbul still bears his name.` (p. 77, folio 46). **Schimmel's
  own attribution: `a charming story was told to me by Turkish friends`.**
- **Shah Ismail (Khataʾi)** — `(d. 1524)`, founder of Safawid rule in Iran, `regarded by the Bektashis as
  one of their own leading poets`, who likened the beloved's eyebrows to `the basmala` (pp. 369, 444).
- **Others named once or twice** — `Khaqani`, `the greatest panegyrist of Iran (d. 1199)`, `a poet who
  cannot be called exactly a mystic` (pp. 47–48, 257); `Nizami` and his `great hymn on love in Khosrau
  Shlrln` (p. 324); `Hafiz`, `whose verses are the perfect flower of the gardens of Shiraz` (pp. 194, 319,
  340, 448); `Fakhruddin Iraqi`, `(d. isSg` [text-layer?: **two middle digits unreadable**]`)`, the Persian
  love poet of Multan and Konya, `buried in Damascus, close to the grave of Ibn cArabi`, and his `Lamacdt`
  (pp. 310, 322, 327, 383–385); `Maghribi`, `Ibn cArabi's faithful interpreter (d. ca. 1406)` (pp. 198,
  313); `ʿUrfi Shirazi`, `one of Akbar's court-poets (d. 1591)` (pp. 52, 165); `Ibn Qayyim al-Jauziyya
  (d. 1350), known as an orthodox Muslim` (p. 195); `Ibn Taymiy-` / `ya (d. igaS)` — **the year is not
  resolved: `g` is both 9 and 3 in this book, so the entry is either 1928 or 1328** (p. 304);
  `al-Jazuli (d. ca. 1465)`, author of `Dalffil al-khairat` (p. 257); `al-Busiri`, `the Egyptian poet`,
  author of `the famous Bur da` composed `toward the end of the thirteenth century`, miraculously cured by
  the Prophet, who `cast his mantle (burda) upon him` (p. 256); `ʿAlī al-Muttaqī (d. 1556)`, `one of the
  leading scholars of sixteenth-century India`, who `reworked the Hikam` (p. 282); `Maulana Qasim Kahi
  (d. 1582)`, `the most skillful Persian poet at the Mogul court in the late sixteenth century`, and his
  `A lif-name` (p. 454); `Muhammad Parsa`, the fifteenth-century Naqshbandi who `compared the Fusus to the
  soul and the Futuhat to the heart` (`N 396`) (p. 296); `Mir CA1I Shir Nawa'I (d. 1501)`, `the vizier, poet
  and Maecenas of artists`, `an initiate of the Naqshbandiyya` (p. 397); `CA1I ibn Husayn Waciz Kashifi`,
  author of the `Rashahat 'ayn al-hayat` (p. 396); `Muhsin Fayd Kashani`, `a seventeenth-century Persian
  author` (p. 331); `Bābā Ṭāhir`, `who died in Khorramabad in the first part of the eleventh century` — no
  year (p. 275); `Salman al-Farisi`, `later regarded as the patron saint of small artisans` (p. 59);
  `Uways al-Qarani` of Yemen, the eponym of the `uwaysi` initiation (pp. 59, 136, 293); `Abu Dharr
  al-Ghifari (d. 653)`, `"un socialiste avant la lettre," as Louis Massignon puts it`, `the prototype of the
  true faqir` (p. 59); `Abu Madyan, the patron saint of Tlemcen (d. 1126)` (pp. 278, 281); `Lalla Mimunah`
  in the Maghreb, `a poor negro woman`, `greatly venerated in North Africa`, who `ran behind the departing
  boat, walking on the water`, praying only `"Mlmunah knows God, and God knows Mlmunah."` (p. 461);
  `Sayyida Zaynab`, `whose sanctuary in Cairo is visited so often that it even provides the setting for two
  modern Egyptian novels` (p. 458); `Sayyida Nafisa`, named as one of `the female descendants of the
  Prophet` — **the sentence is cut off at a chunk boundary** (p. 457); a woman `(d. 824)` **whose name falls
  on the previous page and is not in these notes**, `her tomb in Cairo is still visited by the faithful`
  (p. 458); `Fatima of Nishapur (d. 849)`, Ahmad Khiḍrūya's wife, who `consorted with Dhu 3 n-Nun and
  Bayezld Bistami` until the henna on her hands ended it — `it may well be that a legendary motif was
  applied to Fatima` (p. 458); and `a certain Fatima of Cordova`, `who, in spite of her age of ninety-five
  years, was beautiful and fresh like a young girl (N 629)` (p. 461).

### F. Folk-tale figures, offices, communities and groups

- **The Sindhi heroines of the *surs*** — `Sassui` and `Punhun, the prince of Kecch in Balochistan`
  (pp. 139, 422–423); `Sohni` and `Mehanwal` (p. 422); `Marui`, kidnapped `by the mighty ruler c Umar of
  Omarkot` (p. 423); `Nurl, the fisher maid who wins the heart of Prince Tamachi` — `a story that can be
  historically located in the late fifteenth century` (p. 423); and `the Hindu tale of Sorathi, in which
  the king offers even his head to the minstrel` (p. 423). Indexed bare at pp. 521–526, with
  `Tamachi, Jam (i5th century), 392` and `Llla Chanesar, 391`.
- **The Panjabi figures of Hīr–Rāñjhā** — `Hlr`, `Ranjha`, `the Yogi Balmath`, `Saida Khera` and
  `The lame uncle, Kaido` (p. 420, folio 389); **the two endings**: `According to one version, they are
  poisoned by Hir's family; in another version they die from thirst in the desert during their pilgrimage
  to Mecca and are eventually united in death (or, by drinking the water of life, in eternal life).`
  (p. 419, folio 388).
- **Khiḍr** — `identified with the mysterious companion of Moses mentioned in Sura 18, is the patron saint
  of travelers, the immortal who drank from the water of life`; invests mystics with the khirqa; `there
  were even women mystics who were guided by Khidr himself and received spiritual instruction from him`
  (`N 332`); Ibrāhīm ibn al-Khawāṣṣ `would refuse the company of Khidr … because his graceful company
  seemed to negate his perfect trust in God alone` (pp. 48, 137, 150, 243, 295, 458, 534).
- **Buhlūl** — `a strange character who lived during the caliphate of Harun ar-Rashld (d. 809)`, the first
  `"wise idiot"` of Islamic lore (pp. 50, 328).
- **Offices and groups glossed in the `INDEX OF SUBJECTS`** — `Shaykh ash-shuyukh (head of the Sufis in
  medieval Baghdad), 245` (p. 541); `Molla Hunkar (head of the Mevlevis), 324` (p. 536);
  `Tarlqa-yi muhammadiyya ("the Muhammadan Path," founded by Nasir Mu-hammad cAndalib in Delhi)`, locators
  `227, 373, 374, 380, 403, 404` (p. 542); `Tacziya (mourning for the martyrs of Kerbela), 75, 387`
  (p. 536); `Muridun (mystical movement in Algarve in the 12th century), 264` (p. 537);
  `Marifati (mystical folk song in Bengal), 401` (p. 536); `Mushd'-ira (meeting of poets, particularly in
  Indo-Pakistan), 374` (p. 537); `Kufl (connected with Kufa: Id-kftfi, "non-Kufic")` (p. 535);
  `Nafas ar-rahmdn ("the breath of the Mer-ciful," coming from Yemen)` (p. 537).
- **The *abdāl*, the Forty and the Seven** — `Kirklareli, "the county of the Forty," in the European
  province of Turkey`; `Marrakesh, for instance, claims to be protected by the Seven`; in Turkey `abdal` is
  `a sort of sobriquet for a certain group of mystics`, mainly in the Bektashi tradition (p. 233,
  folio 202).
- **Dervish groups named** — the `Aissawiyya` in North Africa, the `Rifaci` dervishes, the `Heddawa` of
  Morocco (pp. 240–241, 272); the `Ni c matullahl dervishes in Iran` (p. 312); the `donme`, `the followers
  of Sabbatay Zwi in the seventeenth century`, who `had close connections with mystical groups and used
  Sufi poetry in their meetings` (p. 368).
- **Buildings named for their use of music in medicine** — `the basin in the asylum attached to the Great
  Mosque in Divrigi (Anatolia, built in 1228)` and `the Bayezit Kiilliyesi in Edirne (built 1370), with
  its concert-hall arrangement in the asylum` (p. 211, folio 180). Both years print cleanly.
- **Khānqāh Saʿīd as-Suʿadāʾ and the Mevlânâ Müzesi** — `The khanqdh Scfid as-Su^ada*` in Mamluk Egypt,
  `founded by the Ayyubid Sultan Saladin in 1173`; and `the Mevlana Miizesi in Konya`, given as
  `a fine example` of a khānqāh whose dervishes lived in small cells (p. 263, folio 232).
- **Mary's tomb near Ephesus** — `Mary's alleged tomb near Ephesus`, whose veneration `proves that the deep
  love for the model of purity ... is still a living force in Islamic countries` (p. 460, folio 429).
- **Anatolia's women's shrines** — `Anatolia … can boast of a large number of small shrines where more or
  less historical women are buried—simple village girls and noble virgins` (p. 464, folio 433).
- **Non-archive rulers, scholars and moderns named in passing** — `Mahmud of Ghazna (d. 1030)`,
  `the conqueror of northwestern India and supporter of the caliph and of Muslim orthodoxy` (pp. 120, 221,
  323, 520) — **not mapped: the archive row's principal figure is Ayāz, not Maḥmūd**; `Yazid`,
  `the second Omayyad caliph`, `responsible for the tragedy of Kerbela … in 680` (p. 81); `the caliph
  cUmar (634-44)` (p. 145); `Atatürk`, who `in 1925, abolished the dervish orders in Turkey` (pp. 269,
  272, 368, 374); `Hasan al-Banna3`, `the founder of the Muslim Brethren`, who `learned his methods of
  channeling the enthusiasm of people into religious activities` from the Sufis (p. 435);
  `Yakup Kadri Karaosmanoglu`, who published `Nur Baba` in `1922` (p. 372); `Siyah Kalam`,
  `the fifteenth-century painter` (p. 366); `Anquetil Duperron`, who `in 1801 issued its Latin translation
  under the title Oupnek^at` (p. 392); `Hasan-i Sabbah`, `Nizar` and `Alamut` (p. 124); `Averroes
  (d. 1198)` (pp. 123–124, 295); `Ibn Sina (Avicenna, d. 1037)`, who `has become the representative of dry
  rationalism, although he was as much of a mystical thinker as some of those classified as Sufis`
  (pp. 50, 324); and the Western scholars — Massignon, Nicholson, Ritter, Corbin, Nasr, Margaret Smith,
  Tor Andrae, Arberry, Meier, Zaehner, Nwyia, Gramlich, Goldziher, Izutsu, Rudolf Otto, Gershom Scholem,
  Tholuck and Schimmel herself (pp. 41–57, 75, 161–198, 367–368).

### H. Miracle and legend types with no named subject, and the book's own frame for them

*These are the Legends and Miracles items that belong to no archive row and to no one named figure. They
are Schimmel's own typology of karāmāt and are recorded once here rather than scattered.*

- **The technical distinction, given as the theologians'**: `the saint's miracles are called karamat,
  "charismata," whereas the prophet's miracles are classified as rmfjizat [text-layer?: muʿjizāt], "what
  renders others incapable to do the same," and the two types must never be confused` (`K 59-61`) (p. 237,
  folio 206); `The general term for anything extraordinary is khariq ul-^dda [text-layer?: khāriq
  al-ʿāda], "what tears the custom" (of God)` (p. 237, folio 206).
- **The scale of the literature**: `Subki distinguishes twenty-five main types` of miracle, and `whole
  collections have been composed to show the various kinds of miracles performed by Muslim saints.`
  (p. 237, folio 206); `The comprehensive study` is Yūsuf an-Nabhānī's `Jamp karamat al-auliya?` (p. 237,
  folio 206). Schimmel's own comparative caution: `the miracles of saints are the same all over the world.`
  (p. 42, folio 11).
- **Miracles distrusted from inside**: `Miracles, for instance, could be interpreted as divine ruses, for
  they are still connected with the world` (p. 159, folio 128); the subject index files `Miracles …
  questionable, 19, 20, 238, 248`, `Saints … questionable, 20, 159, 238`, and, under `Unio mystica`,
  `denied to miracle mongers, 212` (pp. 536, 540, 542).
- **Types the book names**: *firāsa* / cardiognosy — `"Beware of the firdsa [discernment] of the faithful,"
  it was said, "for he sees by God's light"` (`B 588`), and `Innumerable stories are told about a sheikh's
  insight into his disciple's heart` (p. 236, folio 205); *buruz*, `exteriorization, i.e., he could be
  present at different places at the same time` (p. 236; index p. 530); *ṭayy al-makān* / *ṭayy al-arḍ*,
  `In cases of danger the sheikh might suddenly appear in the midst of a band of robbers to drive them
  away` (p. 236; index p. 542); *himma*, `that "high spiritual ambition," or "power," that is strong enough
  to keep safe those who believe in the master` (p. 110); *munāqara*, `contest of saints` (p. 110; index
  `Munaqara (contest of saints), 79, 211`, p. 537); vicarious suffering, `when the disciple is hurt, the
  master's body shows the traces` (p. 237); `"To enter under the burden of the sick"` (`N 397`) (p. 236);
  *tawajjuh*, `the concentration of the two partners upon each other that results in experiences of
  spiritual unity, faith healing, and many other phenomena` (p. 397); `Uflemek`, healing by breathing on a
  person (index p. 542); the `Salat maqliiba`, prayer performed hanging upside down (index p. 540); and
  `Fertility miracles are one of the most popular aspects of saint worship` (p. 237), including the motif
  `that a saint can grant a hundred-year-old couple three sons only to call them back when the parents do
  not behave decently.` (p. 237).
- **The saints' office in the world**: `Every night the autad must go round the whole universe, and if there
  should be any place on which their eyes have not fallen, next day some imperfection will appear in that
  place, and they must then inform the Qutb, in order that he may fix his attention on the weak spot and
  that by his blessing the imperfection may be removed.` (`H 228`) (p. 234, folio 203) — **reported by
  Hujwīrī, no other speaker named**; `It is through the blessings of the saints that rain falls from
  heaven; on the places touched by their sacred feet plants spring up, and thanks to their help the Muslims
  will gain victory over the unbelievers.` (p. 234, folio 203; index `Rain … falls thanks to the activity
  of the saints, 203`, p. 539); `"Out of jealousy, God puts a veil upon them and keeps them concealed from
  the public"` (`N 442`) and `The hadith qudsi, "verily my saints are under my domes, and only I know them,"
  is often cited to support this idea.` (p. 234); and `many stories are told about secret meetings of saints
  on certain mountains in which, sometimes, an adept is allowed to partake.` (p. 233, folio 202).
- **Food, animals and fire**: `a buck gives milk so that unexpected guests can be entertained; food and
  drink are mysteriously provided in the middle of the desert. Even a great mystic like Hallaj is credited
  with this kind of display.` (p. 238, folio 207), with Schimmel's explanation — `For people famous for
  their hospitality and accustomed to traveling long distances through uninhabited areas, the miraculous
  production of food and water and, very often, sweetmeat would certainly be one of the most coveted powers
  of a saint.` (p. 238, folio 207); `He can, then, sanctify a dog by a mere glance (N 419); even the frogs
  may talk to him, and the crocodile, with tears in its eyes, is willing to surrender its prey to him.`
  (p. 239, folio 208); `the infant saint` who `will not drink his mother's milk in the daytime during the
  month of Ramadan, but only after sunset.` (p. 239, folio 208); fire that does not burn (`X 179`; `N 552`)
  (p. 240, folio 209); and `In a religion in which, according to popular belief, even the sakln a
  [text-layer?: sakīna] (divine presence) is supposed to appear in the form of a white cat` (p. 240,
  folio 209), with the subject index's `Cats: in convents, 209-11` (p. 530).
- **The *majdhūb* and the living dead**: `"attracted" people, who are completely lost and submerged in the
  divine unity, their eyes "like two cups filled with blood" (N 479), inspiring awe` (p. 136, folio 105);
  and the saint's spirit `is thought to be alive, able to participate actively in the affairs of this world,
  often appearing in dreams and guiding the wayfarer on his Path, for "the friends of God do not die."`
  (p. 136, folio 105).
- **Investiture and election**: `Legends then tell that a green bird descended upon the head of the elect,
  and the dervishes had no choice but to believe this sign (N 574)—a motif that is also known from tales
  about medieval papal elections.` (p. 267, folio 236); and sheikhs who `would not accept a new disciple
  unless they had seen his name written on the Well-preserved Tablet among their followers` (`N 536, 538`)
  (pp. 132–133).
- **The Indian frame, which is the archive's most useful piece of this material**: `Sufi hagiography in
  India describes magic-working contests between Sufi saints and yogis or mysterious conversions of Indian
  yogis by Sufis.` — reported as what the hagiography contains, not endorsed, and sourced to
  `Simon Digby, "Encounters with Jogis in Indian Sufi Hagiography," mimeographed, School of Oriental and
  African Studies (London, 1970).` (p. 388, folio 357); `They would usually admit the possibility of
  certain miracles performed by the yogis but would maintain the importance of following the Islamic Path
  for the performance of true miracles.` (pp. 388–389); `as late as the eighteenth century the view was
  expounded that even the greatest ascetic practices and the resulting miracles of a yogi could not
  properly be compared to even the smallest sign of divine grace manifested in a faithful Muslim.` (p. 389,
  folio 358); and `the grotesque stories told about several Sufis of India. Their limbs became separated
  from them during the dhikr and recollected God each in its own Way.` (p. 204, folio 173), sourced to
  Dārā Shikōh, Aʿzam Tattawī and Mīr Dard.
- **Schimmel's own first-person reports** — `or—as I was told several times in Turkey—the master might
  appear, in spiritualized form, at a sick person's bed in order to cure him or at least relieve him
  temporarily from his pain.` (p. 236, folio 205); and the inner heat of a singing mystic in a car in
  `December 1958`, which `warmed up the car to such an extent that the windows became fogged and those of
  us traveling with him felt no discomfort from the cold throughout the last hour of our journey.` (p. 368,
  folio 337).

## Citable passages

Fifteen kept from the 145 candidates the twenty-nine Pass-1 chunks proposed. Chosen for what a
shrine entry can do with them — a named site, a practice described, an institution defined, an
argument stated compactly — not for eloquence. **This book is in English, so no gloss is supplied;
where a passage carries a Persian or Arabic word it is kept exactly as printed.** Page references
are reproduced exactly as the chunk notes give them, and every `[text-layer?]` flag is kept. Where
a quotation's wording sits on or beside one of the damage classes inventoried in §3 below, the
bullet says so and says it should be checked against the page before it enters an entry.

- `His shrine, called that of Data Ganj Bakhsh, is still a popular place of pilgrimage in Lahore.`
  (p. 119, folio 88) — the book's one explicit statement of continuing pilgrimage at
  **data-darbar**. `Data Ganj Bakhsh` prints clean; the saint's own name does not — chunk 006
  records **six different spellings of `Hujwiri` across pp. 118–120** (`Hujwirl`, `Hujwirfs`,
  `HujwIri`, `al-HujwIri`, `al-Hujwln`, clean `Hujwiri`), so the surrounding page is not clean.
- `He later came to Lahore, the capital of the Ghaznawids in India, and he died there in 1071.`
  (p. 119, folio 88) — **all four digits of `1071` survive undamaged** (chunk 006 checked them), so
  the one-year gap against the archive's `1072` is a source disagreement, not damage. See §2.
- `The "fair" of Sehwan—celebrated between Shawwal 18 and 20—has become notorious for its rather
  illicit events.` (p. 386, folio 355) — the ʿurs of **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, with its hijrī dates.
  Chunk 021 reports the four-digit years in its running text as clean, but the Shawwāl day-numbers
  are two-digit and were not separately checked; check them against the page.
- `A lingam close to the actual tomb proves the Shivait background of some of the rites.` (p. 386,
  folio 355) — the book's own argument about **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, and the sentence on which
  chunk 021's eight Shiva-row attachments rest.
- `Around his sanctuary strange groups of bl-shar^, "lawless," Sufis (malang) gathered, dressed in
  black` (p. 386, folio 355). **Two damage classes in one phrase:** `bl` is `bī` under the
  `ī`→`l` long-vowel class, and `^` is the ʿayn/hamza class (§3.2, §3.3). The book glosses the word
  itself at `Bi-shar'- ("without law," dervishes who do not cling to the shan^a)` (p. 530,
  folio 499). Check the printed form before quoting.
- `still celebrated in Lahore near the Shalimar Garden on the last Saturday in March, is famous as
  Meld Chiraghdn` [text-layer?: Melā Chirāghān] (p. 415, folio 384) — the ʿurs of
  **madho-lal-hussain**, with a named locality and a fixed calendar rule. `Meld Chiraghdn` carries
  the `ā`→`d` class twice; the flag is the Pass-1 worker's and is kept.
- `His tomb in Bhit Shah is one of the loveliest sanctuaries in the Muslim world.` (p. 421,
  folio 390) — **bhit-bhit-shah**, and one of the few architectural judgements in the book.
- `Some dervishes who live at the threshold of his tomb perform this music in their gatherings on
  Thursday nights` (p. 421, folio 390) — a resident community and a weekly practice at
  **bhit-bhit-shah**, which no other source in the queue has supplied.
- `the sacred mountain Hinglaj in Balochistan, which is often mentioned as "the goal" in Sindhi
  mystical poetry` (p. 421, folio 390) — **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir**. Note that this
  row was reached from the archive row's own *name*, not from `toponym_map.txt` (chunk 023); see
  §4.
- `The Sufi sheikhs and the feudal lords in the country lost large numbers of disciples and workers,
  whereas the dargah in Jhok flourished.` (p. 428, folio 397) — economy and rivalry at
  **dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed**, stated as a cause of the events of 1718.
- `In wrath a saint can turn a flower into an alligator (as happened in Mango Pir near Karachi)`
  (p. 238, folio 207) — the whole of what this book gives on **shrine-of-pir-mangho**: a
  ten-word parenthesis with no footnote, no siglum and no saint's name. See §4.
- `are still being sung by Pakistani musicians at the door of the master's tomb in Multan` (p. 384,
  folio 353) — continuing practice at the tomb of Bahāʾuddīn Zakariyā,
  **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**.
- `hence the expression post-nishin or sajjada-nishln, "he who sits on the carpet," for the
  successor` (p. 267, folio 236) — the book's own definition of shrine custodianship. `sajjada-nishln`
  carries the `ī`→`l` class.
- `the curs, "wedding," the anniversary of the saint's death` (p. 271, folio 240) — the book's own
  definition of ʿurs. `curs` is the book's printed form: the initial `c` is Schimmel's own
  superscript ʿayn flattened by the text layer (the book says so on p. 29, folio XXVI), not an
  invented letter. Quote it as printed or not at all.
- `Dargdh (dervish convent)` (p. 530, folio 499) — the book's own gloss in the `INDEX OF SUBJECTS`,
  and an **institutional rather than architectural** definition, which matters for any entry that
  uses the word. `Dargdh` carries the `ā`→`d` class.

**Two strong candidates were deliberately left out and are named here so the reason is visible.**
`Mdlang (dervish at the sanctuary in Sehwan), 355` and `Mela chiraghdn (festival in Lahore), 384`
are both printed on **PDF 536**, the page where the index's two columns collide in the text layer
(§3.6) and which is flagged for re-checking against the image. Both are genuinely useful glosses —
and both are also the sharpest instance of the bare-index question in §1.3, because their *glosses*
state place-tradition material while their *locators* are bare pointers. A human should settle
PDF 536 first.

## Doubts and review points

This is everything a human must check before a fact from this book enters an entry. It is assembled
from `s_doubts.md`, the largest single extract in the corpus (4,880 lines, 430,535 bytes), which is
the record of what twenty-nine Pass-1 workers were unsure of. Nothing has been added from general
knowledge (CLAUDE.md RULE 2), including in §2: only the book's reading and the archive's are
reported, never a third value.

**One standing fact about the flags.** The transcription of this book carries **zero pre-existing
`[OCR?]`, `[illegible]` or `[blank page]` markers** — every chunk from 001 to 029 says so
explicitly. Every `[text-layer?]` flag in these notes was placed by a Pass-1 worker on a word they
could read but not trust. There is therefore no `[illegible]` flag anywhere in this book's notes to
carry, and that absence is a checked finding, not an omission. (The brief records one `[blank page]`
marker in the whole transcription; chunk 023 establishes that **p. 441 is not it** — that page
prints the words `This page intentionally left blank` as content.)

---

### 1. The three unruled mapping questions — these are Rauf's to rule (CLAUDE.md RULE 5)

The Pass-1 workers declined all three, consistently, and **enumerated the ids each decline would
take, on one line per chunk, so a reversal is one step per chunk**. Nothing below is ruled here.

#### 1.1 Imprint cities — a publisher's address inside a footnote or a bibliography entry

**What the workers did: declined, in 24 of the 29 chunks, in 44 separate enumerated decline lines.**
Chunks 003 and 004 checked their imprints and found none that resolved to an archive place, and
recorded that negative rather than leaving it silent; chunks 028 and 029 contain no imprints at all
(they are index). The declining chunks are **001, 005, 006, 007, 008, 009, 010, 011, 012, 013, 014,
015, 016, 017, 018, 019, 020, 021, 022, 023, 024, 025, 026 and 027**. (The brief's figure is 23
independent readings; my count of chunks carrying at least one archive-relevant decline is 24. The
difference is worth one look, not an argument.)

**How many mappings turn on it.** Each decline line names the ids it would take: Lahore **35 rows**,
Karachi **11**, Peshawar **10**, Multan **8**, Rohri **2**, Hyderabad **1**. The heaviest single
chunk is 026, twenty-three pages of bibliography, which alone fires `lahore` **20 times**, `karachi`
**13**, `hyderabad` **12** and `peshawar` **once**; chunk 025 fires `karachi` 8, `lahore` 7,
`hyderabad` 4 and `rohri` 1. Reversing this silently would attach "a book was printed here" to 35
Lahore rows dozens of times over.

- **Chunk 026 is the one place a worker FIRED ruling (a) on a non-imprint toponym inside the
  apparatus, and said they were uncomfortable doing it.** `"Les entretiens de Lahore."` (p. 486,
  folio 455) is the only non-imprint `Lahore` in twenty-three pages; it is a toponym inside an
  **article title**, which §6(ii) does not cover, so all 35 Lahore rows were taken. The worker's own
  words: *"I am not comfortable with it and say so rather than deviating quietly."* **This is the
  single inconsistency in the book's imprint handling and it is one bullet to strike or keep.**
- **The name-element sub-case, declined once.** `Multan` occurs in chunk 016 only inside the nisba
  `BahaDuddin Zakariya Mul-` / `tani` (p. 310, folio 279) — a name element, physically broken across
  a line break. Declined; 8 Multan rows. Nothing is lost, because the man himself is mapped to
  **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya** by the person rule.
- **The demonym sub-case is handled TWO OPPOSITE WAYS on what is the same occurrence of the same
  word, and this is the sharpest item in §1.1.** Chunk 022 **mapped** `Peshawari`, in the list
  `Arab, Hindu, Turk, or Peshawari` (p. 417, folio 386), taking all 10 Peshawar rows and calling it
  "the weakest mapping here". Chunk 028 **declined** the index entry `Peshawari, 386` (p. 522,
  folio 491), calling it "doubly refused … a nisba, not a place name". **The index locator `386` is
  a printed folio, and folio 386 is PDF 417 — the index entry points at precisely the occurrence
  chunk 022 mapped.** One word, one occurrence, two contrary rulings, ten rows either way.
- **Two further imprint-adjacent refusals worth naming.** Chunk 011 declined `rohri` because its
  single hit is a nisba inside an author's name, `Bedil Rohriwaro` (p. 216, folio 185, footnote 93);
  chunk 025 declined the same nisba at p. 472 for the same reason; chunk 027 refused it again inside
  `Bedil Rohriwaro (d. 1872), 396` (p. 513, folio 482). Two ids
  (**gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**) turn on it.
- **Three `Lahore` imprints in chunk 025 did not even fire the key**, because the text layer broke
  or truncated the word: `La-`/`hore` (p. 468), `Lahor.` (p. 470, right-margin truncation) and
  `La-`/`hore` (p. 475). **If the ruling reverses, those three need hand-checking** — a substring
  count cannot see them. The same is true of `Hydera-`/`bad` in chunk 026 (p. 487), which is why
  that chunk counts 12 `hyderabad` firings where the measurement counted 11.

#### 1.2 The province-key gap — `sind`, `sindh`, `punjab`, `balochistan`, `kashmir` are not keys

**What the workers did: recorded the gap and refused to invent a join, in 12 chunks — 003, 004, 005,
008, 013, 015, 019, 020, 021, 024, 025 and 029.** `toponym_map.txt` was built by substring match on
`location_short` and emitted only city-, district- and locality-level keys, while **roughly thirty
archive rows carry `Sindh` in `location_short`**.

**How many mappings turn on it, and what it costs.**

- **Chunks 015 and 025 reach zero archive ids because of it.** Chunk 015 states its own result
  plainly: *"This chunk maps to ZERO archive ids, and that is the finding, not a failure."* Its
  p. 279 (folio 248) carries the trees of Sind, the vow-flags, the sweetmeats, the name `Ghauth
  Bakhsh` in `Sind and Balochistan`, and the Old Sindhi songs — all province-level statements about
  Pakistani territory, reachable by nothing. Chunk 025's pp. 464–465 are *"the densest Sind-and-Punjab
  pages in the whole back matter"* and that material *"is currently reachable by no archive id at
  all."*
- **Chunk 005 is the largest single reversal.** Ḥallāj wanders through Sind; `the seeds he sowed
  there grew in later centuries in the mystical poetry of this province` (p. 98, folio 67); the
  Sindhi and Siraiki verse of pp. 106–107 is saturated with him. The worker's own estimate: *"If
  Rauf intends province-level toponyms to fire ruling (a), this chunk would attach the Sind material
  to every Sindh row (~30) and the Panjabi-song material to every Punjab row, which would make this
  one of the higher-yield chunks in the book."*
- Chunk 003 puts the same figure on one sentence: `they also reached Sind, the lower Indus valley,
  and laid the foundation of a Muslim rule that still continues in present Pakistan` (p. 61,
  folio 30) — *"this one sentence would attach to roughly thirty Sindh rows."*
- **Kashmir has the same gap and no one has costed it.** Chunk 025: `Kashmir` appears twice as a real
  place (p. 465; p. 470) but `kashmir` is not a key — **sharada-peeth** is indexed only under `sharda`
  and `neelum valley`. *"If Rauf wants Kashmir-level material to reach **sharada-peeth**, the map
  needs the key, not this chunk's notes."*

#### 1.3 Bare index entries — a pointer, or a statement?

**What the workers did: declined 16 toponym entries across the three index chunks (027, 028, 029),
worth 116 row-attachments — while mapping 10 row-attachments from equally bare index headwords in
the same pages.**

- **Chunk 028 declined nine, on one line each**: `Lahore, 88, 225, 239, 345, 361, 384, 388` (p. 520,
  folio 489, **35 rows**); `Karachi, 207` (p. 519, folio 488, **11**); `Peshawari, 386` (p. 522,
  folio 491, **10**); `Multan, 67, 82, 344, 352, 353, 354, 433` (p. 521, folio `49°`
  [text-layer?: 490], **8**); `Sialkot, 367 .` (p. 525, folio 494, **5**); `Rohri, 396, 397`
  (p. 524, folio 493, **2**); `Sargodha, 388` (p. 524, folio 493, **1**); `Pakpattan, 346, 348, 384`
  (p. 522, folio 491, **1**); `Jhok, 395, 397` (p. 518, folio 487, **1**). Total **74 rows**.
- **Chunk 027 declined five**: `Bhit Shah, 383, 390` (p. 513, folio 482); `Hala, 390` (p. 516,
  folio 485); `Hyderabad Sind, 372 n, 390, 397` (p. 517, folio 486); `Hinglaj, 390` (p. 516,
  folio 485); `Bulrri, 397` (p. 513, folio 482). Five rows; four of the five are already reached by
  the person rule, so only **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** would actually gain — and the
  worker flags that one as *"the likeliest reversal of the four"*, because `Hinglaj` matches an
  archive row's own **name** rather than a `toponym_map.txt` key.
- **Chunk 029 declined two, and both have a GLOSS that is place-tradition material rather than a
  bare pointer.** `Mela chiraghdn (festival in Lahore), 384` (p. 536, folio 505, **35 rows**) — the
  worker's own words: *"a human should weigh this one specifically, because it is not a bare
  pointer: the book's own GLOSS, not the locator, states that the festival is in Lahore, which is
  place-tradition material of exactly the kind ruling (a) exists to catch."* And `Mdlang (dervish at
  the sanctuary in Sehwan), 355` (p. 536, folio 505, **2 rows**) — same shape, same argument.
  **Both are printed on PDF 536, the colliding-column page.**
- **Against those 16 declines, the two rules pull in opposite directions on identical evidence.**
  Chunk 028 **mapped** `Krishna, 271, 434` (**2 rows**) and `Shiva, 355` (**8 rows**) — equally bare
  index headwords — because the 22 September many-row PERSON rule takes them. The worker states the
  tension exactly: *"This is the one place where the brief's §6(ii) reasoning and the 22 September
  person rule pull in opposite directions, and I have followed the person rule because §6(ii) is
  written about ruling (a) only. It is the first thing to ask Rauf."* Chunk 029 mapped the same two
  Krishna rows again from four words inside another entry's parenthetical gloss, `Gopis (Krishna's
  cowgirls), 271, 434` (p. 533).
- **Scale of the question:** all thirteen of chunk 027's mappings, all thirty-two of chunk 028's and
  all five of chunk 029's rest on index entries and on nothing else — **fifty id-attachments with no
  supporting sentence of running prose anywhere in those chunks.** Chunk 027 puts it well: *"The
  value of this chunk is navigational"* — it tells a consolidator which folios to go back to.

#### 1.4 A FOURTH question, raised once and not one of the three — the silsila join

Recorded here because chunk 015 calls it *"the single point a human most needs to rule on"* and
because no settled ruling licenses it. The archive has **thirteen rows whose `silsila` is
`Suhrawardi`**, and pp. 275–276 and 278–279 are the book's account of how that order began — but
Abū Najīb, Abū Ḥafṣ ʿUmar and ʿAbduʾl-Qādir al-Gīlānī are the `principal_figure` of no row, ruling
(a) is about toponyms and the 18/22 September rulings are about principal figures. The thirteen, on
one line: **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht**, **tomb-of-baha-al-halim-uch-sharif**, **tomb-of-javindi-bibi**, **shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-daula-daryai**, **shrine-of-hazrat-muhammad-ayub-shah-bukhari**, **wadpagga-sharif**, **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**.
Chunk 015's own note: *"If Rauf rules that an order's founding narrative attaches to every row of
that silsila, this chunk becomes one of the richest in the wave and the material is already
assembled, page-referenced, ready to attach."* A related string coincidence was also refused: chunk
021 declined to map on `mujaddid-i alf-i tlidni` [text-layer?: mujaddid-i alf-i thānī] (p. 398,
folio 367) against the two rows carrying the silsila string `Naqshbandi-Mujaddidi`.
